BURRILLVILLE, R. I.-The Assembly-Harrisville. Built in 1934, this charming building was donated to the town by the Austin T. 

Levy family for the encouragement of local theatre art group productions. It is located by the picturesque Mill's Pond at the Stillwater 

Dam. The spire in the background is that of the Universalist Church, built in 1886. 

Photo by Earl H. Goodison

GOVERNOR J. JOSEPH GARRAHY

Foreword BY HIS EXCELLENCY, J. JOSEPH GARRAHY Governor of Rhode Island 

It is my pleasure to submit to you this fourteenth Digest of Annual Reports, which summarizes the activities of Rhode Island's state agencies. 

The scope of programs provided by our departments and commissions increases each year, as we serve the people of Rhode Island. While this Digest reflects only a summary of our accomplishments, I invite anyone desiring information to contact the specific agency directly. 

On behalf of all Rhode Islanders, I thank all those who helped bring about the achievements of 1976-1977. 1327159

CENTRAL FALLS, R. I.-Cogswell Tower in Jenks Park, was built in 

1904 and is listed in the National Register of Historic Places. It was built upon Dexter's Ledge, a lookout site for Narragansett Indians during King Philip's War. From here the chiefs directed the ambush and massacre of Captain Pierce and his men in March, 1676. 

Photo by Earl H. Goodison

ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS 

The preparation of this issue of the Digest involved the talents and assistance of a number of state agencies and individuals. Graphs were prepared by the Rhode Island Statewide Planning Program, the Health 

Department, and the Defense Civil Preparedness Agency. Special thanks go to Mansuet Giusti and Peter Ouimette for assistance in the preparation of graphs; to Chester Browning (Department of Economic De velopment) and to Earl H. Goodison (Department of Transportation) 

for photos; and to Marlene McCarthy (Department of Administration) for this year's pictorial theme.

BRISTOL, R. I.-Colt State Park is a picturesque area along a three mile drive around shoreline of the former Colt family estate on the east side of Narragansett Bay. The park features playing fields, a farm, a chapel and picnic areas on the Bay.

EXECUTIVE DEPARTMENT Office of the Adjutant General MAJOR GENERAL LEONARD HOLLAND, Adjutant General Established 1856-Statutory Authority, Chapter 30-2 

Main Office-Armory of Mounted Commands, 1051 North Main Street, Providence 02904 

Full Time State Paid Employees 

Federally Paid Employees

Total State Expenditures 1976-1977 Total Federal Expenditures 

72 539 $681,503.10 $20,137,308.67

The Adjutant General in his capacity as Commanding General and Chief of Staff to the Governor, who is the Commander-In-Chief, directs the establishment, organization and maintenance of the military force of the state, which is comprised of units of the Army and Air National Guard, which are reserve components of the United States Army and 

Air Force. 

The mission of the National Guard is twofold: (1) to prepare for immediate mobilization in the event of war or national emergency; and (2) to maintain peace, order, and public safety in the state in time of disaster or when otherwise directed by the Governor. 

Further information is contained in the annual report published by the Adjutant General. 

Rhode Island Defense Civil Preparedness 

Agency 

SANTO AMATO, Director Established 1950-Statutory Authority, Chapter 30-15 

of General Laws of 1956, as amended 

Office-State House, Providence 02903 

3

Total Federal Reimbursement 

20 $498,357.00 $397,957.00

This agency has the responsibility of planning and coordinating with the federal government the direction of state effort in pre disaster, disaster and post disaster situations. The agency accomplishes its function. through the administration of nine separate programs, each of which is either fully or partially funded by the federal government on a reimbursement basis.

Highlights 

ADMINISTRATION: In fiscal year 1977, the federal govern ment allocated $238,270.00 to the state and ten participating subdivisions for the administration of the preparedness program on a matching funds basis. 

• DISASTER PLANNING PROGRAM: The Agency, maintains the Rhode Island Defense Civil Preparedness Plan which includes the state's Nuclear Accident or Incident Control Plan, and the state's Chemical/Biological Accident or Incident Control Plan. The agency is responsible for the supervision and coordination of Rhode 

Island's Nuclear Civil Protection Plan. This includes: the maintenance of the Nuclear Fallout Shelter Program (NFSP), (Public 

Fallout Shelters); the Community Shelter Plan (CSP) which tells citizens what to do and where to go in case of a nuclear attack; the 

Rhode Island Crisis Relocation Plan, which is currently in the preparation phase.

The Peacetime Disaster Assistance Program is funded by a grant from the Department of Housing and Urban Development, Federal Disaster Assistance Administration. The program is responsible for: 

preparing a comprehensive peacetime disaster plan; providing guidance to city, town, and state agencies in responding to major disasters and emergencies; assisting city, town, and state agencies in preparing applications for, and obtaining federal assistance avail ble under the Federal Disaster Relief Act of 1974 (Public Law 

93-288).

RADIOLOGICAL DEFENSE AND CHEMICAL AGENT PRO- 

GRAM: The Agency provided for the placement, inspection, calibration and repair to 10,722 radiation detection and measuring instruments of several types throughout the state. Emergency

stockpile of 10,670 dosimeters (personal radiation detection meters) were checked and serviced for operability. 

The agency maintained an emergency response vehicle equipped to respond on a 24 hour basis to all radiation and chemical accidents or incidents. During the year response was made to 10 incidents involving transportation or storage of radioactive materials or chemicals.

A training program in nuclear protection and peacetime radiation accidents or incidents was given to 527 persons in emergency services, public works and hospital areas. 

Coordination in emergency planning was made with existing nuclear facilities in the state. Active preliminary emergency planning is in progress with a power company for a potential nuclear power plant facility to be located within Rhode Island.. 

Coordination in planning is also being made with the Health Department and the state Atomic Energy Commission. 

The Agency was actively engaged in peacetime evaluation of fallout in state resulting from China nuclear test detonation. 

Test results were coordinated with the state Health Department. 

COMMUNICATIONS: The Agency provides radio communica tions between the state headquarters and the federal government as well as between the state headquarters and all local subdivisions. A communications vehicle capable of emergency radio service is fully equipped. (available on a limited basis). The agency maintains an FM two way radio communications capability with key staff members and in addition has portable and other radio capability for fieldwork. An answering service monitors the agency telephone switchboard during off duty hours. The agency completed placing into service National Weather Service Station WXJ-39 as a mutual effort between Radio Station WPRO, the 

National Weather Service and the state Defense Civil Prepared ness Agency. The service to Rhode Island residents is on the air 24 hours a day on 162.4 MHz. The agency provides coordination, technical advice and service to the political subdivisions of the state and furnishes reports to the federal government pertinent to communication status in all 39 entities. Seventysix operational tests of communications equipment were conducted in this fiscal year. All Rhode Island communities have approved communications planning accomplished.

EMERGENCY OPERATING CENTER PROGRAM: The fed eral government will match funds with the state or qualifying subdivisions for costs incurred in the establishment of an Emergency Operating Center (EOC) to provide for the direction and control of all governmental forces and equipment under disaster or emergency conditions. At year's end ten such EOC's existed in the state. Federal funding for EOC's in this fiscal year amounted to $45,000.00. 

● EMERGENCY BROADCAST SYSTEM: The Rhode Island Em ergency Broadcast System (EBS) is part of a national network of designated radio stations which remain operational in time of declared emergencies. Direct radio and landline connections exist between the EBS studio and radio station WEAN. Designated stations remaining operational receive and rebroadcast the common program transmitted by station WEAN. 

Plans for the operation of the EBS facility, equipment and personnel assignments are complete and tests conducted periodically. A new plan has been devised in conjunction with the 

National Weather Service, the state Defense Civil Preparedness 

Agency and Rhode Island Broadcasters to use the new EBS Attention Signal to alert radio stations to the imminence of severe weather and other essential warnings. Tests of the new notification system are planned for the general public over all participating Rhode Island stations of the total EBS system. 

• WARNING: The National Warning System (NAWAS) operated by the federal government in cooperation with state and local governments is the main source of warning information including attack warning. This system is monitored on a continuous basis. A state Warning System is also tested bi-monthly. Sirens and other outdoor warning devices continue to be the method of providing a Local Warning System, (LWS) to residents of sub-divisions. Local Warning Plans exist in all 39 Rhode Island communities. To this end, the agency provides guidance to obtain federal financial assistance to maintain and as required, procure new warning sirens to serve populated areas. All 39 Rhode

Island communities have approved Warning Plans. 

• FUEL ALLOCATION PROGRAM: The Agency was delegated authority and responsibility on October 15, 1974 to implement the Federal Mandatory Petroleum Allocation System. During the energy crisis and embargo the emergency was alleviated by equit-

able distribution of available petroleum resources. During the past year petitions of economic hardship were processed from wholesale and retail users of petroleum products. Equitable reallocation of fuel oil was made during shortage period of winter. Allocations were made from state set-aside volumes allocated from prime suppliers inventories in state.

Surveys and price monitoring of fuel oil and gasoline were conducted weekly in compliance with Federal Energy Administration requirements in state. Contact was made with 100 retailers in all parts of state to evaluate average weighted price. 

TRAINING AND PUBLIC INFORMATION: In each of the 

39 municipalities, the state agency plans for and administers general and specialized planning, response and recovery actions in disaster situations. Emphasis is on promoting and increasing the professional capability of directors and staffs for effective application of duties and responsibilities in accordance with federal, state and local directives. The general public, business and industry, volunteer organizations, institutions and other segments of the private sector are provided information and guidance to create an awareness, knowledge and understanding for the need of protective measures and actions to safeguard life and property in disaster situations.

Objectives of the agency are achieved through the conduct of seminars, operational exercises, conferences, lectures, group instruction, news releases and dissemination of liturature. Two training seminars were held during FY 1977. 

OTHER PROGRAMS: The Agency administers the "Loan of 

Federal Excess Property" program which provides for loans of federal property to state and local subdivisions for civil preparedness purposes. Loans of $291,148.00 (acquisition value) worth of federal excess property were made to the state and its subdivisions.

Division of Fire Safety EARL F. SHANNON, State Fire Marshal Established 1973-Statutory Authority, Chapter 23-28 

 Headquarters-12 Humbert Street, North Providence 02911 

23 $357,647.34

The Division of Fire Safety is responsible for enforcing the provisions of the fire safety code and general and public laws insofar as they relate to fires, fire prevention, fire inspections, fire protection

and fire investigation; enforcing all laws of the state regarding keeping, storage, use, manufacture, sale, handling, transportation, or other disposition of explosives and flammable liquids; conducting and supervising fire safety inspections of all buildings regulated by the code within the state and certifying to any state or federal agency whether or not any building covered satisfies the requirements of the fire safety code.

FIRE INVESTIGATIONS UNIT 

This unit conducts investigations into the cause, origin and circumstances of fires occurring throughout the state at the request of, and in conjunction with, local fire and/or police authorities. This unit is also responsible for arraignments of those persons found to be in violation of inspection provisions of the code. Highlights The unit investigated 179 fires and developed evidence sufficient to establish that 50 were of incendiary origin and 61 were accidental. In addition, 58 were classified suspicious and the cause of 10 remains undetermined. The value of the property destroyed in these fires exceeded $5,000,000 and 26 persons expired. 

In 14 incendiary fires, 19 persons were charged in criminal court with arson or related crimes.

Some 26 building owners were arraigned for violations of the fire safety code, due to building construction deficiencies.

In October, 1976 two unit staff members completed the Criminalistics Course conducted by the University of Rhode Island School of Criminology and two are presently enrolled. In addition, one staff member has completed a course in Arson Investigation and Civil Disorder given at Rutgers University.

The unit undertook the arson investigation training of cadets in the four municipal police training academies this past year; also it instructed a joint meeting of the police and fire authorities of the Northern League of Rhode Island. • Disposition of 45 arrests in 1974-1975: Pending-5; Convicted- 

34: Acquitted-6. • Disposition of 39 arrests in 1975-1976: Pending-9; Convicted- 

25; Acquitted 5. • Disposition of 19 arrests in 1976-1977: Pending-13; Convicted- 

6; Acquitted-0.

Statewide fire loss statistics were maintained which reflect that fire authorities throughout the state reported the occurrences of more than 1900 fires resulting in damage exceeding $14,500,000. • The Division of Fire Safety was awarded a $4100 grant from the National Fire and Control Administration to develop, opperate and evaluate a statewide fire incident and casualty reporting system. This unit was charged with the responsibility of implementing this system.

HEALTH CARE INSPECTIONS UNIT 

This unit, under a contractural agreement between this division and the Department of Health, inspected health care facilities throughout the state to determine compliance with state and federal laws and made recommendations to appropriate licensing and/or certifying agencies concerning the various degrees of compliance. Highlights The unit conducted inspections of 56 skilled nursing facilities, 58 intermediate care facilities, 24 community residences, intermediate care facilities for the retarded, 4 sheltered care homes and 6 hospitals.

The unit conducted post-certification revisit inspections or revisit inspections of 13 skilled nursing homes, 54 intermediate care facilities, 6 intermediate care facilities for the retarded and 1 hospital. 

Unit staff members also participated in the following: Variance

Hearings-15; Administrative Hearings-4; Consultation w/Operators of Health Care Facilities-75; Training Seminars-2. 

As a result of these inspections and related activities, the operators of health care facilities throughout the state are making substantial renovations, reclassifying their facilities, or have already brought these facilities into compliance. 

GENERAL OCCUPANCY INSPECTIONS UNIT 

This unit is responsible for inspecting general occupancy type buildings throughout the state, except for one, two and three-family dwellings, to determine compliance with the provisions of the fire safety code. Unit staff members are authorized to grant building owners a reasonable notice of fire safety code violations and establish a timetable for compliance. 10

Highlights The unit conducted 505 inspections of these occupancies: Places of Assembly-105; Hotels/Motels-62; Schools-47; Boarding 

Homes-8; Rooming Houses-21; Day Care Centers-26; Apartment Houses-38; Businesses-105; Mercantile-55; Industrial- 

25; Community Residences-2; Penal Institutions-1; Horse 

Stables-1; Theatres-8; Complaints-11.

Staff participated in 110 consultations with building owners; 41 

meetings and 70 hearings of the Fire Safety Code Board of 

Appeal and Review; 217 reinspections to determine the progress of correction of deficiencies.

As a result of the activities of this unit, 85 buildings throughout the state have been brought in full compliance with the pro visions of the Rhode Island Fire Safety Code. 

• Staff appeared in court in 217 cases resulting from charges of violating the fire safety code. • The unit summarily abated, in conjunction with the Fire Safety Code Board of Appeal and Review, conditions presenting an immediate danger to life in two buildings by permanently closing them. It ordered the removal of combustibles within 48 hours, in one instance.

As a result of these activities, extensive repairs and renovations have been made in buildings inspected, ranging from installing sprinklers to installing proper exit signs. This has substantially increased the degree of fire safety afforded occupants of these buildings. 

SPECIAL SERVICES UNIT 

The basic function of this unit is the management of all programs relating to fire alarms, electric wiring, explosives, flammable liquids, model rocket engines, fireworks, fire extinguishers, research and training. Highlights Staff assisted local fire authorities and building inspectors in upgrading 31 fire alarm systems throughout the state, including assisting in the formulation of plans and specifications for fire alarms and communications for the new high security center at the Adult Correctional Institution. 

The unit participated in the investigation of 15 fires which were suspected of being caused by defective electric wiring or appliances. 11

The unit conducted investigations on complaints as indicated:

Hazardous Conditions-28; Liquefied Petroleum Installations- 

6; Bulk Plant Installations—2; Explosive Emergencies-17; Bomb

Incidents-5; Fireworks Violations-3. 

The unit conducted examinations for and issued certificates of competency for blasting to 39 persons and renewed 200 certificates. 

The unit inspected and issued permits for 20 vehicles for transportation of explosives.

Staff conducted three training sessions for the bomb disposal unit and responded to assist the investigative unit by photographing fire scenes in 10 cases. 'The unit conducted examinations for and issued certificates of competency to 20 persons to use, explode or cause to explode class B fireworks displays. 

The unit conducted a three day seminar on "Interpretation of the Fire Code" for approximately 100 fire service representatives from throughout the state. Attendance at this seminar was mandatory for certification as an Assistant Deputy State Fire Marshal.

The unit issued certificates of registration to 21 companies engaged in the business of selling, leasing and maintaining portable fire extinguishers. It also conducted examinations and issued 60 journeymen's licenses to persons engaged in the maintenance of portable fire extinguishers and issued 9 apprentice permits. 

PLAN REVIEW AND CODE COMPLIANCE UNIT 

The primary function of this unit is to review plans relating to new construction and renovations to existing structures and conduct on-site inspections and consultations to ensure compliance with the provisions of the State Fire Safety Code and Federal Life Safety Code. 

Highlights • Plans relating to the 226 facilities were reviewed and approved. 

Plans relating to an additional 112 were reviewed but failed to conform to the fire safety code; therefore, were not approved. • The unit conducted 271 on-site inspections/consultations of facilities which were being constructed or renovated. 12

Governor's Office on Highway Safety EDWARD J. WALSH, Governor's Representative 

Transferred in 1973 from the Department of Transportation to the 

Executive Department by Executive Order 

Office-345 Harris Avenue, Providence 02909

Full Time Employees Federal Grant Expenditures Obligated For Programs 

$1,000,000.00

According to the National Highway Safety Act of 1966, Public Law 

89-564, funding is provided to administer Federal Highway Safety Programs. Within the state, the governor's representative is responsible for program accomplishment and financial assistance to state agencies and Rhode Island cities and local communities for highway safety projects. in order to reduce traffic deaths, bodily injury and property damage accidents. The state and its political subdivisions—the 39 cities and towns are required to achieve effective highway safety results in accordance with the following 18 uniform Federal Highway Safety Standards: periodic motor vehicle inspection; motor vehicle registration; motorcycle safety; driver education; driver licensing; codes and laws; traffic courts; alcohol in relation to highway safety; traffic records; emergency medical services; police traffic services; debris hazard control and cleanup; pupil transportation safety; recording of traffic accidents; identification and surveillance of accident locations; highway design, construction and maintenance; traffic engineering service; and pedestrian safety.

Highlights • Stepped-Up Law Enforcement. In an attempt to reduce speed and cut down on fatalities, personal injury, and property damage accidents, this office through the National Highway Safety Administration provides necessary funding to state police and city and town police departments. Over $200,000 is allocable for equipment and police personnel for effective safety patrols throughout the state. Projects consist of chaperone details on the interstate highway system as well as fixed radar posts on the interstate and state highway system. Stepped-up law enforcement has been determined to be the best deterrent to the speeding motorist. 13

Update and Purge of Driver Records. The Division of Ad ministrative Adjudication operations handle all minor traffic violations. As an integral part of this program, all driver records must be updated, key punched, and computerized in order that the hearing officers might have access to all records during a hearing. Federal funding will be paying $240,000 over a three year period to complete this project. 

Emergency Medical Services. Since quick and efficient response to personal injury accidents is necessary to saving life, purchases will be made of rescue vehicles for cities and towns which have the greatest need of these vehicles. In addition, power rescue tools on the basis of geographical coverage will be provided, allowing for quick extrication of accident victims when they are trapped inside a vehicle. • Alcohol Programs. The DWI school will continue this year for persons convicted of Driving While Intoxicated. This course will again be run at Rhode Island Junior College and counselling services will be supplied for those in need through funding by this office. In addition, the Administrative Adjudication operations consist of a Youth and Alcohol Coordinator, whose responsibilities will be to coordinate the entire "Driving While Intoxicated" program as well as courses given to first-time offenders between the ages of 16 and 18. • Police Training Course. Police officers will be chosen to attend a nine month "Traffic Police Administration Training Program" 

at Northwestern University. The purpose of this course is twofold: to provide students with the latest information on theory and practice of police administration and to provide a problemsolving method in the form of research and work projects which deals with major issues confronting public safety and law enforcement today. At the completion of the course, the officer will convey this knowledge to all the men training at the municipal police academy.

Highway Safety Annual Work Program. For a 12 month pro gram it is estimated that this office will receive $1,000,000 in federal funding and it is anticipated that a large number of projects relating to the following areas of concern will be encompassed: bridge inspections; emergency voice communications; right cross projects; traffic engineer; pedestrian safety education; update and purge of driver records; alcohol projects; rescue 14

wagons; police safety wagons and radar; power rescue tools; electronic surveillance equipment; engineering seminar; a computerized system for identification of high accident locations; School 

Bus Driver Education; equipment for regulatory and warning signs: stripers; and "F.A.R.E.", Fatal Accident Reduction Enforcement.

Fatal Accident Reporting System. A separate program continuation of federal funding provides the part-time services of two University of R. I. graduate students in the compilation of statistics relating to state traffic fatalities. Through the use of this information, an effective approach towards reducing such accidents will be developed. 

• Four-Year Comprehensive Plan. This plan represents a coordi nated approach to the reduction of highway safety problems in this state. Two separate and unrelated considerations form the basis of this plan. First, data, concerning highway problems peculiar to Rhode Island, are being reviewed in depth. Secondly, evaluations are made to comply with the United States Depart ment of Transportation NHTSA-FHWA Standards. Programs are thus being designed to provide a balanced attack on causative factors involved in fatal and personal injury accidents and on symptoms of those problems.

Public Information Campaign. It is felt that in order to attack the problem of the highway accident the public must be kept aware of all aspects of highway safety. To this end, this office will continue to sponsor campaigns to keep the public aware of their driving habits and the dangers which are often involved by public service announcements, advertising on radio and television, and outdoor advertising.

RACING AND ATHLETICS COMMISSION JOHN F. CAPALDI, Chairman Established 1938-Statutory Authority, Chapter 41-1 

of the General Laws of 1955, as amended 

Office-Roger Williams Building, Hayes Street, Providence 02908 

Full Time Employees

Part Time Employees Total Expenditures 

46 $251,841.00 15

The three-man commission has full power to prescribe rules, regulations and conditions under which all horse racing meets, jai alai meets, dog racing meets, and all boxing and wrestling events shall be conducted within the state. Weekly meetings enable the members of the board to discuss all matters brought before it pertaining to all aspects of the pari-mutuel systems, and to control athletics. Highlights • During the past fiscal year, the commission authorized the sport of jai alai to operate a total of 130 eventing performances and 72 matinee performances in Newport. Jai alai enjoyed a banner year with an average handle of $110,446 daily and a total deposit to the General Treasury of $1,114,074.25. The city of Newport received during this fiscal period a total of $97,477.18. The commission acknowledged the superior management and expertise of the operation. Jai alai players from the Basque country of Spain added a splash of international excitement to the already exciting city. It is anticipated that in the coming year the dates for jai alai will be extended as the sport does not interfere with or conflict with other pari-mutuel systems in the state. • Dog racing was initiated by an act of the General Assembly in 1976, and this commission awarded the license to the Lincoln Greyhound Park, after much deliberation and discussion. A new management, at the large and rejuvenated track, renamed the same. Although only seven racing dates fall within this fiscal year, it is evident that dog racing has a definite spot in the parimutuel system, and it is anticipated that this sport will supply several million dollars and many jobs to the state. A projected figure is that the daily average handle will be approximately $300,000. Dog racing, during this limited period generated a deposit of $126,985.37.

During the 1977 legislative season, a bill was enacted to aid thoroughbred racing, which has been dormant since August 10, 1976, with only 24 racing days for the fiscal year giving to the state a total commission deposit of $486,310.43, and a miscellaneous deposit of $81,775.30. Total deposit to state was $568,085.73.

The bill gives a greater share of the commission to the track and is now one of the highest percentages in the country. Further, the new legislature authorizes a capital improvement fund which has taken one percent to be earmarked for renovations and improvements at Narragansett Park for the next several years. This money, together with the initial restoration, will enable the management of Narragansett Park to hopefully resume racing within the next 16

fiscal year. This commission acknowledges the obligation to resume horse racing to its former prominence in Rhode Island and will continue to aid in this vein. • The toxicology staff of the Department of Health examined racing samples. • Boxing and wrestling shows, both professional and amateur, were in a dormant state, but it is the feeling of the commission that the future looks good for both. This year, a total of 11 events generated a total deposit of $4,723.15 to the General Treasury.

The Governor's Justice Commission PATRICK J. FINGLISS, Executive Director Established 1967, Revised by Executive Order #5-March 15, 1977 Office-197 Taunton Avenue, East Providence 02914  

35 $5,510,812.00

The Commission is the central criminal justice planning agency for state government. The work of the GJC is guided by the Supervisory 

Board, comprised of state, local and public representatives. The objectives of the GJC are to develop and implement a comprehensive state plan for the improvement of state and local law enforcement and criminal justice, and for the prevention of crime and delinquency; to coordinate the activities of governmental agencies and private individuals and groups within this plan; and to provide planning assistance to the Governor, the General Assembly, and agencies of state and local government.

Highlights Block grant funds earmarked from the Law Enforcement Assist ance Administration (LEAA) totaled $3,153,380. Additionally, 

$250,000. was awarded for Juvenile Justice and Delinquency Prevention (JJDP), and $2,107,432 for discretionary funding.

As required by the Crime Control Act of 1976, draft legislation was forwarded to the State Legislature in March 1977 for the creation of this agency by law. 

The Statistical Analysis Center (SAC) of the Commission perform ed analyses of criminal justice data, and produced technical papers on crime statistics and recidivism rates. The SAC provided technical assistance in the areas of statistical and information systems development and implementation, and in statistical analysis, upon request and without cost to all criminal justice agencies within the state. • The Judicial Planning Committee, as required by the Crime Control Act of 1976, was created for the purpose of developing and maintaining an integrated and comprehensive planning capability within the Rhode Island State court system which responds to that systems needs and priorities. $50,000 was awarded to the 

JPC for operational costs. 18

• A $63,000 grant was awarded to The Ocean Tides, a residential treatment center for youthful offenders. This program is designed to be flexible enough to meet the treatment needs of various youths, firm enough to make young people face up to their shortcomings and needs, and foresighted enough to be multi-phased so that each youngster may re-enter the community's life at a pace and in stages that provide necessary supports while developing self-sufficiency.

The city of Pawtucket was awarded $34,960 to continue the 

Family Crisis Intervention Center, which optimizes service de livery within the police departments of Central Falls, Cumberland and Pawtucket in the generic area of family disturbance calls.

The Department of Attorney General was awarded $189,123 to establish a Major Violators Unit to better enable that department to deal with career offenders and their involvement in the crimes of rape, aggravated assault, robbery and burglary. 

The Department of Mental Health, Retardation and Hospitals was awarded $259,900 for the TASC Program which created an intake, placement and tracking system for criminal justice clients with drug abuse problems, provides the judicial system with appropriate placement options as an alternative to prosecution thereby reducing the number of drug offenders awaiting trial. 

Additionally, the grant provides the Department of Corrections with two counsellors who furnish specialized drug counselling at the DOC. • The Department of Corrections was awarded $248,008 to develop a comprehensive health care system which will meet the medical, counseling and supportive service needs of the adult and juvenile populations under the department's supervision. (Health Services Project)

The State Court Administrator's Office was awarded $200,000 for the development of a Statewide Judicial Information System (SJIS) to be shared by all adjudication agencies, which will provide comprehensive, reliable information for adjudication planning and policy making.

The city of Pawtucket was awarded $62,981 to continue the 

Blackstone Valley Crime Prevention Bureau, administered by the 

Blackstone Valley Chamber of Commerce to provide a coordinated crime prevention program among the police departments and residents of Central Falls, Cumberland, Lincoln and Pawtucket, and promote citizen involvement in crime prevention. 19

DETECTIVE DIVISION 

The Division is divided into five integral parts, Criminal Investigations; Intelligence Bureau, Bureau of Criminal Identification; Narcotics Squad; and Auto Theft Squad. Each unit is dependent upon one another for the effective fulfillment of their respective responsibilities.

The primary function of the Detective Division is to prevent and detect crime, to apprehend and assist in the prosecution of criminal offenders and to assist in the investigation and prosecution of any criminal matters within the state. Other duties include locating missing and wanted persons, recovery of lost/stolen property and to maintain a vigorous and vigilant attack against the organized crime element through intensive investigation and arrests in the field of gambling, loan sharking activities, narcotics and vice in all its forms. Highlights • The effectiveness of this Division is reflected in accomplishments during the past fiscal year. Members actively participated in and were directly responsible for the arrest and prosecution of more than 500 persons and the clearance of a variety of criminal offenses consisting of, but not limited to; homicides; rape; manslaughter; robbery; conspiracies to commit crimes; kidnapping; breaking and entering; firearms violations; gambling and vice.

Bureau of Criminal Identification: Members of this section af forded 24-hour service to all investigative units of the Division 24

and also to many other law enforcement agencies throughout the state. They are highly trained in the intricate field of crimi nalistics where various techniques are essential in the preservation of physical evidence. The bureau also conducted lie-detector examinations, presented lectures to law enforcement training schools and civic groups, and maintained valuable criminal information files.

Narcotics Squad: The Narcotics Squad investigated and appre hended individuals engaged in the illicit distribution and trading of narcotic drugs within the state. In the fiscal year 1976-77, this four man squad arrested a total of 115 persons. The year was highlighted in January when members of this unit were responsible for the seizure of 344,000 dosage units illegally possessed narcotic drugs stolen months earlier from a pharmaceutical warehouse in Massachusetts. It was considered to be the largest seizure made in the eastern United States, having an estimated street value of 7.5 million dollars.

In June of 1977, after a seven month investigation involving international trafficking of P.C.P., members of the Narcotics Squad arrested six persons including a Canadian national, and seized more than 60,000 illegally produced tablets of Phencyclidine (PCP). • Auto Squad: The Auto Squad investigates all aspects surrounding the theft and recovery of motor vehicles. During the fiscal 

1976-77, 31 vehicles were recovered with a total value in excess of $125,000.

NATIONAL CRIME INFORMATION CENTER 

On November 1, 1976, the Rhode Island State Police became the first State Police organization in New England to have a terminal on direct line to the National Crime Information computer, based in the 

Federal Bureau of Investigation Headquarters in Washington, D. C. 

A total of 90,377 records have been entered into the computer since that date.

The system is utilized by all police agencies in the State of Rhode Island and makes available to them vast files of documented police information gathered from throughout the country, instantly upon request. This system has brought the most modern technology to law enforcement in the state and will further aid in a continuing effort 25

in the field of crime prevention. A total of 18,308 "Hits" (apprehended wanted persons, recovered stolen property) have been made. to date.

PLANNING AND RESEARCH 

The personnel of the Planning and Research Section directly assists the Superintendent and Executive Officer so that coordinated planning toward department goals may be realized. Other projects undertaken by this section include responses to inquiries, questionnaires and surveys from governmental and law enforcement agencies, drafting and revising general orders, writing new and revising old procedure memos, and researching new and improved police products. 

RADIO BUREAU 

The Radio Bureau is responsible for the installation, operation and maintenance of the Division's electronic facilities and equipment. The 

1adio system consists of thirteen V.H.F. base stations at two remote locations which are interconnected by microwave to State Police Headquarters, State Capitol and all barracks. The microwave system economically replaced telephone wire lines and provides greater realibility. A system design known as "Hot Standby" provides even greater reliability of the critical communication paths between Headquarters and the two base station sites. 

The mobile radio configuration is divided into two systems known as North and South Zones. There are 126 mobile units and 140 hand held portable radios in use by the department. 

A common frequency known as the Rhode Island State Police Emergency Radio Network (RISPERN) has been installed to provide for communications between State Police and local cities and towns. This frequency is for emergency only and has proven to be very useful. 

Other users of the State Police radio facilities are the Governor's 

Office, Adjutant General, Director of the Department of Transporta tion, Warden of the ACI, State Civil Defense, and the Attorney General.

The Radio Bureau does extensive radar work which includes calibration of department car speedometers, routine bench and road checks of all radar units, and expert testimony in speeding trials in court. 

STATE POLICE BLOODHOUNDS 

The State Police Canine Corps, which consists of four bloodhounds, participated in many searches within the state and were made available to other law enforcement agencies for tracking purposes. 26

SUMMONS CONTROL BUREAU 

The bureau is charged with the control of all traffic summonses issued by the Division. Prior to the establishment of this bureau, the trooper, after issuing a summons, was responsible for preparing the warrant for prosecution. The clerk typists within this bureau have relieved the trooper from a great deal of clerical work thus giving him more time for patrol.

TELETYPE BUREAU 

The State Police automatic teletype selective system, which is located at State Police Headquarters, has been in operation for nine years and has been described as the most significant breakthrough in law enforcement teletype communications. At present 30 local police departments, the Division of Motor Vehicles, the United States Weather Bureau, the State Bureau of Criminal Indentification, the Rhode Island 

Civil Defense Headquarters, the office of the State Maintenance Engi neer, the Medical Center Police, Division of Drug Control, Department of Natural Resources and the State Police utilize the system, which is the largest of its type in New England. The system automatically connects stations for fast, efficient and economical methods of transmitting alarms and messages. It operates at 100 words per minute and provides the alternate one-way transmissions. 

The Teletype Bureau is the state warning point on the National 

Warning System (NAWAS) for the Rhode Island Civil Defense Pro gram. Bureau personnel monitor NAWAS equipment 24 hours daily, which has its source at the North American Air Defense Command (NORAD) in Colorado. In the event of an actual alert from this source, the bureau will immediately disseminate such alert via teletype to all cities and towns on the network. Towns not connected to teletype will be telephoned the alert from the nearest State Police Barracks. 

Besides its routine functions as a means of instant communications, the Bureau maintains an extensive cross-indexed filing system of missing and wanted persons, lost or stolen property, lost or stolen cars and a stolen motor number file. The file has led to the identification of wanted persons and to the location of stolen property. 

Other valuable services include the furnishing of road condition reports for Rhode Island and nearby states, and, during seasons of extreme forest fire hazards, the dissemination of the "fire weatherclass day" to all departments. 27

The Rhode Island State Police is a member of the National Law 

Enforcement Teletype System (NLETS). Its purpose is to provide a responsive, efficient telecommunications facility for the interstate exchange of criminal justice information. In 1973 NLETS was able to make improvements in the quality and quantity of the service it provides its users. NLETS now has the capability to handle over 30 times the volume of traffic at 20 times the speed it could just two years ago.

New England State Police Administrator's Conference In-

and upon request basic and refresher courses in law enforcement for local police officers and other state agencies.

Highlights 

The state police participated in the "Career Day" and "Highway

Safety" programs at various high schools and other schools throughout the state during the past year. 

The state police conducted several safe-driving and law enforcement presentations to industry and to naval installations in Newport. Safe Driving and Law Enforcement Programs were pre- 28

sented to the Women's Automotive Clinic, sponsored by the Rhode 

Island Automobile Dealer's Association and the Little Rhody

The state police participated in the "Police Explorer Cadet Programs" for youth interested in law enforcement for the cities and towns of South Kingstown, East Providence, Newport and Cumberland.

The state police conducted a two week "Special Weapons and 

Tactical Force In-Service Training School" in April of 1977 at the State Police Training Academy. In addition to the members of the Rhode Island State Police attending this session were mem bers of the Providence Police Department. The Training School is an intensive two week course of instructions in "Physical Training; Firearms; Hostage Negotiations; Night Firing; Repelling; 

Practical Field Problems; Ballistics and Special Weapons".

The state police conducted two "Mental Health Worker-Forensic Unit Training Schools". The first session of a two week duration was held in March of 1977 and the second session was held in June of 1977. The Training Schools were courses of instructions in "Defensive Tactics; Transportation of Prisoners; R. I. 

Arrest Law; Search & Seizure Techniques; Sociology in Law Enforcement; Advantages of Physical Fitness; Surveillances and Se curity Procedures; Organizational Structure of the A.C.I.; Identification and Investigation of Narcotics; First Aid; Use of Chem icals in Law Enforcement; Hostage Negotiations; Riot Control;

Organizational Structure and Functions of the Institute for Mental Health; Treatment of Character Disorders; Competency or Lack of Competency of Patients—Inmates". 

UNIFORM CRIME REPORTING 

This unit is responsible for collating and formulating all pertinent data, as well as compiling such statistics as may be necessary to present a proper classification and analysis of the volume and nature of crime, to guide law enforcement agencies in the administration of criminal justice within the state. At the present time a separate annual report is being published based on statistics and information gathered and collated under the Uniform Crime Reporting System. This annual report will provide comparative data in all phases of the system on a statewide as well as municipal basis. Selective and coordinate enforcement becomes possible only when the type and volume of 29

crime can be analyzed on the basis of accurate information comprehensively developed and systemmatically collated. 

UNIFORMED BRANCH 

The Uniformed Branch of the Division is charged with the duties of traffic control, accident investigation, prevention of crime and the protection of life and property. They have also afforded information and assistance to the motoring public. Highlights 

Traffic Violation Report: Officers on traffic patrol have reported a total of 57,717 cases of violations on the Motor Vehicle Law. 

Reports of these violations have been forwarded to the appropriate licensing authorities.

Reports of corrections, warnings and assisting motorists, bicyclists and pedestrians have been made to State Police Headquarters. 

These cases totaled 14,945. • Administrative Adjudication: During the fiscal year, 31,397 operators were issued summonses for violation of the Motor Vehicle Law. 38,699 dispositions have been received from this division during the fiscal year. This figure includes a backlog of dispositions from the previous year. • Accidents Investigated: During this period, 2115 motor vehicle accidents occurring on state highways were investigated by the State Police. The department assisted local police departments in the investigation of 232 other accidents. • Hit and Run Accidents: The state police investigated 85 hit and run accidents during this period. Arrests were made in 40 cases. 

In addition to the above apprehensions, five hit and run operators were apprehended for other departments. • Driving Under the Influence: Officers on traffic patrol arrested and prosecuted 47 violators for driving under the influence of intoxicating beverages. 30

Division of Accounts and Control 

The primary functions of this division are the administration of a comprehensive accounting and recording system which classifies transactions of the state departments and agencies in accordance with the budget plan; the maintenance of control accounts for all supplies, materials, and equipment for all departments and agencies; prescription of a financial, accounting, and cost system for all state departments and agencies; the pre-audit of all state receipts and expenditures; the approval of vouchers drawn on the General Treasurer; and the preparation of financial statements required by the several departments and agencies, the Governor, or the General Assembly.

The Controller's Division is responsible for the following publica tions: Annual State Financial Report, Condensed State Financial Report, State Payroll Manual, Chart of Appropriation Accounts, Chart of Receipt Accounts, and Procedural Handbook of Department of Admin istration (coordinator) .

Bureau of Audits The Bureau of Audits represents the auditing arm of the executive branch of state government.

MUNICIPAL AUDIT SECTION 

Staff from this office audited financial records of three cities, eight towns, one redevelopment agency and three regional school districts. At year's end, three municipal auditors were in process with six audits requested but not started. Additionally, they install accounting systems, assist and advise in the preparation of local annual operating and capital budgets, provide advice and counsel in all phases of local financial matters and advise local public officials of the revised procedures and fiscal effect of changes in state statutes. Other activities included; reviewing audit reports of the 22 municipalities that engage public accounting firms to examine their financial records; conducting special examinations of local school department records upon requests of the state commissioner of education; performing interim cash audits for cities and towns upon the change of public officials concerned with local finances; auditing local federal project records and reporting thereon to the appropriate federal agencies; and auditing local revenue-sharing funds and reporting thereon in prescribed form.

DEPARTMENTAL AUDIT SECTION 

The purpose of this section is to conduct all audits required by any state department and agency, and to make comments and recommendations relative to the financial affairs, and the economy and efficiency of management of each department and agency: Highlights com- • Thirty-one separate departmental and agency audits were pleted during the year and 12 separate inventories of the various rotary funds and special funds were certified. At the close of the fiscal year, 12 regular audits and 11 inventory verifications were in process, encompassing 13,404 man hours.

Pre-audits of detailed monthly and quarterly returns submitted by the district courts, superior courts, and sheriffs were duly certified to the General Treasurer for receipt of amount due the state and/ or reimbursement of officers' and witnesses' fees paid. • A continuous post-audit, which necessitated the assignment of four staff members on a full-time basis, was conducted of the financial accounts and records of the Department of Employment Security. 36

• Replies from state departments to recommendations contained. in audit reports and the auditor general's fiscal year post audit report were catalogued and reviewed. • Proper data and information pertaining to indirect auditing costs for federal programs were submitted to the state controller so that the state would recover funds for this service. 

Division of Budget 

The budget office is charged with the preparation and execution of the state's annual financial plan. This function basically consists of: (a) planning for state government's finances in the form of the governor's budget; (b) working with the legislature to enact the proposed budget; and, (c) management and implementation of the approved plan. In discharging both its short range and long range duties, the paramount concern of this division is to maintain total operations within available resources.

Highlights • Budgeting: Operations of the past year were highlighted by the preparation of the state's fiscal plan for the 1977-78 fiscal year. 

The major concerns involved in the development of the proposed budget were the allocation of resources within and across departmental lines and the costing of alternative revenue plans for executive and legislative considerations. This year the always difficult task of balancing revenues and expenditures (a Rhode Island constitutional mandate) was further complicated by the controversy over the 6% sales tax and the rapidly changing national and local economic circumstances.

In the final analysis, it was precisely the improving economy which enabled the state to significantly increase services and exempt clothing and water from the sales tax as well. The major new or expanded initiatives were as follows: an increase in the standards of assistance for all welfare categories; payments to foster families were increased; additional funds were made available to improve community and residential services for children and youth; the state's scholarship program was reconstructed and expanded; landmark “circuit breaker” legislation providing tax and rent relief to aged persons was enacted; senior citizens and handicapped transportation services were expanded; the state's fiscal integrity was protected by fully meeting outstanding moral obli- 37

gation bonds of the Fairmount Foundry; correctional health and education programs were strengthened and a new maintenance unit was created to care for correctional facilities; services to the mentally ill, retarded and other institutionalized persons were upgraded. 

Despite what one might assume to be a prosperous year, the economies (job freeze, overtime constraints, reduction of out-ofstate travel just to name a few) instituted during the difficult economic times of the previous sereval years were not abandoned, and it was primarily these economies which produced the necessary surplus to fund the above programs. 

During the 1977 legislative session 131 "fiscal notes" were prepared for the General Assembly, presenting the current year and prospective two year impact of proposed legislation upon the finances of the state. 

Prospective Budgeting for Hospitals: The prospective rating program initiated on October 1, 1974, as an effort to contain hospital cost increases is in its third and final year as an experimental program. Although termination of the experimental phase means that the Social Security Administration will no longer offset some of the administrative expenses of the program, the participants in the Rhode Island program have agreed to extend the present program another year (October 1, 1977-September 30, 1978) pending Congressional action on hospital cost controls. The participants are 

Blue Cross of Rhode Island, the State Division of Budget, the Hos pital Association of Rhode Island, and the 16 voluntary hospitals in the state.

Thus, prospective negotiation of hospital expense budgets and prospective rate-setting will continue for Medicaid and Blue Cross third party payors. Termination of Social Security Administration participation means that unless Congress enacts new legislation, Medicare will be paying hospitals according to year-end actual costs rather than the negotiated expense levels established prior to the start of the fiscal year.

The program that has been in place during the past three years and that which will be effective for the fiscal period beginning October 1, 1977 basically consists of 1) a negotiations process which establishes an overall expense MAXICAP as a limit to hospital cost increases over prior year recognized expenses, and 2) a subsequent negotiation process with individual hospitals to determine that portion of the total MAXICAP allotted to each hospital. 38

Increases beyond negotiated, prospective expense levels for individual hospials are allowable only for contractually described major contingencies and some portion of expenses attributable to volume increases. 

The track record for total hospital expense growth has been as follows:

In comparison to the MAXICAP increments established every year, the actual or projected actual record does not indicate a perfect score; but the figures do point to an improving situation in 

Rhode Island, especially when the state is compared to the rates of increase of about 15% reported nationally for hospital expense growth in calendar 1976.

In addition, the figures do not describe the growth in overall effectiveness, coordination, and understanding of hospital management and planning among those involved in health care and their impact on quality of care that has occurred through the dialogue between all participants provided by the program. Every goal laid out by hospital planners, regulators, or hospital administrators aimed at halting cost increases or expanding health services has not been achieved over the past three years, but the prospective rating program has allowed some improvement in health services while forestalling the potentially costly excesses in resource allocation and utilization which may have occurred under the unregulated cost-pass through, retrospective reimbursement system that 

Rhode Island operated under previously.

Cash Analysis and Investment: The Division of Budget, working closely with the General Treasurer's Office, recommends to the State Investment Commission the investment of idle funds. On a monthly basis the division prepares an advice and transaction portfolio which includes outstanding investments, cash balances, and recommended investments for the general fund, bond funds, and various special funds. On a daily basis, the division utilizes its revenue estimating techniques in conjunction with the required flow of cash to ensure that only minimum balances are maintained for ongoing expenditures. 39

Investment activity increased throughout fiscal year 1977 with the number of transactions increasing 14.4% from the prior fiscal year. Earnings received from general revenue investments during 1977 increased by $162,633, a growth of 10.2%. The incre-se in investment income for 1977 was higher than anticipated and was primarily attributed to slightly higher rates of return for commercial paper and increased investment activity. 

Investment earnings in the bond funds increased by $136,932, a growth of 4.8%. The higher earnings realized on the bond funds were also, as in the case of the general fund, the result of slightly higher rates of return, as well as, the availability of additional monies for investment purposes.

It appears that current rates of return will prevail throughout 1978, and that the amount of money available for investment purposes will remain the same as in the prior year. • Budget Development: With the introduction of analytic program budgeting (APB) during the late 60's and zero-base budgeting during the 70's, the budgeting function in Rhode Island has undergone a carefully planned and systematic change. The implementation of these innovative techniques has been on a gradual and progressive basis with each annual installment building upon the steps taken during the previous year.

A second key factor which has played a vital role in the success of the Rhode Island experience with these changes (which have failed in many other states and jurisdictions) is the limited amount of paperwork, new requirements, and verbiage associated with the implementation of these concepts.

The more significant advancements made in the field of budget development during the past fiscal year are as follows: -simplification of the zero-base budgeting process, which includes a revised format designed to solicit brief and substantive responses to be used by agency administrators and program man- 40

agers in their day-to-day operation of programs, as well as being of use in the budget process itself. -the development of an "Expenditure Indices" report which forecasts anticipated price changes for various goods and services purchased by the state. This report was distributed to city and town budget officials for their use as well. -development of an econometric revenue forecasting model covered in detail in the following section. 

Revenue Forecasting: One of the newest and most rapidly expanding areas in state budget planning and policy development is the use of econometric models and simulation and techniques. In keeping abreast with the state of the arts, the division this year contracted the services of Data Resources, Inc. to create a model specifically designed for Rhode Island. 

This model consists of a system of equations, most of which describe an explicit behavioral mathematical relationship. Equations describing the state's economy provide forecasts of employment, wages and income by industry or sector in the state. To the extent that national data influence Rhode Island's economy, these equations incorporate national exogenous variables, the forecasts of which are derived from Data Resources National Forecasting Service. The forecast of the state's economy is then used to "drive” a revenue submodel which predicts the various state taxes such as the sales tax and personal income tax. Due to the fact that revenue projections from the model incorporate the impact of economic cycles, greater sophistication and accuracy should be provided in revenue forecasting and analysis.

In addition to generating forecasts of the state's economy and revenues, the model is a powerful policy tool due to its capability of providing forecasts under different scenarios. This means that alternative national forecasts may be utilized to determine their impact on the state's economy and revenues. Also, federal and state tax law changes may be introduced to determine their effects on state tax receipts. Given that the state's economic performance is highly dependent upon the national economy and that changes in state personal income tax revenues are determined by federal law, this type of flexibility should prove very useful. • Budget System Automation: The goals which have influenced the creation and evolution of automating the budget system have been of a two-fold nature: minimization of the manual processing of recurring source documents; and establishment of an efficient and effective management information system. 41

To date, several advances have been made in the fulfillment of these goals.

There also exists for information exchange a reciprocal relationship between the budget system and the appropriation accounting system. This relationship commenced being used in FY '75 to bring about an integrated closing of accounts at the year's end. 

Additionally, the programs have been used to develop a report for each state employee pay period indicating additions and deletions to payrolls. These reports generate a summary of personal services costs by agency and department, on a comparative basis. 

During the last year a new on-line system was implemented for the Budget Division. The system provides a real-time inquiry capability into a personnel/payroll data base. This capability will be extended in the future to include access to the new payroll/personnel and accounting data base systems. • "Planned use" and "actual use" reports were completed for the $9,513,023 in general revenue sharing funds received from the federal government. ● A joint project was undertaken along with the Division of Information Processing in order to develop a data information base to improve the Workmen's Compensation Program. 

Construction Control: Engineering the fiscal control of non-highway capital construction projects also fall within the jurisdiction of the division. The Construction Control Section performs such functions as: serves on board for recommendation of architects and engineers; provides assistance in determining best site selections; establishes architect/engineer contact requirements; provides engineering services to agencies during formative stages of a project; reviews plans, specifications and cost estimates submitted by architect/ engineers and monitors entire design procedure; provides daily surveillance and reporting for projects under construction. During the 

1976-77 fiscal year, 60 plus projects were in various stages ranging from planning, design to construction. 

This section also collaborates very closely, with the budget section in maintaining fiscal control over projects, and from these efforts an invaluable project accounting and reporting system is maintained. ⚫ All claims of damage to state property under the fire and extended insurance coverage policy are examined by this unit for proper 42

assessment of damages. Determinations are also made for proper and adequate insurance coverage of new buildings. 

• Capital Development Budgeting: During the 1977 Session of the 

General Assembly, legislation was submitted that included capital projects previously disapproved by the voters at the 1976 general election. The re-submitted projects included $5,000,000 for the construction of an intake services center-correctional facility; and some $41,500,000 in transportation facilities. These projects were subsequently approved by the voters at a special election held in late June 1977.

In carrying out its statutory mandate "to develop long-term activity and financial program, particularly capital improvement programs", the Division of Budget prepared instructions and forms. for the Governor's review and recommendation to the January 1978 

Session of the General Assembly for the fourth Capital Develop ment Program, 1979-1990. This program will introduce a comprehensively planned and coordinated approach wherein capital acquisition will be integrated with a broad scale, long-term supporting fiscal program. 

Following the established pattern, this program will lay out a 12-year schedule composed of two 6-year phases with project approvals being sought from the public at each general election, that is, every two years. To this end, referenda legislation is expected to be introduced to support a series of projects supported by general revenue bonds. The final proposals after legislative review will be presented to the state's electorate at the November 1978 general election for their approval or rejection.

Development of a system for the Division of Food Protection and 

Sanitation that was started during January, 1977 is now in its final stages. The system will produce tabulations that reflect activity of all line personnel in the Division of Food Protection and Sanitation. They will account for time spent in the performance of line functions by activity type and kind of visit or procedure and the results of time spent by number of sample taken, findings, actions, field tests, condemnations, voluntary disposals, and embargoes. Reporting will be generated at three levels within the division; by individual, by field office or program, and by the division. The system will also support federal reporting requirements in the areas of state and federally shared programs. 

The system will be providing input into a federally sponsored reporting system that has been adopted by the Division of Food Protection and Sanitation. Output from this federally sponsored system will support the activities of food service sanitarians, and their managers at both local and state levels of government.

Mail pick-up and delivery is made to all offices in the State House, State Office Building, Veterans' Memorial Building, Roger Williams 

Building, Winn Hall, Health Building, and to the buildings in the downtown Providence area where state agencies are located, including the CIC complex on Promenade Street. During the last fiscal year, the unit processed 5,568,389 pieces of mail with a total billing of $818,841.87.

Service Center 

This unit provides a variety of clerical services to state agencies requiring assistance for specific jobs or for limited periods of time. In addition, the center provides central duplicating (mimeographying and dittoing) and photocopying for agencies requiring these services. The service center maintains a standard distribution or mailing list on addressograph plates covering all daily and weekly newspapers, radio and television stations within the state, and national press services. The purpose of this service is to provide central facilities for the reproduction and distribution of information originating in the several state departments and agencies so that it will receive maximum coverage. 47

Records Management Program 

The records management program is responsible for preserving, protecting and servicing the records of state government. Since its inception in 1952, this section has succeeded in containing and systemizing the records and related paper work problems of the state. This has been accomplished through the development and implementation of realistic records retention schedules which identify records, separate inactive from active, identify documents having historical or administrative value, and provide for the timely destruction of obsolete records. The application of modern warehousing techniques to storage of inactive records permits the storage of a large volume of records in a relatively small amount of space using inexpensive containers and steel shelving instead of expensive filing equipment. These records are maintained under controlled conditions which provide for pinpoint indexing and location, ready accessibility, security, and follow-up on transfer and destruction.

Highlights At the close of the fiscal year approximately 40,985 cubic feet of inactive records were on file at the Records Center. • A total of 22,795 service requests for records were processed. 

Approximately 89,145 micro-images were added to the microfilm file.

Division of Personnel 

The division is responsible for the administration of the Merit System Law, and in so doing, maintains complete personnel files on all state employees, analyzes position requirements, establishes class specifications, establishes qualifications for candidates, determines salary ranges, recruits and examines potential workers, develops and maintains training programs, operates the employee group insurance plan and the career awards program, conducts research into personnel problems, and maintains employee relationships with workers on a state-wide basis through regular meetings with management officials and representatives of employee groups. The division is composed of four operating sections: administration; classification; recruitment and examination; and training. 

Division activities during the course of the year included: 91 open competitive examinations resulting in 82 employment lists containing the names of 3,151 persons who qualified for appointment to various classes of positions under the Merit System; conduction or supervision 48

of the operation of training programs and under the terms of an Intergovernmental Personnel Act Grant renewal of $36,894, the continued operation of the Walk-In Testing Center.

Current plans involve automation of the following: application processing, notifying candidates of examinations, establishing lists and notifying candidates of test scores. Division of Public Buildings 

The division was established to house and service other agencies of state government, notably the Executive, Legislative, and Judiciary, in the 30 buildings in its care and custody. 

The division staffs the buildings and provides services such as heating, lighting, elevator, central telephone, cleaning, and parking facilities. It also operates a maintenance section for small carpentry, electric and plumbing projects and a painting staff for continuous interior painting. It repairs all buildings with major renovations and structural projects selected from an established work program projected to keep abreast of progress by other state agencies. 

The division is responsible for the operations of the Automotive Maintenance Unit which is charged with the replacement of state passenger vehicles and the maintenance of all state trucks and new highway equipment. The division is also responsible for the acquiring of property located in the Capitol Properties Area for office building expansion. 

To accomplish its work the division has a staff of 350 full-time position, 58 part-time positions and 36 Ceta positions with personnel services of $2,797,116.54 including fringe benefits. The division has an annual appropriation, including federal grants, of $5,069,396.00. Highlights The refurbishing of the Stephen Hopkins House, the Kent County Courthouse, the Bristol County Courthouse and the Bicentennial Building is continuing under the state and federal grant programs.
