Division of Purchases 

Since its inception the division has had the statutory responsibility to purchase and contract for supplies, materials, equipment and services required by a department or agency of state government through a central procurement organization; to establish standards and specifications for supplies and equipment; to provide an inspection service and program for all agencies; to maintain records for state property; to dispose of surplus or salvaged items; to transfer equipment and supplies between state agencies; to serve as the distribution medium for federal surplus food, supplies and equipment to all public and state health and educational institutions; and to operate a Central 

Warehouse and Stationery Store. Subsequently, a State Properties Committee was formed which has the responsibility of approving transactions involving the acquisition, disposal and leasing of real property and similar interests. Also, statutory approval was given the division to purchase for local municipalities when requested by those local governing authorities. This program has been highly successful; 38 communities are currently participating. Highlights 

• An amendment to the Federal Property and Administration Ser vices Act of 1949 was passed by Congress on October 14, 1976, which resulted in the consolidation of various federal surplus and excess property programs. Responsibility for the administration of this program was assigned to the General Services Administration. Since this constitutes a major change, the effective date of implementation for state compliance is October 17, 1977.

During this interim period, all interested states must submit a new State Plan of Operation. The amendment has resulted in 

54

expanding the eligibility lists to include state, local and nonprofit units within the state. A further anticipated benefit is in the upgrading of available equipment, since excess property programs such as Regional Commissions will cease to exist and much of the property customarily available to these programs will become surplus and subject to this revamped program. This division is currently in the process of amending its statewide plan which will also involve holding a public hearing for interested parties prior to forwarding the amended plan to GSA for approval. During the past year there has been very little activity under the existing surplus property program. Most of the property which became available to the state was received through the New England Regional Commission (NERCOM) administered by the Department of Economic Development. 

• The increased availability and utilization of USDA donated commodities has resulted in lowering food costs to various eligible state agencies and programs. This program minimized the effect of rising food costs on agency budget allocations for food. 

The only other method of reducing food costs is by improving the procurement program which would involve providing a substantial warehousing facility that would eliminate small lot purchases and the cost of leasing commercial freezer space due to current inadequate storage space. The anticipated savings through bulk purchases would offset the cost of building a suitable warehouse as well as provide greater economies thereafter. 

The division processed approvals for the expenditure of approximately $165,227,132.26. These expenditures result from formal and informal bids, contract purchase orders, small lot purchases under $50, memorandum purchases involving special programs for the Department of Mental Health, Retardation and 

Hospitals and Social and Rehabilitative Services. 

• The division experienced a 9% increase in purchase orders issued, which reflected an increased interest in state business due to improving economic conditions. Some 18,279 purchase orders were issued, representing 17,803 purchase requisitions. There were 39,829 bid invitations mailed, involving 4,002 formal and informal bids. Highway contracts totalled 20; other construction and repair projects totalled 60, requiring 526 sets of plans and specifications. ⚫ State term contracts available to state agencies for direct purchases totalled 21. In addition, six contracts containing a clause which 55

allows local municipalities to purchase directly from state contractors at state pricing were issued. The division is continually striving to expand this program. 

The Inventory and Conservation Units disposed of surplus property with receipts totalling $84,550-an increase of 35% over last year.

The Central Supply and Distribution sections provided state agencies, institutions and eligible donees with goods and supplies totalling $6,073,311.—an increase of 15% over last year. • Stationery Store receipts totalled $329,310 which represents 37,863 transactions-an increase of 22%. Part of the increase is attributable to rising costs, the balance is due to an increase in business.

The Warehouse Unit distributed food and household items totalling $3,564,339. State institutions purchased $1,373,305. and the state-sponsored School Lunch Program which serviced 282 schools. purchased $2,190,534. This expenditure represents a decrease in purchased commodities of approximately 7% which is attributable to the increase in available USDA commodities. • The Federal Food Surplus Unit distributed 4,252,214 lbs. of various U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA) donated food commodities having a dollar value of $2,179,662.—an increase of 46%. Eligible donees included 41 institutions, 16 summer camps, 366 schools, 12 summer reading programs, 63 breakfast programs and 6 nutrition programs for the elderly. These programs serviced 94,664 recipients and represents in increase of 11/2%. • Food processing contracts are continually researced in order to obtain maximum benefits by utilizing USDA donated commodities which would not be fully utilized in their original form. 

USDA commodities are furnished to vendors for further processing in order to provide various finished products that can then be more readily utilized by eligible recipients. This year the savings amounted to $165,033.—an increase of approximately 38%.

The State Properties Committee considered and approved the following: Condemnations-4; Leases and Rentals-64; Concessions-6; Reversions-2; Drainage Agreements-5; Land Acquisitions-18; Disposal of Surplus Land and Property 20; Easements-5. 56

Statewide Planning Program 

The Rhode Island Statewide Planning Program is the central planning agency for state government. The Program is guided by the State 

Planning Council, comprised of state, local, public, and federal representatives. Major activities include preparation and maintenance of the 

State Guide Plan, an integrated program for the physical, economic, and social development of the state; formulation and operation of executive management mechanisms; and coordination of the actions of public bodies and the private sector within the framework of the state's development goals and plans. This work is supported by state appropriations and federal grants.

The major work of the Program involves the preparation and maintenance of the State Guide Plan. This plan is comprised of a series of functional plans or elements dealing with land use, utility services, transportation, recreation, conservation and open space, historic preservation, and other areas. 

Governmental management and coordination is a second major area of activity. These activities are undertaken to provide staff assistance to the Governor, the General Assembly, and state agencies in evaluating programs, implementing plans, and coordinating public and private development decisions. A key element of this task is the Program's function as the state clearinghouse for the Project Notification and Review System established by Office of Management and Budget Circular A-95 to facilitate coordination of state, regional and local planning and development.

The collection, analysis, and forecasting of socio-economic transportation, and other data is undertaken to support the activities of the Program. This information base is widely used by other public agencies and by the private sector.

Special projects are pursued by the Program to promote implementation of the State Guide Plan and to provide planning services to other agencies.

Highlights The State Planning Council held 12 regular monthly meetings during the year. The Council reviewed and adopted four Water 

Quality Management Plans (for the Blackstone, Moshassuck, Woonasquatucket, and Pawcatuck River Basins) as elements of the State Guide Plan. The Council also reviewed and approved a number of documents prepared by the Program staff including 

The Transportation Improvement Program 1976-79; the water 

57

quality management Continuing Planning Process Description; 

the Unified Work Program for Transportation Planning; and five addendums to the Overall Economic Development Program. Pur suant to the mandate of Section 42-64-14 (a) of the General Laws, the Council reviewed and made a finding of State Guide Plan consistency for 11 project proposals submitted by the Rhode Island 

Port Authority and Economic Development Corporation. In pro cedural matters the Council adopted new rules of procedure for the Project Notification and Review System (A-95 review) operat ed by the Program. These procedures go into effect on September 

1, 1977. In addition to regular meetings, the Council held six public hearings during the year; five on portions of the Water Quality 

Management Element of the State Guide Plan, and one concerning the new A-95 rules of procedure.

The Technical Committee of the Council is comprised of state, local, federal, and private sector representatives appointed by the State Planning Council. The Committee advises the Program staff on technical procedures, monitors work in progress, and reviews all major studies and makes recommendations thereon to the State Planning Council. The Committee also acts on all proposals reviewed under the Project Notification and Review System. 

The Technical Committee held 12 regular sessions and two special meetings during the past fiscal year. The Committee reviewed a number of Program Reports and Technical Papers prepared by the staff and reviewed and acted upon 418 project proposals for federal funding assistance. In addition the Committee reviewed and commented on the draft environmental report filed with the Nuclear Regulatory Commission as a prerequisite to construction of a nuclear-powered electric generating facility at Charlestown, Rhode Island. 

Technical Subcommittees are special purpose bodies appointed as required to assist the State Planning Council, the Technical Committee, and the Program staff. During fiscal year 1977 subcommittees were active in the areas of areawide wastewaster management planning; transportation system management; housing; and federal project proposal screening and review. • A review conducted by the U. S. Department of Housing and 

Urban Development evaluating the performance of the six New

England state planning agencies in all aspects of the comprehensive planning process awarded the Statewide Planning Program the highest rating of the six agencies for fiscal year 1976-77. 58

• Preparation of draft legislation creating a state and local land management program continued as a major activity during the year. The 1976 version of the proposed land management bill was substantially reorganized and revised by the Program staff working closely with the Governor's Land Management Task Force. The final bill, which will be considered at the January, 1978, legislative session, is intended to present a balanced approach to the question of land management which accommodates legitimate state concern while reinforcing the traditional role of local government in the majority of land use decisions. Institution of a land management program was a major recommendation of the State Land Use Policies and Plan adopted in 1975 by the State Planning Council as an element of the State Guide Plan. 

Final public hearings were held on Water Quality Management Plans for four drainage basins: the Blackstone, the Moshassuck, the 

Pawcatuck and the Woonasquatuket. Following revision by the 

Program staff these plans were adopted by the State Planning Council as elements of the State Guide Plan. Plans for the remaining three basins in the state: the Narragansett, the Moosup, and the 

Pawtuxet, were completed in final draft form during the year. In addition, the Continuing Planning Process Description for water quality management was completed by the Program staff, approved by the State Planning Council, and submitted to the U. S. Environmental Protection Agency, which approved it subject to revision.

The Plan for Recreation, Conservation, and Open Space was adopted by the State Planning Council as an element of the State Guide Plan on February 18, 1976. The plan also was approved by the Bureau of Outdoor Recreation (BOR); of the U. S. Department of the Interior, as meeting all requirements for a State wide Comprehensive Outdoor Recreation Plan (SCORP). During fiscal year 1977, a joint recreation planning work program was prepared by the Program staff and the staff of the R.I. Department of 

Natural Resources. The work program, required for continued funding eligibility, was approved by BOR on May 23, 1977. Program staff and staff of the Department of Natural Resources also continued refinement of inventory and demand data contained in the SCORP throughout the year. The Program staff also continued to furnish technical assistance to the Trails Advisory Committee in its study of landowner liability exemption legislation. 

The Rhode Island Transportation Plan-1990, adopted by the 

State Planning Council in December, 1975, consolidates all elements of the State Guide Plan primarily concerned with transpor- 59

tation and summarizes all development proposals for highways; urban public transportation; airports; marine terminals; waterways and harbors; parking facilities; and intercity terminals and related facilities. Work on the plan during fiscal year 1977 focused on reassessing the plan's recommendations and priority schedules based on new information obtained from the Program's Continu ing Transportation Planning Process. 

• The Rhode Island State Rail Plan, prepared pursuant to the "Regional Rail Reorganization Act of 1973", was adopted as an element of the State Guide Plan in February, 1976. The first annual update to the plan was completed in July, 1976. The plan evaluates the existing rail system and services in the state and presents recommendations for future service designed to advance the plan's overall goal of “establishing a safe, efficient rail system on a statewide basis capable of meeting the present and future passenger and freight needs and enhancing the environment of Rhode Island." 

Program staff working with staff of the R.I. Department of Economic Development completed the initial draft of an economic development strategy statement. The goal of the statement is the establishment of a continuing planning process that will serve to reduce unemployment and increase income levels in the state. 

The staff also developed a policy statement concerning the use and disposition of state-owned prime industrial lands. A technical paper recommending implementation of this policy was also completed during the year.

Because of the state's need for continuously and systematically compiled industrial space and site data, Program staff and staff of the Department of Economic Development began development of a computerized inventory which will permit efficient and flexible retrieval of information on existing buildings and vacant land in response to client requests. 

• The Overall Economic Development Program (OEDP) progress report for 1975 and program update for calendar year 1976 and beyond were completed by the Program staff and approved by the State Planning Council. In addition, a total of five addendums amending the OEDP were approved by the Council during the year. Increased interest in the OEDP (as indicated by the increased number of project proposal submittals) was stimulated by enactment of the Public Work Employment Act of 1976. 

• Work on the housing element of the State Guide Plan during the year focused on the enunciation of formal goals and policies for 60

housing. The resulting goals and policies statement, which was reviewed in draft form during the year by the State Planning Council, represents a refinement of more general goals and policies contained in the State Land Use Policies and Plan and Goals and Policies for the Development of Rhode Island. 

A housing site survey, first published in 1973 as Technical Paper 

No. 39: "A Survey of Potential Sites for Low and Moderate Income 

Housing", was updated by the Program staff to include the latest data on site availability, water and sewer service, and soil suitability. 

• Preparation of a revised Historic Preservation Plan continued dur ing the year with Program staff providing assistance to the staff of the Historic Preservation Commission in constructing a thematic inventory of historic places and selecting a suitable format for computerization of the inventory data. • Planning and management services provided to the Governor and the executive office staff during fiscal year 1977 covered a wide range of subjects. These included revision and reorganization of draft land management legislation for introduction in the General Assembly; organizational and administrative assistance to the Gov ernor's Land Management and Environmental Task Forces and to the Human Services Management Council; and technical assistance to the Governor's Office in formulating and implementing the Governor's Policy Process. In addition, members of the Program staff assisted the Governor's Office through the preparation of a draft Executive Order concerning management of state-owned land in flood hazard areas, and through participation in the drafting of legislation for the reorganization of state environmental functions. 

Similar management services were also provided to the General Assembly, federal and state agencies, local governments, other states, and the private sector. • The Program, in it's role as state A-95 Clearinghouse, received 427 applications for federal grants or loans under the provisions of federal Office of Management and Budget Circular A-95, Part 1; 

a 54 percent increase over the preceeding fiscal year total. Review was completed and final action taken on 418 proposals by the close of the fiscal year. In addition, the staff received and reviewed 34 applications for federally assisted housing projects, nursing homes, and intermediate health care facilities. 

Seven proposals for direct federal development projects were received during fiscal year 1977 under OMB Circular A-95, Part II. 61

• A computerized file of federal grants-in-aid was developed by the 

Program to track grants reviewed uder OMB Cinrcular A-95. The 

Statewide Planing Program is designated as the State Central In formation Reception (SCIR) Agency for Rhode Island under the provisions of Treasury Circular 1082. Federal agencies are required to notify the SCIR in each state of the purpose and amounts of grants awarded to units of state and local government. 

Fourteen draft environmental impact statements were received by the Program and made available for public review and comment. 

All projects supported by the Rhode Island Port Authority and. 

Economic Development Corporation (RIPA-EDC) must by law conform to applicable provisions of the State Guide Plan. A total of 11 project proposals were submitted by the RIPA-EDC for review during the year and found to be consistent with the State 

Guide Plan. 

During the fiscal year the Program staff analyzed and circulated for review by other agencies 23 proposals for transactions involving state-owned land. The staff also continued maintenance of the Program's computerized inventory of state-owned land which includes all state-owned land except for highway rights-of-way. As of January 1, 1977, the state owned 54,155 acres of land, a net increase of 

596 acres over the January 1, 1976 total.

The Program, acting as the Rhode Island coordinating agency for the National Flood Insurance Program, assisted municipalities in complying with the requirements of the Flood Insurance Program. In February, 1977, the Program, in conjunction with the 

Federal Insurance Administration, sponsored a one-day seminar to familarize local building and planning officials with the revised Flood Insurance Program regulations which became effective on December 1, 1976. In addition, in response to the revised regulations, the Program staff prepared a draft Executive Order specifying management regulations for state-owned land located in flood hazard areas. The Program staff also developed preliminary draft flood plain management regulations to serve as input to the Program's study of possible flood plain development controls. • The Program has provided secretarial support for the Governor's Human Services Management Council since July 1, 1976. During fiscal year 1977 the Council initiated consideration of the transportation needs of human services programs, and sponsored prepara tion of a "Plan for Development of Group Homes for Children and Youth" by the Children and Youth Task Force. 

62

The Program staff completed Technical Paper Number 63, “Inventory of Rhode Island Framework Plans." The inventory lists and briefly describes all major plans prepared by state agencies, including all plans related to human services programs. 

Staff assistance was provided by the Program to the Governor's Environmental Task Force in its study of the conduct of state environmental affairs; and to the Governor's Land Management 

Task Force during its review of the proposed Land Management bill. Both committees completed their respective charges and reported to the Governor during fiscal 1977. 

Program staff worked closely with the Governor's Office and with legislative committees in formulating and securing enactment of legislation reorganizing the state's approach to environmental matters through the establishment of a Department of Environmental 

Management. 

The Program's Research Section continued maintenance and refinement of the socio-economic measures and forecasts necessary to the informed conduct of Program activities. Development and testing of a predictive model for socio-economic indicators was completed during the fiscal year. Using the model, projections were made by five year intervals for total population, total housing units, occupied housing units, automobiles available, school enrollment, resident labor force, and employment by eight industry categories. 

Forecasts were made for the state, city and town, census tract and traffic zone levels of aggregation. The Program staff also prepared population estimates for inter-censal years by county and municipality for publication by the U.S. Bureau of the Census, under the 

Federal-State Cooperative Program for Local Population Estimates. 

• Expansion and updating of the Program's computerized Environmental Inventory continued durig fiscal 1977. New data elements added to the inventory during the fiscal year included average slope and public transit routes. Data files were updated for the public water and sewer service, primary and secondary wetlands, historic districts and sites, and flood hazard areas elements of the inventory. In addition, the Environmental Inventory was used as a reference for a number of studies conducted during the year by the Program, other state agencies, and private consultants. • Throughout the year the Program's Continuing Transportation

Planning activity obtained, analyzed, integrated and reported the transportation data necessary to ensure the responsiveness of the transportation element of the State Guide Plan. Surveillance data 

63

collected included Rhode Island Public Transit Authority rider ship and revenue, motor fuel consumption, commodities traffic at the Port of Providence, and passenger and commodity movement at T. F. Green State Airport. Reappraisal activities focused on the conduct of a modal survey of journey-to school trips in the state and on the development of airport activity forecast models. Development of the 1971 and 2000 transit and highway network models was also completed during the fiscal year. 

The Geographic Base File for the greater Providence area was extensively updated by the Program staff during fiscal year 1977. 

A process of verifying street data through extensive field checks, greatly improved both the address range and map feature accuracies of the file. Effective in mid-September, 1977 the Program will transfer responsibility for the greater Providence area Geographic Base File to the University of Rhode Island's Urban Field 

Center. 

The Program staff, in cooperation with the Rhode Island Depart ment of Transportation and the Rhode Island Public Transit. 

authority prepared a 3-year Transportation Improvement Program 

(TIP) for the state. The TIP is a staged program of transportation improvement projects extracted from the Rhode Island Transpor tation Plan-1990 and the Transportation Systems Management Plan. 

The preliminary Transportation Systems Management Plan 

(TSM), prepared during fiscal year 1976, was revised and expanded by the Program staff during the past fiscal year. Revisions were made to incorporate an extensive analysis of public transit and highway system improvements, and to reflect comments and recommendations made by members of the Transportation Systems Management Subcommittee.

During the past fiscal year members of the Program staff worked closely with the Governor's Office on the development of the Governor's Policy Process-a procedure designed to integrate the development activities of operating state agencies with the policy directions assumed by the Chief Executive. The resulting statement, POLICY-76, establishes a base line of articulated policy for ten areas of state concern. 

The Program provided technical assistance to the General Assembly's joint committee on highway safety and the environment during their deliberations concerning revisions to the state's vehicle inspection and maintenance law. 

64

Work was initiated during the year by the Program staff, in conjunction with the staffs of the Planning and Airports Divisions of the Rhode Island Department of Transportation, on updating the 

Master Plan for Theodore Francis Green State Airport. Two components of the update, the "Preliminary Airport Layout Plan-— 

2000" and the “Airport Terminal Concept-2000”, were completed in draft form by the close of the fiscal year. When completed, the plan will present the recommended capital improvements for the Green Airport during the period 1976-2000 and will include project phasing, time schedules, and cost estimates for each recommended improvement. • Several transportation implementation studies were undertaken by the Program during fiscal year 1977 in support of the transportation elements of the State Guide Plan and of the work of the Planning Division of the Rhode Island Department of Transporta tion. Preparation of the Unified Work Program for Transportation 

Planning accomplished on an annual basis, is intended to summarize all transportation planning activities to be conducted by the various state and local agencies during the ensuing fiscal year. 

Following its completion by the Program staff, the Unified Work Program for Transportation Planning for fiscal year 1978 was approved by the State Planning Council on May 12, 1977. In addition, during the year Program staff members provided technical assistance to a number of transportation implementation studies conducted by other agencies and private consulting firms. The 

Program furnished socio-economic data and various transportation information including trip tables, highway network loadings, and travel time and patterns to a variety of project feasibility, corridor location, and environmetal impact studies.

The Statewide Planning Program continued to provide administrative and technical support to the Rhode Island Coastal Resources Management Council. During fiscal year 1977, the Coastal 

Resources Management Work Program was composed of nine tasks primarily designed to develop and refine the policies of the draft 

Rhode Island Coastal Resources Management Program 

(RICRMP) published in the spring of 1976. In response to public comment received on the spring, 1976 draft RICRMP, the staff of the Coastal Resources Center, University of Rhode Island, and the staff developed a new format for the plan emphasizing goals and policies for the coastal region. A series of draft "findings and policies" statements were released to the public for review and comment. Comments received over a nine month review period were incorporated into the final report, published as the draft 65

Rhode Island Coastal Resources Management Program (RICRMP), by the Coastal Resources Center in July, 1977. The 

Program also coordinated and supervised the planning activities conducted by the Rhode Island Department of Economics Development and private consultants in developing a master plan for the proposed Quonset Point-Davisville Technical Park. By the close of the fiscal year a plan was completed depicting three alternative land use futures or scenarios for the former military base depending on the magnitude of future offshore oil and gas activity in the Atlantic. Assessments of each scenario's likely socio-economic and environmental impacts on the region were also completed and presented together with the plan for public review and comment. • Work continued throughout the fiscal year on the Program's Areawide Wastewater Treatment Management Study, established pur suant to Section 208 of the Federal Water Pollution Control Act 

Amendments of 1972. The 208 Study is designed to formulate an areawide (statewide) wastewater treatment management process to address severe water pollution control problems created by intensive urban-industrial concentrations and other factors. Despite problems and delays associated with an unsuccessful protest of the 208 study's contract award procedures; substantial progress was made on a number of work items in the study. The 208 Project 

Team successfully negotiated contracts with four consulting firms for the conduct of specific work tasks. The 208 staff also completed and published in draft form a number of inventory reports for study tasks being conducted “in-house”. Due primarily to administrative delays, an accelerated time schedule for completion of the 208 Study was developed calling for final submission of the Area wide Water Quality Management Plan and accompanying Environmental Impact Statement to the U. S. Environmental Protection 

Agency by June, 1978.

Staff training continued to be an important supplement to the Program's research and technical strengths. Program staff members improved their technical competence in a variety of fields by participation in over 40 formal training courses, locally organized seminars, and professional and technical meetings and workshops conducted throughout the year. 66

Division of Taxation 

The division is responsible for the administration of all taxes collected at the state level with the exception of payroll taxes collected by the Department of Employment Security. 

Highlights The Collection Section was responsible for collection of $12,405, 527 of delinquent taxes of previously assessed liabilities. This compares most favorably with the period ended June 30, 1976 wherein delinquent collections amounted to $11,770,960. 

The Field Audit Section continued to make an impressive showing in its assigned area. It conducted 1,730 field audits, 1,408 of which were productive in an amount totaling $5,801,884. The percentage of productive audits to total audits was 81.4%. 

Research continued in perfecting the on line computer system, particularly with respect to the processing of business corporation tax returns.

A multistate tax auditor's unit has been set up this year to engage in field auditing of multistate or multinational corporations. It is expected that the efforts of this unit will result in substantial additional revenue from such companies. 68

DEPARTMENT OF BUSINESS REGULATION THOMAS J. CALDARONE, JR., Director Established 1939-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-14 

of the General Laws of 1956, as amended 

Central Office-100 North Main Street, Providence 02903  

92 $1,354,190.00 st The primary function of the Department of Business Regulation is to regulate and license certain businesses and occupations for the protection of the public in its general business affairs. Some of the businesses and occupations licensed include insurance companies, agents, brokers, and casualty insurance adjusters; motor vehicle damage appraisers; securities dealers and brokers; small loan, general loan, and second mortgage lenders; upholsterers and upholstery manufacturers; real estate agents and brokers; hearing aid dealers; tow truck operators; taxis; buses; interstate motor vehicle carriers; and public accountants. Examinations are also given to insure that persons applying for licenses are qualified to practice their business specialty before the public.

Office of the Director 

The Director's Office coordinates and directs the activities of the department to effectively implement all programs and policies carried out by the divisions. Various specific duties are also accomplished exclusively by this office such as hearings as required for rate adjustments requested by Blue Cross-Blue Shield of Rhode Island and the 

Rhode Island Group Health Association, or response to requests for hearings initiated by representatives of the banking, insurance, and real estate industries.

As an officer of the Board of Incorpoation which conducts public hearings on applications for financial institutions, bank charters, and agreements of associations, the director and other Board members either approve or deny such applications. This Board also conducts informal hearings to approve or deny amendment to charters. and agreements of associations. 73

In addition, the Director's Office is responsible for administration and enforcement of the Unit Pricing Act adopted by the 1972 General Assembly and is charged as well with administering the Act which established the Automobile Repair Shop Commission following the passage of a law enacted by the 1974 General Assembly. Among other things, this act requires the licensing of auto body shops by the De partment of Business Regulation. 

Other duties of the Director's Office include continued coordina tion with the Board of Accountancy, licensing of travel agencies and agents, examination of charitable organizations, and licensing burglar alarm businesses and agents. 

AUTO BODY SECTION 

The Auto Body Shop Commission insures that all auto body shops, new and used car agencies participating in auto body repair in the state are properly licensed for the protection of the consuming public. The Commission also investigates all complaints made against auto body shops and provides for their satisfactory resolution. The 

Commission also periodically inspects all body shops with the state. to insure compliance with the provisions of the law and takes action against those operating without a license. Highlights

The purpose of this board is to license all practicing CPAs and PAs in the state each year, to arrange for and proctor the Uniform CPA Examination given twice a year and to act on any complaints. concerning CPAs and PAs brought to the board's attention. TRAVEL AGENCY SECTION 

The Travel Agency Section, established January 3, 1977, has the responsibility of licensing and regulating all travel agencies and trave! agents within the state and those travel agencies located out of state who directly solicit business within Rhode Island. This section must periodically inspect travel agencies for compliance with the law and rules and regulations. In addition, it must handle consumer complaints relative to travel. Rhode Island is unique in the fact that it is the only state within the territorial United States that licenses and controls the travel industry.

Insurance Division 

The Insurance Division regulates the insurance industry in this state through its various units. The Company Examining Section examines the fiscal strength of domestic, foreign and alien companies. Examiners must be certain that companies seeking admission to transact business in the state comply with financial and other requirements and continue to meet rigid standards thereafter. Complete examinations of all domestic insurers are conducted periodically. 

The Health Insurance Section collaborates with the Department of Health in the administration of the Comprehensive Health Insurance Plan Act. A health insurance auditor assists the health insurance analysts in special review work and an actuary assists in the analysis of rates.

The Rating and Forms Section reviews and approves or disapproves all rate and form filings made by companies and rating bureaus.

These 77

analysts also supervise the activities of rating bureaus and advisory or ganizations and regulate the Rhode Island Automobile Insurance Plan and Fair Plan. Also analysts assist the insurance commissioner in the conduct of public hearings and participate in many seminars. 

Complaints concerning insurance claims or problems are handled by a team of insurance investigators. These people work closely with the analysts whose special knowledge may be required for the problems. 

Activities of agents, brokers adjusters or companies are investigated by the special investigators.

The Licensing Section prepares examinations for persons who have applied for licenses, revises these examinations periodically, conducts written or oral examinations, and issues all licenses and certification of licensing for agents, subagents, solicitors, brokers and automobile body appraisers. Company licenses and certification of licenses are also issued by this section upon direction of the chief examiner. 78

Highlights 

Liquor Control Administration 

• The Enforcement Section enforced statues and regulations as they relate to sales by the bottle in package stores or by the drink in bars, lounges, restaurants and clubs. All retail licenses (except transportation) were issued by local boards, and responsibility for enforcement shared with state and local police. Disciplinary actions are initiated by the local boards or by the Liquor Control Administration which also hears appeals from such action. Some of the decisions from the administrator were appealed to the Superior Court, Supreme Court and in one case the Hearing Board. A total of 1,889 licenses have been issued: 312 for package stores and 1,577 for on-premises establishments. • Rhode Island was unique in collecting an import fee on alcoholic beverages as distinguished from the excise tax in other states. The incoming merchandise is inspected and the fees are collected by the division at the time the shipment is received in the state. 

Those procedures hve been in effect with minor changes since the repeal of the Volstead Act.

This system is probably one of the most efficient among the states. All orders for incoming merchandise must be approved. 

Copies of invoices are received from all out-of-state shippers and matched with the order and inspector's report. Collections of service fees in 1976-1977 were $6,997,813.91. There are seventeen holders of wholesale licenses, each of which maintain an open account with the department against which the import fees are charged when due. • License fees collected amounted to $54,447.72 during this fiscal period. One Manufacturer Brewery's license was issued at an annual fee of $3,000.00. Two Manufacturer Winery's licenses were issued at $1,000.00 each. One original license and forty-one duplicates 

Class G Railroad licenses were issued. Thirteen Class G Marine 

Licenses were issued during this fiscal year. 

The Administration issued 187 permits to salesmen who solicit business for wholesalers in this state. This figure included salesmen working for manufacturing companies which held liquor licenses in Rhode Island. 82

The majority of hearings were held on appeals from decision of local licensing boards. There were 48 such appeals taken of which eight were later withdrawn. Twenty-five decisions were rendered by the administrator on appeals that were filed with the department. Three appeals were taken to the Supreme Court from the administrator's decisions and eight decisions were taken to the 

Superior Court.

Public Utilities Commission and the 

Division of Public Utilities and Carriers 

The Commission and Division regulate Rhode Island utilities engaged in intrastate commerce. Coming under the jurisdiction of this agency are Rhode Island's five electric companies, six water companies, six gas companies, six railroads, and the New England Telephone Company. Also subject to the division's jurisdiction are 173 taxicab and limited public motor vehicle certificate holders, 506 intrastate common carriers, including 169 two truck operators.

Since public utilities are, to a substantial degree, privately owned and operated monopolies free from the restraint of ordinary business competition, they are required by law to justify their rates as necessary and essential to the continuance of full and safe company operations. 

They are also obligated to provide adequate and reasonably high quality service to the public on demand and without discrimination. The major activities of the Commission and Division are: 83

-Rate schedule investigations and analysis-the division fully analyzes rate schedules proposed by the public utility companies and participates in public hearings before the Public Utilities. 

Commission which must authorize any change in published rates. —Energy planning-the division has a responsibility to ensure the adequate supply of natural gas and electrical energy to Rhode Island customers at reasonable prices. In order to meet this responsibility the division has become heavily involved in federal proceedings and regional energy planning. -Consumer assistance-a full and strengthened consumer program to insure that utility customers are treated fairly and receive safe and adequate service: this includes specific rules and regulations on shut-offs and gives the utility customer the right to informal and evidentiary hearings. —Licensing-regulation of public utilities and common carriers offering transportation of people and goods through strict licensing procedures and continuing supervision. -Accounting and engineering investigations and hearings―ongoing inspections and auditing to assure that regulated companies are operating properly; resolving complaints and disputes by holding informal hearings with the utility and consumer and, if justified, directing the utility to take corrective action. -Approval of security issuance-establishment through economic analysis of the necessity and propriety for issuing utility securities. --Enforcement-continuing compliance checks of utility economic and safety regulations; road checks and prosecutions of limited public motor vehicle and motor carrier violations. Highlights During the past year 61 formal orders were issued, 78 formal dockets were assigned, publicly advertised hearings were conducted; more than 8,450 public complaints were investigated; 117,700 registrations for motor carriers were issued; and 402 applications and transfers were processed. 84

Real Estate Division 

This division regulates, licenses, and supervises the activities of all real estate brokers and salesmen in the state. New applicants seeking license are required to take a qualifying examination given by this division. The public is protected by an investigation and hearing procedure on complaint of fraudulent activities. Supplementing this are periodic examinations of real estate offices to insure that laws and new rules and regulations are adhered to, and to check escrow accounts. 

Under the provisions of the latest licensing law, brokers are required to keep records for a period of three years showing to whom the money exchanged in a given trasaction belongs, date of deposit, date of withdrawal, to whom paid, and such other pertinent information as the division may require. Also, all third party funds collected by brokers must, under current regulations, be deposited in escrow or non-interest bearing client accounts.

INTERSTATE LAND SALES SECTION 

Regulation and licensing of all development companies involved in interstate land sales are regulated by this section. Highlights Some 17 out-of-state development companies renewed their registration with this section; one registration was returned to a development company for failure to comply with the state act; 11 complaints were received; 8 settled and 3 complaints are in negotiation.

Upholstery Division 

The division enforces several laws which are seemingly unrelated. The division originally was charged with enforcement of bedding and upholstery laws and through the years enforcement of the following laws has been added to the division duties: regulation of the advertising and sale of motor fuel at retail; regulation of advertising of all goods or services through any media (Its intent is to prevent false or fraudulent advertising); the conduct of examinations for the issuance of "hearing aid dealers and fitters" licenses, the collection of fees and general supervision of this field; the inspection of stores where food of any type is offered for sale (This applies principally to chain stores and so-called milk stores and is designed to help the consumer ascertain the correct price of foodstuff in terms of weight or liquid measure units). Highlights • During the year, the following inspections were carried out by investigator-trainees: Upholstery and Bedding 1735; Unit-pricing (Stores) 1857. 

Merchandise was placed off sale in 282 establishments and released in 255 cases after correction of violations.

DEPARTMENT OF COMMUNITY AFFAIRS FREDERICK C. WILLIAMSON, Director Established 1968-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-44 

Main Office-150 Washington Street, Providence 02903  

109 $16,051,482

The Department of Community Affairs is directed towards three major areas of concern affecting people: 

1. Improving the quality of the physical and living environment. 

2. Improving the quality and responsiveness of local government. 

3. Improving the quality of opportunity for the young, poor and disadvantaged.

The accomplishment of these goals is dependent upon both strategic planning and coordination of available resources from federal, state and local governments and also participation with private citizens. Utilizing all available federal, state and local resources, the department seeks to assist local municipalities in solving the problems of modern government. The department renders technical and financial assistance to municipalities to enable them to plan, develop and conduct physical economic, and human resource programs for effective community development. In accomplishing these objectives, the department actively participates in such diverse areas of government as: housing, municipal planning and zoning, transportation, redevelopment of human resources, youth programs, war on poverty, local finance, land use, public administration, crime and delinquency. In all these areas, the department acts in the capacity of a service agency, effectively concentrating and coordinating federal, state, regional, and local public and private efforts towards solving problems of local government. 89

Division of Administration and Program 

Management This division plans, provides and coordinates all internal administrative and supportive services for the department in addition to the performance of mandated duties related to federal, state and local governments. Duties include: departmental budgeting, fiscal management, personnel administration, auditing, purchasing and various management services, preparation coordination and administration of federal grant programs both for the department and for local government and civic agencies; and recommendations, evaluation and review of departmental goals and long-range programs. Highlights • The division continued to serve as the state disbursing agent for four important state programs: Aid to Cities and Towns, Manufacturer's Inventory Tax, Intangible Personal Property Tax, Manufacturers Machinery and Equipment Reimbursements. 

The division administered major federal grants including planning grants to cities and towns funded through the Department of 

Housing and Urban Development (HUD). 

Policemen's and Firemen's Relief: For statistical purposes, compensation to the famiiles of municipal policemen or firemen for death or disabling injury was carried in the budget of the department.

Division on Aging 

The Division on Aging (after July 1, 1977 the Department of Elderly Affairs) is the official representative of the state's 162,000 citizens age 60 and older. It is the single organizational unit that is solely responsible for the administration of federal funds under the 1965 Older Americans Act, as amended. The division promotes and executes programs to meet the present and future needs of the older residents of the state by encouraging and assisting the development of programs for the elderly both at the state level and in the cities and towns. In fiscal 

1977, the division was responsible for administering programs funded under the Older Americans Act totalling in excess of $2.9 million. Highlights The division's statewide Information and Referral Service Unit answered an average of 45 calls per working day. In addition, the 

Information Service Unit made arrangements for direct service aides to meet elderly persons in their homes to assure that information on such programs as housing, health services, transportation, and finances was made known to older persons. • The 5,586 older residents of Ladd School, General Hospital, the Institute of Mental Health, Zambarano Hospital, and other longterm care facilities made day trips to Rocky Point Park under the division's Summer Recreation Program. 

The division's Health Maintenance Program served more than 

9,000 different older persons through no-charge podiatry services at community health centers and through health screenings, health education, and dietary counseling at the senior luncheon meal sites.

The six Nutrition Projects provided nutritious luncheons at least five days per week at 31 different locations across the state. Last year over 430,000 meals were served to Rhode Islanders 60 years and older-an average of about 1,800 per day. 

The Senior Companion Program continued into its fourth year of providing employment/volunteer opportunities for 60 lowincome older people. Senior volunteers work to establish positive person-to-person relationships with long-term care patients in state institutions and work to prevent the institutionalization of others. 

In 16 different workshops, the division's Training Section provided training for staff of state and local programs serving older Rhode 91

Islanders in order to improve the service, quality, and efficiency of programs funded under the Older Americans Act, plus other agencies serving older people. Some 584 persons participated in this training. 

The nursing home ombudsman responded to over 1,100 requests for information and complaints about nursing home care in Rhode Island. The ombudsman serves as liaison among the various governmental agencies which deal with nursing homes, nursing home administrators, nursing home patients and friends of patients. . The division conducted seven public hearings in June, 1977, to give all citizens the opportunity to comment on the division's state plan for spending federal and state dollars for programs and services for older Rhode Islanders.

The division continued to support the Senior Citizen's Transportation Service for persons 60 and older, or handicapped. Fifty minibuses-two equipped with wheelchair lifts-provided approximately 40,000 passenger-trips per month for physicians' visits, dialysis treatments, group meals, marketing, and other necessary purposes. 

Approximately 600 older Rhode Islanders attended the first R.I. 

Governor's Conference on Aging in November, 1976. Several of the recommendations and resolutions adopted at the Conference were enacted into legislation in the following state legislative session.

Division of Housing and Governmental Services 

The division provides information, advice and technical assistance to state municipalities in matters related to community development in four areas: housing assistance; government assistance; tax equalization; and community development training. The primary objective is to improve the knowledge, proficiency and management capabilities of client agencies throughout the state.

Highlights 

OFFICE OF THE CHIEF 

This office provided liaison among state, local and federal agencies dealing with community development programs. It also provided programmatic direction among the four sections of the division as well as coordination among other divisions within the department and other state agencies performing related functions. 

Information, advice and assistance was provided to all 39 municipalities concerning general revenue sharing. All official communications from and to Rhode Island with the Federal Office of Revenue Sharing were channeled through this office. Highlights 

HOUSING ASSISTANCE SECTION 

This section maintained and expanded the state program of housing in the areas of planning, development, information and technical advisory services. In addition, it supervised and administered the federally assisted leased housing program (Section 8) on behalf of 14 Rhode Island communities with a total of some 288 units as well as a community development housing rehabilitation loan program on behalf of eight Rhode Island communities. 

The department provided $200,000 in loans to non-profit housing groups to enable them to help alleviate housing problems in 

Rhode Island. 

Referrals from HUD/FHA housing programs were carried out by this section to accomplish state and local objectives. 

The section continued housing planning for Rhode Island, preparation and dissemination of Housing Market Analysis studies, maintenance and dissemination of an effective housing data collection program, and preparation of programs to improve existing state housing legislation and develop new legislative inititives for improved housing opportunities. 93

⚫ Staff assisted localities to review, formulate, amend and adopt local housing ordinances and interpret state housing legislation. 

Staff assistance was given to local public housing authorities and non-profit housing sponsors in the area of organization, program development and application processing. Municipal officials were assisted in the preparation of Housing Assistance Plans required by the Community Development Act. Advisory services were rendered to landlords, tenants, mobile homeowners and the housing construction industry, and statewide associations of housing of ficials including the State Building Code Commission. Highlights 

GOVERNMENT ASSISTANCE SECTION 

Research and technical assistance was provided to local officials, boards and commissions in matters pertaining to governmental organization and administration. 

The section provided resources and technical assistance to local governments to improve management capabilities in carrying out community development activities. 

The staff compiled and disseminated timely and pertinent information of interest to local government. 

The section maintained a clearinghouse for all legislation introduced into the General Assembly. Legislation having an impact upon municipal government and local officials was given special consideration and was indexed by subject matter and committee. Highlights 

TAX EQUALIZATION SECTION 

Equalized weighted assessed values for each Rhode Island municipality were certified annually to the Commissioner of Education as mandated by statute.

The total number of tax properties in each municipality were uniformly categorized and each category representing more than 10% of the tax roll was sampled. The purpose of the appraisal was to determine “fair market value" in order to establish assessment ratios. Large properties such as industrial, utilities, railroads and large commercial ventures were appraised by outside consultants under contract. • All real estate sales throughout Rhode Island were compiled, tabulated, compared and used in tandem with appraisals to determine assessment ratios. 94

Local tax rolls were certified annually by the local assessor. The tax rolls were reviewed, catalogued and certified to the State Tax Administrator. Highlights 

COMMUNITY DEVELOPMENT TRAINING 

The training team served eligible individuals at every level of the community affairs endeavor. This included concerns of the entry level, clerical, technical, para-professional, administrative, professional, and managerial levels. Links were built to agencies such as municipal departments, housing authorities, social action corporations, redevelopment authorities, elderly service agencies, youth service agencies, model cities agencies, planning boards, nonprofit housing corporations, and state units which focus upon physical and social development of neighborhoods and communities. • Each specific seminar or workshop was designed to increase productivity of local employees and community agencies. 

Division of Human Resource Development 

The division plans, develops, administers and evaluates all human resource programs of the department. It also develops and administers research and demonstration grants in the anti-poverty field and serves as the contact source for all matters dealing with the Federal Office of Community Services Administration and community action agencies within the state.

Highlights Staff provided support and technical assistance to local Parent 

Advisory Committees and the State Parent Advisory Committee for Operation Head Start. 

• The division assisted in the development of new programs and advised the Governor on the content of legal services proposals. 

● The division administered the R.I. Community Action Fund which provides funds to community action agencies and anti-poverty related pilot projects.

Staff continued to coordinate and provide technical assistance to the Rhode Island minority caucus as it expands its activities. throughout the state. 95

The division administered and provided staff support for the project grant for the Community Food and Nutrition Program for the Tribe of Narragansett Indians. 

• The division monitored the Emergency Loan Fund which provides loans to families who have a heat crisis and monitored a basic winterization and boiler repair program which provided insulation, caulking, weather-stripping and boilers repairs to lowincome families. It also administered a research and demonstration grant for solar hot water and retrofit for 40 low-income families.

Division of Planning and Development 

The function of this division is to provide planning and related staff services to the smaller communities of the state which do not have the necessary financial resources to maintain a local professional staff for these purposes.

Highlights Local Planning Assistance Program. Services were provided to local planning boards, chief executives, councils and other local agencies requiring assistance on matters relating to local development. The 

1977 program assisted 24 communities. • The division coordinated with the Statewide Planning Program in study committees and provision of local planning input to various guide plan studies. This included study and comment on referrals. submitted under A-95 Clearinghouse and other referral systems. 

The division monitored and reviewed legislation affecting local planning and development programs. 

Federal Program and Other Special Assistance. Assistance was rendered upon request on matters relating to the investigation of federal program requirements of a planning nature which were needed for grant applications and also, special studies and activities. which were conducted outside of the normal local planning assistance program such as industrial site investigations, and special impact studies. 996

Division of Youth Development 

The division is devoted to developing and administering educational and delinquency preventive programs for the young, and places primary emphasis on helping the disadvantaged and troubled youth of the state. Youth unemployment and the lack of adult role models to emulate are problems of national magnitude. By providing workexperience, counseling and supportive services to thousands of youth, their parents, educators and social service workers annually, the division strives toward positive development of the youth population. Highlights • Call-A-Teen, which is the only state-wide odd-job youth employment program in the nation, serviced approximately 1,000 teens, age 14-17. Administered through 13 satellite field offices, the program employed economically, needy college students as office coordinators and provided needed services to thousands of homeowners and businessmen in addition to the elderly and disabled who make extensive use of the program. 

The federally funded Youth Conservation Corps Program reach ed more than 179 youths (age 15-18) from varied economic, social and racial backgrounds by providing training and education in the field of environmental protection and conservation. • The Governor's Summer Jobs Council, administered by the division working in coordination with other state and federal agencies, the National Alliance of Businessmen, the Department of 

Employment Security and the CETA Administration, provided summer job opportunities to economically disadvantaged youths age 16-21.

The Rhode Island Intern and Volunteer Consortium serviced between 350 and 400 work-study interns and volunteers by placing them in work-experience slots developed by the division. 

While providing viable work-experience to student and volunteer participants, the program also provided needed supplemental manpower to the state and other non-profit organizations. 

The Rhode Island State Government Internship Program placed interns in the legislative, executive and judicial branches of government. This program is under the general auspices of the Legislative Commission on State Government Internships. 

The Youth Advocacy Unit was telephone-based through the 

Youth Help Line and media based through a weekly column 97

"What's Your Problem?", appearing in the Providence Evening

Bulletin. Staff also provided face-to-face individual counseling. 

Service contacts numbered 2200 and included career guidance, financial aid information, family related issues and others, for young people through age 24. ● The Runaway Service Unit received $36,000 from the Department of Health, Education and Welfare for fiscal 1977. This money was made available to provide assistance to states, localities and non-profit private agencies for the development of facilities outside the legal system to handle the immediate needs of runaway youth.

State Building Code Commission 

The purpose of the commission is to develop and to assist in the uniform enforcement of the State Building Code, to review and update regulations in accordance with new design and construction concepts, and to provide technical services for the local communities in relation to Code interpretations. Highlights • Activities included: provision of code enforcement and plan review for all state funded projects; provision for tests and evaluations of all new materials and systems for use by the building industry; implementation of a certification training program for local building officials to aid in uniform code interpretation; and provision of a continuing education program based on seminars in which topical items were discussed with representatives of the building industry. • The commission acted as a board of appeals for variances from code provisions after local action had been exhausted. Also, it issued rules and regulations on the construction standards for mobile homes and recreational vehicles and provided for reciprocity with other states. . The commission established and implemented a licensing program for concrete testing laboratories and personnel. 

A review and evaluation of all variances required for historic building and district restoration was conducted. 98

SOUTH KINGSTOWN, R. I-Site of the Great Swamp Fight- 

In South Kingstown, an obelisk marks the location of the Great Swamp Fight which took place on December 19, 1675, during King Philip's War and resulted in the near annihilation of the Narragansett Indian tribe.

In addition to maintaining the state's penal system for adult and juvenile offenders, the department, working through its Youth Services and Probation and Parole Divisions, is actively involved in both crime prevention and rehabilitative services to convicted felons. 

Division of Adult Services ADULT CORRECTIONAL INSTITUTIONS

Division of Field Services ADULT PROBATION AND PAROLE 

Adult Probation and Parole is a statewide, centrally administered, system offering its investigative and counseling services to all the district and superior courts of the state. The agency also serves parolees released from the Adult Correctional Institutions and offenders from out of state supervised under the Interstate Compact. This unit provides a comprehensive community-based service, operating in most cases, as a successful and more desirable alternative to institutionalization. As an 101

adjunct to the courts, it is easily accessible to all its clientele with 13 different office locations situated throughout the state. 

Adult Probation and Parole is responsible for implementing programs and services designed to be both preventive and corrective. The unit endeavors to provide an individualized approach in its efforts to assist adult offenders to establish or resume a productive and meaningful role in the community. This agency is charged with the responsibility for the investigation, supervision and control of all adult offenders either placed on probation or suspended or deferred sentence by the district and/or superior courts of the state. The unit also supervises those offenders released on parole from the Adult Correctional Institutions. The Adult Probation and Parole Unit is further responsible for conducting character investigations of applicants for employment at the Adult Correctional Institutions and for applicants for probation and parole counselor positions.

Highlights The Adult Probation and Parole Unit underwent an extensive reorganization during fiscal 1977. Eleven senior probation and parole counselors were assigned to probation offices throughout the state. These counselors are functioning as "office managers"-assuming responsibility for many of the non-professional, but necessary, duties previously performed by supervisors. Probation and parole supervisors, thus relieved of responsibility for routine office managerial duties, are now concentrating their full attention on rendering meaningful casework supervision to probation counselors. The eleven senior probation and parole counselors continue to service full caseloads in addition to discharging their new duties. In addition to greatly strengthening the administration and supervision of the unit, the assignment of these counselors added an important new dimension to the career ladder opportunities available within this component of the criminal justice system. 

During the year plans were made to recruit, train, and utilize senior citizen volunteers on an experimental basis in the Superior Court in Providence. The volunteers after being trained for courtroom duties will be in attendance in all Superior Courtrooms in Providence where criminal matters are being heard. The volunteers will greatly assist the probation and parole counseling staff by monitoring courtroom activity and reporting to probation supervisors any referrals made to the Adult Probation and Parole Unit by the Superior Court. Probation counselors will thus be relieved of these routine courtroom duties. The utilization of volunteers in the Superior Court, already approved by the presiding justice 102

N- and the associate justices of the Superior Court, will thus permit counselors to devote more of their time in rendering meaningful and intensive supervision to the clients they serve. 

The unit initiated the implementation of a new information system that should reflect more meaningful statistics on agency services in the future.

A non-standard workweek for the probation counselors and supervisors continued to provide needed flexibility of personnel to meet the needs of both the clients and the courts. 

Following contract guidelines, caseloads are currently being reduced to 65 active cases, with the suspending of supervision on the balance of the cases. Counselors will thus be able to work more intensively with those offenders who are more in need of assistance. 

Male and female caseloads are being integrated. 

Staff training and development were emphasized through workshops, conferences and frequent staff meetings.

JUVENILE PAROLE UNIT 

The Juvenile Parole Unit renders intensive and meaningful guidance and supervision on a day-to-day basis to those youngsters who are re leased from custody at the Youth Correctional Center or the Training 

School for Boys and Girls and who are placed on probation by the Family Court. Probation counselors work intensively with controlled caseloads that do not exceed 20 cases. In most cases these youngsters are considered to be "hard core", emotionally disturbed, or the most difficult to reach. Many have a long history of recidivism. Counselors endeavor 103

to assist such youngsters to overcome the problems that precipitated their incarceration. Counselors, knowledgeable in existing community resources, make appropriate referrals to public and private agencies. 

Youngsters are assisted by counselors to effect basic changes in antisocial attitudes. Counselors assist the youngster to improve his selfimage and to strengthen his interpersonal relationship with members of his family. Frequent consultation is held with professional and paraprofessional personnel involved in the treatment plan. Highlights • During this year of operation, 288 probation cases and 78 temporary community placement cases received intensive service. 

Only 45 of the 366 youths serviced by the Juvenile Parole Unit were remanded to one of the juvenile correctional institutions by the Family Court of Rhode Island. 

• The Juvenile Parole Unit team continued to successfully reduce the steadily increasing rate of recidivism among juvenile correctional institutions. 

Staff training and development were emphasized through workshops, conferences and frequent staff meetings.

JUVENILE PROBATION 

Juvenile Probation and Parole is a statewide, centrally administered, system which offers investigative and counseling services to the Family Court of Rhode Island. This agency provides a comprehensive community based service, operating, in most cases, as a successful and more desirable alternative to the institutionalization of children in the state's juvenile correctional institutions. The unit investigates and supervises juvenile and adult offenders, male and female, who are referred to it by the Family Court. It also investigates and supervises Interstate Com- 104

pact cases of juveniles and collects restitution and support payments from probationers under its supervision. This component of the Juvenile Criminal Justice System is also responsible for conducting character investigations of applicants for employment at the Adult Correctional Institutions and for applicants for probation and parole counselor positions. 

The agency is responsible for implementing programs and services designed to be both preventive and corrective in nature. It endeavors to provide an individualized approach to assist juvenile offenders to overcome those problems that resulted in their referral to Family Court. Highlights • The Juvenile Probation Unit underwent an extensive reorganization during fiscal 1977. Six senior probation and parole counselors were assigned to probation offices throughout the state. The senior probation and parole counselors are functioning as "office managers”—assuming responsibility for many of the non-professional, but necessary, duties previously performed by supervisors. 

Probation and parole supervisors, thus relieved of responsibility for routine office managerial duties, are now concentrating their full attention on rendering meaningful casework supervision to probation counselors. The six counselors continue to service full caseloads in addition to discharging their new duties. In addition to greatly strengthening the administration and supervision of the Juvenile Probation Unit, the assignment of these counselors added an important new dimension to the career ladder opportunities available within this component of the criminal justice system. 

The Juvenile Probation Unit initiated the implementation of a new information system that should reflect more meaningful statistics on agency services in the future. ● A non-standard workweek for counselors and supervisors continued to provide needed flexibility of personnel to meet the needs of both the clients and the courts. • Case assignment to probation counselors serving the greater Providence area continues to be made on a rotating basis. The immediate result of this change in case assignment from the previously utilized system of arbitrary geographical determination of caseload responsibility has resulted in the establishment of numerically equitable caseloads for counselors. The ultimate result should be a better quality service rendered to the clients. This system of case assignment is gradually being extended to other areas of the state. 105

• Staff training and development were emphasized through workshops, conferences and frequent staff meetings.

STATE MARSHALS (COMMITTING SQUAD) 

The State Marshals are responsible for transporting all prisoners, adult and juvenile, male and female, to all of the courts of the state. The unit is also responsible for transporting all prisoners from the courts to the designated place of detention. The State Marshals are responsible for the custody and safety of all such prisoners while transporting them to and from the courts of the state or otherwise while prisoners are in their custody. The Marshals also maintain custody of prisoners while they are detained in a courthouse, and during the court's hearing of their cases.

PAROLE BOARD 

The Parole Board is an autonomous unit within the Department of Corrections and receives staff assistance from the Division of Field Services. The Parole Board consists of five part-time members, appointed by the Governor, who serve staggered, renewable five year terms. The 

Board meets at least once a month, and as often as necessary to review the applicants for parole who become eligible during the month. In addition to considering new applicants, the Board also considers: all reports of parole violations committed by previously paroled inmates; and parole applications by those inmates who have previously been denied and are therefore eligible for reconsideration at six month intervals.
