Division of Support Services CORRECTIONAL TRAINING ACADEMY 

Professional in-service and pre-service training of personnel within the Department of Corrections is now underway at the newly reorganized and expanded Correctional Training Academy. As the complexity and sophistication of the law enforcement/custodial/rehabilitative process increases, the demands upon staff at all levels becomes greater, and the need for high caliber, specialized training becomes urgent. The 

Correctional Training Academy is Rhode Island's response to this need. 

Programs at the Academy address the needs of adult and juvenile custodial staff, as well as probation and parole counselors, Youth Service Bureau personnel, non-custodial workers and administrative staff. Correctional officer pre-service training is the most conspicuous activity, but in-service programs of many varieties are becoming increasingly active. Highlights 

The Training Academy completed four six (6) week training sessions for correctional officer trainees during the past fiscal year. 

Instruction includes a thorough background in corrections and the correctional process, intensive skills training and one week of onthe-job training. • Newly employed adult inmate counselors, juvenile unit managers and probation and parole counselors have been integrated in the correctional officer trainees program. Identifying those areas of value to their positions, these men and women attend the Academy on a part-time basis. This "melting pot" method of training is intended to open communication between divisions. 

Intensive Training for the new Drug Unit Treatment Team was conducted.

Pre-service training and orientation programs were being designed and implemented for all correctional personnel. • Mini-workshops covering union relations, transaction analysis, counseling models, first aid and alcohol use and abuse were developed and offered throughout the department. 

The New England Corrections Coordinating Council Mobile 

Training Unit conducted a number of two day workshops prepared specifically for correctional personnel. Topics included law and legal responsibilities, supervisory management and interpersonal skills. 108

​Middle Management Conferences designed and conducted by the New England Corrections Coordinating Council were ongoing throughout the year. These were taken advantage of by all agencies within the department. • Film previewing was an ongoing project for the Academy this year. Several films have been purchased for a film library available to all personnel. An annotated log of all previewed and purchased films was kept up to date for review at any time. • A resource library was set up in the Academy. It contains training literature, general information, journals and periodicals, annual reports and papers. 

Seventy-three correctional officers completed pre-service training the past fiscal year. Seventy-three probation and parole personnel including supervisors, counselors and steno-typists; 64 juvenile personnel; 34 correctional officers; twelve correctional educators; three training staff; eighteen adult counselors, and 13 industries personnel took part in in-service training. 

EDUCATION ADMINISTRATION UNIT 

A multi-faceted education division plans, coordinates and implements a broad range of academic, vocational and avocational programs throughout the several components of the department. Funded in large part through federal grants, the education division coordinates programs and personnel for all agencies and institutions operating under the aegis of the department. Certified teachers are available to the adult and juvenile populations.

Highlights The Arts in Corrections program, a joint venture with the Rhode Island State Council on the Arts, continued to provide workshops and classes to residents at both the adult and juvenile facilities. 

Over 150 residents participated in activities which included painting, design, music, creative writing, art appreciation, leather work and ceramics.

The Department of State Library Services continued its support and assistance by purchasing a number of books to stock the library in medium security. All residents in medium are eligible to borrow books. Department of State Library Services provided two staff librarians to assist the inmates in selection of books and to advise the Education Unit on library expansion. 

• The unit also received assistance through the Roger Williams Col lege Special Projects Office. Several residents of the Adult Correc- 

109

tional Institutions and correctional officers received degrees at graduation ceremonies on the Roger Williams campus. • Rhode Island College entered into an agreement with the unit to provide assistance in developing learning programs, needs assessment, and technical assistance. It is anticipated that this arrangement will be beneficial to both parties involved and enable the unit to keep up with the latest developments in the field of education.

The unit implemented a Title III CETA Summer Program for Economically Disadvantaged Youth (S.P.E.D.Y.) at the Youth 

Career Education Center. This program is a vocationally oriented, three phase experience which embodies both the practical and the theoretical aspects of the job situation while the students are being paid. Sixty percent of the time is spent at the actual work site in an on-the-job training environment, while twenty percent of the project time is used for related academic education. 

The remaining twenty percent of program time is used for career exploration. The program encompasses 80 placements on Youth 

Career Education Center grounds and 25 placements in the community. ⚫ The Education Unit continued its participation in the department's Training Academy by assisting in screening applicants, conducting training sessions and developing training formats. 

The Adult Basic Educatin program remained the area of primary emphasis at the adult facilities. This program was one of two selected from 19 throughout the state to be studied and evaluated by the Curriculum Research and Development Center at the

University of Rhode Island. Interim reports have been favorable and it is anticipated that forthcoming recommendations be implemented.

On the juvenile level, nearly 300 different youths participated in the regular education program at the Youth Career Education Center. Program emphasis continued to concentrate on vocational/career exploration, life coping skills and basic academics. 

HEALTH SERVICES ADMINISTRATION 

Beginning in October, 1976, Health Care Services was organized within the department. Within this office were placed Medical Services, Counseling Services, the Classification Unit and drug and alcoholism programming. Health Care Services became responsible for the physical and mental health of all department inmates. Responsibilities for these 110

activities are shared between Health Care Services and the Division of 

Youth Services in the juvenile area. 

Highlights This unit made measurable progress in upgrading medical care; establishing counseling services; intergrating the classification unit with a new record-keeping system; and implementing new alcoholism and drug treatment programs. 

Health Care Services had been funded by LEAA, but as of July 1, 1977, the state assumed fiscal responsibility for approximately 75% of the federally funded Health Care Program, making it a permanent unit within the department. • The Medical Services Section centralized medical personnel so that all of the Adult Corectional Institutions' nurses are based at maximum security; this makes it possible to cover all facilities more effectively when workloads increase in any one facility. • Counseling Services are now doing intake social histories and are providing more comprehensive reports to the Parole Board than in recent years.

RESEARCH AND PLANNING UNIT 

The Research and Planning Unit is responsible for the development of programs to address the immediate needs and long range goals of the Department of Corrections. The unit prepares applications for Law 

Enforcement Assistance Administration funds, secures grants, manages programs, monitors and evaluates project performance and plans for new and innovative approaches to correctional problems. In addition to administering grants amounting to more than $500,000, the unit also prepares research documents and reports on correctional inmates and programs. Highlights The annual traffic report of the Adult Correctional Institutions documented an 8.7% increase in adult population from fiscal year 

1976. The total population rose to 668 on June 30, 1977. Approximately 2,050 persons were incarcerated at the A.C.I. during the year as sentenced, awaiting trial or fined inmates. The traffic report also showed very little change in the juvenile population. 

The average daily on-grounds counts were 71 and 69 for the fiscal years 1976 and 1977. • The department received a grant from the National Institute of Corrections to employ one graduate student intern during the 111

summer. Two papers estimating population projections through 

1985 and examining the impact of determinate sentencing on the population were completed. 

The furlough program was evaluated in November, 1976, and showed that fewer than 3% of the 2,000 furloughs granted resulted in unsuccessful completion. • Planning assistance was given to administrators in the design of the a Detention Center to be located on the grounds of the Training School. The plan was approved by the state and the center will begin operations in 1978. 

The publication of characteristic reports describing adult and juvenile populations according to age, race, sex, prior convictions, length of sentence, drug dependency, etc., was accomplished. 

The unit was responsible for developing grant applications to a number of federal agencies.

Q Work was completed for an inmate handbook. This included a description of departmental policies, procedures, and regulations along with brief explanations of programs and services available to the inmate population. 

The unit managed a $116,000 LEAA grant to secure consultant services through the negotiated procurement method. This required developing request for proposals, drafting contracts and managing all phases of all contract work. 

The unit was also engaged in a large number of special studies for the director. These included: Classification/Parole Study, Awaiting Trial Population Report, School District Plan, Victim Restitution Study, Persons Serving Fines. A series of special reports was also completed: Youth Service Bureau: Cranston Pilot Study, Testing at the Juvenile Diagnostic Center, Overcrowding at the Training School, Comparison of 1973 and 1976 Cohorts: Boys at the Training School. 

112

Division of Youth Services MCCABE DIAGNOSTIC CENTER 

Children are referred to the Diagnostic Center by Family Court judges for diagnosis, evaluation and recommendations. Sixteen boys and six girls may be served at one time. The Diagnostic Center serves children from 7 to 18 years of age. A child brought to the attention of Family 

Court for any reason (acting-out behavior, trouble at home or school, stealing, etc.) may be referred to the Diagnostic Center if the judge feels more information about the child and his motivation would aid in the successful disposition of the case. 

A Diagnostic Center workup varies with the needs of the child but always includes a comprehensive physical examination and referral to one of the full-time clinical social workers. When deemed necessary or indicated, diagnosis and evaluation may contain all or some of the following: examination by a psychiatrist; examination by a chief clinical psychologist; examination by a clinical psychologist; testing for learning disabilities by the coordinator of education and training. 

In addition, children detained at the Diagnostic Center are taken on field trips which provide both a recreational outlet for the children and an opportunity for the staff to evaluate the child's behavior in a social setting. Highlights • During the fiscal year 218 children (156 boys and 62 girls) were admitted. A total of 7,610 days of service were provided. This led to an average daily census of almost 21 children—an occupancy rate of 95%. Length of stay at the center decreased to 33 days. • Through cooperation with the Department of Social and Rehabilitive Services, claims were initiated for $88,421 for services to children under Title XX of the Social Security Act. 

Students from Brown University, through the campus chapter of the Jericho Society, came to the center weekly for educational and recreational programs. 

The agency continued to function as a field work setting for undergraduate students from Providence College. 

Recreational programming was continued without cost through the cooperation of the 455th General Hospital Unit (USAR).

Unit members used the center's facilities for evening and weekend drills. 113

The Center continued participation in the Arts in Corrections program under the sponsorship of the Council on the Arts.

OFFICE OF VOLUNTEER SERVICES 

The Office of Volunteer Services is designed to develop, implement, monitor and evaluate volunteer services for the institutions and agencies of the department. Volunteers are involved in a variety of programs and are used to provide personalization of service for selected department clients. They assist professional counselors with non-professional tasks and have some impact on the problem of idleness within department facilities.

Highlights Volunteers In Corrections became the Office of Volunteer Services. • Considerable effort was invested in the process of needs analysis and program development, culminating in the initiation of three pilot projects using volunteers with adult clients. • In cooperation with the Governor's Office on Citizen Participation, two VISTA workers were assigned to the Office of Volunteer Services in order to develop the Transition Project, an intensive effort to provide job development and readjustment counselling for youthful offenders (18-24) leaving the Adult Correctional Institutions.

Some 318 people attended pre-service training for the various volunteer tasks and, while not all chose to participate, this was a gain in public education. In all, the Office of Volunteer Services staff spent over 100 hours as instructors in pre-service and inservice training. 114

The use of student interns was significantly expanded throughout the department.

A family picnic attended by approximately 125 volunteers and department staff was held in May at the Haffenreffer Estate in 

Bristol. Volunteers received certificates, longevity pins and plaques for outstanding service to the department.

RESIDENTIAL SERVICES UNIT The Residential Services Unit (formerly Foster Placement Program) 

was established to provide for the placement of predelinquent and delinquent youngsters in alternative living situations when the family environment is judged by Family Court to be inadequate for, or incompatible with effective rehabilitation or treatment. The purpose of the program is to demonstrate that troubled youths can receive better and more effective treatment through the use of community-based resources. 

The program's staff provide for the placement of youngsters in halfway houses, group homes and residential living situations, including individual foster homes. 

Referrals are received from the McCabe Diagnostic Center, Youth

Service Bureau, the Training School, Juvenile Probation and Parole 

Unit and the Family Court. Although others are serviced, Training 

School youths are the primary target population for this project. 

Priority is also placed on those youngsters who do not have homes to which they may return.

Highlights • During the fiscal year, 216 referrals were received. Of these, 86 youngsters were placed in group homes, foster homes and other residential centers; 82.5 percent of the youngsters placed were 

Training School residents. 

• The progress of each youngster in placement was carefully supervised and progress reports were prepared. 

As a result of the Residential Services Program, the population of the Training School has been reduced significantly. 115

TRAINING SCHOOL FOR YOUTH 

The primary objectives of the school are to educate, train, rehabilitate, and re-integrate the delinquent and wayward boys and girls detained by order of the Family Court. Programs are offered to secure the guidance and control which serve the youth's general welfare as well as the bests interests of the state; to strengthen and conserve family ties whenever possible; and to provide the equivalent of the supervisory care normally provided by parents. 

There is a basic academic and vocational schedule at the school which operates in conformance with the Department of Education and also with the established guidelines set by the United States District Court. Residents can be trained to achieve skills which will assist them in becoming useful and productive citizens when they leave the school. 

Community schools are available to those students who have demonstrated their desire for higher learning and have shown a sense of responsibility. The emphasis during the past fiscal year was to place residents in community-based programs as soon as feasible: vocational programs, semi-release homes, group homes, and residential treatment centers.

Highlights • The counseling staff was augmented with a unit manager for all cottages and the YCC. All cottage personnel report to a unit manager who is solely responsible to the superintendent. 

The concept of custody and control is being transformed from that of a large school environment to that of a small group facility so that the basic philosophy and goals of the Training School can be communicated to each resident and community worker. The smaller cottage group became a working team through staffing with a unit manager, a senior youth home life supervisor, a counselor, a probation counselor and the youth home life supervisors. The staff was further augmented by volunteers such as senior citizens who are acting the role of foster grandparents to the children. 

The cottage teams were further augmented by an academic and/or vocational teacher, the school psychologists and the recreational staff. All of these are backed up by the medical, dental and psychiatric services to implement the individual plan developed and presented to the court on the treatment of each resident as he moves through the school into the community-based programs. 

Ten to thirty students remained working in community-based workshops so that they can re-enter their own communities as soon as possible. 116

The program of pre-release trial community placements (TCP) which was developed at the school through the cooperation of the Family Court proved very successful. During the past year the program continued to be effective with the assistance of the treatment team and the Control Unit and follow-up programs of the 

Division of Probation and Parole. 

Universities and colleges throughout the state gave support through volunteer student participation in individual and group counseling. 

Some residents were afforded the opportunity to work in alternative programs other than at the Training School with the help of counselors, teachers and other professionals. The Family Court along with the Division of Probation and Parole have been cooperating with the Training School in the development of such programs resulting in a general decrease of residents being held at this institution. These new programs absorbed a number of residents who, in the past, had no other place to go except the 

Training School. 

The superintendent revised the review system within the institution so that all children coming to the Training School, with the exception of holds and those children awaiting trial, have a diagnostic evaluation within a period of thirty days. These reviews are held in the superintendent's office with the superintendent taking the minutes and making those minutes part of the child's record. The primary reason for this review system is so that each child will not be lost within the system and that the goals and objectives as set forth for each child can be monitored closely. 

The recreation department at the Training School was reorganized to include a recreation director and an assistant recreation director as well as two recreation leaders. The programs, as reorganized, includes recreation on Saturday and Sunday as well as the weekdays. In order to better utilize the recreation staff, offgrounds activities have been placed within the jurisdiction of the unit managers.

Work Release programs continued to be used as part of the program within the Training School. The use of Summit House by 

Training School residents for work release and school release programs continued and was more effectively used this fiscal year. 117

YOUTH SERVICE BUREAU 

The Youth Service Bureau's primary goal is to divert youngsters from formal entry into the juvenile justice system and/or adult justice system. Emphasis is placed on short term, one-to-one client counseling, referrals, family counseling and work with other community agencies. Referrals are receive from local police departments, family members, school departments and other social agencies. The Youth Service 

Bureau has eight offices located throughout the state in strategic areas to facilitate easy access to the general population as well as access to various other community agencies. 118

DEPARTMENT OF ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT JAMES O. ROBERSON, Director Established 1974-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-63-1 

Offices-One Weybosset Hill, Providence 02903  

142 $26,166,725

The department was created to take over all of the functions of its predecessor, the Rhode Island Development Council, and to assume responsibility for operating the Governor's Office of Manpower Affairs 

(retitled Division of Job Development and Training) and the Economic 

Renewal Coordinating Center. To further insure a cooperative effort, the department also provides administrative and staff assistance to the state's various development-related auhorities and public corporations: 

the Industrial Building Authority, Recreational Building Authority, Rhode Island Industrial Facilities Corporation, Howard Development Corporation and the Port Authority and Economic Development Cor poration.

With five divisions-Business and Industry, Tourist Promotion, Eco nomic Research, Job Development and Training and Management and Planning and the Administrative and Public Information sectionsthe department encourages the location and development of new industrial, commercial and recreational facilities in the state. 

A program of economic research, planning and forecasting with respect to the economy of the state is maintained by the department. 

ADMINISTRATIVE SECTION 

All financial and budgetary operations are the function of this section as well as all regulative details concerning employees. It coordinates the internal activities of the divisions and acts as the main point of contact with other state departments, agencies and with the general public. 

The section is directly responsible for the provision of administrative services to the state's various development-related authorities and public corporations. 123

PUBLIC INFORMATION SECTION 

The section conducts a continuing public information program which includes preparation of promotional literature, news releases and audiovisual presentations. Public Information assistance is provided by the section to divisions of the department, to the corporations and authorities administered by the department and to other agencies. Public information services to private firms for such events as ground-breakings and plant dedications also are a section responsibility. Highlights • During the reporting period, the section has assisted the Rhode Island Commodores in their efforts to interest out-of-state businessmen and industrialists in Rhode Island, the Gaspee Day Committee and the Department of Natural Resources continuing promotion of historic Fort Adams in Newport. • The section was singularly responsible for all liaison between the department and the department's advertising agency. 

The Public Information Section assisted reporters from various regional and national publications preparing news and feature stories on Rhode Island, including: New York Times, Washington 

Post, Boston Globe, Christian Science Monitor and Forbes Magazine. 

In an effort to stimulate industrial growth in Rhode Island cities and towns, the section assisted local area development agencies in the preparation of promotional literature. It also gave extensive assistance to the Greater Providence Convention Bureau in the development, design and preparation of special promotional pamphlets to attract convention business to the capital city area. 

The group visit of 12 journalists to Rhode Island was coordinated by the section for national publications including: U.S. News and World Report, Journal of Commerce, Christian Science Monitor, Washington Star Syndicate and Wall Street Journal. 

One of the department's major undertakings during the reporting period was the installation of a Briefing Center at the Green State Airport to provide industrial prospects with a "total information and conference" locale to eliminate unnecessary, time-wasting travels about the state. The entire development and construction project was coordinated by the section which currently is responsible for management of the Center, and the preparation and updating of customized business-type audio-visual presentations. The Center is comprised of a large reception area with limited kitchen. 124

facilities, a sound-proofed oval auditorium with rear-screen projection system and other media facilities, an operations center for audio-visual equipment, a computerized control room that oversees the auditorium and a small office for the Center manager. 

Built at a cost of $250,000, the expense of construction was shared with the Department of Transportation which also included expansion and upgrading of terminal storage and security areas. 

In cooperation with WPRI-TV, East Providence, the section provided extensive assistance to Channel 12 in its effort to launch a statewide image campaign to help Rhode Islanders better understand the endowments of their state. Staff provided historical and statistical materials as well as several hundred 35mm slides for use in development and presentation of the campaign. Also being used is the "state song"-"It's All Here”—which is used as background music for certain portions of the audio-visual presentation at the department's Briefing Center. 

The section continues to assist the Tourist Promotion Division in the design and printing of all of the state's tourist promotion literature.

As part of a continuing cooperative editorial program, the section prepared articles and photographs for placement in the August 

1976 issue of U.S. Investor/Banker-a survey of the economic conditions in Rhode Island. Assistance also was given to numerous other publications and organizations nationwide working on 

Rhode Island oriented projects.

Business and Industry Division 

The division's primary function is the creation of new job opportunities for Rhode Islanders. This is pursued within the professional and confidential standards acceptable to the National Association of Corporate Real Estate Executives and the American Industrial Development

Council. Division standards are measured by the manufacturers' technical and factual data supplied toward particular corporate needs in their relocation and expansion questions. Highlights 

Direct Contact With Rhode Island Manufacturers: Direct field assistance was provided for financing, manpower training, site selection and international trade opportunities. Fiscal year ended 125

calculations show 427 personal calls on Rhode Island manufacturers.

Manufacturing calls begun in 1975, have accumulated data supportive to the continuing need for more involvement in this area. Several major interests begun in 1975 have culminated during this fiscal year and include: Unit Tool Company, Avid Corporation, Fram Corporation, A & Z Chain Company, Union Camp, Cooley Incorporated, Janus Manufacturing Company, Centredale 

Sign, Poly Dyeing and Yankee Seafoods.

One industrial representative's records reflect totals on manufacturing calls alone as assisting 10 companies which were included in 107 calls. These 10 companies increased employment by 205 jobs, with plant expansions of 203,000 square feet, and totaling just under $5 million in new capital investments. Other new industrial construction either underway or announced reflects approximately 1,000,000 square feet of new space to be built at an approximate cost of $25 million.

Contacts With Manufacturers Outside the State of Rhode Island: 

This was accomplished in several ways: by national and international advertising, by letters and telephone, and by personal contact. This objective is being accomplished by a particular campaign aimed at the nation's non-polluting, high technology, and laborintensive industries. Letters, advertising brochures, and personal contact explain the business climate and the benefits to be achieved by an expansion or relocation to Rhode Island. Corporations were carefully screened to insure their compatibility with the state's economic development goals. "Standard and Poor's Register” and "Dun and Bradstreet" are utilized for this investigation. Coupled with this is the use of the concluded study by Harbridge House. 

This was a market analysis for target industries holding all the compatible and idealistic characteristics suitable to the Rhode Island economy. A letter campaign was conducted, related to and in conjunction with a publications advertising program. To date, some 

2,000 letters have been sent to specific companies and personal contact results from this have been rewarding. 

There were also 87 individual out-of-state personal manufacturing calls. These calls were primarily concentrated in the competitive New England states area; other personal out-of-state calls were made in the states of Louisiana, Texas and Illinois. 

The opening of the new Briefing Center at the T. F. Green 

State Airport the first of the year has had a marked effect on the 126

state's overall capabilities. This facility is currently being used daily, with an attendance per briefing average of seven persons. 

Not only have executive-level decision makers in search of ecoomic facts influencing expansion or relocation been in attendance there, but also audiences from the various cities and town council members, various state departments, and various private groups representing Rhode Island business interests, and members of local news media. This particular tool, a personal touch visual aid, is doing more to project the state's attitude toward business than possibly any other segment of operations.

Advertising Campaign: In conjunction with letters and the Briefing 

Center, was a national and international advertising campaign. The following is an analysis of the response to this advertising campaign: Requests received to date total 9,341. Requests have come from 50 states and Puerto Rico, and from 76 foreign countries. The top 7 states whose manufacturers requested information are represented by the State of New York, with nearly 1,600 letters of inquiry; the State of California second, with some near 900 requests; 

Massachusetts, with over 600; Connecticut with over 500; Illinois with over 400; New Jersey with over 400; Pennsylvania with over 

300 and Michigan with over 300.

During May 1977, the division published a 21-page profile story of Rhode Island in Forbes Magazine. Part of this venture was the receipt of 20,000 copies of the story for private mailing. 

Community Officials Contacts: The division has established work ing relationships with local government officials, chambers of commerce executives and development organizaions to aid in establishing and aiding direction in their industrial development program. Within this area, the division has representation on the newly formed Woonsocket Regional Industrial Development Cor poration and works closely with the Providence Chamber of Commerce industrial program in downtown redevelopment interests as well as Aquidneck Island Development Corporation, Pawtucket- 

Blackstone Valley and others.

Properties Department: Staff maintained, a full-time properties inventory of sites and buildings monitored various publications and made contact with all Rhode Island's certified industrial realtors and the State Board of Realtors in acquiring available properties that are for sale, lease or rent, including lands and buildings. The state has a total of 19 fully-utilitied industrial parks. Of the 19, five have partial utilities and three have rail service. There is now a total of 452 available acres in fully-utilitied 127

industrial parks. Rail is available in some 285 acres of industrial park spaces. In addition, the division has inventoried over 4,000 acres, ranging in parcels from one to 500 acres in size, of land that is zoned for idustry but does not necessarily have utilities. 

Summary of major requests for buildings are as follows: 60% for 10,000 to 30,000 square feet; 25% for 5,000 to 15,000 square feet; 10% for 50,000 to 100,000 square feet and above; and 5% for more than 100,0000 square feet. Requests for rail users has dropped some from previous reports and now is about 30% overall. 

Navy Properties: The current job population at Navy excess property is approaching 6,000. Electric Boat Division at Quonset Point is now the state's single largest manufacturing employer with 5,400 workers. There are now 64 companies within the Quonset/ 

Davisville complex. Included are those 34 oil and oil service companies who pay and wait for exploration go-ahead on the Outer Continental Shelf. These and other potentials of related companies to this exploration could push job potentials into 3-6,000. 

All is dependent on a substantial oil/gas "find." 

The COST-Atlantic Project, begun in late 1975, was concluded in mid-'76. However, another "core-analysis" well was authorized in late '76 and is concluding its contract now. Previous experience in this endeavor has given these job figures: 176 jobs generated with 83 of these positions being filled by Rhode Islanders. 

Job Development and Training Division 

The division administers and operates programs under the Comprehensive Employment and Training Act of 1973 (CETA), as amended. 

Specifically, the division is responsible for developing job and training opportunities for the unemployed and economically disadvantaged. Highlights • During the federal fiscal year-1977 (Oct. 1, 1976 to Sept. 30, 1977), the division plans to serve 4,100 individuals in various programs such as classroom training, on the job training, and work experience programs. As a result of these program activities, an estimated 2,367 individuals will enter unsubsidized employment. 

The division administered the Summer Program for Economically Disadvantaged Youth (SPEDY). Through various local agencies throughout the state, young people 14 to 21 years of age earned wages while developing good work habits. It is estimated that 128

3,individuals will be served under this program. In addition to the wages these low income participants earn, they will be exposed to pre-vocational training, job interview skills, remedial training and vocational exploration in many labor market and career clusters.

The division also administered the Summer Recreation and Transportation Program, which gives economically disadvantaged young people the opportunity to enjoy local recreation and cultural atractions. It is estimated that 9,000 youngsters between the ages of 

8 and 13 years were served through this program. 

The division administered Title II and VI of CETA. An estimated 

2,949 individuals will be employed in temporary public service employment with government or not-for-profit agencies to provide needed community services and to develop new skills, career opportunities, and work experience for the participant. The estimated annual cost for this program activity is $17,484,169. There are 380 positions authorized under Title II. Of the 2,113 positions authorized under Title VI, 616 are temporary project-oriented public service jobs which will be the future emphasis for Title VI activities. 

Management and Planning Division 

The division prepares development plans for surplus federal properties in Rhode Island and provides a continuous input into preparation of an overall economic strategy for the state. The division works with other state, local and federal agencies and interested-person groups. Highlights A prime responsibility of the division continued to be the reuse and development of excess Navy property in Rhode Island. A prerequisite for fulfilling this responsibility is the purchase of the property through a negotiated sale from the General Services Administration. To this end, the division has participated in a number of meetings with GSA in an effort to agree on a satisfactory purchase price. Before transfer of the property can take place, however, GSA must complete an Environmental Impact Statement (EIS) on the property. This department, in cooperation with the 

Congressional Delegation, was responsible for getting funds released to GSA to complete the EIS. It is expected that the EIS will be completed by the end of 1978. 129

The division's efforts to formulate a reuse plan for the surplus Navy property resulted in the completion of the Quonset Point Master Development Plan. The devision supervised the efforts of three consultants and coordinated a task force of various state agencies to develop a facilities plan and accompanying environmental and socio-economic assessment. This plan presents three development scenarios which realistically could occur when the state receives title to the surplus Quonset property. The division aided in the development of a reuse proposal for Coddington Cove envisioning a major shipbuilding facility. This industry could employ several hundred people over the long term. Also, on the east side of the bay, the division assisted in developing a proposal for the Melville area to be used as a major marina and boatbuilding facility. • Other areas in which the division was involved included information and planning assistance to various local projects. Among groups which received assistance are the Downtown Providence 

Development Task Force and the Blackstone Canal River Com mittee. The division also aided local communities in pre-application and application activities for funds which were provided under the Local Public Works Act of 1976. These funds were used to alleviate severe unemployment and underemployment in Rhode 

Island. 

Research Division 

Working largely behind the scenes, the division is responsible for compiling, analyzing and disseminating to interested parties statistical information on all aspects of the Rhode Island economy. Much of the division's work is directly related to the department's industrial development program but many more requests for information are received from other state agencies, banks, business firms, individuals and schools. Highlights • The division prepared 265 special comprehensive Fact Books on

Rhode Island for use by the Business and Industry Division in supplying key information to industrial and commercial prospects -an increase of 32% over the 201 reports prepared in the previous year. Included in these reports are detailed sections dealing with availability of sites and building space, labor and wages, taxation, financing, population and housing, transportation facilities, markest and liveability. This publication is considered one of the most attractive and useful of its kinds in the country. 130

Approximately 4,200 community monographs on individual Rhode Island cities and towns were distributed describing the various economic and social aspects of a community including population characteristics economic and industrial development, municipal government, finance and services, educational facilities, local points of interest and history. During the year, 15 of the 39 community monographs were revised. 

More than 1,500 general inquiries from business, students, educational institutions and the general public were answered by the division. Most requests for economic information dealt with 

Rhode Island's market potential, trends in housing and population, retail sales, income distribution, employment, taxes and 

Rhode Island living. 

Approximately 750 copies of the 1975 edition of the Rhode Island Basic Economic Statistics Digest were distributed. The 1977-78 edition was completed and at the printers at the end of the fiscal year; it will be available for distribution in July 1977. Included in this 200-page report are facts and figures on the Rhode Island. economy, markets, population, income and wage rates, retail sales, construction, manufacturing, transportation, taxes and assessed valuations, education, banking, natural resources and utilities. • The division continued to distribute its monthly Rhode Island Economic Trends indicator to the Providence Chamber of Commerce and 180 businesses and individuals throughout the country. 

During the fiscal year, the division distributed over 1,000 copies of its publication-Starting A Business In Rhode Island-designed to assist the Rhode Island businessman.

Approximately 200 copies of the division's-Rhode Island Of fices, Divisions and Subsidiaries of the 500 Largest Corporationspublication were distributed during the year. 

The 1977-78 edition of the Rhode Island Directory of Manufacturers was completed and was at the printers at year's end. This publication contains information on over 2,500 Rhode Island firms and will be available for distribution during the summer of 1977. 

Up-to-date information on Rhode Island again was supplied for a number of key reference publications including the Providence 

Journal Almanac, Information Please Almanac, Stateman's Year book, and the World Almanac during the reporting period. Special information on state economic growth and new industrial construction was supplied to several firms and publications including 131

Conway Research, Inc., the Federal Reserve Bank of Boston, the

New Englander Magazine and the Providence Journal-Bulletin. 

Local planning and development groups such as the Burrillville Industrial Commission also were supplied information. 

Economic data on Rhode Island and its various communities was supplied to the Industrial National Bank for use in a prospectus for the sale of muicipal bonds. These facts are reported to have aided materially in the sale of bonds at favorable interest rates.

Tourist Promotion Division 

The division promotes the state's tourist and visitor business through a program of media advertising, publicity, public relations and distribution of information and materials relating to tourist and leisure-time activities.

Highlights The Bicentennial Year was the best tourist year in modern times for Rhode Island and was the third year in a row that seasons surpassed previous ones. • The 1976-77 tourist promotion campaign utilized for the first time in the state's history the medium of television. Contracts were made with two Philadelphia television stations to run 30-second spots featuring slides of Rhode Island attractions with a brief narration ending with the view/listener invited to call for addition, al information via the division's toll-free number. The response was gratifying enough to consider expanding the use of television in the future.

Again, ads were placed in several national and Canadian newspapers and magazines. • During the fiscal year, over 101,000 inquiries were received and processed. This represents a slight drop from the previous year. 

However, there is no way of measuring the "impulse" reaction to the television commercials which may have motivated people who did not call or write for information. The end result countstourism fared well in Rhode Island. 

United Campgrounds Distribution Service of Keene, N.H. distributed literature at all principal sports and camp shows in the northeast, as well as the top shopping malls where travel promo- 132

tions are now becoming popular.

The division continued to co operate with the Rhode Island Campground Owners Association by supplying literature for distribution at shows in which they participated. This was the first year in 25 that the division did not physically attend a sports, travel or camp show. However, a booth was established in the Rhode Island building at the Eastern 

States Exposition at West Springfield, Mass. 

• Through the Rhode Island Film Cooperative, prints of films produced by the department were distributed throughout the country. More than 86,000 viewed the films. The American Cable Television service reported more than one million viewers saw "Rhode 

Island State Capitol” and “Historic Houses of Rhode Island” via stations they service in 22 states. Publishers of magazines, books, encyclopedias, travel editors and travel writers, as well as tour brokers preparing new promotional tours folders, again called upon this office for black and white photos and color transparencies. Over 600 black and white pictures were distributed in this fashion as well as 166 color transparencies.

The division sent out 57 press releases to the media across the country and eastern Canada. The subjects ranged from May Day breakfasts to Winter Surfing championships. During the same period, 31 weekly fishing reports were sent to fishing news editors and columnists. The division also supplied information and art work for several feature articles in national publications. • Division personnel appeared on television programs in Providence and Boston, and on radio stations in Austin, Texas, Providence. and Warwick. Assistance for personalities for various interviewtype radio shows was given to stations in Providence and Woonsocket. Holiday World, a sydicated travel promotion radio feature, beamed five different Rhode Island vignettes to over 400 stations across the nation.

The division published nearly one and a half million pieces of literature, including the official state highway map and the annual tourist guide. Two new pieces were added to promote visitor business: "Golf in Rhode Island" and "Change Your Mind About

Winter" kits. Both editions were exhausted in record time. "A Black Heritage Tour," originally published in the East Side News of Providence, was reprinted by the division in cooperation with the Urban League of Rhode Islad. 

The state information centers at Hopkinton and the Theodore Francis Green State Airport in Warwick, assisted over 100,000 133

visitors, tourists, and business travelers. Out of state tourist information centers were supplied with Rhode Island literature as were many distribution points in Canada.

Convention groups continued to receive support from the division. 

Committees promoting conventions and conferences were supplied with literature, slides and posters. The division worked with the new Greater Providence Convention and Visitor Bureau and referred many contacts to that office for action. Hotels were also notified of prospective convention business. Hospital Association, American Association of Motor Vehicle Ad ministrators, Optical Manufacturers Association and the National 

Figure Skating Club. 

The Block Island Race Week, a biennial event, again received department and division support. The event attracted 260 yachts and about 25 to 30 auxiliary craft which made up the largest yachting event held on the east coast. 

Tauck Tours received cooperation when their spring tour of travel agents from the midwest arrived in Rhode Island for a familiarization tour. Assistance and cooperation was also given to several organizations. In addition, numerous speaking engagements were fulfilled. • The division nominated the American Sail Training Association for a Bicentennial Award and was successful in its presentation. The award was made by the U.S. Travel Service.

The last month of the fiscal year saw success in the division's quest for more on-location TV and motion picture productions. 

Numerous producers contacted the division for possible locations including a film scheduled by Stanley Kubrick and ABC-TV's 

Eleanor and Franklin series. In May and June, contact was made with "The Betsy", a multi-million dollar production and locations in Newport were found to be exactly what was required. 

The division also received opportunities to assist Canadian producers.

Rhode Island has become aware of the value of yachting and much has been done to promote these salt water events through the years. The area as a yachting capital was recognized when 134

the U. S. Yacht Racing Union moved its headquarters from New
