DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION DR. THOMAS C. SCHMIDT, Commissioner of Education Established 1961-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-15 

Central Office-199 Promenade Street, Providence 02908 Full Time Employees Total Available for Expenditures (State Funds) 

​t 4.658 $178,373,841 The fifteen member Board of Regents has the responsibility of establishing policy for the governance of all levels of education in 

Rhode Island. 

The 1973 Education Act established three subcommittees of the 

Board of Regents: Subcommittee for Special Populations, Subcommit tee for Postsecondary Education, and the Subcommittee for Elementary/Secondary Education. They have specific responsibilities for the advocacy and budget preparations of their areas. The Department of Education established by statute is maintained by the Board and is assisted by its chief executive, the Commissioner of Education. 

Office of Commissioner 

The Commissioner serves as the chief executive officer of the Board of Regents and is selected by a decision of the Board and the Governor in a joint meeting. He assists in formulating policies and in promulgating regulations established by the Board. The Commissioner is responsible for the maintenance of the Department of Education, for assisting in budget preparation, in establishing a systematic program gathering, processing, and analysis, for the coordination of the various educational functions among all agencies in the state, and for exercising general supervision over all public education. 

OFFICE OF EXECUTIVE ASSOCIATE COMMISSIONER 

This office is responsible for the supervision and coordination of the operations of the Department of Education as it relates to the Board of Regents, local school districts, and the various publics. The executive associate commissioner is also responsible for the preparation and management of all meetings of the Board of Regents. 139

Division of Development and Operations 

The division provides technical assistance to local school systems, administers federal grant programs, has responsibility for all regulatory functions, and operates certain state educational programs, and is divided into four bureaus.

BUREAU OF TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE 

The bureau provides help to local school districts in improving the quality of their educational programs. The bureau is comprised of four units 1) program development, 2) teacher center, 3) support services, and 4) dissemination services. The help provided to local school districts takes the form of planning and designing needs assessments, demonstration of innovative and validated educational programs, retrieval and interpretation of educational information, testing services, in-service training workshops, program planning, project writing, and linkage to specialists in various curriculum areas. In addition to the services provided to local school districts, the bureau provides operational planning support for the department's Bureau of Planning. Highlights 100 Program development consultants conducted more than 1900 visits to the state's forty school districts and a number of other agencies with educational programs. The visits resulted in the provision of approximately 2500 separate activities to some 7500 of their clients. 

The consultants also assisted half of the local school districts to explore, plan or implement inter-district cooperation in the delivery of educational programs. 

The teacher center planned, organized and implemented in-service : training for over 4000 teachers, administrators, parents and school committee members. Training focused primarily on reading, mathematics and special education. Three statewide conferences aoin were held to introduce the state's forty school districts to validated aim programs. 16 The support services unit tested approximately 11,000 fourth and eighth graders and seventeen year olds. The fourth and eighth graders were tested in the traditioal basic skills of reading, mathematics and language while the seventeen year old students' tests included the additional areas of science, health, career and occupational development, citizenship and consumer economics. In addition, an optional testing program was provided for 16,000 students. at the kindergarten, eighth and ninth grade levels. 140

Field testing was conducted of the local planning and assessment program, adding five districts to bring the number of districts. involved to eight. Provisional regulations for the program were approved, and a specific needs assessment system was designed and implemented. • Technical assistance was provided to 18 local school districts in the implementation of the Regents' uniform accounting standards.

The dissemination services unit responded to 600 information requests, developed two resource guides, expanded research, programs, and legal information files, and assisted 15 units within the department in developing dissemination strategies and materials.

BUREAU OF VOCATIONAL-TECHNICAL EDUCATION 

The bureau is structured into three units: operational planning, programs and services.

Highlights • Staff assisted school districts in the state in developing long range plans for vocational education. • The bureau developed state plans for vocational-technical adult, and career education and set priorities for each program. • An operational research effort was initiated which is supportive of activities that take place throughout the bureau. ● The bureau developed and implemented a plan for establishment of five vocational-education statewide youth organizations. Also, it assisted in the planning, funding and creation of the first statewide industry education labor council in Rhode Island. • The bureau provided leadership in planning, funding and monitoring expanded postsecondary adult and apprenticeship programs. • A comprehensive monitoring system which was used in several vocational and adult basic education programs in the state was initiated. • In-service training for adult basic education teachers, counselors and administrators through Rhode Island College and the University of Rhode Island was provided.

Staff managed a special U.S. Office of Education program grant in experienced-based career education in cooperation with seven school districts in the state. 141

• The bureau administered comprehensive employment training act special grant funds for vocational education in cooperation with the sponsors and the Governor's special grant office. • The bureau planned and monitored education professions development act funds used for in-service training of vocational education teachers, counselors and administrators.

BUREAU OF GRANTS AND REGULATIONS 

The bureau functions as a consolidated operation for all grant programs for elementary and secondary schools, includes the state regulatory functions unit, and administers federal and state funded educational programs through four units: compensatory education; innovative and support programs; programs for the handicapped; and the unit for bilingual-bicultural education. The bureau also administered the grant of $6,324,000 from the Economic Development Administration of the 

U.S. Department of Commerce (Title X) to hire unemployed persons in educational programs. 

Two highlights of the year for the bureau have been the organization of a unit to administer the bilingual-bicultural grants in education and the mandating of new regulations for the special education of the handicapped children.

The major function of the bureau is to assist local school districts provide quality compensatory education programs which meet the most critical needs of educationally disadvantaged students. Programs were funded through Title I of the Elementary and Secondary Education Act; 

State Section IV Compensatory Act; Title X of the Economic Develop ment Act; and School Assistance to Federally Affected Areas Act. 

The major compensatory education instructional activities emphasized reading, English-as-a-second-language, mathematics, and learning dis abilities. The major emphasis of supportive activities was in the area of counseling and social work. Also, federal funds were used to support programs for neglected and delinquent children in state institutions. Highlights • Efforts were initiated to improve the quality of reading programs in local communities through the development of "reading standards" as criteria for successful reading programs for disadvantaged children. These criteria are being developed with local input and will be utilized statewide to improve the quality of reading programs. 142

● A monitoring team was formed which conducted comprehensive multi-day reviews of compensatory education operations in four communities. • Newport's Title I program, designated as a model program for national dissemination, was developed into a "Program Information Package", and will be studied by several school systems across the nation for possible adoption. • The beneficiary program for the blind and deaf blind was conducted resulting in wider improvement; improved record keeping; reviews of pupil progress; and more frequent on-site observation of the child's program. • Six communities were awarded program expansion grants to expand their existing bilingual programs and serve an additional 460 students. • A project was also undertaken to identify students in grades K-12 who spoke a language other than English. • The office of teacher certification issues teaching and administrative certificates to all qualified public school personnel employed in this state, and carries on a continuing verification of teachers' certificates. The office also serves in an advisory capacity with officials of elementary and secondary schools and colleges on matters relating to the development and revision of teacher education and training programs. Approximately 6200 certificates were issued, of which 27 were emergecy certificates.

Some 111 private nursery schools and kindergartens were evaluated and approved to operate for the 1976-77 academic year. Some 73 on-site visits were conducted as a means of assisting pre-schools in maintaining existing standards or in strengthening their early childhood education programs. A total of 371 elementary schools, public and non-public, were approved for operation in the state and 100 secondary schools were evaluated for the purpose of approval. Twenty-one program visits were made to private special education programs for the purpose of evaluation for approval. 

Three new non-public school established to conduct special education programs were visited and evaluated. 

The office completed approving the standards of progress and conduct for all institutions of higher learning enrolling veterans or eligible persons.

New regulations for the education of handicapped children were approved by the Board of Regents. 143

BUREAU OF OPERATIONS 

The bureau consists of seven operating functions by which the state agency delivers a direct service to pupils and learners throughout the state.

School Food Services 

The Office of School Food Service administers state and federal funds to schools, child-care centers and summer camps under contract with the National School Lunch Program, and other feeding programs. The basic purposes of the School Food Service Program in Rhode Island are to teach children to eat properly balanced meals to meet their energy, growth, and health requirements, and to make nutritionally balanced meals available to children, particularly “needy" children. Both the federal government and the state appropriate substantial funds to achieve this objective.

Highlights • Consolidation of food services in state sponsored programs continues to achieve results in cost effectiveness. Reducing the number of preparation centers and converting small on-site operations to satellite programs has increased productivity in five communities this year. Breakfast programs have been extended to 17 additional schools this year. • A pilot program in cost-based accounting was effected in 15 selected schools. Next year, this program will be extended to all schools with on-site food service or preparation centers. 

The child care food program has been extended to 371 family day care sites under the sponsorship of the Department of Social and Rehabilitative services. An additional eight family day care sites were added under the sponsorship of Child and Family Services of Newport County. New legislation extends the program to include the supper meal. 

In the past year, 7,358,035 free or reduced price lunches have been served to needy children.

RHODE ISLAND SCHOOL FOR THE DEAF 

The Rhode Island School for the Deaf which includes the Hearing Center offers diagnostic and educational services to hearing impaired children and adults. In addition to administrative and operational staff, personnel includes teachers of the deaf, support professionals and diagnostic staff. The Hearing Center includes an audiologist, audiometrists and clerical staff. 144

Enrollment at the school for the deaf in 1976-77 stayed consistent with an average of 160 students, and applications indicate that enrollment will remain constant in 1977-78. Educational service was provided from early education through high school, including vocational training in business, jewelry, machine shop, woodworking, graphics and photography. Evening classes were provided for adults in sign language, and adult basic education. Highlights This year marked the centennial of the Rhode Island School for the Deaf with festivities involving alumni, including the oldest living alumnus.

A bi-lingual program for Portuguese and Spanish families was conducted with several very successful social/cultural programs to establish a relationship with parents. 

A career-orientation program was initiated with a preparation component and career-orientation placement for 10 students. 

A wide variety of adult services was provided to adult deaf people. 

Five students gradudated with high school diplomas and will go on to postsecondary programs.

A student teacher practicum relationship was maintained with 

Gallaudet College, Washington, D.C. 

WILLIAM M. DAVIES, JR. 

VOCATIONAL TECHNICAL SCHOOL 

Davies School trains high school youths so that on graduation they will meet the entry level requirements for jobs in one of 16 occupational areas. The school also operates an adult program to teach skills for entry level positions or to up-grade skills for underemployed adults. Highlights School staff developed a curriculum which establishes minimum competency criteria in several areas. 

The occupational program for disadvantaged youth had a total enrollment of 84 students for the academic year. 

A new federally-funded program for "mainstreaming" handicapped students in a vocational setting was implemented in December, 1976.

The cooperative work program had a total of 119 involved, 33 of whom were placed in an early work-release program for seniors. 

Placement data for graduating co-op seniors was 100%. 145

Seven dollar scholarships were awarded to graduating seniors by the parent-faculty organization. 

The adult education evening program served approximately 1,800 individuals during 1976-77.

EDUCATIONAL TELEVISION SERVICES 

Educational television services provides instructional television programs to the schools of Rhode Island and public television programs to the general public through WSBE-TV (Channel 36). Educational television services also provides advice to schools and state agencies concerning the programming which the station telecasts, and it provides assistance in the selection and use of television facilities; e.g., closed circuit television facilities within a given school building. Highlights ⚫ The staton aired 55 hours of instructional programming per week to the schools and the general public in the area of continuing education.

On April 15 at 7 p.m. WSBE-TV instituted its first news program "Newswrap 36".

CAREER COUNSELING SERVICE 

The Career Counseling Service is an information, guidance and referral service which provides career counseling by telephone and through the mail to individuals who are planning to enter or reenter the job market, enroll in school, or are seeking help with career decision making. It disseminates career, educational and occupational information; designs and compiles educational and supportive service directories; and maintains a resource center of career-related materials. Highlights • A total of 1300 clients and information callers were served. 

Presentations were given at 14 national, state and regional conferences and workshops.

Directories of educational and training resources and supportive services were compiled. 

OFFICE OF STUDENT ASSISTANCE 

The office is responsible for the administration of the R.I. state scholarship program; R.I. nursing education scholarship program; R.I. war orphans' scholarship program; and the R.I. business education scholarship program. 146

Highlights Staff developed and implemented a system for estimating the financial profile of Rhode Island high school seniors. 

The unit analyzed and reported the basic grant application behavior of 1977 Rhode Island high school seniors. It also developed and implemented a system and materials for the training of counselors in student financial assistance matters. 

A comic book for students whịch demonstrates the availability of student financial assistance and how to apply was developed. 

The office provided a comprehensive financial assistance referral and counseling service.

EDUCATIONAL PROGRAMS UNIT 

The unit is responsible for the following functions: operation of the high school equivalency diploma program to provide adults the opportunity to qualify for the state high school equivalency diploma; classroom instruction in driver education and a basic knowledge of motor vehicle operation; operation of the itinerant teaching services for blind and visually handicapped children to provide instructional, consultative, referral and technical assistance, and the necessary special equipment and material support needed by those children in their educational programs; assistance to local school districts in providing citizenship instruction for adults in the English language and in the history and government of the United States to enable them to obtain the assistance to local school districts and other public and private agencies in their adult education programs. Highlights • High school equivalency diplomas were issued to 2,379 successful applicants, 796 transcripts were furnished to institutions of higher education and other agencies, and 4,186 individuals were tested or retested. 

Four additional general education degree test centers were established in Warren, Cumberland, East Providence, and Warwick, bringing the total number of test centers to 11. 

A total of 505 courses with a total enrollment of 16,665 students were conducted by 73 part-time driver education teachers. • A driver education advisory group was organized to look into means of improving and expanding the driver education program. 147

A parent participation guide for the behind-the-wheel practice sessions by teenagers was developed to be pilot tested during the next year. • Itinerant teaching services were provided to 52 legally blind students in 9 communities by two full time itinerant teachers. • Technical assistance was provided to local education agencies in general adult education.

Citizenship textbooks (354) were issued to local school districts for use in English-as-a-second-language classes. A limited correspondence/home study course in citizenship was conducted for individuals who could not attend classes. 

Division of Planning and Management OFFICE OF COMMUNITY RELATIONS 

The office works closely with department staff in disseminating information relative to all levels of education in the state. A monthly publication, Interrobang, provides information to the educational community and selected publics on activities and programs taking place in education and keeps department staff and the Board of Regents informed through a periodic report on the status of legislation. The office also meets regularly with a reaction committee in the department which reviews education legislation and reports its reactions to the Commissioner and the Board of Regents, and General Assembly. The community relations officer also testifies before legislative committees and provides information to the various committees on legislation. 

OFFICE OF APPEALS AND GRIEVANCES 

The responsibilities of this office include: holding hearings on matters appealed to the Commissioner from decisions of school committees; holding hearings on grievances submitted to the Commissioner from the bargaining units under the jurisdiction of the Board of Regents and writing decisions thereon; interpreting school law and the rules and reglations of the Board of Regents and of the Commissioner; and representing the Commissioner on the State Retirement Board. 

The number of appeals from decisions of school committees was substantially greater during the past fiscal year than in the preceding one. 

Forty such appeals were heard during the 1975-76 fiscal year, whereas 

70 were heard during the 1976-77 fiscal year. 148

OFFICE OF FEDERAL/STATE RELATIONS 

This office is responsible for informing the Executive Office, the Board of Regents, the Commissioner of Education and department staff, school superintendents, and the education community on all aspects of federal legislation which will effect education in Rhode Island. In collecting, analyzing, interpreting and disseminating information on federal educational legislation, all current and proposed legislation, rules, regulations and other federal agencies are considered. Liaison is maintained with national, state and local educational groups, congressional representatives, other state educational agencies, the U.S. Office of Education, and other educational organizations. Highlights The office disseminated federal program information through its semi-monthly paper, the Federal Newsbrief and the Federal Register Review and the department's monthly newspaper. • Frequent in-service sessions were held for department staff, school superintendents, postsecondary institutions, and allied agencies and organizations. Technical assistance was provided to all levels of education to assure the orderly transition from old legislation to new.

A process was developed for reviewing and approving all state plans and federal applications in order to assist in the grants process for federal funding. 

BUREAU OF POSTSECONDARY EDUCATIONA 

The bureau is responsible for: 1) the coordination of department activities relating to postsecondary education; 2) staff support for the 

Commissioner and the Board of Regents; and 3) the administration of programs as assigned, including Titles I, VI and VII of the Higher Edudcation Act of 1965. The bureau also participates in planning projects for postsecondary education; develops alternative policies and practices for the achievement of postsecondary educational goals; and provides analysis and evaluation on issues related to postsecondary education. Highlights 

The Board of Regents approved two new program proposals:

a 

Master of Social Work program at Rhode Island College, and a Master of Arts in Marine Affairs at the University of Rhode 

Island. 

The bureau also participated in the selection process for a new president for Rhode Island College. 

149

Seven programs were approved and monitored under Title I of the 

Higher Education Act of 1965 (Community Service and Continu ing Education); eight projects have been approved for the coming year.

Eight programs were approved and monitored under Title VI of the Higher Education Act of 1965 (Instructional Equipment); 

seven have been approved for the coming year.

Title X funds from the U.S. Department of Commerce in the amount of $950,000 were awarded to six institutions of higher education for the renovation and modernization of undergraduate facilities and hiring the unemployed. 

The bureau also negotiated and administered student contract programs with the University of Vermont for five medical students, and the University of Pennsylvania and Cornell University for three veterinary medical students. • A contract with the New England College of Optometry provides for spaces for four optometry students from Rhode Island beginning in the fall of 1977. • Several study documents were developed including those for monitoring student financial assistance and for increasing federal funding for financial assistance to students in Rhode Island. • Federal higher education general information surveys were collected and aided in the compilation of additional information: a degree inventory including all public, independent and proprietary institutions in the state; a booklet listing all available programs at those institutions; programs available to Rhode Island residents as part of the regional student program; student financial assistance information; a guide to sources of further information; and a set of enrollment projections for higher education to 1983. • Through the bureau, Rhode Island served as a participant in the state level information base project and as a pilot test state in the outcome measures project, both sponsored by the National Center for Higher Education Management Systems. 

The bureau developed a prototype annual report which will organize data related to postsecondary education in such a way that the performance and outcomes of institutions may be assessed. 

Statements of institutional role assessment were initiated and will be revised on an annual basis. 150

C 

BUREAU OF RESEARCH, PLANNING AND EVALUATION 

The primary mission of the bureau is to promote sound educational decision-making and policy development through the activities of research, planning and evaluation.

Highlights • A criterion-referenced testing program has been developed and implemented and is related to the performance indicators developed from Rhode Island's educational goals. 

Grants were made to eight teachers who applied for funds to conduct research projects in the classroom. These projects were monitored, the results reviewed, and plans are made to disseminate this information to other interested individuals. 

An investigation was made into the questions of why fourth graders outperform eighth graders, and why non-public school students outperform public school students in the Statewide Assessment Program. Some student characteristics and instructional differences were identified which might account for these differences. • A system has been designed and implemented which collects information periodically on the number and characteristics of secondary school students who dropout, are suspended, are habitual truants, etc. This system provides an analysis of the types and frequencies of the transactions which students undergo. • Enrollment projects for elementary and secondary schools and for higher education for the next ten years were developed. The projection information is particularly useful as a planning tool. • Technical assistance on information collection, analysis and the development of long-range plans and budgets for the management information system were provided.

An analysis of Rhode Island educational priorities was conducted resulting in an organized review of the major activities of the Department of Education in order to assist in the determination of educational priorities over the next few years. • A task force of staff and guidance counselors was formed to study the functions of guidance and to recommend educational policy in this area.

A position paper recommending policy changes for disadvantaged students in grades 4-12 was developed and a public review process was begun. 151

• A public committee on fundamental education for disadvantaged adults was formed to help improve adult education. It has planned for securing an increased financial commitment to adult education; organized a promotional campaign aimed at increasing adult education enrollments; and developed alternative programs such as an external high school diploma program for adults. 

Demographic information on the labor market, vocational education and manpower needs was developed and disseminated. 

A statement of purposes of postsecondary education was adopted by the Rhode Island postsecondary education commission as the framework for development of postsecondary education in the years ahead. • Recommendations on transitional educational programs were adopted by the Board of Regents. A conference was held for secondary and postsecondary educators to discuss ways to implement the new policy. ● Four state agencies were brought together to manage the delivery of career counseling and information services through the Career 

Counseling Service, Building 880, Quonset Point, Rhode Island. 

• An operating plan for a clearinghouse for information and referral in education was developed with emphasis on involving all postsecondary education institutions in an effort to make programs in continuing education more accessible to disadvantaged adults. 

BUREAU OF PERSONNEL 

The bureau proposes plans, implements, and improves the effective use of human resources and equal opportunity within Rhode Island's educational community. It is also responsible for developing, implementing, and administering contracts and Regents' policies relative to all 

Regents' employees.

Highlights • The bureau negotiated and implemented all Regents' collective bargaining agreements without substantial labor unrest. 

The unit provided local education agencies with technical tools to affect stronger affirmative action policies by improving existing school desegregation. • The bureau expanded its role of legal representation in judicial and quasi judicial forums as representative of the Regents. 152

• The bureau coordinated with the Departments of Commerce and Labor, Title X grant which served to put many unemployed back to work and it provided technical assistance to many communities regarding bilingual activties. 

BUREAU OF BUDGET 

The bureau is concerned with the management of the entire budget development and execution process for all accounts and activities under the supervision of the Commissioner and the Board of Regents. The bureau is responsible for state aid computation; capital budgeting and development; school construction necessity review; standards for design and construction of school buildings; energy management and control; planning, programming, budgeting system development; zero-based budgeting; management information systems; development of standard accounting systems and standards for auditing; financial planning, analysis and control; and management of Board of Regents' insurance program. Highlights • This bureau assisted local school districts in developing initial fiveyear school construction, maintenance and rehabilitation plans. 

The staff provided fiscal analyses of Coventry, Warren, and Exeter- West Greenwich school district financial crises. • The unit refined standard accounting system guidelines for local school districts.

The state was selected by the National Center for Higher Education Management System as a pilot state for the implementation of higher education financial manual. 

The bureau developed four planning, programming, budgeting systems models for local school districts. 

Additionally, the bureau reviewed and updated existing capital development regulations and standards; revised and published a school plant planning handbook; developed a Board of Regents' insurance policy; and established a management information system as part of department organization. 153

University of Rhode Island 

The University has 12 academic divisions-College of arts and sciences, business administration, engineering, home economics, nursing, pharmacy, resource development; the graduate school; the graduate library school; the graduate school of oceanography; the division of university extension and university college. 

The University enrolled 9,154 full time students last year, and an additional 1,721 taking courses part time at the main campus in Kingston. Almost 3,400 attended credit courses and another 987 attended noncredit workshops through university extension either in Providence or community centers in Westerly, Davisville, Kingston and Middletown. 

Another 4,770 students attended the summer session. 

The University also operates extensive public services and research, including a science education center and a conference center at the W. Alton Jones campus in West Greenwich and a major marine and energy research facility located at the Narragansett Bay Campus. Cooperative extension offices are located in Newport, Greenville, East 

Greenwich and Providence.

Highlights • The college of nursing moved into its new building, the first constructed at URI with strict energy conservation in mind. • A three-year budget task force completed its review of all uni versity departments and divisions, making more than 600 recommendations to improve both the quality and efficiency of the university. • The university acquired the research vessel Endeavor, funded by the National Science Foundation. • An interdisciplinary center for energy research has been organized to study various means of energy conservation and to advise other state agencies in conservation techniques. • A Phi Beta Kappa chapter was authorized at the university and the first student members were inducted in May. • A center for ocean management studies has been organized to research the body of water in the 200-mile zone. 154

E E 

Rhode Island College 

Rhode Island College is an urban state-supported general purpose college which provides regionally and nationally accredited undergraduate and graduate programs in the arts and sciences and in education. The main campus of 125 acres is located three miles from the center of Providence and includes twenty-three modern buildings, all constructed since 1958. The college also operates an urban educational center located in the Providence inner city. 

Degrees offered include the Bachelor of Arts, Bachelor of Science,

Bachelor of General Studies, Master of Arts, Master of Arts in Teaching, Master of Education and Certification of Advanced Graduate Studies. 

There is a comprehensive range of udergraduate offerings including nursing, industrial technology, medical technology, economics, management, social welfare, and pre-professional programs. There are also over fifty graduate programs in education and in the arts and sciences. Highlights • In June 1977 David E.

Sweet became the sixth president of Rhode 

Island College. 

At the undergraduate level, full-time enrollment was 3,444 and part-time enrollment was 2,444. Graduate enrollment was 3,104. ● In 1976-77, 1,033 students enrolled in newspaper and television courses; 2,739 teachers and other school personnel enrolled in on-site in-service courses; approximately 1,000 adults enrolled in a wide variety of continuing education courses, and over 50 students studied abroad. 

Construction was begun on a $2.6 million addition to Adams library.

A year round program in the arts included productions in theater and dance, concerts by the Rhode Island College orchestra, a summer chamber music festival, the popular cabaret summer theater, and a fine arts series. 155

Rhode Island Junior College 

The college offers degrees in arts, science, business, engineering, nursing and applied science upon satisfactory completion of two years of course work. A wide range of career-oriented programs are offered in vocational-technical education to prepare students for employment as industrial technicians, mid-management personnel, para-medical technicians and secretarial personnel. The career-oriented programs are designed to train personnel to meet the needs of Rhode Island businesses, industries and professions.

Over two-thirds of liberal arts program graduates continue their education at four-year colleges and universities in all 50 states, England and 

Australia. 

Highlights Construction of the Blackstone Valley Campus, the second permanent campus, located on a 300 acre site off Louisquisset Pike and Route 146 in Lincoln, was completed in 1976 and the college opened in September 1976 with an enrollment of about 1,700.

The projected enrollment for the fall semester of 1977 is about 3,900 full-time students at the Knights Campus in Warwick and about 2,000 at the Blackstone Valley Campus in Lincoln. • The continuing education program continued to expand with the addition of new courses and programs. An enrollment of close to 

4,600 students is expected in the day and evening courses at both campuses in the fall of 1977. • The college has the highest enrollment in the nursing program in the New England region. Three hundred students were admitted to the nursing program for the fall of 1977. ● A total of 535 courses were offered in the day and evening sessions at both campuses. 156

MIDDLETOWN, R. I.-Purgatory Chasm, Purgatory Road. A narrow cleft in the rock ledges on the east coast of Easton Point formed by the eroding action of sea water over thousands of years. Scenic overlook.

DEPARTMENT OF EMPLOYMENT SECURITY MARY C. HACKETT, Director Established 1949-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-19 

Administrative Office-24 Mason Street, Providence 02903  

706 $11,180,763.05 The department is composed of seven divisions: the Benefits Division which processes claims, determines claimant eligibility and pays unemployment insurance benefits to unemployed workers; the Temporary 

Disability Insurance Division which administers the payment of benefits to workers who are certified by medical authority as being unable to perform their job duties as a result of non-work connected illness or injury; the Job Service which administers the Employment Service program, placement, worker recruitment, employment counseling, aptitude testing and special assistance to veterans, youth, older workers and the handicapped; the Division of Management Services and Data Processing which has responsibility for program planning, procedures and evaluation of management problems and insuring department compliance with state and federal standards; the Fiscal Division which is responsible for the registration, status and experience rating of all employers and collection and accounting for all taxes paid by subject employers; it is also responsible for the department budget; the Labor Relations, Research and Information Division which administers personnel, training, public relations and informational programs of the department and has supervision of the Research and Statistics Unit which develops and maintains management information systems, conducts studies and reports on unemployment benefit insurance financing, industry and occupational projections and labor market information; the Legal Division which prepares legislative proposals and changes in regulations, renders interpre tations of the laws pertaining to Employment Security and Temporary 

Disability Insurance, and represents the director and the department in any legal action.

The department provides technical and research assistance to all levels. of management in Rhode Island business and industry. It compiles and publishes, through its own printing facilities, various kinds of labor market information for the state and the Providence, Warwick, Pawtucket standard metropolitan statistical area. Through formal and in- 159

formal pacts with other local and state agencies as well as those in the private sector, the department cooperates in matters affecting manpower and economic development and planning. Each month statistics are compiled and widely published through the media which summarize departmental activities as well as unemployment estimates for the state. Highlights • Substantial improvement in the Rhode Island economy caused the state's rate of unemployment to drop from 9.4 percent in

FY 1976 to 7.4 percent in FY 1977. The average number of unemployed fell from 40,900 in FY 1976 to 32,100 in FY 1977. • Economic improvement in the state was also reflected in total non-agricultural wage and salary establishment employment, which rose from 357,000 in FY 1976 to 371,600 in FY 1977, a gain of 3.9 percent. Nonmanufacturing payrolls averaged 246,900, a gain of 

7,500 over the FY 1976 average of 239,400. Manufacturing industries reported an average of 124,700 jobs, an increase of 6,600 over the FY 1976 total of 118,100. • In fiscal 1977, a total of 901,411 weekly Employment Security benefit payments were made for an expenditure of $64,493,548. 

Comparable data for FY 1976 showed 1,055,346 benefit payments with a dollar value of $72,639,229. The drop in the number of benefit payments was large enough to offset increases in dollar disbursements resulting from a rise in the weekly benefit rate. The maximum weekly benefit allowed for new claims filed during the year was $100, exclusive of dependency allowances. During FY 

1976 the maximum was $94 per week. • Employment Security contributions during the fiscal year amounted to approximately $55.3 million, significantly less than was needed to finance the payment of over $63.8 million in regular benefits and almost $8.6 million as the state share of extended benefits. Since there were no available reserves to counter this imbalance the Employment Security fund began FY 1977 with a deficit of $49,816,209-it was necessary to request further advances of $9 million from the Federal Unemployment Account in the Federal Unemployment Trust Fund. Including advances received during the preceding fiscal years, the state E. S. fund liability to the federal government amounted to $74.8 million at the end of FY 1977. • The Extended Benefits Program provided 231,198 weekly payments to eligible claimants who had exhausted their regular employment security credits, representing a 34.5 percent decrease 160

from the 352,722 benefit payments made during the previous fiscal year. The payments amounted to $17,163,411, a 28.8 percent decrease from the $24,104,312 paid in FY 1976. Half the cost of this program, $8,581,706, was debited to the state E. S. fund and the balance to the federal government. 

• The Federal Emergency Benefits Program and the Special Unem ployment Assistance Program, both operative since January, 1975, are entirely financed by the federal government. During FY 1977, a total of 305,393 weekly FEB payments were made for an expenditure of $22,887,723. These figures were substantially below the FY 1976 level of 589,541 weekly payments for a total amount of $38,590,807. While most of the decline may be attributed to an overall improvement in economic conditions, a federal reduction in the maximum number of weeks of individual eligibility for these benefits was also a factor. Beginning in May, 1977, FEB claimants were limited to a maximum of 13 weeks of benefits in stead of the previous 26. Weekly SUAP payments during FY 1977 

numbered 74,370, representing a dollar amount of $5,205,978. In the preceding twelve months 86,415 weekly SUAP payments were made for a total of $5,171,472.

The Temporary Disability Insurance Division authorized $18,- 

277,748 in 271,499 weekly benefit payments to workers unable to perform their jobs due to illness or non-work connected injury. 

With a reserve of $16,324,638 at the beginning of the fiscal year, the Temporary Disability Insurance Funds had a total income of $23,098,638 from interest on the invested reserve and from the contributions withheld from wages of workers protected by the program. Expenditures, including administrative costs, amounted to $19,375,057. The fiscal year ended with an increase of $3,723,581 in the fund reserve, thus making a total of $20,048,219 available for payment of future claims.

The Temporary Disability Insurance Division changed to a sys tem of automated check payment processing in June, 1977, resulting in much prompter delivery of TDI checks to claimants. The computer prints 4,000 TDI checks in less than one hour, a significant improvement over the previous manual system which required 3 to 41/2 workdays to process and mail a check. 

The Job Service offices served 106,354 individuals seeking employment related services during FY 1977. These services included. 

7,685 counseling interviews, 33,571 job referrals and 16,319 job placements. Included in these totals were priority services performed for nearly 21,000 veterans. Overall, 1,889 veterans were 161

counseled, 6,690 referred to jobs and 3,168 placed in employment.

In addition, 596 veterans were enrolled in various job training programs. • In FY 1977 the Benefits Division inaugurated a photo identification system, issuing a plastic identification card to all claimants of unemployment insurance benefits which contains the claimant's photograph, signature and benefit year ending date. This procedure was adopted as a positive means of claimant identification and prevention of fraudulent collection of benefits by anyone attempting to impersonate a claimant. As the fiscal year ended, the department was preparing, in a cooperative effort with the office of the General Treasurer, to institute a check payment system to all eligible claimants to replace the cash payment system. 

Public relations and public information activities were maintained during FY 1977. Efforts were underway to create an increase in public awareness of the facilities of the Job Service and plans for an extensive promotional campaign in this area are being formed. 162

WOONSOCKET, R. I.-Church Street in the Cato Hill Historic Dis trict is located close to the city's busy commercial area. Buildings here were built and occupied by early Irish immigrants who worked in nearby mills and factories. These buildings date from 1840 to 1880 and are within Woonsocket's first historic district. 

Photo courtesy of Rhode Island Historic Preservation Commission

DEPARTMENT OF HEALTH JOSEPH E. CANNON, M.D., M.P.H., Director Established 1939-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-18 

Office-Davis Street, Providence 02908  

612 $17,020,531.50 

Division of Adult Health 

The goal of the division is to eliminate or minimize mortality and morbidity from diseases which affect adults in the community. To this end, programs are initiated, supported and encouraged for the prevention, early detection and control of the common chronic diseases. 

In the past year, the division was concerned with cancer and which hypertension, but was fully cognizant of notable activities of other agencies in the state concerned with matters of health. Highlights 

Cancer Control: Cancer is responsible for approximately one fifth of the deaths in Rhode Island. A strong community-based program of Cancer Control is in the early stages of implementation with expectation that it will have a major impact on the prevalence, mortality and morbidity from cancer in this state. The program was planned under contract with the National Cancer Institute and implementation was started on 30 June 1977 as a demonstration project. The project is expected to operate over a period of five years and to demonstrate whether or not a coordinated approach to interventions in cancer control is more effective than uncoordinated interventions acting in isolation. A secondary objective is to demonstrate whether or not this kind of program can be conducted within the administrative structure of a Department of Health. The program is funded by the National 

Cancer Institute at a level of approximately 6.5 million dollars for the five years.

The division, with the American Cancer Society and the National Cancer Institute, supports the Breast Cancer Detection 181

Project based at Rhode Island Hospital. This project is now in its third year. Through July 1977, it screened 9,691 women over the age of 35. Among these, 68 were found to have cancer of the breast, the majority between the ages of 50 and 69. 

Efforts toward early detection of cancer of the cervix have continued. The division paid for 4,793 Papanicolaou cytologic smears for women who otherwise could not have had this test done. Most of these smears were prepared in the Health Centers and in hospital clinics with special screening programs. Women registered in the Breast Cancer Detection Program were offered the opportunity to have a Papanicolaou test done. • Hypertension: Cardiovascular disease remains the principal cause of death in Rhode Island, and in the nation as well. As in the case of cancer, early detection of hypertension is an item of very high priority. For several years, through its Committee on Hypertension, and more recently with the assistance of its Alliance for the Control of Hypertension, the Rhode Island Affiliate of the American Heart Association has conducted screening clinics throughout the state. In the year 1976-1977, 200 screenings were held. Approximately 31,000 individuals were screened of whom three thousand were found to have elevated blood pressure, of which they had not been aware. Forty thousand educational pamphlets were distributed at the clinics. 

Starting in November of 1976, this activity was strengthened by the financial support received from the division ($24,029) to assist with control of hypertension. In this intensified program, in the first eight months, 22,672 individuals were screened. Blood pressures were above 140/90 in 3,236 of these (14%). The total number included 343 Blacks, among whom 43 were found to have blood pressures above 140/90 (13%). Among 89 Blacks over the age of 40, 19 had blood pressures higher than 140/90, or 21%.

Obviously, techniques must be developed for screening more of the minorities. In the meantime, a most important feature of this strengthened program is the follow-up with pharmacists and physicians. 

Other Chronic Diseases: Efforts have been continued toward the control of diabetes, pulmonary disease, kidney disease, arthritis, and blindness through community agencies concerned with these conditions. The activities of one such agency, the Diabetes Association of Rhode Island, are illustrative. In the year 1976-77, 182

-- this organization obtained two Eyetone Meters with the help of the Lions Club and Telephone Pioneers for diagnostic purposes.

Detection clinics were held in various parts of the state in which more than 1,000 individuals were screened and followed-up. A strong public education program (radio) was conducted and a symposium was held for nurses in the month of April, 1977. Three hundred and thirteen nurses attended.

Division of Air Pollution Control 

The division was formed to preserve, protect and improve the air resources of the state so as to promote the public health, welfare and safety, prevent injury or detriment to human, plant and animal life, physical property and other resources and to foster the comfort and convenience of the state's inhabitants. Highlights A reduction in suspended particulate matter was achieved and, at present, only one monitoring station is exceeding primary standards. This is the unit positioned at the laboratory located at the Dyer Street entrance ramp to Route I-195. The division is currently developing a maintenance plan for sulfur dioxide. and particulate matter. The plan will provide for maintenance of air quality standards for the next 20 years. Control strategies are being developed to reduce the level of particulate matter. 

One of the strategies under consideration is a replacement of rotary cup burners with air atomization. This should reduce particulate emission as well as improve fuel economy. 

Pollution from photochemical oxidants is a problem which plagues the entire northeast. Every summer levels exceed the ambient standards as well as the alert levels for this pollutant. The division issued its first air quality advisory for photochemical oxidants this year. The advisory warns elderly people and people with respiratory illnesses to remain indoors when possible and to minimize their activities. It also cautions motorists against unnecessary driving. Plans to control this pollutant are currently being implemented. Air Pollution Control Regulation No. 15 has required industries to curtail the use of highly photochemically reactive hydrocarbons. Most industries have already complied with this regulation. In addition, the Department of Transportation will now add emission tests to its existing safety program. This 183

should cut down the amount of hydrocarbons and carbon mon oxide emitted from mobile sources. 

Several industries have installed control systems this year. The 

Pawtucket Municipal Incinerator completed a $2,000,000 scrubbing system to bring this source into compliance with emission standards.

The following figures represent the division's activities during fiscal year 1977:

Incinerators Checked Division of Animal and Dairy Industry 

The purpose of this division is to eradicate, if possible, all transmissible and infectious diseases of animals, and to provide a wholesome supply of milk and all dairy products. Highlights • Veterinary paramedic personnel have established themselves as an integral part of disease-eradiction surveillance programs. Rhode 

Island is the first state to employ paramedics in routine surveil lance, and a similar program has been adopted in California. 

There is every indication that the use of paramedics will become a part of the Uniform Methods and Rules this year. • A Hog Cholera surveillance force is still covering Rhode Island and southeastern Massachusetts. To date, there are a few hightitered herds in the area, but no evidence of the active virus. 

The nation, however, has not been declared free of the disease since the last New England outbreak. 184

་ 

An increase in frozen dessert manufacturing licenses issued was noted this year. The advent of frozen yogurt as an adjunct to the traditional ice cream was the principal reason for this increase. Highlights 

Division of Chest Diseases 

Tubercluosis Register: There were 90 newly diagnosed cases of tuberculosis reported to this department during the fiscal year ending 30 June 1977, including 1 primary infection and 15 forms of tuberculosis other than pulmonary. Of these cases 85 were active.

This represents a morbidity rate of 9.2 per 100,000 population (on the basis of estimated population of 927,000 for 1976). The morbidity rate reported for fiscal year ending 30 June 1976 was 10.7 per 100,00 population (estimated population of 927,000). 

Five deaths occurred from tuberculosis in the state during the fiscal year, showing a mortality rate of .5 per 100,000 population. 

No change from previous fiscal year.

Drug Program: A special anti-tuberculosis drug program, financed by the United States Public Health Service, has operated successfully for another year. The drugs are dispensed free to patients who are under the supervision of private physicians or clinics. 

The drug depots are located in designed district nursing association offices or clinics located conveniently throughout the state. 185

This program has been of immense help and value, not only in providing drugs at no cost to the patient, but also in keeping them under medical supervision in order to insure continuous and adequate treatment. Since the program started 7,490 patients have received drugs, and 6,395 of these have had their drugs discontinued. As of 30 June 1977, 1,095 were on drugs.

For certain groups of individuals who are considered to be "at risk" of developing tuberculosis, prophylaxis isoniazid for one year may be considered. At the present time there are 836 indi viduals taking this treatment. Anti-tuberculosis drugs for prophylaxis and treatment, required an expenditure exceeding $30,- 

000 for the fiscal year. • Field Clinics: (Portable x-ray unit 14 x 17 chest x-ray). There have been 24 chest x-ray field clinics throughout the state during the fiscal year where 347 individuals were seen and 365 x-rays were obtained. Among these were 26 diagnosed cases of pulmonary tuberculosis classified as follows: minimal, 6; moderately advanced. 

7: far advanced, 8; primary infection, 5.

The state mobile x-ray unit (chestmobile) conducted mass surveys throughout the state and 9,152 miniatures were obtained; of these, 93 were screened out for further evaluation and study, and 42 of these responded. A continuing effort is constantly being made to reach the remaining 51 for further study.

The results obtained from the 42 individuals are as follows: negative, 29; pulmonary tuberculosis, 3; deferred, 8; other chest diseases, 2. 

Of the 3 tuberculosis cases, 1 was minimal and 2 were moderately advanced. Of these, 2 were known to this division and 1 new case. There were 2 inactive cases and 1 active case. • The chestmobile was in operation for only four months during the fiscal year. As of 11 November 1976, the chestmobile was no longer in operation for mass surveys. The unit will be used in 

Newport as a stationary unit and will be in operation one day a week. 186

Division of Child Health 

The goal of this division is to maintain and improve the health of mothers and children. In accomplishing this task, the division fosters programs and services emphasizing genetic counseling, family planning, prenatal and postpartum care, continuing pediatric health supervision, and early detection and treatment of physical handicaps of children. 

It contracts for monitors and evaluates specific services for accomplishing these purposes and develops and implements state plans for maternal and child health and for crippled children.

Highlights 

Child Health Conferences: These provide primary health care and limited treatment for infants and children up to school age who do not have access to private medical care. There are 41 child health conference sites throughout the state. The services. provided are: physical examinations, monitoring of growth and development, immunizations against diphtheria, pertussis, and tetanus, polio, measles and rubella, P.K.U. screening, hemoglobin levels, nutrition counseling, lead screening, sickle cell screening, tine testing, hearing and vision screening. Health education is an important aspect of these conferences and parenting skills are taught. Referrals to other facilities are made when indicated. There were 1,307 child health conference sessions held and 7,430 children made 25,218 visits to these conferences.

Maternal and Child Health Home Visits-M.C.H. Coordinators: 

There are M.C.H. Coordinators in seven hospitals in Rhode Island offering a maternity or newborn service. These M.C.H. Coordinators teach and screen mothers while in the hospital to determine if they or their infants are in the high risk category and in need of home visits following discharge from the hospital. 

Criteria for these high risk mothers and infants has been set up by the Division of Child Health. Each home health agency also has an M.C.H. Coordinator for the promotion of maternal and child health visits. This is an active preventive health program for low income, high risk mothers and children through home nursing visits to selected families. Families are encouraged by the hospital M.C.H. Coordinators into a primary health care system whether it be a family physician, a pediatrician, a child health conference or a neighborhood health center. Families are taught the fundamentals of parenting, care of themselves and upbringing 187

of their children. Demonstrations of certain procedures and treatments are taught by the nurse making the home visit according to need. There were 4,318 visits during the past fiscal year. Childbirth education courses are taught to the mothers during the prenatal period in the hospital by the M.C.H. Coordinators. Some home health agenices also offer childbirth education classes. 

Regionalized Intensive Care Unit: This regionalized intensive care unit is located at Women and Infants Hospital. Using the neonatal approach the intensive care unit provides modern definitive diagnosis and therapy of the high risk infant utilizing all modalities of radiology, laboratory and neonatal physician and neonatal nursing services. There were 941 admissions to this unit. 

Of this number 291 high risk infants were transferred to this unit from other hospitals in Rhode Island, lower Massachusetts and Connecticut offering a maternity and newborn service. A two weeks educational and clinical course in neonatology is offered for nurses continuously throughout the year. One hundred and twenty-four nurses were enrolled during the past year. There is also a followup pediatric clinic at the hospital for high risk infants where care is given the infant until one year of age. During the past year a neonatal seminar was held for professional and lay people and was well attended. • Erythroblastosis Prevention Program: An injection of Rh Immune 

Globulin was given to the Rh-negative mother 72 hours following delivery of an Rh-positive infant. This prevented Rh iso-immunization of the Rh negative mother. Direct payment for Rho-Gam, its administration and tests, is reimbursed by the division if the person did not have Blue Cross, Medicaid, Champers or other commercial insurance and was a Rhode Island resident. There were 339 administrations of Rho-Gam during the past year. 

Children and Youth Project: This is a comprehensive project for children from infancy to age 14, and is located in the town of 

Burrillville. The project provides health care services to Burrillville children in low income families by increasing the quality of needed health services and making them more readily available to the children to be served. It guides families to community resources appropriate for the needs of their children and reduces preventable illness and disability among children and ameliorates chronic conditions amenable to treatment. The project develops tests and applys new methods of providing health care to chil- 188

dren. A sliding scale fee schedule is used. Third party payment is collected when available. Three children and youth clinics are held weekly. Services provided are physical examinations, diagnosis, treatment, immunizations and screening procedures, inhospital care when indicated, counseling and referring for corrections of defects and aftercare as needed. There are presently 520 children in this program. Health education has been a highlight in this project such as: nutrition teaching, classes for obese adolescents, pamphlets to parents, a newsletter, mini conferences and team conferences. • Maternity and Infant Care Project: This project provides a comprehensive program of preventive health services and medical care for maternity patients and their infants (to age one year) in selected geographic areas with low socio-economic characteristics. Maternity care includes diagnostic, prenatal, in-hospital, postnatal care nutrition service, social service, public health nursing, dental and family planning. In-patient care is provided at 

Women and Infants Hospital or Memorial Hospital. All infants of project mothers are eligible for pediatric care, including hospitalization up to one year of age. At one year of age the infant is enrolled in a primary health care facility or referred to a private physician according to family choice. Third party payment is utilized for services when available. Presently there are three sites: Women and Infants Hospital, Rhode Island Hospital and Memorial Hospital. During the past year 827 new patients were admitted to this program. 

Child Development Center: The Child Development Center located in the Ambulatory Patient Center Building at Rhode Island 

Hospital provides interdisciplinary evaluation and treatment services to children with developmental disabilities, mental retardation, and/or multiple physical handicaps. Special programs in the center are: Cystic Fibrosis, Down's Syndrome, Early Inter vention Program, Genetic Counseling, Spina Bifida, Mental Re tardation, Muscular Dystrophy, Neuromuscular Disabilities, P.K.U. and Renal Disease. During the past year Epilepsy was added to the project to identify children under 21 years of age who have Epilepsy and who are in need of a coordinated medical program for rehabilitation. These children are provided with a multi-disciplinary treatment program which consists of medical services, public health nursing services and social services. Medical services include ambulatory care, some medications, labora- 189

tory tests, and possibly, in rare cases, in-patient hospitalization and surgery. Public health nursing services include the services of a registered nurse and also services provided by the registered nurse making home visits. Social services include casework, counseling, and referral to other appropriate public and private agencies when needed. The breakdown of patients during the past year on the individual programs is as follows: Neuromuscular disease, 127 patients; Meningomyolocele, 77 patients; P.K.U., 40 patients; Cystic Fibrosis, 67 patients; Renal Disease, 71 patients; 

Epilepsy, 87 patients; Muscular Dystrophy, 56 patients; Mental 

Retardation, 1003 patients. The total caseload in the Child Development Center program was 1,528 patients. 

Mobile Child Health Unit: The Mobile Van provides primary health care to children in isolated rural areas who do not have private pediatricians as well as to those who are Child Development Center patients. The purpose is to educate these serviced populations toward improving health practices leading to higher levels of preventive health care. This van services Washington 

County, Kent County and Blolck Island. The services provided are physical examinations, immunizations, screening tests, nutrition counseling and social service. There were 18 clinics held which serviced 1,292 children.

Genetic Counseling Center: The center provides expert informa tion regarding the probability of parents having children with a genetic disorder. Located within the Child Development Center at Rhode Island Hospital, it educates the professional community (medical and paramedical) and the general community about the indications for and benefits of genetic counseling. Complete family and medical histories are obtained, prior medical records are requested, and necessary examinations and laboratory procedures are performed in order to establish an accurate diagnosis. Genetic counseling conferences are held weekly to discuss each case. The patient or family is then given an interview during which all findings and suggestions are explained in detail. 

A letter is sent subsequently summarizing all points discussed. 

To meet the educational goal there are television and radio public announcements, distribution of literature and lectures and seminars to high school and college classes as well as to professional and non-professional groups. Any Rhode Island resident is eligible for this program. 190

Family Planning Program: This is a voluntary program which will improve the physical and emotional health of the residents and provide families with freedom of choice in spacing their children and the size of their families. Medical services of the program are under the supervision of a board certified ad/gyn physician and a comprehensive physical examination is performed. 

Genetic counseling and infertility services are also available. 

There are 11 family planning sites in Rhode Island. During the past year 18,500 visits were made to these sites. A sliding free schedule according to family income was used. 

Prenatal Clinics: There are two prenatal clinics located in the 

Northwest and Washington County communities. These prenatal clinics were established because of a shortage of obs/gyn physicians. The patients from the Northwest Community are delivered at Women and Infants Hospital and the patients in Washington County are delivered at South County Hospital. During the past year in the Northwest Community, 52 pregnant women made 343 visits to the clinic. In Washington County, 82 pregnant women made 679 visits to the clinic. Transportation to the hospital is a problem in these two areas; therefore, they are able to receive their prenatal care right in their own communities.

Sudden Infant Death Syndrome: This is a program of counseling service for parents of infants who died of sudden infant death. 

Some parents are counseled by a professional and other parents are counseled by a peer group. Parents are given the choice of counselors. There is also a parents council group which is made up of professionals and paraprofessionals. With parental permission an autopsy is performed on all sudden infant deaths by the medical examiner and a report and consultation is provided to parents. There were 5 sudden infant deaths during the past year. 

Lead Screening Program: There were 2,048 children screened for lead poisoning at the child health conferences during the past year. Any child with a high lead content received treatment at 

Rhode Island Hospital. 

Chad Brown Health Center: There were 103 child health conferences held at this center. Four hundred and ninety-nine children made 2,250 visits. The division sponsors the child health conferences at this center and also sponsors the nurse. It is a health center which offers a full degree of heath services and is supported by St. Pius Church. 191

• Crippled Childrens Program: This program is the only direct program serviced by the division. There is a cardiac program and an orthopedic program conducted at Rhode Island Hospital and St. Joseph's Hospital. There are also sub-specialty clinics for scoliosis, cleft lip and palate, cerebral palsy, upper limb prosthesis and hearing and speech. One hundred and forty-six cardiac clinics were held and 751 children made 1,060 visits. There were 

151 orthopedic clinics held and 618 patients made 1,270 visits.

There were 287 children on sub-specialty programs. 

Consultation Services: Maternal and child health medical and nursing consultation was available to any community agency to help in the development of new programs in the area. It was available to help in planning, orientation and training of agency staff as well as for the evaluation of ongoing programs. 

Office of Data Evaluation 

This office exists to provide the department and the general healthinterested community and the general public with timely, valid and appropriate health information together with reliable interpretations. 

The data consist of material bearing on health status, health services, health facilities, health hazards and the like and are used by the department and others for public information and education, identification of needs for health programs, enforcement of health standards, evaluation of health change and program outcomes, support of administrative action and research. 

This office engages in very little direct data collection. It either coordinates data collection activities of other divisions within the department or sub-contracts with Rhode Island Health Services Research, Inc., SEARCH, which is the state health statistics center for Rhode 

Island. The office is responsible for liaison with SEARCH and in this capacity it administers and monitors all contracts, sub-contracts and other projects involving SEARCH and the department. Highlights In conjunction with SEARCH the office administered three data acquisition and reporting contracts from the National Center for 

Health Statistics/Cooperative Health Statistics System in the fields of health manpower, health facilities, and vital statistics.

Division of Dental Health 

Functions of the division include the development of a statewide dental health program; conducting dental health education activities in the schools, for the public, and for the dental profession; developing methods and procedures for improving dental conditions among preschool, school and adult populations; providing consultation and assistance to local health authorities, dental organizations, and other groups and agencies interested in the implementation of dental health. programs; and providing dental services to groups with special needs. 193

Highlights The division continued to provide topical applications of fluoride solutions to the surfaces of the teeth for children in Grades 2, 3, 4 and 6 in several towns. In fiscal 1977, a total of 1,296 received a dental prophylaxis and fluoride treatment. . During 1976-77, the division expanded its efforts in the area of cancer prevention. Spot announcements on oral cancer were aired by local television stations. Over 1,200 booklets entitled "Cancer of the Mouth" were distributed to state residents. 

Efforts were continued in the area of cancer education for health professionals. Considerable effort was devoted to activities of the 

Cancer Control Board of Rhode Island and developing a model statewide cancer prevention program. Seminars on oral cancer detection were presented for 150 nurses. Copies of a publication 

"Oral Cancer Detection-A Nursing Responsibility" were dis tributed to 125 registered nurses working in community programs. 

A seminar on oral cancer was presented for physicians and other paramedical personnel at the New England Public Health Association, Portsmouth, New Hampshire. ● A total of 509 patients were screened for oral cancer in 7 community Cancer Detection Day programs held throughout the state. A total of 38 patients displayed some form of lesion of the soft tissue of the mouth and were referred for followup to their private dentist, physician, or the Oral Tumor Clinic at Our Lady of Fatima Hospital. 

The division continued to provide for the reading of oral cytology and biopsy specimens submitted by private dentists to the Oral Tumer Clinic. During fiscal 1977, 271 tissue biopsis (an increase of 6.7% over 1975-76) and 166 oral smears (24.8% more than 1975-76) were submitted by dentists for pathological evaluation. Thirteen of these specimens showed pathological changes that were malignant in nature and in each instance, appropriate treatment was initiated. 

The division remained active in the provision of dental care for groups with special needs. The dental care program for preschool children conducted at the Joseph Samuels Dental Center offered an excellent opportunity for the application of secondary preventive practices-i.e., early diagnosis and prompt treatment. 

In this program, 13,445 different dental operations were performed on children of preschool age. 194

​Mentally retarded children up to age 21 also are eligible for dental care under a state-federally supported program conducted at the Joseph Samuels Dental Center. A total of 1,115 mentally retarded patients made 1,382 visits to this program. 

The dental care program for rural school children was continued during the past year in 8 rural communities of the state where dental disease is of high prevalence, where there is no community water supply available for fluoridation, and where dentists are in scarce supply or completely lacking. A total of 2,940 teeth were treated in this program, 2,704 of which were restored to normal health. In addition, in compliance with the state School 

Health Law, 4,448 examinations were performed at no charge to the state on children by a dentist who had retired from the division.

The program to provide preventive and therapeutic dental care for residents of Block Island continued on a joint state-community basis with Department of Health (Dental Division) and the town of New Shoreham sharing in the financing. 

The dental care program conducted in association with the Maternal and Infant Care Project sponsored by the Division of Child Health was continued at the Pawtucket Memorial and the

75% over the previous year.

In cooperation with the Forsyth Dental Center, Boston, Massachusetts, a program of preventive dentistry has been initiated in 7 parochial schools in the Blackstone Valley area. The purpose of the program is to evaluate the effects of brushing with a fluoride dentifrice once each day in school supplemented by a program of oral hygiene education on the reduction of dental caries when these preventive measures are superimposed on the benefits of community water fluoridation. A total of 1,305 children in Grades
