Division of Drug Control DRUG ABUSE CONTROL SECTION 

This section conducts investigations into suspected criminal violations of the Uniform Controlled Substances Act, and participates in a compliance surveillance program to circumvent the diversion of legal inventories of controlled substances to illicit uses. Highlights Nineteen hundred and one investigations were made which involved illegal sale and/or possession of controlled substances, unauthorized filling of prescriptions, theft of drugs, and theft of prescription forms. Six hundred and thirty-two cases involving two hundred and fifty-four persons, required official action. Five persons were referred under the provisions of the so-called Civil Commitment Law for treatment of conditions resulting from the use or abuse of controlled substances. Six hundred and ninety-five court appearances were made in the interest of the prosecution of cases and the civil commitment of drug dependent persons. 

Nine thousand six hundred and seventy permits, authorizing possession of hypodermic instruments by persons legally entitled to do so in the judgment of the attending physicians were issued. 

After investigation had been made into the processes and the provisions for security and methods of disposal of such instruments, 53 permits were issued for the purchase, possession, and sale for use in various industrial processes. Instructions for the proper disposal of unusable hypodermic instruments were routinely disseminated. • Sixteen hundred and sixty-seven instances of liaison in cooperation with local, state and federal law enforcement agencies occurred during the year.

Six persons or firms were issued registrations authorizing the manufacture of controlled substances and preparations. Sixteen persons or firms were registered to sell controlled substances and preparations at wholesale. 197

Two thousand four hundred and seventy-eight registrations were issued to persons engaged in the manufacture, distribution, prescribing, administering or dispensing of controlled substances, or to conduct research in those substances within the state. 

PHARMACY SECTION 

The State Board of Pharmacy, under the general supervision of the director of health, controls the sale of drugs, medicines and poisons generally. This section of the division acts as the administrative agency for the implementation of this program. This is accomplished by the examination and registration of pharmacists and the licensing of manufacturers and wholesalers of drugs, medicines, and poisons, community pharmacies, pharmacies within the medical institutions and clinics, and public auctions of such products. These activities include the compilation and preparation of written and oral examinations on pharmaceutical subjects as pharmacy, pharmacology, chemistry, pharmaceutical mathematics, jurisprudence, public health, and the practice of pharmacy. The regulatory phase of the program includes: the inspection of establishments of all dealers in drugs, medicines and poisons to determine compliance with the standards of operation provided therefor under the laws and regulations governing the practice of pharmacy; and the annual registration of pharmacists and the licensing of pharmacies and other dealers and establishments authorized to engage in the manufacture, sale and dispensing of drugs, medicines and poisons. Highlights The board held monthly meetings and conducted two sessions for the examination of candidates for registration in pharmacy. Sixtysix candidates qualified to become registered pharmacists upon examination.

Oral examinations were given, and registrations were issued to sixteen candidates upon the reciprocal basis of registration by examination in participating jurisdictions. 

On 1 July 1977, 1367 persons held outstanding, unrevoked certificates as registered pharmacists, and 20 persons held certificates as registered assistant pharmacists. Also, 205 pharmacies were licensed to offer pharmaceutical services within the state. During the year seven pharmacies were discontinued and seven pharmacies were opened. There were 7 manufacturers and 16 wholesalers of drugs, medicines and poisons licensed to do business in the 198

state. Twenty licenses were issued to conduct a pharmacy within and for the services of a medical institution. 

One hundred and twenty incidents of input into pharmacy programs within hospitals were made for various purposes which included: annual inspection of facilities, personnel, and stocks of pharmaceuticals, chemicals, and drugs. • The division assisted the Division of Licensure and Construction in the appraisal of pharmaceutical services in nursing, rest and convalescent homes. Thirteen formal surveys of those services were made; advice and assistance was provided on a daily basis upon the request of the facility administrators and/or their pharmacy consultants. Pharmacy personnel of this division contributed 30% of their time in providing participation in the certification and continued education of nurses' aides in the administration of drugs; supervision of pharmaceutical services generally provided, control of and accountability for drugs and hypodermic instruments, labeling and storage of drugs, and maintenance of records; participation in 31 formal conferences and seminars on the providing of patient care sponsored by the Division of Licensure and Construction; and investigations. In charges in which disregard for, non-compliance with, or violation of the pharmacy laws and the rules and regulations promulgated thereunder were established as fact, the persons responsible were summoned before the board for hearing and appropriate disposition. BREATH ANALYSIS PROGRAM 

Responsibilities of the Department of Health under the provisions of the "Implied Consent Law" include promulgation and/or approval of satisfactory methods and techniques for determination of the amount of alcohol and/or drugs in a person's blood, urine, or other bodily substances; to ascertain the qualifications of individuals and the competence of equipment to perform such tests; to certify breath-testing techniques, methods, and instruments, the monitoring of such instruments, and the training and certification of operators and inspectors; and, to issue permits therefor which are subject to termination or revocation at the discretion of the department. Highlights • Forty-nine breath-testing instruments are now used throughout the state. Six instruments are used by the state police, one by the 

Division of Enforcement of the Department of Natural Resources, and 42 are used by the police departments of the cities and towns. 

The department has certified 308 trained operators to those agencies. In August, 1976 the division conducted, with the cooperation of the University of Rhode Island, a comprehensive 80 hour course for the training of 36 operators. Three hundred and ninety-nine operators have been trained since the inception of the breath analysis program in 1959. 

Standards adopted for the determination by a chemical breath analysis of the amount of alcohol in a person's blood require, among other things, that: all breath-testing instruments be equilibrated at intervals not exceeding 30 days, all operators demonstrate proficiency at intervals not exceedings 90 days, and chemicals used in the instruments be ascertained to be of proper strength and volume. The monitoring requirements prescribed by law and regulation were performed on the schedules indicated, and personnel of this division appeared before the courts of the state to give testimony thereof. This involved the following certifications: 

Instruments-609 times; operators-1158 times; and repairs and adjustments-51 times. 201

Emergency Medical Services 

This division coordinates and combines emergency medical services throughout the state in an endeavor to improve methods and procedures in this activity. It is closely associated with organizations and facilities that are concerned with emergency medical services and is the coordinating agency to the Citizens' Advisory Committee on Emergency 

Medical Services, a committee established to render assistance and guidance in matters pertaining to emergency medical services. It develops educational programs in the fields of accident prevention and injury control and coordinates educational programs and exercises related to civil defense and preparedness.

Highlights The division, in cooperation with the Rhode Island Medical Society, formulated an 81 hour training program entitled "Rescue 

Practices and Emergency Aid" in order to achieve equivalency with the Department of Transportation's recommended curriculum. Thus far, 2,181 ambulance and rescue attendants have been certified from this program as emergency medical technicians. 

Also formulated was a program whereby selected emergency medical technicians were trained in the use of an electro-cardiographic telemetry system which transmits a cardiac victim's electro-cardiogram via a portable two-way radio to a hospital where a cardiologist analyzes the severity of the attack and transmits instructions as to care and treatment of the victim to the emergency medical technician at the scene and en route to a hospital. The training program includes not only the use of special equipment, but the administering of injections and other lifesaving procedures. One hundred fifty emergency medical technicians completed this program and have been certified as advanced emergency medical technicians. • The division, in cooperation with Health Planning Council, Inc., assisted in an emergency patient flow study and the development of emergency patient referral guidelines relative to categorization of hospital emergency care capabilities; the development of transfer agreements; and a system of medical control via radio. The objectives of this study have been adopted. • The division, after a study, concluded that integrated emergency medical services would more likely be achieved if encouraged on a regional basis. Seven locations were selected for health service 203

areas: Northern Rhode Island, Providence, Pawtucket, Kent 

County, South County, Westerly, and Newport. Seven area based 

Emergency Medical Services Policy Committees have already been established and are functioning for planning and coordinating emergency medical services. The present statewide Citizens' Advisory Committee on Emergency Medical Services will continue as an advisor to the department in emergency medical services matters and will function as a clearing house for information and coordination among the area-based policy committees. 

Through the advocation of this division, in addition to all hospitals with emergency department facilities having installed the 

HEAR system (Hospital Emergency Administrative Radio) where they communicate from hospital to hospital on frequency 155.280, fifty-two ambulance and rescue squads have also installed the HEAR system and can communicate with a hospital on frequency 155.340. The remaining ambulance and rescue squads in the state will have the HEAR system installed by the end of this year. The 

HEAR system has also been installed at the Veterans Administration Hospital and the Naval Medical Center in Newport, along with several private ambulance companies. 

An inspection team has been set up by the division and has embarked on the task of inspecting ambulance and rescue vehicles for conformance with the minimum standards set by regulation. 

The division supervised and coordinated the training of approximately 12,000 individuals in the Medical Self-Help Training Program covering the care for the needs of the sick and injured in an emergency situation when professional medical services are not available. Of particular significance is the fact that many states abandoned this program because of a halting of federal assistance. 

Through foresight, this division was able to amass sufficient materials and supplies to continue this basic first aid survival concept. • The division prepared a written, comprehensive plan of action for mutual aid in a mass casualty situation, a supportive document to the Rhode Island Emergency Health Service Plan. It defined a statewide emergency medical assistance program based on the concept of mutual aid that will ensure that persons who require medical services resulting from emergencies will receive appropriate assistance efficiently and rapidly. • Through its Injury Control Program, the division conducted training programs for nursing associations, for the elderly and 204

convalescents at various facilities throughout the state, and for many schools. Health and safety newsletters were prepared covering timely subjects on accident prevention and injury control. 

There has been a tremendous demand for the showing of a film depicting the "Heimlich Maneuver" on choking victims. This division responded by the showing of the film to numerous restaurant owners, hospital groups and nurses, accompanied by talks and demonstrations in the technique of saving a choking victim. 

Division of Epidemology 

'The division is charged with communicable disease surveillance and control in Rhode Island. In this capacity its major activities include venereal disease control, immunizations, epidemic investigations and special disease control activities; such as the unprecedented influenza immunization program conducted this past year. The division is also responsible for operating a statewide lead poisoning control program and has recently been involved in planning for diabetic and hypertension control programs.

Highlights Disease survelliance was conducted by the division through several mechanisms. A panel of 56 consultant physicians reported disease occurrence to the division weekly. Telephone reports were also received from a variety of sources and a nurse epidemiologist from the division made weekly visits to all hospitals in the state to search for reportable diseases.

The bi-weekly Communicable Disease Newsletter with a circulation of about 2,800 was mailed to health care providers and other interested parties. Consultation on the public health aspects of communicable disease problems was a daily activity and numerous lectures and seminars were conducted by members of the division. 

Special investigational, control and educational efforts of 1977 included a wide variety of activities. Lyme arthritis information was disseminated through the Newsletter and a collaborative study with the Connecticut Health Department of this entity was begun. 

A study of babesiosis on Block Island by the Center for Diseasse Control was stimulated by the division. Malaria in Indochinese immigrants was investigated. Rabies treatment following bat bites was coordinated. Epidemic assistance was provided for foodborne outbreaks, hepatitis, rubella and measles outbreaks. 205

Another area of division activity was that of international travel. 

The division provided physicians and travelers with health advice and immunization requirements. It also is the only source for yellow fever immunizations for travelers in the state; in fiscal year 

1977, 290 doses of yellow fever vaccine were dispensed. • Hepatitis continues to be an endemic disease of importance in the state. In fiscal year 1977, 217 cases were reported. Noteworthy is the increase in the amount of serum or type B hepatitis from about 50 cases per year to 89 in the past year. This probably reflects the increased used of a serology test for serum hepatitis. • Rhode Island experienced no poliomyelitis, diphtheria, tetanus, primary encephalitis or trichinosis this past fiscal year. The frequencies of reported cases of meningitis, salmonella, shigella and streptococcal infections did not differ appreciably from that found over the past five years. For venereal diseases, a 10% increase in gonorrhea over that found for fiscal year 1975 occurred in spite of control efforts. Syphilis cases have continued their five year decline. Influenza reached its lowest frequency in recent years with only 2,149 cases reported. Mumps, also, continued to decline probably because of the use of mumps vaccine. • A sharp increase in rubella and measles occurred in the state in fiscal year 1977. Control measures were aimed at the containment of these diseases through vaccine use. 

Venereal Disease Control: Veneral disease control continued to make significant progress during fiscal year 1977. The primary goal of the veneral disease control program is the identification and treatment of those persons in the community who have the disease, who have been exposed to the disease or who may be incubating the disease. The primary challenge confronting public health officials concerned with venereal disease control is how to ensure that every affected individual receive therapy before any spread cases develop.

The specific methodology of venereal disease control depends upon the coordinated implementation of four activities: screening, epidemiology, surveillance and education. Screening provided the patient the opportunity to discover the unknown. In fiscal 

1977, 43,364 females had a culture test for gonorrhea, 925 (2.1%) were positive. Since 80% of these patients were asymptomatic, gonorrhea screening is the only method of disease identification. 206

Syphilis screening is routinely done by blood tests for such things as marriage license applications and prenatal examinations. All blood test results suggestive of syphilis and positive gonorrhea culture results are followed by the Venereal Disease Control Unit. 

Epidemiology provided for the identification and treatment of the potential patient in the incubation stage before the patient became infectious and spread the disease. In fiscal 1977, the epidemiologic process was applied to 26 cases of early syphilis and 2,225 cases of gonorrhea. The epidemiologic process involved interviewing a person who is a known case of syphilis or gonorrhea to obtain names of persons who might have been exposed to the disease. Contacts are then located and notified of their possible exposure and referred to a private physician or one of the four state sponsored free VD Specialty Clinics for medical evaluation and treatment.

The VD Control Unit aided in the treatment of venereal disease by providing drugs to medical providers involved in routine screening for syphilis and gonorrhea. The unit also has a program which provides private physicians free culture materials, delivery services, laboratory processing and in some cases drugs to treat patients with venereal disease. 

Surveillance refers to the detection of the infectious case. Venereal disease cases must be reported by law, and all public, private and commercial laboratories approved for premarital and prenatal blood testing for syphilis reported weekly to VD Control all positive laboratory results indicating the presumptive presence of venereal disease. New cases were also detected through the epidemiological process.

Veneral disease education enabled the patient to use prophylaxis and prevent infection, or lacking that, to seek examination and treatment when infected. Various information-education activities have been conducted to provide the people of Rhode Island with necessary venereal disease information. The mass media has cooperated by sponsoring programs and news articles concerning the current VD trends. Newspaper articles and clinic advertisements are probably responsible for the increase in clinic attendance and the increased number of phone calls to the VD 

Hotline number. The Venereal Disease Control Unit noted an increase in requests by organized groups for films, pamphlets and presentations. Several presentations were given to high school 207

students, PTA groups, civil organizations and medical students. 

Working relationships continued to be established with several poverty agencies, family planning centers, gay organizations and community health centers. Over 20,000 venereal disease pamphlets have been distributed throughout the state. The Veneral Disease 

Control Unit also continued to provide treatment recommendations, drugs, laboratory service and epidemiologic follow-up services to the medical community. 

• Influenza Immunization-The Fight Flu Program: The depart ment began planning the Fight Flu Program in July, 1976 in response to the threat of a Swine influenza epidemic. To accomplish the goal of making influenza vaccine readily available to all eligible citizens of Rhode Island, the division enlisted support from a large number of health and community organizations as well as the private sector of medicine. The division provided overall planning, coordination, publicity, training, vaccine guidelines and vaccine distribution channels. Actual vaccine administration was largely done by participating community based groups. 

Financing for the program was provided by both federal and state monies. The federal government provided free vaccine (valued at $300,000) and $100,000 in grant monies. To this, the state added about $100,000.

Program implementation was divided into several phases each with a different target group. During the two month period of actual immunization, almost half of the adult population of the state was immunized. Throughout, the program was plagued by controversy concerned with product and professional liability, questions of vaccine safety and the justification for the program itself. Many felt that an epidemic was unlikely. These issues re duced turnouts in spite of motivational publicity efforts. 

On December 16, the program was prematurely suspended nationally because of an association between the vaccine and a rare paralytic disorder, the Guillain-Barre Syndrome (GBS), was shown. Although the risk of the syndrome was felt to be very low, in the absence of epidemic influenza it was considered prudent to suspend immunizations. Three cases of vaccine associated GBS occurred in Rhode Island.

Influenza surveillance for fiscal year 1977, ironically showed an unusually small amount of influenza occurred in the state. The 208

threat of an epidemic never materialized. It is felt that the Fight 

Flu Program must be viewed as a preventive health measure taken because the threat was very real; only retrospectively is it now possible to say that, indeed, the program was not necessary. 

Immunization Program: Activities during this budget period have significantly raised immunity levels to preventable diseases through both ongoing and special immunization programs and educational efforts. Disease surveillance and outbreak control activities have functioned well during this year. A system of assessment of immune levels of pre-schoolers and school-enterers has been continued and maintenance programs were implemented where immune levels were found to be low. Increased requests for vaccines through the Biological Distribution System reflects the increased awareness of the importance of routine immunizations in the private and public medical sectors. The medical community receives the latest immunization recommendations and morbidity. reports through regularly published newsletters and parents receive information regarding immunizations through the media and informational material distributed by the immunization program. 

Several special immunization clinics were conducted throughout the Rhode Island school system during the past year. The immunization project continues to support ongoing school health pro grams with vaccine and/or personnel in all school systems in Rhode Island. The latest survey of school-enterers in Rhode Is land indicate the following immune levels of five-six year old children: a. measles, 91.1%; b. Rubella, 89.9%; c. Polio, 89.4%; d. DTP, 90.3%; e. Completed Basic Series, (M,R,P, 3+ DTP 3+), 86.9%. This survey samples 11,226 children entering school for the first time in Rhode Island.

A sample survey of the immune status of two year old children reveals the following progress: a. Measles, 93.0%; b. Rubella, 88.6%; c. Polio, 92.3%; d. DTP, 96.0%; e. Completed Basic Series, (M,R,P, 3+, DTP 3+), 83.7%. The percentage of children completing a basic series of immunization increased by 1.4% in 1976 as compared to 1975. Vaccine usage for routine immunizations of pre-schoolers include 19,251 doses of measles/rubella vaccine, 64,256 doses of polio vaccine, 39,115 doses of DTP and 2,811 doses of DT antigens during 1976.

The Consultant Reporting System continued to provide disease reports throughout the state. Telelphonic reporting of dis- 209

ease entities has been strengthened through personal contact by program personnel with nurseries, day care centers, school systems, and private physicians throughout the state. Both measles and rubella case reports increased in FY 1977. Most of the rubella activity was on college campuses. Outbreak control for both rubella and measles consisted of case identifications and the immunization of susceptibles.

The bi-weekly Communicable Disease Newsletter was used to inform health personnel of the latest immunization recommendations. In addition, special emphasis was directed towards the medical community during Immunization Action Month in October, 1976, stressing the importance of auditing every patient's health record to insure adequate immune levels for preventable diseases. 

During Immunization Action Month in October, 1976, Rhode 

Island's program directed towards parents was prompted by several newspapers, TV stations, radio stations, pamphlets and posters. 

• Lead Poisoning Control Program: The lead poisoning control pro gram continued to be an important part of the division; activities were conducted in cooperation with the Division of Laboratories, the Lead Clinic at the Rhode Island Hospital, participating public health agencies and private physicians, and local housing agencies. During fiscal year 1976-1977 a total of 5,009 children were screened. Of this total, 528 children with elevated lead levels were detected. Seventy-six children were referred to the Rhode Island 

Hospital Lead Clinic for the first time this past year bringing the clinic total for the year to 348 patients. Of this caseload, 52 children underwent chelation therapy to reduce extremely elevated lead levels.

During this fiscal year, the lead program underwent several changes due to the receipt of a federal grant award specifically for childhood lead poisonings. Additional staff was hired to intensify screening efforts in high-risk areas and to undertake community education activities. An environmental component was established to inspect the homes of lead-burdened children for lead hazards. Federal guidelines established by the United States

Center for Disease Control were adopted which provide the program with a focus on children between the ages of 1 and 5, and a framework under which to carry out follow-up responsibilities. 210

Division of Food Protection and Sanitation 

The division, the largest in the department, operates through four field office locations and two specialized program areas; namely, control of individual sewage disposal system (ISDS Program) and meat and poultry processing inspection (Federal/State Wholesome Meat and 

Poultry Inspection Program). This operation provides the closest liaison possible with each community. 

Food protection responsibilities combine the prevention of diseases which may be transmitted through food as well as protection against economic fraud. More often than not, these overlap. The division provides surveillance of all food businesses, including activities following harvest, and is carried through to the consumer who may bring complaints regarding food being prepared or served in the home. The division inspects all food businesses and carries out regulatory actions when necessary. Special coordination and cooperation with federal agencies is required, specifically the Food and Drug Administration and the 

United States Department of Agriculture. 

211

The division conducts the regulatory program controlling the installation and maintenance of individual sewage disposal systems. The property and constitutional rights of the individual become an important issue here if the balance between public health protection and those rights come in conflict. Attention to changing standards or scientific information and the development and maintenance of a permanent records system with simple recovery of data is paramount. Coordination and cooperation with other state agencies as well as other 

Health Department divisions is necessary. 

Through its field offices, the division receives complaints about alleged public health nuisances caused by the insanitary handling of waste, overflowing sewage and the presence of vermin. Investigations are made and conditions alleviated. The division also conducts a program of approving funds for rodent control programs in communities. applying for such funds.

Highlights The division was hampered somewhat during the year by a severe shortage of personnel. This resulted both from the elimination of frozen positions and a relatively large number of illnesses and deaths, coupled with the swine flu program. The food programs of the division were unable to fulfill anticipated objectives. This is particularly reflected in the reduced number of food samples collected for laboratory analysis. 

The majority of the division personnel in field offices were active in the swine flu program. Personnel delivered flu vaccine to locations where clinics were held. The field offices themselves were used for clinic sites and were also distribution centers for delivery of flu vaccine.

Legal procedures were enhanced by the appointment of a fulltime adjudication officer. This improved the situation by allowing a greater objectivity in the hearing process in that the division chief was not required to be both judge and jury. 

Preparations were initiated to implement Chapters 23-55 of the 

General Laws entitled "Percolation Tests and Water Table Ele vation Determinations". Although the law was to be implemented on 1 July 1977, considerable preparation and interpretation of the law was required. Its impact on home building is expected to be considerable. The purpose of the act is to protect persons buying land from the possibility of not being able to use the land to construct a dwelling or other structure. 212

Implementation of the SPIF Program, which puts the food service program into automatic data processing, advanced towards its goal of full operation with the design of the necessary records and forms. • Microfilming of all records in the Individual Sewage Disposal System Program was initiated during the year including a readerprinter. 

• Chapter 5-56, "Licensing of Installers of Individual Sewage Dis posal Systems", went into effect in September and 439 licenses were issued, 421 of them under the "grandfather clause". Several examinations were held for those not qualified under the "grandfather clause".

As a result of a tractor/trailer accident, 40,000 pounds of meat were embargoed and released to the U.S. Department of Agriculture for action.

The field office personnel in the area in which the Adult Correctional Institution is located became involved in the problems regarding the facilities and became witnesses in the court case which ensued.

Division of Health Insurance 

The objective of this division is to promote personal and family integrity by providing protection against catastrophic health expendi tures through administration of the Rhode Island Catastrophic Health Insurance Program (CHIP). CHIP is designed to help Rhode Islanders who find themselves faced with large medical bills. All permanent residents of Rhode Island are covered, there are no premiums to pay, and there are no forms to fill out until a person actually applies for CHIP benefits. CHIP will help to pay for a family's medical bills after their health insurance plan has paid its share. The "out-of-pocket" costs beyond what their health insurance plan pays must exceed a certain amount for the family in order to qualify. This is referred to as a deductible and it can be as low as $500 or 10% of income, whichever is greater, if they have a good private health insurance plan, or as high as $5,000 or 50% of income, whichever is greater, if they have no health insurance.

The division is responsible for all aspects of eligibility including requests for information from the public and health care providers. 

Other duties include program publicity, developing, monitoring and auditing programs for CHIP claim administration with insurers, appeals procedures and hearings, certifying health maintenance organizations, and preparing required annual reports. Highlights A total of 173 families were determined eligible, representing a 

30% increase from the previous year. • Some 411 persons were eligible for benefits, an increase of 12% from the previous year.

A total of $1,095,937 were paid for claims. Although this represented a 60% increase from the previous year, an accurate comparison will not be available until the next fiscal year since the fiscal year beginning July 1, 1975 was the first full year of operation and program promotion and publicity had not reached its full potential. A total of $1,372 was the average CHIP deductible per family; and $6,335 was the average expenditure per family from CHIP funds, which was a 19% increase from the previous year. • The 17 appeals indicated a 10% reduction from the previous year and this perhaps can be attributed to a better understanding of the program by all concerned parties. 215

Three health maintenance organizations (HMO) were approved by the division according to the criteria established for certification within the CHIP regulations. The law requires that all employers in the state offer the choice of an HMO to their employees if a "qualified" HMO is available. Therefore, it was an objective of this division to establish and implement guidelines for qualification.

Office of Health Systems Development 

The Office is responsible for developing, reviewing, monitoring and evaluating developmental projects and programs of public and private agencies to which grants and contracts have been made for activities consistent with the goals and objectives of the State Health Plan. Highlights 

Phaseout of CHP (314a) Program: All relevant closeout activi ties were completed. Records and files were transferred to the state agency created and funded under P.L. 93-641. The CHP program is fully phased out and no further action is required. 

• Establish Project Review, Grants Management, Resources Identification, and Program Development Processes: A systems man agement project was developed and its execution by Boston Uni versity Center for Health Planning (Region I Technical Assist ance) was negotiated. A final report is due by the end of FY '77. 

In addition, the Office provided continuing assistance on request, by assignment and through regular staff meetings for all adminis trative activities incident to this phase of program activity. Resources identification results are available in Rhode Island Health 

Agency Guide, 1976 

Provide Indian Tribes With Information on Available Federal

Funds: Contact sources to which this information is to be provided have been identified. No further action was taken during 

FY '77 in the absence of available developmental funding.

Miscellaneous Activities: Not specifically assignable to any one of the three foregoing projects were numerous ongoing activities associated with the general implementation of P.L. 93-641. These activities related to critique and comment on request of the proposals made for other areas (e.g., planning and regulation) or 216

were designed to reflect the potential involvement of the developmental function in the planning and regulatory activities of the state agency.

Office of Health System Planning 

The primary responsibility of this office is to develope the three pre liminary plans required by the National Health Planning and Re sources Development Act: the State Health System Plan; the Annual Implementation Plan; the State Medical Facility Plan. 

Highlights Staff recruitment, training, and organization was completed. A professionally competent planning staff with the appropriate mix of planning skills, now exists. 

Planning-for-planning requirements have been accomplished with the completion of the following guide documents: 1) Health System Planning: An Introductory Guide for Public Law 93-641 Participants, 2) Rhode Island State Health Plan: Working Outline, 3) Health System Planning Working Papers, 4) Health Sys tem Indicators.

A coordination committee was established to keep other state departments involved in the office's work. Several state departments are represented on the committee.

Every hospital in the state was site visited in an effort to become better acquainted with the specific characteristics of each of the 

21 hospitals in Rhode Island.

In an effort to promote open discussion of the major health issues facing Rhode Island, a colloquium series was conducted. 

Twelve key actors on the Rhode Island health care scene, representing various viewpoints, entered into a dialogue with state government health professionals. • Special efforts were made to facilitate citizen participation in the health system planning process. First, an opinion survey instrument was distributed to 195 consumer and provider organizations in Rhode Island. This survey produced a respectable 57% response rate. Second, 19 nominal group meetings were conducted to insure that the perceptions of the following groups are well understood: the elderly, rural residents, the poor, and minorities. 217

• In an effort to take full advantage of Rhode Island's rich health data base in the health system planning process, 43 special data requests were submitted to Rhode Island Health Services Research, Inc. (SEARCH). 

• A Technical Reports series was established in order to provide a strong foundation for the upcoming preliminary plan documents. 

Nine Technical Reports were distributed covering such subjects as: the planning process, plan document development, CAT scanners, Rhode Island categorical health plans, health behavior, regionalization, geographic boundaries for planning, health opinions, and priority setting.

A highly innovative Health Status Index which incorporates both mortality and morbidity was designed, developed, and utilized to study the distribution and determinants of health in Rhode Is land.

The unit calculated for the first time in Rhode Island 18 age and sex specific hospital utilization indicators (such as patient days per 1000 and operations per 1000) for each city and town. This information has already proven to be valuable to the Prospective 

Reimbursement and Certificate-of-Need processes in Rhode Island. 

• A refined outline for the Preliminary State Health System Plan was developed and agreed upon.

• The System Description chapter of the Preliminary State Health 

System Plan was virtually completed.

Division of Laboratories 

The division provides analytical laboratory support to the various program agencies within the department, community health agencies, hospitals, and private physicians. The division provides a comprehensive program to support most of the health, consumer, environmental, and forensic toxicological needs of the state. Highlights Construction on the new laboratory building continued to progress throughout this fiscal year with approximately 60% of the project having been completed on June 30th. It is anticipated that the division will be able to begin moving shortly after the first of the year. 218

The Air Pollution Laboratory recently reorganized to maximize the technical capabilities of the laboratory staff. The laboratory evaluated and implemented a new analytical procedure for the determination of lead levels in ambient air. • The Animal Diseases Laboratory continued to function as a federally approved regulatory laboratory, performing tests for state and state-federal programs that have legal, economic, and public health significance. They also participated in the USDA serological control program for brucellosis and salmonellosis.

A new federally funded program for lead screening resulted in a 17% increase in the number of specimens submitted for lead analysis in the Biochemistry Laboratory. The laboratory also conducted research on vitreous humor in conjunction with the state medical examiner.

The Food Chemistry Laboratory evaluated and implemented several new analytical procedures, inclulding niacin in fresh meat, mold counts in tomato products, and histamine determinations in meat products. Research conducted in cooperation with URI resulted in the publication of a paper dealing with the electrophoretic identification of pigmented trout. Some major violations this past year were decomposition of protein foods, quality of olive oil, rodent hairs in imported foods, and copper levels in shellfish.

The Food Microbiology Laboratory participated in a study with the Medical Examiner and the Division of Epidemiology to determine the possibility of a link between infant botulism and sudden death syndrome. Salmonella surveillance increased to include many of the perishable food samples sent to the laboratory. 

The Pesticides Laboratory implemented a new procedure that is specific for organophosphate and developed a procedure for determining hericide residue levels in water. 

The total number of specimens examined in the Diagnostic Microbiology Laboratory is essentially the same as the past year. 

However, the number of parasitology specimens increased by 31% and the rate of positive recoveries was 30.8%-one of the highest in the nation. The laboratory also recovered 176 salmonella, 17 shigella, and 9 enteropathogenic E. coli. Two bats were: found rabid of the 112 animals submitted for rabies examination.. 219

The Gonorrhea Screening Laboratory was the first laboratory on the east coast to isolate a penicillin resistant N. gonorrheae. • The workload in the Serology Laboratory has increased significantly, with syphilis serologies increasing by 3,500 specimens and rubella serologies by 3,700 specimens. In addition to the routine work, the Serology Laboratory evaluated new serological procedures and prepared specimens for the proficiency testing program. 

The Toxicology Branch experienced a 59% rise in the number of samples from the rape program and the paternity testing program. 

Further, this year marks the first year of dog racing in Rhode Island. Dog racing samples are three times the number of horse racing samples usually received.

Division of Licensure and Construction 

The goal of the division is to assure that the health and safety of the patients of health care facilities and agencies are maintained at the levels required by state and federal statutes and regulations. This is accomplished through licensure and/or certification and surveillance of the facilities and agencies and their programs. Highlights On 25 May 1977, the Director of Health, acting under authority granted to him by the General Laws, ordered all patients to be immediately removed from a licensed nursing home because conditions at the facility were such as "to constitute a present danger to the health, welfare and safety of the residents of the State of Rhode Island and in particular to the residents of that facility”. 

The order was appealed to the Superior Court and after a week of exhaustive testimony with daily coverage by the news media, the court upheld the department's original order. The remaining 

65 patients, all of whom were Medicaid supported, were transferred to other facilities by the Department of Social and Rehabilitative Services within 24 hours of the court order. All involved in the department were extremely pleased with the expeditious manner in which the entire incident was handled by the court and with the extreme cooperation of the Department of Social and Rehabilitative Services. Of course, all were elated that the patients were transferred to facilities where their needs would be more adequately met. • Decisions of the department were appealed to the Superior Court by three other nursing care facilities. In one case the department's decision was completely supported, i.e., the facility's license was revoked and its certification to the Medicaid Program was terminated. In the second case, the court supported the department's requirement that the facility have a sprinkler system but gave the owners an additional ninety-day extension in which to have it installed. The court has not yet heard the third appeal. • Medical and independent professional reviews, requirements of the Medicaid Program, were completed on all Medicaid certified skilled nursing and intermediate care facilities and the Institute of Mental Health. These reviews are conducted for each patient/resident who is supported to any extent by Medicaid funds. A team composed of a physician, a registered nurse and a social 221

worker interviews each patient/resident and reviews the records (medical charts, nursing notes, social services notes, etc.). The team then makes a recommendation that the person either continues to be properly placed or now needs a different level of care. The teams conducted 8322 such reviews and recommended to the Department of Social and Rehabilitative Services, the single state agency for Medicaid, that 1152 changes be made in levels of care.

Fifty-one skilled nursing facilities were certified for Medicare and/or Medicaid. These facilities have a capacity of 3489 skilled nursing beds and 1484 intermediate care beds. 

Fifty-three general intermediate care facilities with 2193 beds and five intermediate care facilities for the mentally retarded with 985 beds were certified for the Medicaid Program. 

Twenty-eight independent laboratories and 12 home health agencies were surveyed and certified as Medicare facilities. • Approximately 780 unannounced visits were conducted in the state's nursing and personal care homes as required by Chapter 

23-17.1 of the General Laws of Rhode Island. 

Over 100 facilities were visited and inspected to determine if they either were providing any health care or were subject to the provisions of the "Sheltered Care Home for Adults" legislation. The majority are now licensed by local communities as boarding homes but had at one time been licensed as nursing and personal care homes. To date, no facility has been identified as illegally provid ing health care services. Nine applications for certification as a sheltered care home for adults have been filed. • Six workshops, each lasting from one to three days, were conducted. Over 500 participants, including nursing home administrators, directors of nurses, aides, dietary staff members, physical and occupational therapists, pharmacists and surveyors attended.

999

Division of Management Services 

The division is responsible for the general management and internal administrative control functions of the entire department, including fiscal and personnel control. It implements departmental fiscal and personnel policies and has the responsibility for the collection of revenues, the control, accounting, and reporting of all expenditures. It initiates and coordinates all personnel activities and serves as a central repository for all personnel records. It prepares and implements budgets and work programs for all divisions. It accepts and fulfills all federal contracts and interagency agreements for the department. It has responsibility for procurement of supplies and equipment, control of inventory, and assignment and use of facilities. Fiscal and personnel policies and procedures for good administrative practice are formulated and effected by this division. Highlights Total receipts for the year amounted to $6,,216,241.68 from a variety of sources.

During the period, 24 employees were converted from non-status to status as a result of qualifying by examination. 

There were three competitive promotions of permanent employees from Promotional Lists. An additional 21 more employees were promoted provisionally pending qualification by examination.

Seven interviews of permanent employees by the Classification Section of the Division of Personnel resulted in reallocations to higher classes.

One of the newer Classifications was coded and incorporated into the official pay plan. 

Pay Plan Adjustments were effected for 140 Classes in the Classified Service. 223

Division of Medical Care Standards 

The division promotes the public health and welfare through development of an improved health care delivery system that is reorganized through exercise of the various regulatory authorities over the allocation of health resources to health care providers licensed by the department and through the promulgation of regulations and standards consistent with cost effective quality medical care and manpower usage. Highlights The division continued to staff the Health Services Council in the latter's role as advisor to the director on matters relating to health resource allocation and regulation of institutional providers. • During the fiscal year, 13 separate formal applications for special expenditures review were filed with the division in accordance with the provisions of Chapter 23-166 of the General Laws. The applications in total requested authorization for capital expenditures which exceeded $30 million. 

Decisions were rendered on ten applications for program and capital projects. Six applications were approved involving capital expenditures of $12,663,220 and additional annual operating expenditures of $2,091,924. One application was disapproved involving capital expenditures of $493,000 and additional annual opcrating expenditures of $341,950. In addition, one previously approved proposal was authorized additional annual expenditures of $12,800 while another previously approved proposal was modified, resulting in a decrease in capital expenditures of approximately $1.2 million and, in annual operating expenditures, of ap proximately $140,000. The Director of Health also mandated the undertaking of capital expenditures at one facility, on the basis of safety considerations, involving $255,767 in capital expenditures with an operating component of $27,770. 

During the past fiscal year, two applications for construction of nursing homes were submitted, one of which was approved at a total project cost and annual incremental operating cost of $1.6 million and $550,00 respectively, and one of which was withdrawn prior to the rendering of a final decision. 

Of the 49 nursing or personal care home projects originally exempted from the review and approval process, 13 projects, representing 1471 beds and estimated total costs of $31 million, were 224

abandoned; 8 projects were operational as of 30 June, 1977 providing 798 beds at an estimated cost of $16.3 million; and 28 facilities proposing an additional 2862 beds at a total cost of $65.0 million were in various stages of construction. ● During FY 1977, 34 nursing or personnel care home requests for approval of changes of a substantial nature were reviewed. Fifteen requests were approved: 7 for reclassification of level of care, 6 for adjustments in licensed capacity and 2 for transfer of ownership.

As required by regulations relating to administration of the Hill- Burton program, 14 hospitals and five skilled nursing facilities reported provision of $2,135,663 in uncompensated services for the fiscal year 1975-1976 and budgeted $1,245,681 in uncompensated services for 1976-1977.

The division provided staff assistance to the Health Services Council in the review of three capital development proposals which were voluntarily submitted. Although the voluntary review process lacks statutory basis and, thus, is not binding, it does represent an opportunity to influence the evolution of the health systein since such proposals will impact on the delivery of health care services as well as upon health care costs. In the aggregate, the three proposals involve capital expenditures exceeding $1.2 million.

Rules and Regulations were developed, promulgated and filed with the Secretary of State on 8 December 1976 for Sheltered 

Care Homes and on 28 February 1977 for Nursing and Personal

Care Homes. In addition, the Rules and Regulations for Hospital 

Licensure, section 113.0 Emergency Services were amended and filed with the Secretary on the 5th of May, 1977. Technical assistance was also provided in the development of rules and regulations to the Office of the State Medical Examiners, and to the Ad Hoc Committee on Midwife Licensure. The division served as a coordinating agent with other divisions within the department to avoid conflict and duplication of effort in setting rules and reguiations. In addition, rules and regulations are currently being developed in the areas of renal dialysis, health maintenance organizations, hospital licensure, and determination of need. 

As part of an educational program designed to increase cooperation and coordination among all the community hospitals in the 225

state, the division sponsored a symposium for hospital Boards of Trustees to review collectively the annual short and long range plans submitted by each individual hospital. The symposium represented an extension of the on-going hospital plan development process and provided a forum for promoting a community perspective relative to the allocation of finite resources. • In order to assess and to prioritize the merits of individual capital development proposals within a framework of community needs. and finite resources the division established an ad hoc commission to determine an appropriate level of capital expenditures. Also, staff assisted in reorganizing the Health Services Council review process in order to implement a community approach to future resource allocations, essentially involving a reduction in the number of committees with a focus on prioritization of applications submitted.

Division of Occupational Health 

The division conducts professionally staffed programs in two major areas-Occupational Health and Radiation Control. In addition, personnel of the division provide consultation and technical assistance on a variety of other public health concerns related to exposure to chemical, physical and biological hazards. Highlights 

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH 

● The division provided assistance to 257 Rhode Island workplaces in the identification, evaluation and control of health hazards. 

This required more than 1000 separate visits and contacts with these facilities. During these visits and contacts, employers, employees and other interested parties were assisted in a variety of ways. Emphasis continued to be placed on the requirements of 

OSHA regulations and proposed OSHA regulations. Assistance was also provided on methods of controlling health hazards in the workplace, including: the institution of medical programs for employees; the use of protective equipment; changes in work practices; environmental monitoring program; employee training programs; and, the installations of industrial ventilation systems and other engineering controls. 226

On occasions workplace industrial hygiene studies were conducted to assist in the evaluation of employee exposures to chemicals, noise and other hazards. In the course of these studies more than 700 samples were collected for analysis of hazardous agents. These samples were processed by the Health Department's 

Laboratory and by the laboratory of the National Institute for 

Occupational Safety and Health. 

The State Plan for Occupational Safety and Health has remained unapproved for several years. After consultation with the State 

Department of Labor, the division has recommended that the present plan be withdrawn and that alternative opportunities for the state's participation in the OSHA program be explored. 

The emphasis on health in the OSHA program continued to grow during the year. Although this has not been translated into comprehensive health standards at this point, it is expected that these will be shortly in place having a large impact on Rhode Island workplaces.

Medical services were provided for a number of employees of small businesses in Rhode Island during the year through a joint program conducted by the division and Rhode Island Hospital. 

A paper outlining the features of this program was presented at a national meeting on occupational medical programs in May 1977. 

It is hoped that this program will be able to be expanded during the coming year to accommodate the developing demand for employees medical services in Rhode Island.

Highlights 

RADIOLOGICAL HEALTH 

Activities during the year were directed toward the division assuming its role as the state Radiation Control Agency under the new Chapter 23-1.3 of the General Laws. Many developmental steps were addressed including the formulation of regulations for the x-ray control program. These are scheduled for adoption in the fall of 1977. It is expected that a registration and inspection program for x-ray facilities will be operational by the spring of 1978. Unfortunately, the agency will be unable to proceed with a regulatory program for radioactive materials during the coming year due to a failure to obtain sufficient funding for the additional manpower required. The new state Radiation Advisory Commission, which held its first meeting in May, has begun its review 227

of the agency's regulations prior to adoption. Pending the implementation of the x-ray control program, the division continues to provide information and direct assistance to Rhode Island users of radiation sources. These efforts are now heavily oriented toward the requirements of the upcoming regulations. • The division continued to cooperate with programs of the various federal agencies involved in radiological health and those of the 

Conference of Radiation Control Program Directors. An example of this cooperation is participation in the Nationwide Evaluation of X-Ray Trends (NEXT) program. This year, NEXT surveys involving 59 diagnostic x-ray facilities and 111 x-ray machines were conducted. Other radiation protection surveys were conducted of 118 facilities including 167 radiation sources. During the coming year the division plans to initiate a program to survey mammography machines and to assist these facilities in reducing the radiation exposure associated with mammography. • The division managed to maintain a capability for radiological emergency response and environmental radiation surveillance during the year despite the greatly increased demand on staff time in other areas. The "fallout episodes" of October and November of 

1976, which were the result of fallout from Chinese thermonuclear testing, were the first to cause elevated levels of environmental radioactivity since the early 1960's. These episodes received nationwide publicity and indicated a need for better state surveillance and interstate communications. • The division's inventory of x-ray facilities and x-ray machines indicates that there were 708 facilities and 1285 machines being used in the state as of 30 June 1977. This represents a net increase of 51 machines.

Highlights 

SUPPLEMENTAL ACTIVITIES 

The division continued to respond to requests for assistance in various areas related to exposures to chemical and physical agents which are not directly connected with its major functions. These activities become more difficult to maintain during the year due to pressing demands in the major areas. 228

Division of Professional Regulation 

The function of this division is to regulate the health oriented arts, callings, and professions within the jurisdiction by licensing, registering, investigating and carrying out of disciplinary actions with respect to some twenty-two (22) such professions or callings. The program objective of the division is to provide for the evaluation, certification, licensure and appropriate control of these practitioners for the benefit of the approximate 949,000 users of their services in this state and to protect these persons from unqualified, incompetent or unprofessional practitioners. Highlights There has been no change in the total number of Registered 

Nurse Programs in Rhode Island. Nationally, diploma programs continue to decline, and associate degree and bacculaureate programs are increasing. St. Joseph's Hospital School of Practical

Nursing terminated their program this year and graduated their last class of 43 students in June of this year. The total admissions to Registered Nurse programs was 751 and to Practical Nurse programs 155. 

The most significant activity which occurred during the year was the promulgation of rules and regulations by various Boards. requiring continuing education as a pre-requisite for licensure renewal. Legislation was enacted in 1976 mandating continuing education for medical and osteopathic physicians in accordance with regulations promulgated by the appropriate Board. Under said legislation all medical and osteopathic physicians must submit evidence of continuing education every third year after the 1977 renewal in order to maintain their license. Each applicant must include evidence of having completed a prescribed course of continuing education established by the appropriate medical society and approved by rules and regulations of the Board. The 

Board of Examiners in Medicine accepted the recommendation of the Rhode Island Medical Society and adopted regulations requiring all physicians to submit evidence of 60 documentable credits of continuing education every three years. The Board of 

Examiners in Osteopathy accepted the recommendation of the Rhode Island Society of Osteopathic Physicians and Surgeons and adopted regulations requiring 75 hours of continuing education every three years. All physicians were notified of this requirement. 229

Legislation was also enacted in 1976 authorizing the Board of Examiners in Optometry to require continuing education as a condition for re-licensure. The Board promulgated regulations requiring evidence of 8 hours of continuing education each year. All regulations were promulgated after a public hearing and it is noteworthy that few, if any, objections were voiced at these hearings. The amendment to the law requiring continuing education for optometrists did not include this requirement for opticians; however, the opticians agreed to voluntarily attend continuing education courses. Legislation was enacted by the 1977 session of the General Assembly requiring Chiropractors to present 12 hours of continuing education each year in order to maintain li censure. The Board of Nurse Registration and Nursing Education submitted legislation to require continuing education for both registered and practical nurses. However, this legislation did not secure passage. Legislation was also introduced to require continuing education for Psychologists but this did not secure pas sage. The Board of Examiners for Nursing Home Administrators has recommended to the Director of Health that legislation be submitted to require continuing education for this group of licensees.

The 1976 General Assembly enacted legislation requiring meetings of public bodies to be open to the public except in certain specified instances. All meetings conducted by the Boards within this division were held in conformity with the provisions of this law. 

The Conflict of Interest law effect 11 January 1977 resulted in the resignation of a number of Board members. All members of the 

Board of Examiners in Medicine and the Board of Examiners in Optometry submitted resignations to the Director of Health on the ground that in principle the members objected to the provisions of this law and in particular to the filing provisions of the law as constituting an invasion of privacy. Various members of other 

Boards also submitted resignations as a result of this law. 

The Board of Examiners in Dentistry pursuant to the authority vested in it by the 1976 amendment to the Dental Practice Act promulgated regulations relative to the duties that may be performed by a dental hygienist. The Board has currently under review regulations governing the duties of the dental assistant. 230

The Board of Examiners in Optometry updated its regulations and established minimum standards for optometric equipment. 

Once again legislation was introduced into the General Assembly which would have created examining Boards and licensure requirements for marriage counselors, occupational therapists, and dental laboratories and dental technicians. However, this legislation did not secure passage. 

Legislation creating the Board of Medical Review (which has jurisdiction over all complaints and charges of unprofessional conduct by physicians) became effective 1 September 1976. • The Board of Exxaminers in Optometry reviewed a number of complaints and resolved most of the matters without recourse to formal proceedings. The board was also confronted with much activity relative to advertising by opticians. 231

Public Health Nutrition Service 

This service works with public and private agencies to stimulate the incorporation of nutrition education and nutritional standards into health services and programs. It serves as a nutritional information resource for both the public and personnel in health, education, and social service agencies. The service's functions are consultative, administrative, educational, and informational. Highlights • Development of operational guidelines, certification procedures, administrative forms, educational pamphlets, publicity materials, and computerization of data for the Supplementary Feeding Program for Women, Infants, and Children (WIC Program) occupied a large part of the service's limited manpower hours this past year. This program now provides selected nutritious foods to 3,000 low-income, nutritionally-needed pregnant and postpartum women, infants and young children. Only about one fourth of potentially eligible participants can be enrolled, due to a low funding level for this preventive health program. 

The service provided technical consultation and consultation on program development to 46 separate private agencies and units of government. Of these, 22 agencies received consultation more than once, with 13 receiving continuous consultation throughout the year. • Career guidance and employment counseling for nutrition and dietetics students, and graduates have occupied an increasing amount of the service's time in the past year. The markedly increased number of students majoring in nutrition and dietetics, the scarcity of entrance level positions in these fields, the failure of certain federal agencies to set minimum professional standards for nutrition personnel hired with federal funds by community agencies, which often results in unqualified persons taking positions ordinarily filled by bachelor's level nutrition personnel, and the general underfinancing of nutrition programs throughout the state all contribute to a growing and serious employment problem for the entry level dietitian nutritionist. 

The service's 22 speaking engagements included 9 public education programs at the request of organizations, 11 in-service programs for health agencies, and 3 television and radio interviews. 233

• The service responded to 699 telephoned or written requests for nutritional information, 506 (72%) from the public and 193 (28%) from agency personnel. The increasing volume of calls from the public (109%) demonstrates a growing awareness of the importance of nutrition. Calls received highlight (1) a growing dissatisfaction with the amount and quality of instruction and assistance with prescribed therapeutic diets offered in the private physician's office (2) increasing skepticism on the part of the public that their physicians understand nutrition and (3) the scarcity of diet counseling resources in the community. In this regard, one of the two full-time professional diet counseling services in the state closed on June 20, 1977, after 7 years of continuous operation, due to lack of community financial support and inadequate physician usage. • The service distributed 40,515 nutrition pamphlets and other nutrition educational materials, an increase of 32%, via its centralized ordering system, waiting room displays, public and professional requests, and speaking engagements. Forty-two agencies ordered these educational materials. In addition, 13,659 diet sheets were ordered by 31 physicians and 23 health agencies. • Distribution of press releases, governmental regulations, copies of legislation, new publications, and special notices to key nutrition personnel throughout the state was continued, as was surveillance of, and response to, proposed legislation and regulations affecting nutritional health.

Division of Solid Waste Management 

'The purpose of this division is to provide for the regulation and control of all solid waste management facilities within the state and to administer the state's Solid Waste Disposal Grant-in-Aid Program to the cities and towns.

Highlights The division instituted comprehensive solid waste management facility rules and regulations and licensed the first solid waste management facility within the state. The division distributed $275,924 to the cities and towns within the state in support of their solid waste management operation. 234

Division of State Medical Examiners 

The division investigates all known or suspected homicides, suicides, accidents, drug and toxicologic related deaths which include those that are environmental and occupational, medically unattended deaths, and deaths that constitute the threat of an epidemic or in any other way endanger the public health and safety. This division also keeps complete records and presents such records as needed along with expert testimony in court.

Highlights Approximately 3200 medico-legal deaths were reported for investigation. Of these cases reported, 750 were transferred to the 

State Morgue for further examination, 400 of which required autopsy. • The approximate number of deaths reported increased from the preceding fiscal year by 200 cases; the total number of cases transferred to the State Morgue increased from the preceding fiscal year by 25 cases; and the total number of autopsies increased by 

100 cases. There were 142 traffic fatalities, 109 suicides and 51 homicides during the past fiscal year. • All cremations that take place in the state were investigated. A certificate of cremation was issued to the funeral director to accompany the body to the crematory before cremation could take place.

Division of Vital Statistics 

The purpose of this division is the registration, collection, preservation, amendment, and certification of vital statistics records and activities related thereto, including the tabulation, analysis, and publication of statistical data derived from such records. This division supervises the vital statistics activities of the 39 local town and city clerks, funeral directors, and hospital personnel. Highlights The number of births increased three per cent over fiscal 1976. 

The rate was 12.3 per 1,000 estimated population. • Marriages increased seven per cent when compared with 1976. 

The 1977 rate was 7.6 per 1,000 estimated population. 235

The number of final divorces filed increased for the ninth consecutive year. The 1977 rate was 4.0 per 1,000 estimated population. 

Deaths declined by two per cent over the number that occurred in fiscal 1976. The 1977 rate was 9.8 per 1,000 estimated popu lation. 

Infant deaths (those occurring before the child's first birthday) and neo-natal deaths (those occurring before the 28th day of life) were 8 per cent and 1 per cent, respectively, below 1976 figures. 

Fetal deaths (products of 20 weeks or more gestation delivered without signs of life) decreased by 2 per cent over fiscal 1976.

Eleven years of birth records prior to 1901 have been repunched and merged indexes received which will greatly facilitate legal and genealogical searches. A federal contract, awarded in June, 1974, made possible the installation of key punch equipment. 

The ten leading causes of death in calendar year 1976 to Rhode Island residents accounted for 86 per cent of all resident deaths. 

Table III shows the number of deaths and the rates per 100,000 population. The figure that follows displays the rates for the ten leading causes in 1976 over a ten-year period for Rhode Island residents. 236

Division of Water Pollution Control 

The primary responsibility of the division is to control and abate sources of water pollution. To this end, the division is divided into four functional sections: Industrial Wastes & Oil Pollution, Water Quality, Municipal Wastes, and Individual Sewage. Analysis of waste and water samples is performed by the Water Laboratory of the Division of 

Laboratories. 

Highlights East Providence recently expanded and upgraded its wastewater treatment facilities. The treatment facilities are designed to accept all the wastewater generated in Barrington as well as the southern areas of East Providence. Extensive sewer construction is currently underway in Barrington. 

• The South Kingstown-Narragansett regional wastewater treatment facility employing the activated sludge process has been completed. 

Additional intercepting sewer and pumping stations and force mains in South Kingstown are expected to be completed in early 

FY '78. The pumping station and force main in Narragansett to divert the sewage from the Narragansett Pier area to the new facility has recently been completed. Separation of the combined sewers in Narragansett Pier has been completed. • Several projects are currently under construction in Newport, including construction of a microstraining unit to treat excessive flows from combined sewers, major pumping stations and force mains, and sewer separations. Final plans and specifications for the upgrading and expansion of the wastewater treatment facility have been completed and are currently under review. Plans and specifications for the second microstraining unit have also been submitted for review and approval.

Facility plans are currently being prepared for Providence, Paw tucket, Central Falls, Warwick and Bristol. The Facility Plan was completed for Tiverton. • Plans and specifications for the proposed Jamestown wastewater treatment facility, pumping stations and force mains have been submitted for review. • Plans and specifications for the upgrading and enlargement of the Scarborough wastewater treatment facility in Narragansett are 240

currently being prepared and will be completed in the very near future. • Johnston has currently begun a sewer system infiltration/inflow evaluation prior to the construction of interceptors to relieve subcharged sewer lines within the system. 

Plans and specifications for construction of wastewater treatment facilities for Burrillville have been completed and are currently under review. Construction has begun on the sewer system. 

Design for the upgrading of Warren's wastewater treatment facilities have begun. Construction has begun on the installation of an interceptor and pumping station to serve the northeast section of the town.

Federal and state grants will shortly be awarded to Cranston for the preparation of plans and specifications for the enlargement and upgrading of the existing wastewater treatment facilities, a major pumping station, force main and several interceptors. The facilities plan and Environmental Impact Statement were completed during the year.

Construction has nearly been completed on the New Shoreham sewerage system which includes a wastewater treatment facility, pumping station, force main and lateral sewers. 

Woonsocket wastewater treatment facilities are under construction along with an interceptor along the Blackstone River to serve 

North Smithfield and Blackstone and Bellingham, Massachusetts.

A major construction program has begun in Smithfield which includes a wastewater treatment plant and many miles of intercepting sewers and lateral sewers. It is expected to be in operation in early summer of 1978.

Westerly has begun construction on the enlargement and upgrading of the existing wastewater treatment facilities along with interceptors, pumping stations and force mains. 

North Smithfield is currently advertising for bids for the construction of intercepting sewers and a pumping station and force main. 

A contract award will be made in late FY '77. The collected sewage will be diverted to the Woonsocket Sewer System for treatment and disposal. 241

Diversion of the wastes into municipal sewer systems were made from several companies and waste discharges were eliminated from several others. Also, improvements to existing treatment facilities were made to several industries.

Assistance to the Coastal Resources Management Council in the developing management plan for the state's coastal waters is continuing. In addition, review of all minor NPDES permits has been completed. • An intensive sampling survey of the Blackstone River will be completed in the summer of 1977 in cooperation with Massachusetts. An intensive sampling survey was conducted of the south branch and main stem of the Pawtuxet River in August 1976. A shoreline survey was conducted in March 1977 of the Pt. 

Judith Pond (Galilee) area in preparation for reclassification of the waters in the area.
