Division of Water Supply 

The primary responsibilities of the division are to assure that public drinking water supplies are safe for human consumption and public swimming pools are maintained in a safe and sanitary condition. Highlights Rhode Island, through the Division of Water Supply, progressed toward meeting federal requirements pertaining to public drinking water system so that Rhode Island may qualify for the primary enforcement role under Public Law 93-523, the Safe Drinking Water Act. In working toward this goal, Rhode Island's statute pertaining to public water supplies was significantly amended, and the first set ever of drinking water regulations for Rhode Is land were put into a draft form for promulgation in the near future. A public hearing on the proposed regulations was held on 

6 June 1977. A grant in the amount of $170,600 was awarded to 

Rhode Island by the U. S. Environmental Protection Agency dur ing the year for Rhode Island's Public Water System Supervision 

Program. 

• Management of public water systems in Rhode Island were advised on numerous occasions as to their responsibilities under the Safe Drinking Water Act. Also, the assistance which will be rendered by the Department of Health in meeting these responsibilities were made known. A one-day seminar was held for waterworks management and government officials at which various provisions of the impending public drinking water surveillance program were reviewed. • Personnel of the division expended a considerable amount of time and effort in dealing with a pronounced taste and odor prob- 243

lem in the drinking water supplied to the communities of Barrington, Bristol, and Warren, through the Bristol County Water

Co. distribution system. Substantial improvements were made in the company's treatment facilities and management practices as requested by the division.

Another well site in the Manville section of Cumberland was approved. A well site for the Shady Harbor Fire District in 

Charlestown was also approved during the year. 

Plans and specifications of major improvements to the Ladd School water distribution system were approved. • Regular sanitary inspections of public water system treatment facilities were routinely made during the year. Public drinking water samples collected regularly by division personnel before, during, and after various stages of treatment were submitted routinely to the department's water testing laboratories throughout the year for bacterial, chemical, and radiological analyses. A total number of 3737 such samples were collected during the year. • Numerous persons served by a private household well were given advice on matters relating to their drinking water. 

During the period 1 July 1976-30 June 1977, 165 licenses were issued by the department to operate 75 indoor and 129 outdoor public swimming, wading, and therapeutic pools which were inspected regularly throughout the year by representatives of the division. A pool water sample for a bacterial examination in the department's water testing laboratory was collected routinely each time an inspection was made. A total number of 1839 such samples were collected and examined during the year. Only one license was required where more than one pool was maintained on a single piece of property by the same owner. Three stateowned swimming pools were also included in the department's inspection program even though state laws exempts state-owned pools from the license requirement. 

Plans and specifications of five new swimming pool facilities were approved during the year. 244

NORTH KINGSTOWN—Saunderstown, R. I.-Gilbert Stuart Birth place. Stuart, born here in 1755, was a noted colonial portrait artist who painted the first five Presidents and was the foremost painter of George Washington. The Snuff Mill, the first in America, was built in 1751.

Apprenticeship Council

The prime objective of the Council is the development of a cooperative relationship between employers and employees for the purpose of providing apprenticeship training.

Highlights Seventy-one newly established programs were ratified by the Council in the fiscal year. This was an increase of 7% from the previous year. Twenty-five programs were cancelled due to either poor economy or business closings. Three hundred and fifty programs were considered active training establishments. This was an increase of 30 active programs from the previous year when 320 programs were considered active. Four hundred and sixty-nine new apprentices were registered and entered apprenticeship training. One hundred and forty-one completed their training. There were 161 cancellations in this fiscal year. This was a gain of 7% over the previous fiscal year on apprentice registration. Most building trades in the state again either curtailed or accepted few- 

(*) Involves Workmen's Compensation/State Employees' Fund. 

247

er apprentices due to the poor economy in construction work. 

The number of veterans in apprenticeship training totalled 486 trainees. Minorities in training totalled 152 trainees. Seven minorities were cancelled. Twenty-five new females were also indentured into apprenticeship for a total of 55 females in training. There were 17 approvals for new on-the-job training programs for specific jobs that require two years or less or a total of 44 OJT programs. Thirty-seven new apprenticeship programs for veterans were also approved for a total of 120 approved training programs for veterans.

Two coordinators of apprenticeship in the Council participated in career days at public high schools and other facilities to advise youth of the apprenticeship opportunities available in the state. • The new apprenticeship/vocational film in cooperation with the Department of Education has now been shown at all middle, junior and senior high schools throughout the state. This film is also available for use by industry and other agencies to promote and develop more apprenticeship opportunities. 

The most significant accomplishment of the Council was the increase of female and minority participation in apprenticeship programs through the well defined and strict affirmative action of the adopted State Plan of Apprenticeship Council of September 

14, 1971. The Rhode Island State Plan adopted and implemented new regulations which require each apprenticeship sponsor to adopt objective standards for a selection of apprentices that comply with the State Plan. No complaints of discrimination in approved apprenticeship programs in Rhode Island were registered. • A trades skill survey also was conducted by the Council to project the needs of industry in five years. This survey was directed not only to service presently registered program sponsors but to solicit and encourage participation of all industrial groups not yet participating in an apprenticeship training program. Personnel of this division also spoke to civic, social and business groups to promote and advise on apprenticeship training. CETA funds are being requested through "Manpower Affairs" for apprenticeship related activities. 

• The new federal regulations Title 29 CFR Part 29-"Labor Stan dards for the Registration of Apprenticeship Programs" are in the process of being documented. The new regulations require that a currently recognized council desiring continued recognition by the Bureau of Apprenticeship and Training and must be in conformity with the regulations by July 18, 1977. 248

Dr. John E. Donley Rehabilitation Center 

The Center was established in 1943 for the purpose of making available all the resources of modern medicine for the rehabilitation of men and women injured in the course of their employment. Highlights A total of 257 patients received 9051 physical therapy treatments and 5604 occupational therapy treatments. There were 88 more patients treated this year over last. More doctors are referring their patients to the Center than before and the insurance industry is showing greater interest in the services provided by us. This increase in patient caseload reflects the growing awareness by everyone concerned of the need to reduce Workmen's Compensation medical costs. Increased case load reduces the Center's expenses in treating patients. By full utilization of the Center's services medical expenses can be drastically reduced. 

Of 191 patients discharged, 101 were able to return to their former or similar employment. Sixty-five patients were sent back to their referring physician for further advice, surgery or treatment. Twenty-five patients were discharged for inattendance. As a result of the Center's efforts, more than 50% of all patients treated during the year were successfully rehabilitated and returned to work. 

The figure is significant since a substantial number of patients referred to the Center have had their injury for months and sometimes a year or more. Best results are generally obtained when a patient is referred to the Center as soon as possible after the injury. 

The Center's Advisory Board held two meetings during the year, at which time, two recommendations were adopted. The first sets the treatment fee at $1.00 per day until March, 1979. The second requires the Center's surplus fund be kept intact until it has accumulated $2,000,000.00, at which time there will be a review of the Center's finances.

The Division of Vocational Rehabilitation (DVR) provided, without cost to the Center, all services necessary to evaluate and vocationally re-train any patients whose disability prevented their return to their employment.

The Workmen's Compensation Law provides that every insurer and self-insurer shall pay into the Curative Center Fund 112% of the gross premiums received from Workmen's Compensation insurance. Some 256 insurers paid a total of $553,728.32; 50 self-insurers paid $26,051.39. Interest from investment was $59,102.-

Many patients were advised to submit applications for the federal social security disability program. Many were eligible and are now receiving payments to supplement their workmen's compensation payments. The Center also referred patients to various other state departments for public assistance, food stamp programs, or other programs. 

Investigators visited 2272 places of business throughout the state to determine compliance with provisions of the Workmen's Compensation Laws. Twenty-eight employers were found in violation of the provision which provides for an employer of four or more workmen to be subject to the Workmen's Compensation Laws. 

After reasonable notice was given, each employer complied.

Hoisting Engineers Licensing Division The principal functions of the division are as follows: 

—Licensing and regulating of hoisting engineers. -Inspection of all industrial plants to see that all operating engineers employed by them are licensed. -Investigation of all road construction by private construction companies to insure the employment of only licensed operators. -Issuance of specialized licenses in all plants using hoisting machinery to operators in order to limit them to the operation of engines in which they are skilled. -Cooperation with the administration of the safety code dealing with precautions to be taken in the proximity of overhead high voltage lines for the prevention of accidents. 

A Board of Examiners meets as frequently as is necessary to properly conduct its work without delay, and so far as possible act upon all applications within one month of the date received and the chief of the division acts as an investigator with respect to the enforcement of all provisions of law relative to the licensing of hoisting engineers. Highlights Forty violations were reported by the Division of Occupational Safety for non-compliance with the hoisting engineers laws and the safety code high voltage line laws. The chairman and members of the Board were contacted and after proper investigation and inspection of the violations, the owners of the equipment agreed to comply with the law. Throughout the year this program was so successful on an industry-wide basis that the safe operation of engines and competent handling by licensed operators was assured. 

Two safety investigations were made during the year at General Dynamics. • During the fiscal year there were four examinations conducted by the Board with 40 applicants. There were 32 original licenses issued and 192 licenses renewed. 251

Division of Labor Relations 

The principal functions of this division are to: -Furnish free voluntary conciliation service for the mediation and settlement of work stoppages. -Administer the state anti-agediscrimination law. -Investigate, determine, establish, promulgate and enforce the prevailing wage rates and fringe benefits to be paid by contractors and subcontractors on publicly financed state and municipal public work projects and by printers providing state printing services. -Handle complaints of individuals or groups pertaining to all aspects of state labor laws and labor relations situations. 253

-Furnish information including legal opinions to employees, employers, and others concerning all phases of state labor laws and labor relations situations. -Co-operate with state and federal agencies in handling of labor cases falling within the coverage (or possible coverage) of both the state and federal labor jurisdictions. -Furnish department officials through the legal officer, legal advisory services such as the new laws on prevailing wage, minimum wage, OSHA, work permits and Sunday sales. -Draft labor acts (or amendments to existing labor laws) for introduction in the General Assembly. 

Highlights • A total of 20 work stoppages took place involving 4,008 workers, and resulted in 73,158 employee-days of lost working time. Total employee-days of lost time represented less than 1/10 of 1% of the total number of man-days worked during the year by all employed workers, exclusive of the self-employed, domestic and farm labor, and military personnel. This figure is one of the lowest figures in recent years. This compared favorably with a national average of 14 of 1%.

The two most serious strikes that occurred were Taco, Inc., which started on January 5, 1976 and ended on March 14, 1976 when the union was decertified. It involved 91 employees for 174 days into the 1976-1977 fiscal year. The other strike involved the Guardian 

Alarm Company which started on February 15, 1977 and has run 94 days into the current fiscal year and has not yet been resolved. 

Approximately 860 permits were granted allowing work to be performed on Sundays and holidays to various firms. • Rhode Island has long maintained an effective voluntary mediation service to keep the occurrence of strikes and their duration to a minimum. Mediators handled 246 Taft-Hartley notices including about 165 formal meetings and 49 informal meetings. Of the total number of disputes only five resulted in strikes.

Numerous requests for information were processed during the fiscal year relating to the entire field of labor law and labor relations. 

The division distributed printed summaries of labor laws upon request and collaborated in the preparation of material on changes in existing law as well as a number of new laws that have been enacted during the last session of the legislature, to be included in the next issue of the Digest of Labor Laws. 254

'The division published a number of new or revised pamphlets and bulletins, each of which explains a major labor law or is a compilation of administrative rules useful in making employers, workers, and others familiar with state labor laws. 

The division investigated, determined, and promulgated a list of prevailing wage rates covering scores of different crafts employed in the building and construction industry to be used when contracts for a public works projects amount to $1,000 or more. Lists were revised and kept up to date. Additionally the division ascertained whether or not certain prevailing fringe benefits were an established feature in any particular craft. 

Upon request of an "interested person", investigations were made of the payrolls of the firms awarded a particular public works contract to determine compliance in matters of wage payments to employees. During the year many such payroll examinations were made, most of which necessitated hearings and collection proceedings. Bulletins containing the new applicable law together with a letter explaining the provisions of the law were sent to all of the cities and towns in the state. 

Staff advisory service projects during the year included the drafting and conducting of public hearings on the amendments of rules and regulations involving the Industrial Code Commission and the Mercantile Division establishing a new metrology laboratory. 

Labor Relations Board 

The Labor Relations Board provides that employees shall have the right of self-organization, to form, join, or assist labor organizations, to bargain collectively through representatives of their own choice, and to engage in concerted activities for the purpose of collective bargaining and other mutual aid or protection, free from actual interference, restraint or coercion by employers. The methods and procedures for the protection of these rights and for the determination of appropriate bargaining units are provided for in the Act. 

The State Labor Relations Act established the Board which con sists of three members, one representing industry, one representing labor and one representing the general public. This Board administers the provisions of the Act, and all such members of the Board are appointed by the Governor with the advice and consent of the Sen- 255

ate. The Act defines and specifically prohibits nine unfair labor prac tices by employers which experience has proved interfere with the rights of employees to organize or to bargain collectively. 

Two separate and distinct administrative procedures are established by the Act; first, unfair labor practices proceedings for the prevention and/or redress of interference with the rights to self-organization guaranteed by the Act to employees; and second, representation proceedings for ascertaining the wishes of employees with respect to their collective bargaining representative. Highlights • To date some 2,631 municipal employees, 4,117 state employees and 1,326 teachers have participated in numerous elections to determine their collective bargaining representative. This necessitated 81 elections being held which requires numerous and varied clerical procedures including the preparation of notices of election, the posting of the place of the election and arrangements for suitable quarters and hours of the election which would be most convenient relative to participation by all eligible employees. Approximately 71% of the elections resulted from consent agreements, the other 31% requiring detailed and numerous hear ings by the full Board and a detailed written decision and order. 

Despite the ever-increasing workload of both the Board, its investigators and clerical help, there remains numerous pending petitions and approximately 2,288 state and municipal employees are still awaiting the opportunity to be represented by a collective bargaining organization. 

It must be recognized that although most of the firemen, policemen, school teachers, state employees, municipal and county employees have participated in elections, the Board is constantly called upon to handle new petitions for recertification as most contracts expire either after one or two years, at which time some other labor organization usually intervenes requiring a formal hearing by the members of the full Board and a written decision setting forth its findings, conclusions of law and its order. 

The Board is also being called upon with increasing frequency to conduct hearings on unfair labor charges filed by collective bargaining representatives of these groups and those charges are requiring more time and attention then did the original petitions or the petitions for recertification. 256

T To date the Board has entertained petitions for certification involving more than 4,289 employees, and has resolved either informally or after formal hearings by written decision 97 unfair labor practices filed the last fiscal year. 

Division of Labor Standards 

The Division of Labor Standards was established in 1936 as the Division of Women and Children with the purpose of enforcing the laws governing the hours of work and conditions of labor of women and minors, the minimum wages established in various occupations, and the control of industrial homework. The addition of other labor laws such as statutory minimum wage, payment and collection of wages, equal pay, and premium wages for Sunday and holiday employment provides the division with increased responsibilities and services to the entire labor force in Rhode Island. 

Audits and investigations are made on a routine or complaint basis in all manufacturing and non-manufacturing enterprises as well as state and local government agencies to determine compliance with these laws. Unpaid wages are collected from employers and distributed through the division to the wage earners. Audits are also made of businesses declaring bankruptcy or petitioned into receivership for the purpose of determining the unpaid wages, holiday and vacation. pay, insurance and retirement benefits due employees. Claims are filed in behalf of employees in State Superior Courts and Federal District Courts with proceedings monitored on an on-going basis by division staff to protect the employees' interests.

Flagrant violations and unwillingness on the part of an employer to make restitution or agree to compliance result in the issuance of summons and arraignment in district court by the staff investigating auditor.

Under contract with the United States Department of Labor the division supervises and reviews the issuance by local school departments work permits and certificates of age required for the employment of minors by federal and state law. Additionally, the division issues permits for the employment of handicapped workers at less than the minimum hourly rate, issues licenses for the distribution of industrial homework and certifies the individual homeworker.

Primarily seeking compliance on the premise of educating employees and employers in their rights and responsibilities under the laws, 257

the division offers resource services as a social and economic data center. Staff members with expertise in labor laws, women's rights and cqual employment opportunities are available, upon request, for instructural lectures before business and industry groups, school assemblies and social organizations. Highlights • New Legislation-Amendments to the labor laws enforced by the division made in the 1977 session of the General Assembly have expanded coverage of the law governing the payment and collection of wages to include the 48,000 employees of the state and its political subdivisions. All employers are required to establish a regular pay day and notify employees in advance of any change in the schedule. Upon the request from an employee the employer must include an itemized statement of deductions made from gross earnings with the payment of wages. Employers may also enter into an agreement with an employee to deposit net wages in a checking, savings or share account in a financial institition selected by the employee. 

A Proof of Claim was filed in the United States District Court, Southern District of New York on behalf of former workers of W. T. Grant Company employed in Rhode Island. The division is seeking over a quarter million dollars in vacation and severence pay. At the initial hearing in April, Rhode Island was the only state labor department presenting a claim on behalf of the former employees of W. T. Grant stores. The case is still pending at the close of the fiscal year. 

The division collected and distributed, at no cost to the employee, $192,014 in wages due and unpaid to 2576 Rhode Island workers during the fiscal year. Over 45% of the monies collected ($87,964.) were for failure to pay premium wages for weekly overtime; unpaid wage and salary collections amounted to $70,139. or more than 36% of all monies collected. The balance of collections were for failure to pay for a minimum shift, basic applicable wage and premium rates for overtime. 

The field auditors made 3815 auditing and investigating inspections in business and industry. Over 72% (2767) of these investigations arose from complaints filed with the division. Operation with a reduced field staff continued to limit the routine compliance efforts through education of employers in the provisions of the laws. 258

Of the 809 individual complaints filed over 74% or 601 were in regard to unpaid wages and salaries. The remaining complaints filed concerned allegations of violations of the several other labor laws enforced by the division.

Work permits issued by the 39 school departments for youth 14 and 15 years old totaled 2857 or 11% more than in fiscal 1976. 

Certificates of age were issued for 9420 workers 16 and 18 years of age, an increase of nearly 12% over the prior year. Permits and certificates are reviewed in the division to determine adherence to the child labor laws. One hundred and seventy-one were found questionable and required an investigation of the job site. 

The division continued to monitor the issuance of licenses to employers of handicapped workers and individuals processing work. at home.

Technical and clerical assistance to the Advisory Commission on Women was continued. Office facilities were made available to two special projects of the Commission-A Dialogue with Low- 

Income Women and The State Convention for International Women's Year. 

Mercantile Division - Weights and Measures 

The Mercantile Division-Weights and Measures administers the laws and regulations applicable to the sale of coal and coke and the laws and regulations applicable to weights, measures, and balances. Highlights Coal and Coke Licenses: There were five first class licenses, four second class licenses, and one branch license issued during the year. The revenue collected for the licenses amounts to $85.00. 

During the year, five inspections were made pertaining to the sale of coal and coke; no violations were found.

Thirteen newly appointed town and city sealers received instructions and training by the division. 

The annual reports received from the town and city sealers show that they tested and sealed 4,763 scales and 4,013 meters during the year. Four percent of the weighing and measuring devices tested were in need of adjustments. Forty-seven of them were condemned and/or destroyed as being beyond repair. • In cooperation with the Department of Natural Resources and the Department of Economic Development scales were installed and calibrated for eight fishing tournaments throughout the state. 

A member of the division acted as official weighmaster for the tournament. 

At the request of the U.S. Navy, 20 scales were tested and sealed in Newport. Also tested and sealed for the U.S. Navy were two heavy duty platform scales. The U.S. Air Force refueling units were tested and sealed in Warwick.

At all the state institutions, the weighing devices in the receiving rooms and food dispensaries were tested and corrected when needed. 

Twenty-one types of scales were found to conform to standards and were permitted within the state as legal weighing devices after testing and sealing. 260

Approximately 21,000 pre-packaged items were checked for contents. As a result, some 2,800 items were ordered off sale until properly marked. • Deceptive packaging violations were found in the detergent, dairy, fruit, seafood industries resulting in merchandise ordered off sale and returned to the companies for correct packaging. • Seventy-five newspaper violations pertaining to the advertisement of packaged items were corrected. 

Forty complaints were settled by the division that were referred to it by the Rhode Island Consumer Council and the Attorney 

General's Consumer Unit. 

The administrator has been appointed to direct the voluntary conversion to the metric system by the Governor. At the request of the administrator, the Governor has appointed a metric coordinating committee; its purpose to educate the public and to assist industry with metric conversion. The division opened its 

Metrology Laboratory at 470 Allens Avenue, Providence. 

Permanent Advisory Commission on Women in Rhode Island 

The Permanent Advisory Commission on Women was created to serve as a continuing advisory body making recommendations for constructive action related to opportunities for education and training of women; measures to strengthen family life through programs and services; policies and practices in public and private employment and development opportunities for women; labor laws and laws providing for social insurance; citizens and volunteers; and legal rights of women. 

In 1976-77 the Advisory Commission on Women adopted the following priorities: monthly newsletter focusing on legislation in the areas of concern to Rhode Island women; study the needs of elderly women and make recommendations for needed support; monitor and initiate legislation; study and report on women offenders; monitor compliance with Title IX by the education subcommittee; implementing the work of other subcommittees. Highlights .

During 1976-77 the Commission displayed throughout the state the 27 photomontages of the Oustanding Women of the Year 

261

chosen during International Women's Year 1976. The photo montages will go on permanent display in the State House during 

International Women's Decade. 

A regular monthly newsletter provided a communications network to inform women of local, state and national activities important to them along with the monitoring of legislation of concern to them. The number of organizations and individuals requesting the newsletter is continually increasing. Currently 80 groups representing 20,000 women are being served by the Advisory Commission's publication. 

The Legislative/Legal Rights subcommittee concentrated its forces on the Domestic Assault Bill, Temporary Disability Insurance for pregnant women, inclusion of sex and marital status of fair housing legislation and a bill to establish a commission regarding displaced homemakers.

The computerized Talent Bank resource file continues to draw resumes from qualified women in the state. The Commission has used the Talent Bank to make recommendations to the Governor for women to serve on state Boards and Councils. 

Research on the status of the women's facilities at the Adult Correctional Institution continues. Areas of importance where reform is needed include: no separate budget allocation of funds for women, inferior housing, and inadequate recreational and rehabilitative programs. • Task Force on Aging studied the social security program as it relates to elderly women both as workers and homemakers. • The Commission published a Credit Information brochure for women entitled "Creditability".

One member of the Advisory Commission was elected Vice President of the National Association of the Commission on Women. 

In July, 1976 the Commission participated in a "Celebration for 

Women" in Newport. The one day event was sponsored by almost all of the women's groups in Rhode Island in an effort to provide a vehicle to educate the people of the state on the activities and purpose of the various women's groups. Despite rainy weather the event drew nearly 500 persons to the lawn of Salve Regina 

College. 

On August 26, 1976, Women's Equality Day was held on the

State House lawn co-sponsored by the Governor and the Advisory 262

Commission on Women. The event was attended by about 300 men and women.

The Education Subcommittee continued to monitor the implementation of Title IX, the purpose of which is to gain sexual parity in the state educational system. A Title IX conference was held at Bryant College in February, 1977 under the joint sponsorship of the Education Subcommittee, the Community Advisory Council and the EEO office of the Department of Education. The event was attended by over 80 school administrators. Three workshops were conducted: "How to Conduct a Self Evaluation", "Equality in Physical Education and Athletics", and "Overcoming

Sex Stereotyping in Counseling and Curriculum". Enthusiasm generated prompted planning for similar events. 

The Commission in conjunction with the Women's Political Caucus held a "Women's Forum" in Providence the week of March 

21-25, 1977. Approximately 300 persons participated in these forums each day. • On May 10, 1977 "A Dialogue with Low-Income Women", a conference on the economic and employment issues of low-income women was held in Providence and co-sponsored by the U.S. Department of Labor, Women's Bureau and the Advisory Commision on Women.

On June 11-12, 1977 the International Women's Year State Meet ing was held at Brown University. The members of the Commission worked in conjunction with the state convenor in coordinating the activities and workshops for the two day event. The event was attended by thousands of men and women from the State of Rhode Island who discussed issues such as the Equal 

Rights Amendment, Abortion, Day Care, Women in Business, 

Welfare, Employment of Women, Education and many more. 

14 delegates were elected to attend the National Conference in Houston, Texas. A report from each state will be compiled and a report with recommendations for constructive action will be sent to the President of the United States by the National International 

Women's Year Committee.

Industrial Code Commission 

The creation of the Occupational Safety and Health Commission insures the best of standards for the prevention of injuries in work places. 

In many instances in Rhode Island there are employers that have factories in other states. The Commission took this under consideration, and in 1974 adopted the Occupational Safety and Health Administration federal standards 1910 and 1926 so that the laws would be consistent with other states.

Highlights Sixty meetings were held during the year. • The Commission members assisted the Division of Occupational Safety in many areas involving amendments of standards that were outdated. The Commission members attended special seminars to improve information relative to amendment, and reviewed all standards during the year and suggested ways and means to eliminate repetition thereof.

Division of Occupational Safety 

This division's personnel assure that places of employment meet the requirements of the laws and safety standards pertaining to the health and safety of the state's work force.

Highlights There were 2044 industrial inspections resulting in 1043 compliance orders, most of which were abated immediately. Seventytwo police reported injuries were investigated. One hundred fortyfive complaints were received and investigated, and where necessary compliance orders were issued to eliminate hazardous conditions. 

Forty-two written complaints of health hazards were referred to the Department of Health. Two fatalities of different categories were reported by police departments. 

There were 593 general contractors. Construction sites were inspected regularly and contractors were made aware of the provis ions of the Occupational Safety and Health Administration 1926 

standard. One of the provisions is the responsibility of sub-contractors to comply with the standards as it relates to his employees. 

Compliance orders are issued directly to sub-contractors for compliance. 264

A total of 6057 unfired pressure vessels and boilers were inspected. 

There were 1093 certificates of operation for high pressure boilers. 

City, state and charitable institutions were inspected at no charge. 

One thousand six hundred forty-two unfired pressure vessels were inspected. 

One thousand nine hundred seventy-three vertificates of operation were issued for elevators, escalators and dumbwaiters; 689 compliance orders were submitted for corrective action. Thirtyseven applications were approved for new installations; 32 new installations were inspected and approved; 28 units were tagged "out of service" for non-compliance and/or non-use; 25 complaints. required investigation.

Division of Statistics and Census 

The division collects, compiles and publishes labor statistics, prepares data on economic subjects relating to labor and industrial conditions and issues state census information. This division works in cooperation with the United States Department of Labor in collecting employment data and follows the latest statistical methods developed by the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics. The division in its cooperating program with the U.S. Department of Labor, Bureau of Labor Statistics makes a monthly survey of approximately 1,400 business, industrial and government establishments and conducts the occupational injuries and illnesses survey in collaboration with the Bureau. 

The annual "Digest of Labor Laws," a pocket pamphlet, is compiled, edited and published by this division. This booklet contains a concise explanation of labor laws enforced by the department with the latest legislative changes and new administrative rules and regulations. Highlights Revised industry employment data, for Rhode Island (from 1972) and the Providence Metropolitan Labor Areas (from 1975) were issued in February, 1977, reflecting both a more current benchmarking as well as the results of a one-year effort to convert from the 1967 to the 1972 Standard Industrial Classification Manual. 

The employment data collected in monthly survey is compiled and published in cooperation with the Department of Employment Security in the "Employment Bulletin." The average annual employment in 1976 was 366,300. 265

✪ In November, 1976, when employment was at a peak, factory production workers in Rhode Island were averaging $169.15 per week and $4.25 per hour. The factory work week in November averaged 

39.8 hours.

In the calendar year of 1976, average weekly earnings of production workers were $163.93, an increase of $14.89 over 1975. Average hourly earnings in 1976 were $4.15 per hour, an increase of $0.31 per hour. Average weekly hours worked were 39.5, an increase of .6 of an hour from average hours in 1975. 

Analysis of major manufacturing industries revealed that the ratio of women workers showed mixed trends in this fiscal year but varied only fractionally from recent years. In the state's total employment women made up 43.0% of the total, 44.1% in nonmanufacturing and 40.9% in manufacturing. Women production employees worked on 29% of the jobs in the durable goods segments and 49.3% in the non-durable goods segments. Among these industries women made up 70.8% of apparel, 58.2% of jewelry manufacturing, 56.9% of leather products work force, 50.5% of electrical machinery work force, 46.2% of textile, 45.9% of instrument manufacturing and 43.9% of the rubber-plastics work force.

Job opportunities in non-manufacturing industries continued to expand. Analysis of non-manufacturing industries indicates the following estimates of female employment.

The average U.S. Consumer Price Index for calendar year 1975 was 161.2% of the base period. The average C.P.I. for calendar 266

year was 170.5%, a gain of 5.8% over the previous year's average-a drop of 3.3% from last year's increase of 9.1%.

At the beginning of this fiscal year in July, 1976, the Consumer 

Price Index was at 171.1% and rose to 180.6% in May of 1977, an increase of 5.2%. Last year's increase for the same period of time was 5.4%. Simplified, these statistics mean that for each $10.00 a worker's family spent in the base period 1967 it would now cost $18.06 to buy the same goods and services. • A report on the statistical data on occupational injuries and illnesses in 1975 gathered in the 1976 OSHA survey, has been submitted to the Director of Labor and sent to the Boston office of OSHA. In February, 1977, OSHA survey forms were mailed to employers. The 1976 statistical data on occupational injuries and illnesses gathered is presently being compiled and analyzed. 

On an OSHA Quality Assurance Program a sample number of business establishments were visited for a detailed check of their occupational accident and illness reports. 

Occupational injuries continue to occur in Rhode Island. The incidence rate for occupational injuries and illnesses, which for 1975 was about the same as that for the nation as a whole, showed a slight, though perhaps significant, decrease in 1975. Division of Workmen's Compensation The division administers the Workmen's Compensation Act covering all phases of the law not involving litigation as to claims for disability compensation. These claims are the concern of the Workmen's Compensation Commission. The Division receives and reviews injury reports filed by employers. It reviews and approves agreements affecting compensation, maintaining index files relating to these documents. The Division maintains files concerning the issuance, renewal, or termination of workmen's compensation insurance policies. The Division is responsible for the administration of the Second Injury Indemnity Fund, collecting assessments due from insurers and self-insured employers and authorizing disbursements from the Fund to reimburse insurers for payments made to totally disabled persons as required by the law. The 

Division is the delegated agent of the Attorney General in the disposition of claims of state employees arising under the Act. Highlights Certificates of issuance of policies written by insurers on Rhode Island employers for the fiscal year totalled 13,835. 

Voluntary acceptances filed by employers employing less than four persons totalled 151.

There were 578 reservations of right of action filed during the course of the year. This action enables small employers to reduce their annual premiums to some extent, and this number is the largest filed in any year for many years. ⚫ There were 28,700 injury reports filed. Compensation cases totalled 6846. • There were 10,594 agreements affecting compensation filed with the division during this period. These included 3,084 suspension agreements. 

The Workmen's Compensation Commission forwarded 1,491 de crees to this division during the fiscal year. • The State Employees' program received 1,916 injury reports. These were categorized as follows: 684 compensable claims; 164 assault claims; 1,068 medical expenses only.

The Second Injury Indemnity Fund balance as of May 1, 1977 was 

$284,562.59. 270

DEPARTMENT OF STATE LIBRARY SERVICES JEWEL DRICKAMER, Director Established 1964-Statutory Authority, Chapter 29-31 

Central Office-95 Davis Street, Providence 02908 Full Time Employees 

Total Expenditures (does not include funds committed to public library construction projects) 31 $1,617,960.08 

A seven member Advisory Board of Library Commissioners advises the director, functions as a board of appeal on library services and construction, acts as a board of review and appeal on library standards and serves as a representative of the people by securing a comprehensive program on library service in the state. 

In addition to the board, a State Advisory Council on Libraries ful fills requirements of the Federal Library Services and Construction Act, as amended, and assists the department in its Five Year Long-Range Program. Its 25 members include: representatives from libraries serving the handicapped and public, school, academic, special and institutional libraries; users including the disadvantaged; and youths from a high school, a college and the University of Rhode Island Graduate Library 

School. 

The purpose of the department is to improve library services so that citizens may have access to all forms of information. Charged by law to coordinate library resources on a cooperative basis throughout the state, the department in 1968 established a network of five Interrelated Library Systems as a means of accomplishing this goal. In administering the statewide library program, the department has responsibility for the total public library construction program, library development, regional services to the blind and physically handicapped, and library services for the state institutions. Additionally, planning, accounting, budgeting and reporting are carried on by the department. 273

Division of Administrative Services 

Highlights At state and national levels planning was initiated for a Governor's Conference on Library and Information Services to be held in 1978 and to be followed by a White House Conference on 

Library and Information Services in 1979. 

The Affirmative Action Plan of the department was updated and the concept was extended to the major public libraries which serve the statewide library network: libraries at Providence, Pawtucket, Warwick, Barrington, and Westerly.

Public Library Construction continued with the completion of the renovation of the Warwick Public Library. The following cities and towns received annual state payments toward public library construction: Cumberland, $20,760.99; Newport, $29,954.27; North

DEPARTMENT OF MENTAL HEALTH, RETARDATION AND HOSPITALS JOSEPH J. BEVILACQUA, Ph.D., Director Established 1970-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-12

4,601 $83,369,544

A variety of health care and related services are available to Rhode Island citizens through the department. The Divisions of Mental Health, Retardation, Rehabilitative Services and Substance Abuse are the four principal service agencies of the department. Supporting these program divisions are the Division of Management Services, Division of Facilities and Maintenance Services and the Division of Planning, Budgeting and Program Evaluation. 

Major facilities, operated by the department, include the Institute of Mental Health and General Hospital at the Medical Center in Cranston, Zambarano Memorial Hospital in Burrillville and Ladd School in Exeter. Besides operating these institutions, the department funds and licenses community mental health, alcoholism, drug abuse and retardation services for children and adults. 

While major departmental programs in terms of staff and funds are devoted to institutional programs for people with a variety of emotional, neurological and physical handicaps, and disabilities, the department's policies, plans and goals emphasize rehabilitation and development of community treatment and rehabilitation programs that will prevent or replace the need for long-term institutional care. 281

Office of Director 

Supporting the seven main divisions at the director's level are the staff functions of legal counsel, public information and licensure and standards. 

OFFICE OF LEGAL COUNSEL 

The Office of Legal Counsel represents the department in all litigation or legislation matters. The office provides labor relations, consultation, legal research and consultation pertaining to laws affecting department program operations. It also drafts contracts for purchase of services and legislation required to implement department program plans. 

The office also represents the department before the courts involving people served by department programs. Highlights A full time attorney was added to the legal counsel staff. This gives the department greater capability to respond to all legal matters confronting the department, and maintains closer relationships with the courts, the Attorney General's Office and legislative commissions.

OFFICE OF LICENSURE AND STANDARDS 

All department funded or directly operated programs, the facilities of which are not part of a hospital (licensed by the Health Department), are licensed through this office. Responsibilities of this function include preparation of program and facility standards for group homes for alcoholics, mentally retarded children and adults, former patients of psychiatric hospitals and emotionally disturbed children; communitybased drug abuse programs; and rehabilitation centers for mentally retarded adults.

INFORMATION AND PUBLIC RELATIONS 

The staff office coordinates the research and release of information concerning department policies and programs by providing a central source of information for newsmen, distributing news to the media and arranging and conducting press conferences. The unit also publishes newsletters for employees and persons interested in department programs. It also investigates many complaints received by the department and participates in planning and implementation of special events and programs. 282

Highlights The Public Information Office, in cooperation with other department agencies, began to implement a service of special events designed to draw public attention to the major renovations now underway both at the Medical Center and at Ladd School. 

Division of Facilities and Maintenance Services 

It is the responsibility of this division to provide support services to the other divisions within the department. It also provides some services such as utilities and limited maintenance to the Department of Corrections. The division is composed of five sections: the Office of Assistant 

Director which oversees the overall operation of the division as well as providing engineering and technical assistance to the major program agencies within the department, and oversees the Capital Development Program; the Maintenance Section which handles the preventative maintenance program for all facilities on the Cranston complex and provides minor renovations; the Transportation and Grounds Section which provides the transportation network for the facilities at the Cranston complex and maintenance of the grounds themselves; the Central Power 

Plant which provides major utilities for the entire Cranston complex including the Department of Corrections; the Property Police Section which provides the general security for the Cranston complex and on occasion emergency transportation and services to the General Hospital and the Institute of Mental Health. 

Highlights Steam and condensate return lines were replaced in the Howard Avenue section of the Medical Center as the first phase of a fuel conservation program. Phase two of this program, through which lines to the Central Power Plant will be replaced, was also started. 

Renovations of the "I" Building of the General Hospital were completed and this facility was reopened. 

Renovations in the "H" Building, Varley and Mathias Buildings were started as well as renovations to the Dix, Pinel, Meyer and 

Hazard Buildings. 

Renovations were completed on the five cottages and in the Fogarty Building at Ladd School and the contract to renovate the Rehabilitation Building at Ladd was awarded. 283

• Improvements have been made to IMH and General Hospital in order to meet the standards of the Joint Commission on Accreditation of Hospitals.

Division of Management Services 

This division is responsible for proving certain supportive services to other divisions within the department and to the Department of Social and Rehabilitative Services and Corrections. 

ADMINISTRATIVE SERVICES 

The administrative functions of Administrative Services include Em ployee and Labor Relations, Facilities Construction and Dietary Ser vices.

Employee and Labor Relations: This unit processes all personnel ac tions and develops intradepartmental personnel procedures and management policies. This unit also meets employee representatives and represents the department before the State Labor Relations Board, Personnel Appeals Board and Labor Relations Administration. 

Facilities Construction: The coordination of expenditures of bond issue funds for the development of community based facilities is accomplished through this staff function. In 1969, $6.5 million dollars was approved for community based retardation facilities and another $11 million dollar bond issue was approved in 1972 for both community facilities and institutional development. A $15 million bond issue which was for similar projects was approved in 1974. A bond issue approved last year provided additional funds for community based retardation programs. 

Dietary Services: The unit supervises the preparation and distribution of food to all department institutions. This involves the establishment of adequate nutritional standards, preparation of menues for each institution, establishment of sanitation procedures, preparation of all budgets and maintenance of central records. 

FINANCIAL AND MANAGEMENT SERVICES 

Financial and Management Services is a central service agency composed of four major sections. The Central Management Section provides both technical and administrative management support for agencies within the department. The Budgetary, Accounting and Control Section is responsible for the execution of the department's annual budget.

284

Operating agencies within the department are provided with budgetary and statistical reports on a periodic schedule. The Patients' Resources and Benefits Unit is responsible for investigating the resources of patients to pay for services and care provided to them at the Institute of 

Mental Health, Center General Hospital, Zambarano Memorial Hospital and the Ladd School. The Rate Setting and Hospital Cost Section de velops, implements and enforces "Principles of Reimbursement" and rate payment schedules. Cost studies, research and auditing procedures are utilized by this section to administer its functions which include not only state operated hospitals but also regional centers for the mentally retarded and group home facilities. Another function is to develop and maintain cost accounting systems for the Institute of Mental Health, General Hospital, Zambarano Hospital and the Ladd School. 

SPECIAL FACILITIES 

The unit coordinates institutional supportive services. Consolidation of food processing, drug purchasing and laundry operations permits management to realize maximum production and efficiency at a minimum cost.

Central Food Plant: provides 15 agencies and institutions with bakery, meat and cooked food products. Additional products are developed on an ongoing basis, tested and added to the menu. 

Central Laundry: collects, processes and delivers all flatwork linen tor the General Hospital and the Institute of Mental Health. 

Central Pharmacy: provides drugs, medicines and medical supplies to the various state hospitals, institutions and agencies. The objective of this unit is to purchase, process and deliver these items at lowest possible cost through centralized volume purchasing. A federal government inventory of drugs and supplies available to the state in the event of an emergency is also maintained.

Highlights • New methods of production were introduced in the Food Plant and new freezer equipment installed which will increase freezing capacity, improve quality and reduce processing time for greater productivity. • The Central Pharmacy has continued implementation of a system for the purchase of certain medication by the generic rather than the brand name thereby realizing substantial savings to the state through more competitive bidding and the elimination of duplicate items. 285

Division of Mental Health 

Programs operated under or funded through the division provide care and treatment for persons who have emotional problems. The Institute of Mental Health, a psychiatric hospital at the Medical Center, is oper ated under this division. Other major divisional programs include Mental Health Services for Children and Youth, which has responsibility for planning and funding mental health services including residential treatment services, and community mental health services, which is responsible for the planning, funding and coordination of community mental health centers and other community based mental heal programs. 

OFFICE OF ASSISTANT DIRECTOR 

The major functions of this office are to foster development of a comprehensive and coordinated range of mental health services through development of an integrated mental health network; to coordinate program planning and administration of agencies within the division; to establish standards, information systems and other monitoring techniques to develop a base for guiding allocations of resources, improving program effectiveness and ensuring standards of quality in programs operated, provided or funded by the division; to implement policies and programs designed to address the intent of the state Mental Health Law; to identify needs for new legislation and regulations and to encourage effective adherence to existing legislation and regulations associated with the delivery of mental health services and to establish a mechanism for effective citizen and professional involvement in division planning, program development and evaluation. 

This office is responsible for overall direction of the major units within the division. Liaison with the Institute of Mental Health, Mental

Health Services for Children and Youth, Community Mental Health 

Centers/Clinics, and other community mental health agencies, is maintained by staff of this office. The liaison activities include technical assistance in administration, planning, and program development; initiation of program development; and monitoring of programs developed. 

A major intent of the office is to link the various institutional and community service components of the Division of Mental Health so as to establish a single integrated system of mental health services throughout the state. 286

Highlights The Institute of Mental Health (IMH) was reorganized into nine specialty units to improve administrative and clinical operations and to facilitate a closer working relationship between the IMH and community based mental health centers and other community based mental health services.

The division planned a reorganization of the administration of Drug Abuse and Alcohol Services resulting in the establishment of a separate Division of Substance Abuse. 

Planning was completed for the establishment of Community 

Mental Health Centers in the Northern Rhode Island, Pawtucket and Kent County catchment areas. Applications for federal funding were submitted. • The division received federal grant awards to provide training to Mental Health Board members and to provide training to paraprofessional staff at the IMH.

The state's third community residence for adult mental health clients was established in Providence. 

A comprehensive mental health manpower study was implemented through a contract with the National Institute of Mental Health. Under the contract, an assessment of clients served by mental health agencies throughout the state was performed to compare existing services to ones that might be offered. A comprehensive manpower plan is now under development. 

Grants from the CETA Administration were received to develop a plan for the phased deinstitutionalization of many IMH patients and to conduct an evaluation followup study of children served by 

Mental Health Services for Children and Youth and the IMH 

Adolescent Unit.

INSTITUTE OF MENTAL HEALTH 

The Institute of Mental Health is a 1,781 bed psychiatric hospital offering a broad range of both in-patient and partial hospitalization services for patients with a variety of mental health needs. It has many special psychiatric rehabilitative services.

The hospital underwent a major reorganization in January, 1977. 

The reorganization was designed to permit the hospital to become an 287

integral part of the statewide, comprehensive community mental health system now under development. Three distinct units for psychiatric patients between 18 and 64 were organized to relate to three geographic areas of the state. One unit serves patients from the city of Providence. 

Patients from Kent County, Washington County, Newport County and the East Bay communities, are served by a second unit and a third serves patients from Pawtucket, Woonsocket and other northern Rhode Island communities. 

To support these three units, seven other programs for patients with special needs were organized. These include an adolescent unit, an alcohol unit, and a forensic unit. A geriatric unit serves patients over 65 and a rehabilitation unit provides a special program for long term hospital residents preparing for life in the community. Finaly, a unit for patients who have emotional problems and are also mentally retarded was also established.

MENTAL HEALTH SERVICES FOR CHILDREN AND YOUTH 

The unit plans and implements preventive, diagnostic, treatment, rehabilitative and after-care services for emotionally disturbed children and their families. The unit assesses eligibility of children and youth for mental health services such as residential and community-based treatment, and arranges for and funds such services. It monitors the quality of these services and follows the progress of the children. Approximately 

220 children are served at any given time.

The unit has continued to provide psychological and psychiatric staff to the Juvenile Diagnostic Center and Family Court and consultant services to the Adolescent Unit at the Institute of Mental Health and the 

Rhode Island Training Schools. 

Highlights • A primary focus of the unit this year was on the development of rules and regulations for the operation of Mental Health Services for Children and Youth. The rules were developed and revised through the public hearing process, and filed with the Secretary of 

State on June 14, 1977.

A major effort was made to implement some components of a plan to reorganize services to children and youth and to develop relationships with community based services, particularly the community mental health centers. 289

• Through the request-for-proposal process Mental Health Services for Children and Youth contracted with the Behavioral Develop ment Center to provide a program for autistic children. The program began operation in September, 1976. 

Negotiations began with Kent County Mental Health Center for the establishment of a group home for adolescent girls in the Kent 

County catchment area.

A resource book of services available to emotinally disturbed children in Rhode Island was developed through a Title X grant. • Through special education consultation, on-site visits to residential treatment facilities for case and program review were initiated. • Initial steps were taken to develop a mechanism for improved communication between Mental Health Services for Children and Youth, the Department of Education and the local education agencies. • A special project was implemented through a private consultant to complete a case-by-case review of all youngsters placed through Mental Health Services for Children and Youth for the purpose of assessing the services provided and the progress achieved in the treatment programs.

Division of Planning, Budgeting and Program Evaluation 

This division is responsible for integrating program and fiscal planning for the department and its agencies. In carrying out this responsibility the division developes the department's operating budget and capital development plans. Another major responsibility of the division. is to create an overall departmental plan which articulates broad goals. and objectives and to develop a fiscal plan for resource allocation in accordance with these goals and objectives. The division is also responsible for assessing goal achievement through evaluation and auditing processes and has responsibility for the department's data systems. Coordination of all department programs funded under Title XX of the 

Social Security Act and Title I of the Elementary and Secondary Edu cation Act is another responsibility.

Division of Rehabilitative Services 

This division is responsible for assuring improved health care delivery for a substantial segment of the department's long term hospital population. Primary organizational responsibility includes planning and assuring implementation of health care programs at Dr. U. E. Zam barano Memorial Hospital and the General Hospital at the Rhode Is land Medical Center. Principal emphasis is placed on developing services which encourage appropriate intake, functional evaluation and treatment and rapid discharge. Through a variety of university affiliations, the division has reoriented programming to improve the quality of life and independence for those individuals who will require continued stay in either hospital. The division has also assumed responsibility to assist with development of effective options to hospital care in community settings. 291

Highlights Patient services have been improved through revision or establishment of programs with Brown University, Providence College, the 

University of Rhode Island and Rhode Island College. Student intern and training programs and direct patient programs and research in psychology, gerontology and health care administration have been operating.

Re-evaluation of a vocational rehabilitation grant at Center General Hospital resulted in staffing changes to support reactivation of a 25 bed Rehabilitation Unit to serve post-acute patients in physical medicine and rehabilitation. This inpatient unit is the only one of its kind in the state. It is expected that over the current year, services within this facility will be fully operational. 

Capital development programs to correct fire and life safety deficiencies and provide for environmental renovations were coordinated through the division. Projects at the General Hospital are approximately 70 percent complete. Zambarano Hospital's fire and life safety renovation program will also be completed during this fiscal year.

The Office of Assistant Director has undertaken the process of planning a proposed Health Care Services Building which will be located at the Rhode Island Medical Center and which would be designed to serve the long term needs of patients within the state. 

This proposed facility will emphasize improved clinical services, rehabilitative programming and research in geriatrics and gerontology and is scheduled to be completed by 1981. 

ZAMBARANO MEMORIAL HOSPITAL 

The 430 bed Dr. U. E. Zambarano Hospital in Pascoag, provides treatment for persons suffering from long-term medical problems. The general medical program is the hospital's largest. There are 326 beds available for patients who are admitted during the post acute stage of illness and who require intermediate or long-term hospital care. This program is staffed by nurses, a variety of physician specialists and medical, surgical and rehabilitative consultants. 

An important part of the services provided to patients at Zambarano includes intensive physical rehabilitation. Registered physical therapists and other paramedical specialists provide rehabilitation for individuals recovering from physical disabilities.

Zambarano Memorial Hospital also serves a pediatric population which has been admitted to the hospital for medical services. This pe- 292

diatrics unit is functioning at capacity and efforts are underway to reduce this population through appropriate placements. Besides intensive medical care, a developmental program designed to assist these children in realizing their maximum independence is provided. 

Education and training for children who exhibit severe and profound retardation is provided in a cooperation with consultants available in clinical psychology, physical and occupational therapy, special education, audiology, psychiatry and other specialties. 

Zambarano Memorial Hospital continues to maintain a small number of beds for in-hospital care of tuberculosis patients. The average census within this unit during this past year was six. 

Zambarano Memorial Hospital operates a student nursing program which is affiliated with St. Joseph's School of Nursing and approved by the National League of Nursing. The hospital also provides intensive staff development programs for medical nursing and ancillary personnel. 

Volunteer services provide a model for other hospitals. Admissions Discharges

CENTER GENERAL HOSPITAL 

The Center General Hospital at the Medical Center in Cranston is a 700-bed facility specializing in long-term care with supportive acute services. The Hospital is dedicated to assure quality health care in an environment which conforms to standards designed to permit patients to function with maximum independence. Periodic assessment of patient progress provides the opportunity to determine the appropriateness of programs and alternate placement potential. 

General Hospital meets the needs of geriatric and other patients who have long-term medical, nursing and psycho-social needs. These needs are not and cannot be met in the acute community hospitals. Directcare programs are available to individuals referred to the hospital from the community, as well as state-supported agencies. Provision of these services to a geriatric population involves nursing, medical and paramedical hospital services. Increasing emphasis is being placed on multidisciplinary rehabilitative programs that improve individual function and enrich and enhance social interaction among all patients, some of whom may not be able to leave the hospital. 

The Hospital maintains a broad spectrum of services including laboratory testing, programming in physical and psycho-social rehabilitation and specialized clinics where expert consulting physicians treat varied conditions.

Patient population on July 1, 1976, was 828. By June 30 ( 1977, the 

Hospital census was reduced to 697 patients, a trend that is expected to continue, but at a slower pace. An overall target bed capacity has been established at 650. This lower census figure will allow for a higher quality of care to be provided for the patients with current personnel. 294

For the first time in its history, the Hospital was surveyed by the Joint Commission on Accreditation of Hospitals as a long-term care facility. As of June 30, 1977, the Hospital had some 160 acute beds previously accredited by the Joint Commission. The remaining 700 plus beds are long term care. The Joint Commission requires that the Hospital be accredited in both areas of specialized care.
