Division of Retardation 

The division is responsible for planning and implementing a statewide program for the identification of the mentally retarded and for their care, developmental training and education. This is accomplished through two major activities of the division, the Dr. Joseph H. Ladd 

School and a Community Services Unit. 

The division is also responsible for coordination with other state departments and municipal, federal and private agencies providing services to the retarded. These responsibilities include establishing program standards and providing technical assistance. Functional independence for all the retarded in the state is the division's major administrative and programatic objective.

Highlights The division continued to develop plans to provide community based residences and programs for persons now at Ladd School as well as other retarded adults currently in inappropriate settings within the community • Regional rehabilitation centers in North Providence and Westerly were opened during this fiscal year. • A Parent Subsidy Program was implemented to provide financial assistance to the parent or "parent applicant" who receives and takes into their care, custody and control a retarded person who resides at Ladd School or other state institution. 

COMMUNITY SERVICES UNIT 

Three major activities of Community Services are Rehabilitation/ 

Developmental, Residential and Social Work Services. 

Rehabilitation/Developmental Services: This unit purchases programs for clients who live in the community. This involves purchase of service from eight regional rehabilitation centers which provide programs for the mentally retarded within their areas. Funded programs include early intervention, recreation, vocational training, sheltered employment, job placement and follow up, special therapies, continuing evaluation, training in personal care, and health services. Services were purchased for 794 persons during the year. 

Health/Early Intervention: Coordination of Early Intervention Services with several community based agencies is provided through the division Health Unit. Consultative services are also provided to public health nurses with this program, and with services to adults with handicaps 296

as well as to nurses who work in community residences. Additional services include transportation to and from rehabilitation centers and homemaker visits to retarded adults living in their own apartments. 

Community Residences: This section has two functions-planning and coordinating the development of residences, and purchasing services from privately operated homes. Residential services were purchased for 

94 persons in eight group homes. Most of these individuals attend regional center programs and contribute to their own support. 

Social Work Services: These services are provided statewide by a staff of caseworkers assigned to designated regional areas to assist retarded clients and/or families in obtaining programs and services that meet their needs. These services include casework, building personal relationships, vocational and residential placement, recreation and budgeting. 

Social service staff relates closely with community agencies in an information referral resource system and also provides consultation to private and public agencies.

Highlights The social services staff was redistributed to accommodate two new specialty units, one for a pediatric/adolescence population and a second for adult services.

A revised Social Service Policy and Procedure Manual was de veloped and implemented.

The social service staff participated in the screening and planning for the placement of clients in alternative residences. • Efforts to establish a dental unit for adult handicapped people who require hospitalization for dental needs were continued. 

A workshop for 60 inter-disciplinary personnel involved in early intervention was conducted by the New York United Cerebral 

Palsy Association. 

Through the Title XX Task Force a viable child find program was established on a statewide basis.

DEVELOPMENTAL DISABILITIES 

The Developmental Disabilities program is mandated by the Developmentally Disabled Assistance and Bill of Rights Act which authorizes grants to states for the purpose of developing and implementing a comprehensive and continuing plan for a system of services; providing services to developmentally disabled persons; and developing or demonstrating new or improved techniques of services. The developmentally disabled include persons who are retarded, or those who have cerebral 297

palsy, epilepsy, or suffer from autism. The federal legislation also provides for a mechanism to establish and protect the rights of persons with developmental disabilities and to assure that they obtain the quality of services needed for physical, psychological, and social development. Highlights The Developmental Disability Council continued to fund the following programs: a recreational program for moderately to profoundly retarded, physically handicaped children and youth in 

Kent County through the J. Arthur Trudeau Memorial Center; 

early screening, diagnosis, and evaluation of developmentally disabled infants through Women and Infants Hospital; a program for personal advocacy on behalf of the developmentally disabled through the Diocesan Apostlate for the Handicapped; a family counseling, training, and volunteer service to strengthen and maintain families with developmentally disabled children through Woonsocket Family and Children's Services; and a program to provide developmentally disabled persons an opportunity to live and work semi-independently through Training Thru Placement, Inc. • The Council also awarded the following grants: $41,277 to the 

Four Agency Consortium for the development of a statewide early intervention plan for developmentally disabled persons under three and an adult supportive services plan for developmentally disabled persons over 21. The Four Agency Consortium is composed of Meeting Street School, United Cerebral Palsy, J. Arthur Tru deau Memorial Center and the Child Development Center at 

Rhode Island Hospital; $32,500 to the Rhode Island Coalition for persons with Handicaps for planning and implementing a Protective and Advocacy System, which will include individual or personal advocacy, legal advocacy, and systems advocacy; $22,525 to the Division of Retardation to develop a respite care/crisis intervention program. The major purpose of the program will be to establish a small, centrally located, barrier-free living space staffed with trained personnel; to set aside one room in new group homes throughout the state as a guest room where residents may have guests; and to offer a training program for companion sitters; parents and group home personnel; $5,000 to the New Eng land Developmental Disabilities Communications Center for the development of a slide/tape and associated products for use in the Council's public information and education program. • The Council assisted in the planning of the Rhode Island White 298

House Conference on Handicapped Individuals which took place on October 16, 1976. The Council also played a supportive role in the planning of five local forums which culminated in the Statewide Conference.

DR. JOSEPH H. LADD SCHOOL 

This residential developmental training center in Exeter provides a variety of programs for approximately 760 retarded children and adults. 

The programs, designed to stimulate social adjustment and physical and mental growth, include self-help skills and training, family life development and occupational preparation. Academic subjects are offered to those who can benefit, but emphasis is placed on the development of daily living skills needed to meet the demands of community life. 

Ladd School is organized into three units-Child Development and Education, Adult Rehabilitation and Supportive Services. There is also a Medical Department. The Child Development and Education Unit has approximately 330 residents, half of whom are under 21 and involved in a school program. These children and adolescents also need training in self-help and communication skills. Those over 21 receive training in daily living skills through a variety of resources both at Ladd and in the community. The Adult Rehabilitation Unit has approximately 425 residents 21 years of age and over who need daily living and vocationally-oriented programs. The Supportive Services Unit administers all functions not in the category of direct resident care. These include dietary, housekeeping, accounting, personnel and maintenance services. The Medical Department has approximately five residents of varying ages who require intensive medical and/or nursing care. 

The development of a variety of community programs for the retarded has resulted in a change in the kind of client served by Ladd School. 

Presently, 92% of the residents are considered severely or profoundly retarded, with 30% physically handicapped as well. 

Program goals at Ladd School are designed to move the resident from a structured to an unstructured environment, from large group living quarters to small group living quarters and from dependent to independent living. The various activities provided for the residents to help achieve these goals include physical development and communications programs, work activities and recreation. 

A new adult habilitation/rehabilitation program will provide prevocational and vocational training, classes in activities of daily living, social and communication skills. A sheltered workshop certified by the 

Department of Labor will provide a base for preparing residents for 299

community employment. Eleven Ladd School residents are presently employed part-time as laborers, kitchen helpers, mail clerks and cleaners. 

Leisure activities include camping and visits to beaches and parks plus trips to shopping malls, restaurants and other places of interest. A clothing store on the grounds provides residents an opportunity to select their own clothing and to learn about retail stores. A canteen is available for residents to spend money on whatever they choose. Camps available to Ladd residents include the school-operated camp at Burlin game in West Charlestown, Camp Wakanda in Hopkinton and Camp 

Sunshine in Slocum operated by the Rhode Island Association for Retarded Citizens but funded in part by the Division of Retardation. Highlights • The most significant program development at Ladd was participation in the Rhode Island Medical Assistance Program as an intermediate care facility (ICF) for the mentally retarded. A plan was developed to describe how Ladd would comply with federal ICF regulations.

Residents from the Medical Unit were transferred to the Child 

Development and Education and Adult Rehabilitation Units. The 

Medical Unit is now a Medical Department responsible for an infirmary/clinic complex, plus the medical care of all residents at the institution. • Education Department has continued certification under state statutes and regulations. Eight more residents will begin participation in public school program this September. • Major activities in the Adult Rehabilitation Unit included continued efforts to comply with ICF regulations, placement of residents in the community, transportation of residents to community based workshops, movement of 64 residents from large open-ward buildings to semi-private rooms in the Newport House, the hiring of additional professional staff, including speech and occupational therapists.

Division of Substance Abuse 

In December of 1976, through Executive Order, the Department of Mental Health, Retardation and Hospitals reorganized merging the state's alcohol and drug authorities into the Division of Substance 

Abuse. Previously, there were two separate units, one for alcohol, the other for drugs, operating within the Division of Mental Heatlh. The 300

creation of the new division reflects some 18 months of study, and illustrates the most significant shift in the department's history concerning these areas. The division's four major areas of responsibility are: grants administration and technical assistance; statewide planning and program development; program operations; and education, prevention, training and counselor certification. The principle thrust of the division will be to more appropriately meet the needs of Rhode Islanders who have problems of substance dependency through the establishment of a network of comprehensive services. 

DRUG SERVICES 

The division is responsible for all drug abuse treatment, rehabilitation and prevention programs in the state. The two basic functional responsibilities are community program development and direct delivery of services. The staff works closely with the Governor's Permanent Council on Drug Abuse assisting drug programs in planning, financial management, the acquisition of grants, medical recordkeeping, staff training, clinical and medical matters and program development. It also coordinates drug abuse education and prevention programs designed to reach those who have not started using drugs or those who may have already had some exposure.

In accordance with the priority of shifting direct treatment services to the communities, the unit now funds 14 community-based treatment programs throughout Rhode Island. The only state-run program is the in-patient detoxification program located in the Jonathan Arnold Building of the Institute of Mental Heatlh.

The treatment services offered by drug abuse programs include drugfree out-patient counseling, in-patient detoxification, out-patient methadone maintenance, out-patient detoxification, therapeutic residential treatment and day care. In addition, the unit operates the Central Intake 

Unit at the Institute of Mental Health which provides medical, psychological, diagnostic services.

Community-based programs proviing drug-free outpatient treatment include Alternatives Drug Action Program, Inc., in Providence, Drug Abuse Service Headquarters (DASH) in East Providence, Community Organization for Drug Abuse Control (CODAC I) in Cranston, SPAN 

in Providence, Junction in Providence, The Center in Pawtucket, South 

Shore Drug Counseling Service in Charlestown, Parents Against Drugs (PAD) in West Warwick, Warwick Drug Abuse Program in Warwick and CODAC III in Newport. 

CODAC also offers outpatient methadone and outpatient detoxification services, and the Junction provides day-care services. Both Marathon 301

House and Caritas House offer residential, drug-free treatment services.

The Secured Intensive Treatment Program is operated by Marathon House. 

Highlights During the fiscal year 1977 the unit admitted 1,119 persons to its programs. Most of these were treated in community programs. 

The Secured Intensive Treatment Program, previously operated by the state, was subcontracted to a private vendor. • Five community prevention programs were funded. • Twenty in-service training programs were designed and delivered. 

A counselor certification model was adopted and a Certification Board was created. 

Academic programs have been established at Rhode Island Junior 

College and Roger Williams College. 

⚫ A third party payor profile was produced for the state. 

Newport County has been provided with an outpatient drug treatment service.

A needs assessment of the quality of care in drug programs was conducted, focusing on training needs. 

Some 260 clients were diverted from the criminal justice system and placed in treatment under the auspices of the Treatment 

Alternatives to Street Crime Program. 

• A modified continuation grant application review process has been created and implemented.

A financial audit was conducted on all drug treatment programs funded by MHRH. 

ALCOHOL SERVICES 

The division also provides comprehensive inpatient and outpatient services, information and education about alcohol abuse and alcoholism. 

The inpatient services with 60 beds are located in the "L" Building at the Institute of Mental Health. The outpatient clinic, outpatient detoxification unit and inpatient services are also located in the "L" Building. In addition, the division subcontracts alcohol treatment services to community-based programs. 

Community-based programs include Cranston Community Action Program, Halfway House, Inc. in Pawtucket, "Hope" Council on Alcohol- 302

R ism, Inc. in Providence, Kent House, Inc.

in Warwick, Joslin Community 

Development Corporation in Providence, Rhode Island Family Court in Providence, Talbot House in Providence, Tri-Hab House, Inc.

in 

Woonsocket, Tri-Town Economic Opportunity Committee in North Providence, Self-Help in Riverside, and Washington County Mental Health Center in Charlestown. 

An important support area to the division is the State Alcohol Advisory and Coordinating Committee. The extremely active group advises the director and the division in the area of policy on legislation, education and prevention, funding priorities and the State Plan, as well as provide a critical role in grant application review. Highlights 

There were 2,054 admissions to the inpatient services.

In lieu of the establishment of a computerized data system, a data needs assessment survey was conducted statewide, involving all alcohol programs.

Eleven community-based programs were funded this past year. 

The state operated alcoholism program was established as a separate unit in IMH. 

Two new community-based programs were funded; Self-Help, Inc. in East Providence and Washington County Community Mental 

Health Center. 

• The division fostered and supported a social-setting detoxification center in Providence by granting funds to Talbot House. • Two buildings were purchased and renovated to house two halfway house programs (Halfway House, Inc. and Urban League) . 

Formalized linkages were made between community-based alcoholism programs in Washington County and the Mental Health Clinic in Charlestown. • Primary care linkages have been made with existing inpatient detoxification facilities in Kent County and Newport County. 

A program evaluation methodology has been developed and implemented on alcohol programs. 303

DEPARTMENT OF NATURAL RESOURCES WILLIAM W. HARSCH, Director Established 1965-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-47 

of General Laws of 1965, as amended 

Main Office-83 Park Street, Providence 02903 Full Time Employees Seasonal Employees Total Expenditures 

361 471 $9,091,266

Protection, development, and utilization of the state's natural resources are the functions of this agency. In creating the department, natural resources were defined as including air and water, but the responsibility went elsewhere. On October 1, 1977 this agency will merge with environmental units of the Health Department, and others, to form the Department of Environmental Management, thereby eliminating the original flaw. New practices to assure accountability and improve management techniques have been instituted in anticipation of the ensuing change. Allocation and use of natural resources continue to reflect concern with minimizing impact on the resource base. The relationship between natural and man-made environments has become. more significant.

Division of Agriculture 

The functions of this division include programs of plant protection, pesticide use and sale regulation, and programs which concern the marketing of agricultural products.

Highlights • Inspected and certified were 130 nurseries, comprising 2,650 acres of stock. Nurserymen were issued 125 licenses. Nursery dealers licenses were issued to 74 individuals. • The aerial gypsy moth defoliation survey conducted in early July, 1976, revealed low defoliation on 7500 acres. Completion of the ground biological survey indicated that egg mass counts were generally low. High counts existed in relatively small areas in a widely scattered pattern. Spraying was limited to using 307

ground equipment and approximately 5,000 acres were treated. 

A gypsy moth parasite, Apanteles liparides, was released. It is hoped that this biological control agent will become established. 

This agency cooperated with United States Department of Agriculture in compiling information for the Cooperative Plant Pest 

Report for Rhode Island. 

Certain areas of the state, where white pine is prevalent and of economic importance, are set off as control areas and the planting of currants and gooseberries (Ribes sp.) the alternate hosts of white pine blister rust disease within these areas is prohibited. 

Requests for permits to ship currant and gooseberry plants into Rhode Island are granted to out-of-state nurserymen only if they are to be planted outside of the restricted areas. Nineteen permits were issued.

Mosquito control consisted mainly of brush clearance and drainage improvement work on state owned lands. • Licenses to practice arboriculture in some or all of its phases were issued to 242 persons. Twenty-one of these were issued to persons who successfully passed the examination required of those candidates seeking their initial license. 

Apiaries registered numbered 172 having a total of 773 hives. 

Inspection for discases included 949 hives. 

Economic poison pesticide products, comprising all agricultural insecticides, fungicides, herbicides, rodenticides, disinfectants and other household pesticides, must be registered before they are offered for sale. Registration serves to assure that labels of such products have ingredient statements, full directions for use and adequate precautionary statements. The number of such products registered totaled 3,304.

Four training sessions, and four review and examination sessions for commercial pesticide applicators were held. The examination was administered to 29 license applicants seeking their first license. Licenses were issued to 182 individuals in the custom applicator category.

Under the new State Plan for Certification of Restricted-Use 

Pesticide Applicators, 259 private applicators (farmers) and 193 commercial applicators qualified for certification. 308

⚫ Sixteen phytosanitary certificates were issued covering export shipments of 10,200,000 pounds of potatoes.

MARKET SECTION 

The purpose of the market section is to execute rules and regulations relative to the grading, labeling, weight and marketing of agricultural products at the production, wholesale and retail levels to assure ultimate consumer acceptance. The market section is also responsible for federal state cooperative agreements with the United States Department of Agriculture on voluntary and mandatory grading, and/or inspection programs covering fruits and vegetables, poultry, shell eggs, egg surveillance, market news service, agricultural seeds and animal feeds. 

The market section is also assigned the responsibility for programs. charged to the department by virtue of the state laws such as the 

Rhode Island State Conservation Committee, the Rhode Island Board of Inspection of Horse Riding Schools and various promotional activities. Highlights • Marketing statistics-Daily, Wholesale and Weekly Retail,

Poultry and Egg and Apple Reports continued to show increased demand. Over 120,000 copies were distributed. The mailing list was completely updated. 

Commercial Feeds Some 1524 brands were registered at $20.00 each and 239 samples of various brands were selected for testing of guaranteed analysis at the University of Rhode Island. Annual circulars of analysis were printed within the section and distributed.

Commercial Fertilizers-Some 540 brands were registered at $10.00 per guaranteed element and 187 samples of various brands were selected for testing of guaranteed analysis. Annual circulars of analysis were printed within the section and distributed. Tonnage fees and volume of fertilizer used remain stable. 

The 1977 session of the General Assembly passed state laws for commercial feed, fertilizer, liming materials, soil amendments and shell eggs. The Shell Egg Law became effective July 1, 1977, the other laws become effective January 1, 1978. • Agricultural, Vegetable and Lawn Grass Seed Some 118 samples were selected for testing of guaranteed analysis. Testing was done 309

at lowa State University and the U.S.D.A. Seed Laboratory. When violations were found involving interstate shipments they were handled by the U.S. Department of Agriculture. 

Potato Seed-Some 71 samples of potato seeds and fertilizers were tested and results were returned to the farmers prior to planting season. This program was carried out in cooperation with the University of Rhode Island. 

Horse Riding Stables—A total of 27 riding schools were registered, 71 inspections were made. There were three Board meetings and action on two unregistered stables remains in the Attorney General's office. • Federal/State Programs-Egg Surveillance-48 producers and distributors were registered, 191 visits were made with over 500 gradings. No major violations were encountered. Monthly allotment of fees from USDA were increased 20%. Potato Grading-174 inspections were made covering 8,005,010 lbs. Rhode Island was very active in the exporting of over 20,000,000 lbs. of the commodity through local producers and distributors to the European market. One ship was loaded at the Providence docks containing over 8,000,000 lbs. Poultry and Egg-410 gradings were made on 

144,962 lbs. of chicken, 58,095 lbs. of turkey at wholesalers and quality control sample checks representing thousands of cases of shell eggs being sold at retail. Section personnel covered 20 work days at USDA licensed shell-egg plants within the state. 

Fruit and Vegetables-397 car lots of produce were inspected at the wholesale level for condition and grade. • A total of 1822 regulatory gradings were made at 894 retail stores. on shell eggs and potatoes. • A direct marketing program was started this year. Farm surveys have been completed on potatoes, turf, vineyards, poultry and eggs. When the program is completed the department will have current agricultural statistics and will also be able to supply to consumers and food groups up to date information on location, name and commodities of R.I. farmers in the direct marketing program. 

Grammer school children were taken on tours of farms and agricultural processing plants. • The Market Section was responsible for running the Rhode 

Island Building at the Eastern States Exposition in West Spring- 

310

field, Mass. Personnel were involved in extensive repairs to the building, preparing a display theme and meeting with building exhibitors. 

The department hosted the 48th N.E.P.P.C.O. Northeast Poul try Producers Council, Egg Quality School in Newport on June 12-15th. Fourteen states were represented in the student body. 

Total receipts for the fiscal year were $77,255.19. 

RHODE ISLAND STATE CONSERVATION COMMITTEE 

The Committee, a seven-member state agency, has responsibility to preserve, protect, and develop the state's renewable natural resources; land, soil, water, air, vegetation, trees, fish, wildlife, coastal and tidal areas, natural beauty, scenery and open space of the state. Within this agency are the three state conservation districts, each with five directors serving as a governing body. The Northern District includes 

Providence and Bristol Counties. The Southern District includes Wash ington and Kent Counties. Eastern District, Newport County. The accomplishments of the state committee and districts resulted from the cooperation of individuals, municipalities, state and federal agencies. Highlights State Committee: The Committee signed a cooperative agreement with the Statewide Planning Program Division and has started its project proposal on rural erosion and sediment control under 

Federal Program 208. 

The State Committee held joint meetings with each of the three Districts within their Districts. Two new members from the 

Districts were selected to serve on the Committee and part time secretarial assistants have been given to the Committee from the Marketing Section within the department. Also, a small budget has been given to the State Committee to assist Districts with their expenditures.

Districts: Each of the three districts entered into memorandums of understanding with the Army Corps of Engineers and the individual County Committees of the Agricultural Stabilization and Conservation Service. The purpose of these memorandums is to : coordinate programs resulting in better service to the public and improved resource use. 311

Southern District established an annual $500 scholarship fund with the University of Rhode Island for a deserving student studying soil science or soil conservation. 

Division of Boating Safety 

The division establishes and enforces the state's boating rules and regulations. It is responsible for boat registrations, accident reports. crash and rescue services, and investigations of all accidents on Rhode Island's fresh and salt waters. Besides assisting fire and police departments and the State Fire Marshal's Office, the division provides crash and rescue services for the Army Air Guard unit at Quonset Point. It also works closely with the United States Coast Guard in search and rescue and informs the Guard of all accidents on Rhode Island waters. 

The reports are forwarded to the Coast Guard in Washington, D.C. for use in determining federal rules and regulations and setting safety standards for manufacturers of boats and related equipment. 

The division maintains a communication center for relaying information and reporting boating mishaps, and provides additional services such as an extensive educational program. Highlights The division maintained a 24-hour patrol during the "Tall Ships" celebration. Units assisted in controlling traffic in the harbor and escorting the "Tall Ships," keeping spectator-craft clear of the vessels.

During Queen Elizabeth's and President Ford's visits to Rhode 

Island, the division worked closely with the Secret Service and United States Coast Guard to provide security patrols in Newport Harbor. • The division assisted the Coast Guard in the Newport to Bermuda Race, Blessing of the Fleet in Galilee and patrolling of the 

Rhode Island Tuna Tournament. 

Increased recreational boating has resulted in more boating accidents and consequently, more investigations. • With increased pleasure boating, public demands for all types of assistance has increased. The U.S. Coat Guard, because of increased need for its services off-shore, referred more calls for near-shore search and rescue to the division. 312

Division of Coastal Resources 

This division is concerned with the development and improvement of the state's navigable waters and coastal zone, in cooperation with federal and local agencies, other state agencies, private concerns, organizations and individuals. It is responsible for navigational and shorefront improvement, flood control, shore-erosion control and hurricane protection. Duties performed include staff services for the Coastal 

Resources Management Council and the State Pilotage Commission. 

The agency operates, maintains, supervises and rents properties under the division's jurisdiction; these include land, buildings, wharves, piers and other structures. Other services include issuing assents for waterfront structures and miscellaneous work in the state's tidal waters, consideration of and/or establishment of changes in harbor lines, and investigation of complaints of alleged illegal coastal activity. 313

Division of Enforcement 

The division enforces the state's conservation laws to maintain law and order in state parks and beaches and manages the state's natural resources. 

CONSERVATION OFFICERS UPLAND PATROL - MARINE PATROL INVESTIGATION SECTION 

This unit's 33 full time conservation officers enforce laws relating to hunting, fin fish, shellfish, wetlands, forest fires, marine larceny, boating safety, birds, mammals, and state health regulations for shellfish. The duties of the 2 officer investigative section concern incendiary forest fires and marine larceny.

PARK POLICE 

This section employs 26 full time and 120 seasonal auxiliary officers. 

The park police enforce state laws and department regulations on all recreational lands administered by the department. These areas include 18 major parks and beaches and 32 picnic-grove-type areas. 

LICENSING RECORDS COMMUNICATIONS 

The central office staff issues all licenses related to natural resources and maintains records of all divisional activities. This section also operates a 24-hour complaint-answering service and provides radio communications for park police, conservation officers, and the state's fire marshals.

Highlights • The division received 3478 complaints regarding incendiary forest fires, animals, shellfish, and vandalism. • Park police and conservation officers completed extensive inservice training programs coordinated by the division's two newlyappointed training officers.

The Upland Patrol maintained surveillance on upland areas where more than 16,000 hunters and 20,000 freshwater fishermen were afield. 315

The Marine Patrol maintained 24 hour patrols on the bay with special emphasis on polluted shellfish grounds, lobstering, and shellfish activities. • Qualifications and training for the Park Police Auxiliary force was upgraded. Part-time officers must pass a psychological examination and a 30-hour, mandatory training program in basic law enforcement.

Division of Fish and Wildlife 

The division is responsible for the proper utilization, management and human enjoyment of the state's fresh water, marine and terrestrial fish and wildlife resources. Although originally established to enhance resources of primary interest to hunters and fishermen; in today's environmental society the division provides a large number of services for those interested in environmental management. Highlights Marine Fisheries: A new 42 ft. marine fishery research vessel was purchased to replace the Wanderer, built in 1932. The new vessel was purchased with 50% funding from the Federal Commercial 

Fisheries Act of 1964. 

• Survey work began evaluating ocean quahaug stocks off Rhode Island. This species has become very important due to dramatic declines in surf clam populations.

The effect of pesticides, heavy metals and hydrocarbons in selected salt ponds was evaluated. 

Several shellfish management areas were established to protect soft clam, quahaug and bay scallop stocks. • Considerable work was accomplished with lobsters, primarily preparing a report of Rhode Island lobster populations, lobster pot escape vents and fishermen log books. 

Cooperative work was started with a new regulatory body, the Rhode Island Marine Fisheries Council, while participation in the New England Regional Fisheries Council, the New England 

Fisheries Steering Committee, and various state-federal marine fisheries programs continued. 

Anadromous and Freshwater Fisheries: Cold water hatchery op erations were again very successful considering the antiquated type 318

of hatcheries in operation (see Table). Three thousand five hundred yearling northern pike from Minnesota were stocked in five ponds, and 180,000 pike fry were stocked in the new rearing reservoir. Over 40,000 fingerling American shad reared at the 

Arcadia Hatchery and 2,400 adult shad transplanted from the

Connecticut River at Holyoke, Mass., were stocked in the Pawcatuck River. Smelt egg screens containing 5,000,000 eggs were transplanted from Massachusetts to Wallum Lake, and over 4,500 alewives were transplanted to various areas. Engineering designs were obtained for four new ladders. More than 17,000 people visited the two trout hatcheries. 

• Reports entitled "Rhode Island Salt Pond Fisheries Inventory" and "Fisheries Investigations and Management in Rhode Island 

Lakes and Ponds" were published and made available to the public. 

More than 90 public fishing areas were maintained, with new facilities constructed at White Rock on the Pawcatuck River and Chapman's Pond, both in Westerly. A substantial dredging operation to provide better fishing was completed at the Ponagan sett River Fishing Area. 

• Wildlife:

The hunter safety program provided 124 courses train ing 1,104 new students. Youth Conservation Corp personnel con structed a small bore and archery range at the Great Swamp Management Area for training purposes and use by the general public. 

The Ashaway Wildlife Marsh was completed; the caretaker's trailer at the Great Swamp was completely installed; approximately 65 acres of new wildlife clearing were established in different management areas; and 5,000 black pine were planted at the East Beach Management Area. 

Wildlife operations included the establishment of Hope Island as a management area for over 450 pairs of various species of wading birds, a study of 25 percent of the hunter population with regard to economic and sociological factors, the preparation of new reports, including ones on mourning doves and Canada geese. Goose wings were prepared for lead analysis, and deer counts were made on Prudence Island. 

Some 3,900 permits were issued to hunt state lands with more than 

4,000 hunter trips recorded through the hunter check stations. 319

The stations are only operated at specific areas and only at certain times of the year.

All sections of the division were involved with public relations with 300 or more weekend and night affairs attended. Numerous youth and other groups were given tours of division facilities.

Division of Forest Environment 

This agency administers forests and natural areas with programs for utilization, conservation, forest fire protection and improvements and assists other agencies and local governments in urban programs relating to trees, forests, green belts and environmental enhancement. 

Its primary functions are management and protection of forests to benefit people, wildlife and watersheds. It also is responsible for forest management and fire protection on lands acquired by the State Water Resources Board until time of specific development. A fleet of patrol trucks working through radio contact with lookout towers protects 512,000 acres statewide. It cooperates with nearly 60 fire departments in fire suppression activity including equipment loans. It administers state grants to fire departments for forest fire suppression costs and trains local fire fighters. Guided by a commission of appointees of the 

Governor and the Legislature, the agency represents the state in the 

Northeastern Forest Fire Protection Compact whereby mutual aid is rendered or received during fire emergencies.

Professional assistance in forest management and marketing wood products is available to landowners and woods operators. Technical 320

assistance in administration of the forest land classification law is also provided to tax assessors and landowners. In addition the division promotes a statewide education program in elementary schools and summer camps. The agency cooperates with the U.S. Forest Service in administering federal grants for forest fire control, forest management and tree planting. It administers a federal excess equipment program which benefits state and local forest fire agencies. On a departmental basis this agency manages approximately 30,000 acres for maximum utilization of forest recreation, wildlife, watershed and commercial values. The division maintains gravel roads, fire trails, bridges, waterholes and signs; operates a communications system with state police, civil defense and fire departments; and runs a sawmill which provides lumber for departmental use. Highlights Divisional foresters provided forest management assistance to 979 owners of 20,345 forested areas, 210 of whom were new contacts. 

Under the state's service forestry program, landowners planted 205,250 seedlings on 205 acres. More than 23,000 trees were placed in a transplant bed for future planting. 

The department gave away 3,000 cords of firewood during the permit period November '76-January '77 and gave 110 cords to the Fuelwood for the Needy Program. 

The division serviced 209,100 feet of 11/2" hose used by local fire departments. Four patrol trucks and a slip-on tank were loaned to Providence for July Fourth bonfire control and six local fire departments received training. 

The education program reached 37,662 children and adults during 320 visits to schools and daycamps and in parades and exhibits. Over 25,000 pieces of literature were distributed. Appearance of Smokey the Bear in parades is being reduced, while ef forts are being made to bring fire prevention and conservation messages to nursery schools, kindergartens and Head Start Programs. 

• Through the Rural Community Fire Protection Act, the division subcontracted with Rhode Island College to provide training to 

1,061 volunteer firefighters.

Foresters processed 16 applications to classify 596 acres as forest land. The reclassification helps relieve landowners of a heavy 321

property tax because forest land is assessed on its present rather than potential use.

Vandalism in management areas-e.g., destruction of signs—has increased to such an extent that marking of road and trails with signs was eliminated. This is viewed as indicating a shift from the designed use, extensive, to a more intensive use. ⚫ Technical assistance was provided to the Department of Correc tions in the development of a woodcutting program for A.C.I. inmates and assistance was given in the dredging project at 

Galilee. 

There was a 75% increase in fuelwood cutting permits, but the state properties committee has ruled that wood must be sold through the bid system as surplus property. 

The division ran a successful cooperative program with Youth Conservation Corps paying high schools boys and girls to work on forestry projects in state-owned management areas. 

An urban and community forestry specialist was hired and will develop an urban forestry program working with the state's cities and towns.

A three-day Safety Systems Seminar was conducted which resulted in the creation of a divisional safety committee. The com mittee will investigate, review and analyze accidents and raise the consciousness of divisional employees for the importance of safety. Categories

Division of Parks and Recreation 

The division operates, services and maintains the state system of parks, beaches, roadside rest areas, tourist information areas and other recreational facilities. It conducts programs supplementing community and private efforts which serve the recreational needs of the public. 

The state park system totals 90 areas covering nearly 10,000 acres. At all bathing, surfing and skiing areas in the state, the division has legal responsibility for proper administration and supervision of: safety personnel; drowning prevention, lifesaving, first aid and safety programs; and safety equipment. In addition, the division is charged with the care of certain parkways, historic monuments and sites. Major emphasis continues on improvement of existing facilities and development of new facilities.

Highlights An underground electrical system was installed at the George 

Washington Camping Area. 

323

• A visitors center with concession area and sanitary facilities was constructed at Fort Adams State Park. 

Little League fields were constructed at Ten Mile River State 

Park and at Diamond Hill State Park. 

A road system was built at Fort Wetherill State Park in Jamestown. • Two thousand cubic yards of sand at both Roger W. Wheeler and 

Scarborough State Beaches were used to eliminate rocky areas. • The International Boat Show was held at Fort Adams State Park

September 8 through the 11. Also, military units including the 

United States Marines and regimental bands throughout the New 

England area performed weekly at the park.

Youth Conservation Corp Programs were conducted at Colt, Lincoln Woods, Diamond Hill, Burlingame, and Pulaski State Parks with emphasis on cleanup and establishment of walking trails. • World War II Memorial State Park in Woonsocket was completely rebuilt. Additions to this area include: two swimming areas, tennis courts, shuffle board courts, picnic areas and observation points.

The first phase of a three-year program for returfing and landscaping Meshanticut State Park in Cranston was completed. 

The Wally Byam International Caravan was held at Fort Adams 

State Park in August. Some 350 trailers participated in this event. 

A Hang Gliding Safety Clinic was held at Diamond Hill State 

Park in May. • Free swimming, senior lifesaving, first aid and golf lessons were conducted at several parks and beaches. 

The annual lifeguard championships were held in August at the 

South County surf beaches; and teams from New York, New Jersey, Connecticut and Rhode Island participated in these events. 

• Performances by the University of Rhode Island Circus Wagon 

Theatre were held at Goddard, Colt and World War II Mem orial State Parks. 

• The Beach Bus Program to South County surf beaches was enlarged to include the cities of Pawtucket, Providence, Woonsocket, North Providence and Central Falls. 

324

The Fiftieth Anniversary of Goddard Memorial State Park at which descendents of the Goddard Family were honored was observed on April 30.

The beach in Lincoln Woods State Park was dedicated July 1 

and renamed Frank Moody State Beach in honor of its former recreational safety inspector.

The annual meeting of the National Association of State Park Directors was held in Newport. Thirty-nine states were represented.

Division of Planning and Development 

In conjunction with the Statewide Planning Program, the division formulates Rhode Island's Recreation, Conservation and Open Space 

Guide Plan, which it implements through land acquisition and development. It also assists local governments in acquiring federal funding for recreational and conservational acquisition and development programs. In addition, it administers the Fresh Water Wetland Act and the Dam Safety Act and provides engineering and support services for the department.

Highlights The only change to the 1971 Wetland Act was an amendment. allowing Warwick to issue cease and desist orders. 

In the area of development, major efforts centered on clearing projects through departmental and governmental administrations. 

East Matunuck State Beach development was approved by permit from the Coastal Resources Management Council; Snake 

Den Park development and the Galilee docks replacement still await approval by the federal E.D.A. Construction began at the 

Colt Park sea wall repair and the forty-two new campsites at Fishermen's Memorial. Two sizeable sanitary and information buildings were completed at Brenton Point Park and Fort Adams in Newport. In addition, five smaller projects were finishedbulkhead repair in Galilee, roads at Wetherell, a ballfield at Doggit Avenue and electric service at George Washington camping area and Fort Adams. Eight other projects have been initiated, the most important being improvements to the Lincoln Woods swimming area. 325

The partial gift from T. Dexter Clark of 447 acres in Exeter,

Rhode Island was the only property acquired. The land is an integral part of the Arcadia complex. Twenty-two other sites are under consideration for acquisition, particularly North Prudence, Hammersmith Farm and Brenton Village, a 2,000-acre, federal transfer at Fort Adams. It is expected that several of these projects will be completed this coming year. Although state monies are limited, much can be accomplished through partial gifts. East 

Matunuck acquisition was concluded, but three, long-standing projects remain incomplete-East Beach (Charlestown), J. L. 

Curron Park (Cranston) and Carlos-Plante (Glocester). 

• Federal Community Development and BOR funds continue to finance a percentage of local projects. Thirteen towns and cities cleared 14 developments, three acquisitions and five planning projects at an estimated cost of $777,300.00. • After several months, the BOR accepted the state's Outdoor 

Recreation Planning Project. Eligibility continues for Federal 

Land and Water Conservation monies for the state.

Division of Community Services CHILD WELFARE SERVICES 

Child Welfare Services, a statewide, multiple function program for children and their families, is a specialized unit within the Division of Community Services. This unit is responsible for providing the appropriate service for all dependent and/or neglected children in need of service, care and protection. Service is available to every child whose family needs help in meeting his basic needs, without regard to race, color, national origin or economic status. The preservation of the child's own family is the primary goal. However, it is recognized that separation of children from their natural families is sometimes essential to children's well being and subsequently the provision of a sound substitute care program is a vital component of a comprehensive child welfare services program.

The prevention of family breakdown has become more of a challenge because of the multiple problems and pathology present in the families referred for service. Every situation requires intensive study and ongoing assessment to ensure that the best interests of children are protected. 

Major functions include preventive and rehabilitative services, child care, social studies relative to children who are of concern to the Family Court, and licensing and maintaining standards of care for all children while away from their natural family. 331

Highlights A total of 7692 children were serviced during the year. Service was initiated for 3972 children; complaints of possible neglect or abuse were received on 2242 children in 941 families; and 3893 children were active with the agency on June 30, 1977. 

Foster Care Services: As of June 30, 1977, 2010 children were receiving service while living away from their families. Some 1090 were in foster family homes; 290 were living with relatives; 405 were in group care facilities, including the O'Rourke Children's 

Center; 67 in shelter boarding homes; and 45 in small group homes. • Throughout this year there has been great emphasis on the deinstitutionalization of children: those who would be in residence at the O'Rourke Children's Center and those children who might have been admitted to Zambarano Hospital or Ladd School. In order to expedite the development of specialized placement settings for the extremely hard-to-place adolescents previously absorbed in the O'Rourke Childlren's Center Program, Social and

Rehabilitative Services has contracted out and purchased care and intensive supportive services for these children. A total of 28 of these adolescents are currently living in the community, receiving intensive supportive services working toward the goal of independent, self-sustaining living.

Child Welfare Services' efforts to make and sustain communitybased placements for the developmentally disabled child have also continued, but without the concentrated effort of a special project. Child Welfare Services works cooperatively with the Department of Mental Health, Retardation and Hospitals in planning for children who are both retarded, disabled and emotionally disturbed.

This year the department moved into a purchase agreement with the Council of Community Services for the recruitment and orientation training of prospective foster parents for children of all ages and with varying special needs. Child Welfare Services has attempted to work cooperatively with the private sector to further the delivery of services to children. . During the year, also, there has been a greater emphasis on establishing realistic goals for all children in foster care. The department responded to an offer made by the federally sponsored 332

project designed to develop expertise in providing permanency for children within the foster care system, and to provide technical assistance to Child Welfare Services. A two-day seminar for 

Child Welfare Services staff was given by the project staff, who are located in Oregon. This workshop has griven great impetus to Child Welfare Services staff to involve parents on an ongoing basis and to be more positive and realistic in working with them toward developing permanency for their children in foster care, either to return home or into adoption.

The need for training for foster parents is recognized and has been carried on through use of federal funds contracted out to local and nearby colleges and universities. Foster parents seek training and are, for the most part, receptive of it. Throughout the state, foster parents in the different communities meet together with agency staff to discuss common problems and areas of interest.

Shelter Care Program: There were 373 requests for temporary shelter care for children during the year. There has been a decrease in the demand for this in the last few years, attributable to the closing of navy facilities. Shelter care is requested when children do not have a responsible caretaker, often needed in an emergency after office hours without any prior planning when families are in crisis. Child Welfare Services provides 24 hour emergency services for children through a purchase of service contract with the Council of Community Services Information and Referral Service, with Child Welfare Service staff on duty around the clock to service families with children in need of emergency service or placement. The request for shelter care is not always an unplanned emergency situation. Shelter care may be a planned temporary need. In all instances every effort is made to avert placement and to provide in-house care for children. • Adoption Placement: The long range goal for all children in the foster care system is security and permanency, either in their own family or in an adoptive family. There has been increased activity toward implementing this goal for children in the foster care system in Rhode Island this year. Casework with parents of children in foster care is more goal-oriented and geared to an early diagnosis and the establishment of a treatment plan in terms of the foster child's future. Return to the natural family is the first objective. Permanency through adoption is the second goal of 333

choice. This goal was accomplished by finalization of adoption for 42 children during this year.

Protective Services: There has been a significant increase in the number of referrals of possible child abuse and child neglect this year. Complaints were received on 2242 children in 941 families in contrast to complaints involving 2014 children in 774 families the previous year. It was found after investigation that the complaints on 1661 children in 691 families were valid referrals and accepted for ongoing service. 

Protective services are designed to help families to improve living conditions and to eliminate the elements of neglect, not to prosecute neglecting parents or to immediately remove children except when children are in danger. Most families respond to the offer of help. Neglecting parents are helped to motivate themselves, to utilize community services, and to participate in community programs all geared to enriching their own lives and the lives of their children.

There has been a real significant increase in the number of reported child abuse cases. This increase could be a result of more public awareness of the responsibility of each and every individual in the community to report suspected child abuse coupled with the fact that there is more violence in society today. Statistics on child abuse reporting are tallied on a calendar year. The 181 child abuse complaints were received in the calendar year January 1976-1977. This figure corresponds to 103 the previous year and 

75 for the earlier year.

Services to Children and Families With Children in Their Own Homes: This is a family-centered service, geared to helping parent (s) to fulfill their role and responsibilities as parents and to help them to provide a more constructive goal-oriented atmosphere with the family group. Contact with these families may be initiated by a Family Court order to provide service to a family and supervision to the child who is before the Court. In these situations, service is extended to the entire family. A total of 

1883 children were being serviced in their own homes as of June 

30, 1977.

Social Studies: Social studies, upon request of the Family Court, are related to child marriages, the finalization of adoptions of children within some kinship, plus studies related to the placement of children for adoption independently, not by a licensed 334

child placing agency. There were requests for 45 social studies in the child marriages category; 329 studies related to the finalization of adoption of children within some kinship, and 9 social studies related to the finalization of adoption for children placed independentally of agency service.

Licensing: Child Welfare Services is mandated by law to maintain standards of care for all children living away from their own home. The Licensing Unit carries out this mandate. This responsibility extends to all child caring institutions, child placing agencies, group day care facilities, family day care homes, foster boarding homes and group homes. There continued to be a good deal of community activity around developing more group home facilities, particularly for the adolescents.

There is also a great demand made upon the Licensing Unit in the area of family day care. Much of this activity is related to providing appropriate day care for the mothers who have been on AFDC and are moving toward employment through training programs. Day care is extremely important, particularly in Rhode 

Island, because of the large number of working mothers. There is always a waiting list for the established group day care fa cilities. • Operational Costs: Total expenditures for this year amount to $6,551,435. Of this amount $6,303,863 were state funds and $247,- 

572 was the federal government's share for the cost of the public child welfare services in Rhode Island. However, the federal government reimburses the state for a portion of the cost of the program. The federal reimbursement amounted to $3,470,257. Of the total expenditures, $3,521,052 (53%) was spent for the actual cost of children in substitute care.

Revenue for Cost of Care of Children: The agency has the re sponsibility to tap every source of benefits available for the cost of the dependent children in foster care under the care of the agency. This year the revenue from all sources totaled: 

Federal benefits for dependent children 

Federal benefits for dependent disabled children

Total $231,578 $247,972 $479,550

During this year, Child Welfare Services was re-established as a separate division, with an administrator who is able to devote his entire attention to the children's program. One of the major 335

breakthroughs during this year was the passage of the legislation providing for a Child Abuse Hotline, which included four caseworkers for Protective Services. There was a great deal of community support behind this. The community recognizes the need, along with the State Coordinator from the New England Resource 

Center for Protective Services, to strengthen the Protective Services

Unit and to involve the community programs and agencies in sharing responsibility with the public agency. 

The relationship between the Foster Parents Association and the agency has been more positive and more productive. During this year a joint committee of foster parents and agency personnel worked together to establish the actual cost of foster care per week, per child. There was a five dollar increase in the weekly rate of payment to foster families, but the foster parents support further increase.

An appropriation for the development of group homes was included in the Governor's budget and was passed by the legislature in the 1977 legislative session.

DAY CARE SERVICES 

The Day Care Unit's primary function is to provide adequate child care arrangements for children of AFDC parents that are participating in the W.I.N. (Work Incentive) Program, employed or in training. 

Services are purchased from licensed group care facilities and from licensed and/or approved family day care providers which this unit recruits, trains and supervises. Day Care is also provided in situations where, due to physical or emotional problems of parents or children, such care for a short term is a viable alternative to bring about a strengthening of the family, and often can avert foster home placement. Highlights In fiscal year 1976-1977, Day Care Services provided group care for an average of 741 children per month and 931 children in family day care. In addition, over 837 children attended summer camp through Day Care Services.

A major achievement was the development of sponsorship for family day care providers to participate in the Child Care Feeding

Program. Rhode Island was the first state in the country to develop a program which would allow all family day care providers caring for A.F.D.C. children to participate in this program. The 

Child Care Feeding Program, under the auspices of the U.S. De- 336

partment of Agriculture, makes it possible for providers to be reimbursed for meals served to children in their care. This program helps to assure that nutritionally balanced meals are served to day care children and, at the same time, enhances the level of financial reimbursement available to the 450 dedicated family day care providers. This program is funded 100% by the U.S. Department of 

Agriculture. 

Another significant achievement was in the area of training.

With the participation of Rhode Island College and with Title XX 

training funds, a unique and innovative program was designed and implemented under the direction of Day Care Services. Initially, twelve family day care providers were selected to become Home Day Care Consultants which provided them with a new career opportunity. They were trained through an intensive program in the areas of child growth and development, creative activities to meet the child's developmental needs, human relationship skills to accomplish successful individual home visits, as well as conducting group discussions, nutrition, accident prevention and first aid, also organizing the home for family day care needs. 

With their experience as day care providers and newly learned skills, they have been working with family day care providers towards the overall major goal of improved quality family day care. This program has been most successful and the model has received national attention.

Other accomplishments included the increases in the rates of reimbursement to family day care providers, group care facilities and camps. These increases were necessary and have helped to stabilize the day care community in this state.

This fiscal year also saw the addition of new positions within the Day Care Unit which has signaled the attainment of a long-awaited goal. It is expected that with the additional staff, several areas of concern can be strengthened such as eligibility, services to children and family day care providers, as well as numerous internal controls to achieve a more efficient and effective program. 

The numbers of children served during this fiscal year, were the most ever recorded by this unit.

FAMILY SERVICES 

The department provides social services to 51,865 individuals receiving AFDC through the family service agency. Such services are intended to insure that families with children who are unable to adequately pro- 337

vide for themselves economically maintain adequate levels of self-sufficiency and acquire resources that are supportive of greater participation in society. Assistance in the attainment of basic needs for families continues to be a major social service function with fully 57% of the caseload seeking some type of help in acquiring food, fuel, clothing, and furniture which are beyond the apparent reach of their allotted incomes. 

Problem solving services are also extended in such critical areas as housing, family planning, health care, protection of children and in other basic areas that are crucial to the proper functioning of families. 

The activity of staff is divided into specific areas of practice so that measurements of usefulness and impact can be more quickly understood and planned.

ADULT AND SPECIALIZED SERVICES 

The department provides a comprehensive range of social services to the aged, blind and disabled in conformity with Title XX legislation. 

There has been a rapid growth in the SSI caseload within the state and the demand for SSI has been ever increasing. Despite a relatively small staff, Adult services are provided in a variety of areas to help the citizens remain independent and in their own homes. In addition, services for the elderly are geared toward the protection of adults from abuse and exploitation and, in general, trying to provide the type of service that will enable people to be comfortable in the community while, at the same time, remaining self sufficient. The Adult Service staff maintains a strong outreach component informing people what is available for them and helping them to utilize eixsting services, not only in the department, but throughout the state.

Deinstitutionalization has continued to be a major goal within the state. As people leave institutions, they need proper support services to live in the community. The most valuable of these services is provided by the Homemaker Unit which served seven hundred clients through the Homemaker Service Program. In addition to homemaker service, social service staff provides health services, home management services, and information and referral to help the elderly. 

In addition to the direct services provided to the elderly and disabled, the department maintains a social service program for residents of nursing homes and intermediate care facilities. Services are geared mainly toward the intake and actual placing of the patient, maintaining the patient's funds, determining continuing eligibility, and where possible, working with families of the patients. 

The department operates a Volunteer Service Program geared toward providing the services of a volunteer to an aged, or disabled person either living alone in the community or in a nursing home. In the past fiscal year over 200 volunteers were recruited and trained. Highlights • The unit continued emphasis on the outreach method plan of services to the people. 339

De-centralization of social services continued with the move from 

Mystic Street Social Service Center to the South Providence Social Service Center operated by the Urban League of Rhode Island. 

The number of volunteers increased with exploration of their use with other client groups such as foster parents and abusive parents. 

The unit explored utilization of Homemaker Service funds to help support protective day care centers and continued to develop a close working relationship with the Department on Aging. 

DR. PATRICK I. O'ROURKE CHILDREN'S CENTER 

The Center has traditionally provided group care for children who needed to be separated from their families and who were unable to live with substitute families in the community. Children in care receive services of both group and individual nature. Group services are provided through a cottage program, school program, recreation, chaplaincy service, and volunteer services. Individual services include complete preventive and corrective medical care, as well as psychological, psychiatric and case work services. The Children's Center goal for each child in residence is to provide the care and service that will facilitate as early as possible a successful return to his family or to another living situation in the community, e.g., foster family, group home, or boarding school.

During the past three or four years, the Center has been under the scrutiny of the department and of the community to determine whether the services of the institution were meeting the needs of the children it served and whether in reality the institution could be expected to meet the needs of so many different children with a variety of serious problems. In January, 1976, the Rhode Island Council of Community 

Services, at the request of the director, undertook a study of the Center which focused on its organizational structure and relationships, social work services, staff and educational, psychological and medical services. 

The Council recommended, in May, 1976, that the operation of the Center be terminated by November, 1976, with a goal of reopening it in 1977 to provide short term shelter care and a short term residential treatment program for up to 80 adolescents. 

The Council's report was reviewed by a Task Force of representatives of various state agencies. This committee sent its recommendations to the Governor in July, 1976. Among the twelve recommendations, which addressed both the Center and issues related to the Center were the following: 1) The inclusion of a reception center as a necessary component of the child welfare foster care system; 2) The conversion of the 341

Center to a non-medical treatment center for adolescent children; and 

3) The target date for the closing of the Center begin on January 1, 1977.

The recommendations of the Task Force were sent to the Governor who early in September, 1976, announced his decision which was to phase out the Center's operation and to convert it to a non-medical residential treatment center for adolescents. He further asked the Council of Community Services, with the participation of state agencies, with responsibilities for child care to design a program proposal for the Center by November 15, 1976. In a report submitted to Governor, the 

Design Committee recommended the closing of the O'Rourke Center by January, 1977, and a gradual conversion to include both an 80 bed residential treatment unit, and a Reception Center for the temporary placement of 20-30 youngsters. No final decision has been made on the residential program. However, the process of phasing out the Center's present operation and establishing a Reception Center on grounds, began in January, 1977, although still largely in planning stages. 

The Reception Center will be staffed to provide group care for up to 30 children, age 7 to 18, who are in need of an immediate placement, generally exclusive of shelter care, because of the breakdown in their current placement. Length of stay is determined by casework consideration, but limited to 60 days. The staffing pattern calls for a staff of 40, under the administration of an assistant superintendent, who is responsible to the administrator of Child Welfare Services. Highlights Staff was reduced from 116 to 94 during this time. • The following programs terminated because of pending changes at the Center: a. The Vocational Rehabilitation Re-Entry Unit was closed in December, 1976; b. The fall of 1976 saw the termination of the residential treatment cottage. • During the year, the Center complied with the Federal Court's requirements relative to the suit filed by the Rhode Island Society for Autistic Children. Changes were made in curriculum as well as procedures and an educational diagnostician, a psychologist, a reading specialist and three teacher aides were added. However, this program ended June 30, when federal funds terminated. • In January of this year, A and B building, which housed Child Welfare Services administrative and casework staff was seriously damaged by fire and was later razed. D building, also wooden frame, was considered to be a fire hazard and was also demolish- 342

ed. Staff which occupied these buildings were relocated to Perry and Slater Cottages. • During the year, 394 were served; 260 less than last year. There were 344 children discharged, 205 less than last year. The year closed with 50 children. The average daily population was 54 children; 70 less than last year. • While the Center had a smaller daily population, and considerably fewer admissions, the children served were difficult to place and point out the strong need for more placement alternatives, foster homes, group homes, and improved procedures for the placement of children in need of residential treatment. The average length of stay of the 344 children discharged increased from 86 days in 1976 to 115 days in 1977. The median length of stay increased from 31 days in 1976 to 32 days in 1977. There were 311 children or 91% in residence at the Children's Center for less than one year, 25 children or 7% for at least one year and for less than two years, and 8 children or 2% in residence for two years or longer.

SERVICES FOR THE BLIND AND VISUALLY IMPAIRED 

This agency provides a comprehensive program of rehabilitative services to all known blind and visually impaired citizens of the state regardless of age, sex, creed, color, national origin or income, in order that these individuals may become self-sufficient, financially independent and lead useful, productive lives. Vocational rehabilitation is provided to those persons age 14 through 65 under a planned program which includes counseling, medical and psychological diagnosis, physical restoration, training, job finding and placement, tools, equipment and licenses, plus related services. Social services, consisting of casework, medical diagnosis, surgery and treatment, training, camping and related services are provided those persons from birth to age 14 and those age 65 and over, or those who do not qualify for vocational services. 

The Business Enterprises Program establishes new vending facilities, trains blind operators, and supervises operations of 21 existing facilities and their operators. In addition, several multiply disabled blind workers are provided industrial, subcontract homework. 

The Special Services Branch delivers services of mobility-orientation 

(travel-training) to job opplicants, college students and others, plus rehabilitation-teaching (homemaking, communication skills and crafts) to all age groups. In addition, this unit processes all requests for agency service, maintains the register of the blind and visually impaired and coordinates all medical consultations. 343

The Accounting Unit provides the following fiscal services: profit/loss and weekly statements for all vending facilities, payroll and billing disbursements for vending facilities and industrial homeworkers; control and computation of all federal-state grants and expenditures; and preparation of all quarterly federal-state financial and annual budgetary documents. Highlights Some 1582 blind and visually impaired clients received services during the year. A total of 99 vocational rehabilitation clients were placed into jobs. There were 35 blind students attending universities, colleges and residential schools who received scholarship aid and other services, while 50 clients attended adjustment and vocational training centers. 

The 21 vending facility operators grossed $583,389 in sales and earned an average of $8,119 for the year. • A total of 107 learned to travel independently through use of a cane or other device. Also, 228 clients received 928 visits at home from rehabilitation teachers. • Some 941 talking book machines were maintained while 201 new clients received machines. • Eighty-five clients received real estate/income tax certificates. • This agency implemented the 1974 Amendments to the Randolph- Sheppard Act by initiating a new organization called the Associat ed Blind Vendors of Rhode Island. 

• H-7870 was passed and provides for delivery of rehabilitative services by this agency to all visually impaired as well as legally blind residents of Rhode Island.
