VETERANS AFFAIRS UNIT 

+15%

The Veterans Affairs Unit provides a broad range of services to veterans, servicemen, their dependents or survivors in matters pertaining to veterans affairs. These services include: social services including counseling and referral services; temporary financial assistance to eligible veterans; graves registration and death benefit information; historical cemetery restoration and documentation program; administration of the 

Veterans' Cemetery in Exeter; administration of the Veterans' Home in Bristol.

The administrator of the Veterans Affairs Unit (Chief) is also Commandant of the Rhode Island Veterans' Home. There are 174 employees, including 4 physicians at the R. I. Veterans' Home providing specialized medical services in the Home's medical rehabilitation programs. The total budget for the period of this report was $2,476,461 for the Veterans' Home in Bristol and $230,224 for the Providence Office including the operation of the Rhode Island Veterans' Cemetery in Exeter. Federal assistance in the form of a grant-in aid was received from the Veterans Administration for the operation of the Rhode Island Veterans' Home for fiscal 77 amounting to $854,478 or approximately 35% of the cost of operation. Additionally, patients in receipt of VA Pension benefits remit that portion of their pension designated for aid and attendance purposes to the Home to assist in defraying the cost of their care. This A and A benefit amounted to $178,003 or approximately 7% of the cost of operation. The balance of $1,443,980 or 58% was provided by the state from traditional state revenues. 345

Highlights New State Veterans Laws Booklet: After two years of intensive research the Veterans Affairs Office has compiled a booklet updat ing Rhode Island laws passed by the General Assembly pertain ing to Rhode Island veterans who provided honorable service in the Armed Forces of the United States. The booklet contains 74 pages of veterans laws and benefits passed by the General Assembly up to and including the 1976 session and 200 copies were distributed.

The financial assistance program of the agency continued to occupy the brunt of the social services staff's time due to the many attempts by ineligibles to obtain assistance and the need to investigate those receiving who purposely fail to report correct information or changes in their economic circumstances. 

With the passage of 77-H 5528, an act amending the definition of Korean Service, manual revisions are planned to extend assistance benefits, R.I. Veterans' Home admissions and R.I. Veterans' Ceme tery eligibility through the additional period of time between July 27, 1953 to January 31, 1955. The Vietnam dates are to be similar to the Vietnam Bonus dates. ⚫ The greatest number ever of R. I. Veterans' Home interviews, 163, were accomplished in this reporting period in conjunction with individual applications for the Home. These are an essential part of the application process which contributes to the final decision on the applicants level of care and his place on the waiting list. 

With the opening of the new building which is to house 150 nursing patients, about October, 1977, the average yearly number of admissions will increase. 

• Graves Registration, and Historical Cemetery Program: The 

Graves Registration Unit served veterans and/or deceased veterans and their families by offering death benefit information. It is the official agency for the processing of all requests for federal government headstones. The unit was responsible for the distri bution of state headstones and markers, and the flag program for appropriate decoration of veterans' graves. A permanent registry was maintained of all deceased R. I. veterans, their place of interment and the war in which they served. This registers veterans from the Colonial Wars through the Vietnam Conflict.

The Historical Cemeteries Program was involved in a statewide documentation and restoration of all registered historical ceme- 346

teries. Of the 1902 recorded cemeteries 687 are classed abandoned (owner unknown) and 1100 neglected. Restoration efforts are being coordinated by this office and major clean-up campaigns are underway. The documentation process continued to provide new information on the 1905 historical cemeteries. Groups of private citizens volunteered time and information in an effort to record the gravestones of many forgotten Rhode Island families. 

Rhode Island Veterans' Cemetery: The administration of the new 

Veterans' Cemetery in Exeter, is rapidly becoming a major function of Veterans Affair. This office processes and certifies the eligibility of all interments at the cemetery. The rules and regulations defining eligibility, procedures and pertinent information have been distributed to all funeral directors throughout Rhode Island and copies are available at the Providence Office. The Veterans' Cemetery completed its third annual full year of operation. Since its opening, 769 burials with reservations have been recorded. The orderly development of the Cemetery is now possible because of the study plan by the R. I. School of Design's Landscape Architecture 

Department under a matching grant from the Federal Government and the committee representing R. I. Veterans Organizations. This master plan calls for a single, continuous road through the Cemetery which is the main access and exit road. On June 28, 1977, the first half of that two mile road was oiled, sealed and sanded. 

Construction work on the second half of the main Cemetery road is underway through the generous cooperation of the Departments of Transportation and Natural Resources. 

On February 16, 1977 the State Properties Committee met and granted an additional 32 acres of state land for the development of the Veterans Cemetery. The total amount of land is now 52 acres. Within this newly acquired land plans are being formulated to construct a ceremonial terrace and a non-denominational memorial chapel. A group of R. I. veterans, representing veterans organizations have formed a committee to raise funds privately to assist with the development.

A total of 176 burials took place in fiscal 1977 including 146 veterans and 30 dependents. The total number of interments is now 477. The reservations are 287. The first burial section design ed to accept 800 burials has 769 graves with burials or reservations.

Veterans Affairs: On October 13, 1976, the state office of Veterans 

Affairs moved the offices from the 3rd floor to the 4th floor at 46 Aborn Street in Providence. Cramped quarters have given way to 347

comfortable office space. In mid-January, because of the move to larger quarters, the Vietnam Bonus Board established its offices in the new facility.

Grave markers, distributed through this office, were redesigned during this fiscal year. A total of 1865 R. I. grave markers were distributed together with 53,554 U. S. Flags for the appropriate decoration of veterans graves on Memorial Day and Veterans Day. • The special distribution of 1200 Betsy Ross Flags to mark the gravesites of R. I. Revlutionary War Patriots was a part of this agency's contribution to the R. I. Bicentennial Celebration. The recording of Revolutionary War gravesites resulted with the addition of 200 gravesites to the master register and 1800 patriots gravesites have been recorded and flagged with the Betsy Ross Flag. • Historical Restoration: This program coordinated clean-up efforts throughout the state-approximately 400 abandoned and neglected burial grounds are being preserved through the efforts of CETA workers, Army Reserve and National Guard Units, Boy Scouts and Girl Scouts, historical societies, local governments, state agencies, the federal government, and private individuals. Major restoration programs include gravestone restoration. 

Rhode Island Veterans' Home: The Rhode Island Veterans' Home in Bristol is a 303 bed facility providing long-term convalescence and skilled nursing care for severely disabled and chronically-ill veterans. Presently, there are four nursing care wings of 175 beds staffed with registered nurses, licensed practical nurses, and institution attendants. Two additional wings of 64 beds each continue a program of domiciliary care for ambulatory or self-care veterans. 

Additional space provides the essential supporting services to include dietary, medical, recreation, religious, occupational therapy, physical therapy, library services, social services, volunteer services, and administration.

Short of surgery, a complete medical rehabilitation program was available to all veterans in residence. Under a mutually beneficial agreement, members requiring surgery, advanced diagnostic work or intensive hospital treatment were promptly transferred to the Veterans Administration Hospital in Providence at no cost to the state. The Home in turn, contingent upon availability of beds, accepts those eligible Rhode Island veterans at the Veterans Administration Hospital who have achieved maximum hospital benefits and now require skilled nursing care. 

A nursing service organizational manual was designed and im- 348

plemented during this past year in order to define, assess, implement, and evaluate the nursing care being delivered. An abbreviated staffing study was also done in an effort to determine the staffing needs. An additional patient classification system was designed and implemented. • The dietary service under the supervision of a dietitian provided daily dietary requirements for approximately 300 patients. During the second half of the year a new food service meal distribution was instituted to better serve the needs of those veterans requiring tray service in their rooms. 

A continuous rehabilitative program, including physical therapy, occupational therapy, inhalation therapy, and dental services was maintained. • The Pharmacy Services Unit during the past fiscal year has completed the Uni-Use distribution on three of the four nursing wards, and has recently completed labeling all patient medications in the Uni-Use system.

The Social Service Department held 1573 interviews with the Home's members. One hundred seventy-two collateral interviews and 520 collateral phone calls were registered with families, friends, and agencies pertaining to the Home's members. Five hundred ninety-two pieces of correspondence with regard to member's personal, social, financial, and physical well being. 

During the past year 77 new members were admitted and 33 members were discharged from the Veterans' Home. 

A total of 45 alcoholics' meetings were held at the Home, with a total of 666 members in attendance or 14.8 per meeting. These meetings are scheduled with the cooperation of the Rhode Island 

Central Service Committee of Alcoholics Anonymous. 

For the fifth consecutive year the Patient Review Committee continued its meetings on a weekly basis. The main functions of the committee were to determine any problem areas, to familiarize members with new patients admitted to the Home, to explain the services of the various departments and to make referrals to the appropriate department when applicable. • Residents were once again given the opportunity to voice their opinions, grievances, and suggestions to the Home's staff via a resident's council. This year's council expanded the Home's recreational program distributing recreational interest questionnaires 349

to the Home's entire population. Since this time several new programs have been initiated.

For the second year the Veterans' Home has had the services of a psychiatrist on a consulting basis. Over the past year, 312 interviews were held with patients experiencing emotional problems. 

These patients were seen on a weekly, bi-weekly, or monthly basis and then followed by the Home's clinical social worker during the interim periods. • During the past year on statistics compiled by the Supervisor of 

Volunteer Services, 183 active volunteers have contributed 8,425 hours of various volunteer services to the Home. Some 126 volunteers were honored at the Annual Recognition Awards Night held in May of 1977.

VOCATIONAL REHABILITATION SERVICES 

Vocational Rehabilitation identifies and rehabilitates disabled Rhode Islanders whose handicaps represent vocational impediments and who have a reasonable chance of obtaining employment after participating in the vocational rehabilitation process. Physical, mental and emotional disabilities which create a substantial handicap to employment are the areas of dysfunction which the agency staff attempt to alleviate or reduce toward the end of utilizing the clients' residual functional capacities to help them achieve satisfactory employment in the competitive labor market. A priority emphasis is given to the severely hanicapped; i.e., those clients who require a comprehensive program of services over an extended period of time in order to achieve functional employability. The primary emphasis of the vocational rehabilitation program is the development and provision of an individualized program of service to meet the unique needs of each agency client, all of whom require specialized services to preserve, restore or develop their self-sufficiency. Highlights • The agency has adopted CARF Standards as appropriate guidelines for evaluation of rehabilitation facility program structure, policies and performance. There have been on-going efforts to familiarize local rehabilitation facilities with these standards and to help them comply with the guidelines so that they might move toward formal accreditation. A CARF review team has made onsite visits to two local facilities during this year. 

During the Interim Quarter (July 1, 1976-September 30, 1976) the agency served 7,407 clients and rehabilitated 188 of them into gainful activity. Of the 188 rehabilitants, 48% were severely disabled. 350

At the end of nine months of the current fiscal year, the agency has served 8,744 disabled Rhode Islanders and rehabilitated 933 of them into employment. Of this number of rehabilitants 46% were severely handicapped. The agency, through various activities, has increased the caseload mixture of severely handicapped clients from 32% to 46% between last October and the end of June. • Clerical staff participated in a three day intensive course entitled, Introduction to Automatic Data Processing. Six members of the clerical staff successfully completed a 25 hour course in sign language for receptionists. • This year the agency implemented a formalized planning process to set goals and objectives for FY '78. This process began in February with a request for regional forecasts on numbers to be served and rehabilitated. A training session on planning and goal-setting for regional supervisors was held in April and similar training sessions for the staff of each regional unit were held between the months of April and June. All units are now working on their plans which must be in by early September. Each regional unit will be monitored on a quarterly basis. 

Since last July the Program Evaluation Unit has been deeply involved in the implementation of an automatic data processing system within the agency, This has involved form revision, secretarial manual revision, the development and revision of a data processing manual, the backloading of 8,000 cases and a continuing quality control of the entire process. The system should be operational shortly and should contribute substantially to the overall efficiency and effectiveness of the agency.

The agency implemented an annual review procedure whereby clients whose cases were closed due to severity of disability have an opportunity to have their situation reviewed by an agency counselor. The same holds true for clients who have closed as sheltered workers.

The Program Evaluation Unit developed a monitoring instrument for regional units to use in charting their progress toward established goals and objectives.

The agency has purchased its own double micro-fiche reader and the Department of Employment Security provides a daily up-to-date job bank fiche which gives the rehabilitation counselor easy access to current job openings for their clients. 

Staff Development has been active and productive during the past 351

twelve months. Workshops, seminars, post-induction training, full and part-time educational leave, regional seminars and institutes and other forms of training have all been sponsored or conducted by the Staff Development Unit. 

This past year witnessed the passage of Section 504 of the Act which has been described by many professionals as the Bill of Rights for the Handicapped. In line with the mandate of this section, the VR staff in conjunction with their landlord, planned and implemented an accessibility package for their office building. 

This included a concrete ramp at the entrance to the facility. 

The VR staff also successfully competed against three other New England rehabilitation agencies for a federal grant which will establish a client assistance project in the state. The purpose of this program is to inform and advise clients on a statewide basis of all available benefits under the Rehabilitation Act of 1973 and to assist them in their relationships with the projects, programs and facilities providing services to them under the Act. ⚫ Agency staff have worked closely with the Rhode Island Independ ent Living Authority, Brown University School of Medicine and members of the Governor's staff to develop an independent living program for severely handicapped Rhode Islanders. Key segments of this program will be transitional housing and activities to remove architectural barriers. • The agency has renewed establishment grants to the R.I. Re habilitation Center and the Institute of Mental Health to identify institutionalized patients who can benefit from rehabilitation planning for re-entry to community living. 

WORK INCENTIVE PROGRAM 

The Work Incentive Program is designed to provide social and manpower services to assist AFDC clients attain the goal of self-support and eventual independence from public welfare. Utilization of the casework method and cooperation with community resources such as, the De partment of Employment Security and the Department of Economic 

Development are the primary functions of the W.I.N. Program staff.

Highlights • Utilization of Intensive Manpower Services Component made significant impact on the Unemployed Father Category of Assistance. 

This was especially true in the W.I.N. northern region. 352

Approximately 4900 welfare recipients were registered for the program; 2600 became participants and 1900 persons entered employment. The job retention rate is approximately 80%. Job placement activity became more successful during this fiscal year due to the easing of the recissionary economic trend. 

Undergraduate students from Roger Williams and Providence College served internships in the regional offices. 

A combination Work Experience and Group Work Experience was continued in the central W.I.N. region. Groups of women participated and results are favorable in terms of client attitude and job placement.

Greater emphasis on social worker accountability was initiated by the development of a statistical management and monitoring system. • Cooperative efforts with the Department of Employment Security and the Department of Economic Development continued to produce positive results.

The Department of Employment Security conducted an administrative review of Job Bank Activity and the results were positive. 

However, the recommendation for expansion of this activity outside the city of Providence was not accepted by the Department of 

Employment Security. 

•W.I.N. Financial Management Seminars were conducted in Region I for both S.R.S. and D.E.S. administrative staff. 353

Division of Management Services ASSISTANCE PAYMENTS 

Assistance Payments provides financial and medical assistance to individuals and families who meet eligibility factors of statute and/or established state policy. Assistance is given to eligible persons and families through three programs-Aid to Families with Dependent Children and Medical Assistance which are financed by federal and state funds; and General Public Assistance which is financed by state funds. Highlights • Aid to Families with Dependent Children:

This federal/state pro gram is authorized under Title IV-A of the Social Security Act to provide financial and medical assistance to families with eligible minor children who are deprived of parental support or care and are living with a parent or a caretaker relative. The average number of families assisted monthly this year was 17,070 which included an average of 502 in the unemployed father program. The monthly average number of children was 36,914 which included an average of 1,329 in the unemployed father program. The total average regular monthly AFDC money payment per person was $1,604. (up $94.64) and $843. (down $2.88) on unemployed. 

Eight thousand six hundred and sixty-two applications for AFDC were disposed of for this year (1,147 yess than last year). Seven thousand one hundred and forty-eight were approved for AFDC payments and one thousand five hundred and fourteen were denied or terminated. The total expenditure for direct assistance was $52,832,910. The federal share was $29,877,011. 

Medical Assistance: Title XIX provides medical coverage for re cipients of the federal program of Supplementary Security Income. This is the program initiated in January, 1974 which provides federal money payments with a state supplement to aged, blind and disabled persons who meet these aforesaid characteristics and financial eligibility requirements. Title XIX also provides medical coverage for persons who do not need a money payment but who have the same eligibility characteristic as those receiving Supplementary Income or Aid to Families with Depend ent Children and whose income and resources are within the eligibility requirement of Medical Assistance. 

There were 90,783 persons who used the medical care program this year. These included 19,760 Supplementary Security Income recipients, 53,174 Aid to Families with Dependent Children re- 357

cipients, 1,683 foster care children and 16,256 medically needy only persons. The total expenditures were $106,533.34. The federal share was $60,736,440.04. It is significant to note that of the total expenditures $61,803,270 was for Supplementary Security Income recipients, $19,450,229 was for Aid to Families with Dependent Children recipients and $24,800,359 was for the medically needy only. 

General Public Assistance: This state financed program provides financial and medical assistance to individuals and families who are in need but do not meet the required statutory or program policy characteristics for assistance under the federal/state programs. It includes individuals who are unemployable or who are pending Supplementary Security Income determination of eligi bility and families whose earned and other income is not sufficient to meet the family's needs. 

The total General Public Assistance money payments for the year were $8,331,255, a decrease of $3,273,342. Total General Public Assistance medical payments for the year were $5,678,633, a de crease of $888,332. Total General Public Assistance expenditures totalled $14,009,888, a decrease of $4,161,674. The total amount of federal payment to the state's general fund for payments to clients assisted-through General Public Assistance pending Supplementary 

Security Income (Title XVI) determination of eligibility was 

$194,088.00, an increase of $67,296.

There were 13,869 applications for General Public Assistance disposed of during the fiscal year of which 13,093 were approved and 767 were denied or terminated. There were 14,103 closed during the year. There were 10,376 less approved than in the fiscal year 1975-76. The average number of persons receiving General 

Public Assistance monthly this year was 9,555; 5,970 less than last year.

Bureau of Family Support: The Bureau of Family Support iden tifies, locates and brings to judicial hearing, legally responsible relatives of welfare dependent children and collects support from these relatives through a cooperative agreement with the Rhode Island Family Court. These colections are used as recoveries to the Aid to Families with Dependent Children Assistance Program 

(AFDC). It is responsible for providing the service of location and judicial action on a cost deduction basis to those non-welfare applicants who desire to establish an enforceable private child sup port order. It administers the Title IV-D Program of the Social 

Security Act, and assures that it is in conformity with the specific requirements stipulated in Title IV-D and other official regulations 358

issued by the Department of Health, Education and Welfare. The Bureau assures compliance with the requirements of Title IV-D 

by any other state or purchase of service contract. The total dollar value recovered for the fiscal year was $3,065,816, an increase of $551,216 from last year.

The Food Stamp Program: The Food Stamp Program provides welfare clients and others who live in the community, who are eligible, an opportunity to increase purchasing power through the food stamp bonus. The Food Stamp Program is funded through three distinct sources. The cost of the determination of eligibility of Non-Public Assistance households is shared equally with the federal government and the state and all cities and towns who participate in the program. The federal government pays 50% of all the administrative costs of determining eligibility and issuing stamps, and 100% of the bonus value of the stamps issued. The total value for food stamps issuance for the period July, 1976 through June, 1977 was $38,807,419. The bonus stamps issued was $19,880,833. The average number of households served during the year was 30,005 representing approximately 81,936 people. It is assumed an improvement in the economy led to the reduction in the number of participating applicants in comparison to last year. • The payment levels for Supplementary Security Income recipients was increased effective July 1, 1976.

Medical Assistance was revised to provide all medical benefits included in the scope of the Title XIX program to those recipients whose income equalled or was less than money payment standards. 

The full consolidated standard of assistance for AFDC was reestablished by averaging the shelter costs per size family and an associated standards increase retroactive to July 10, 1975 for eligible families.

The General Public standards were increased and there was a sizable reduction in the number of General Public Assistance recipients by implementation of changes in the program mandated by the General Assembly. 

⚫ The standards of assistance have been revised to provide for summer and winter standards which recognize the additional heat and utility expenses encountered by applicants and recipients in the winter months.

An on-line terminal has been programmed for Aid to Families with Dependent Children. 359

• The General Assembly established an emergency program for heat this year and the agency has developed a program for its implementation.

The Aid to Families with Dependent Children Quality Control error rate is the lowest in New England and one of the lowest in the United States. 

• The Bureau of Family Support increased support collections by 

23.5% this year. • Bonus Payments (40% of the first $50.00 in support received by the Bureau of Family Support on behalf of an Aid to Families with Dependent Children family) were made to 3,567 families for a total dollar amount of $492,611.

Phase I of the study of the consultant firm of Arthur Young and Co. to develop an automated system for the IV-D Program was 

• The Providence Food Stamp Office secured more adequate quarters to better serve the clients and to improve the working conditions of the staff. • The Providence Food Stamp Office on-line terminal system became fully operational for non-assistance and Aid to Famiiles with Dependent Children cases. The new computer system substantially reduces the amount of interviewing time, thus accelerating the application and re-application process. 

● The establishment of a Food Stamp Office in the northern part of the state was approved and should be operational in September, 1977.

FINANCIAL CONTROL 

This unit has the responsibility of the budgetary process and the control of department accounts. The functions and responsibilities have been expanded to include all fiscal aspects of the department. It also represents a resource to agencies of the department. Highlights The subdivision in this unit dealing with federal grants, cost allocation and sub-accounting systems was further developed. The state-wide cost allocation factor for departmental federal claiming was assumed by this section. 

This unit has had further success in structuring fiscal units in the various programs of the department. 360

The unit conceived and arranged for the training of agency administrators and fiscal personnel in the form of seminars conducted by the budget office dealing with the entire budgeting process. 

This unit assisted in the establishment of training grants in conjunction with the departmental staff training unit. 

STANDARDS AND PLANNING SECTION 

• Program Planning Unit: The purposes of this unit are to assist the administration in the formulation and implementation of new policy and disseminate information on both new policy and changes to current policy; maintain, update and modify the various program manuals used by field staff; publish, for use by field staff, all information relative to changes in current procedures; maintain the chronology for the department which is concerned with the recording and distribution of all incoming and outgoing correspondence between this department and all outside agencies; act as policy coordinator and policy consultant working closely with all units of the department in the implementation of programs developed by units within this agency; coordinate the dissemination, of all policies and procedures relating to those programs. 

Management Information Systems Unit: The purposes of this unit are to aid in the design and implementation of computer systems relative to the functions currently performed manually throughout the department; aid various SRS agencies in deriving maximum benefit from systems currently operational in the field; investigate management information needs for the department, recommending changes in information system requirements; act as information systems consultants to all of the agencies within SRS; provide the necessary liaison between the department and the 

Information Processing Division of the Department of Adminis tration relative to systems and programs currently operational in the field; conduct effectiveness studies on systems currently operational, evaluating these systems and make recommendations for change to better meet department needs; evaluate reporting structures, recommending modifications to internal control procedures, internal reporting procedures and revisions of internal reporting forms; evaluate management information needs for the department, recommending changes to the current information reporting systems to better deliver the information required by management for decision-making functions; monitor the procedures established to control the flow of and requests for computergenerated reports; monitor the reporting functions to insure inclusion of information relative to federal/state reporting require- 361

ments and to delete and/or eliminate extraneous and duplicate reports and reporting items. 

Social Audit Unit: The purposes of this unit are to conduct continuous reviews of the AFDC program sample cases relative to the determination of eligibility and overpayment and/or underpayment; conduct continuous reviews of the Food Stamp program sample cases relative to the determination of eligibility and overpayment and/or underpayment; conduct continuous reviews of 

Medical Assistance program sample cases relative to the determination of eligibility and overpayment and/or underpayment; make field visits relevant to the cases drawn in the sample and make collateral visits relative to the entire review process; prepare reports for state and federal reporting requirements, including the identi fication of errors in the AFDC program, the Food Stamp program and the Medical Assistance program; aid in the monitoring of the errors, relative to the levels determined by federal policy, and used in the corrective action procedures for each program. 

Research and Statistics Unit: The purposes of this unit are to conduct fiscal and statistical studies for department administration, furnishing administration with statistical data necessary for implementing changes to current programs or for implementing new programs; and prepare reports required by federal/state agencies and by this department. • Staff Development Unit: The purposes of this unit are to plan, coordinate and conduct training sessions for all staff members within the department relative to the training, development and maintenance of job skills for all service and eligibility staffs. 

• Evaluation and Program Monitoring Systems Unit: The purposes of this unit are to monitor, evaluate, audit and edit the data forms and other items relative to the social service reporting system; and monitor, evaluate and modify, as necessary, the procedures and instructions required to maintain the reporting system throughout the state.

Division of Medical Services 

The division is responsible for the direct administration of the Rhode Island Medical Assistance Program and the overall general supervision of all other medical programs within the department. The division in the course of conducting its administrative responsibilities, maintains close professional contact with participating members of medical and 362

allied professions and the various professional advisory committees representing all participating practitioners providing services for eligible recipients of the Medical Assistance Program. 

The Medical Assistance Program presently provides a comprehensive Medical Care Program for the following groups of individuals: money payment recipients in the federal-state category of Aid to Families with 

Dependent Children; recipients of Supplementary Security Income (SSI); recipients identified as Medically Needy Only; children in foster family care.

Highlights • Medical Standards and Review was re-organized as a Division of Medical Services within the Department of Social and Rehabilitative Services. The magnitude of medical programs in the department, particularly the Rhode Island Medical Assistance Program, mandated the development of this separate division. The impact of the department's medical programs upon the total medical care area in Rhode Island warranted this significant departmental reorganization. • The transfer of the rate setting and auditing functions for Skilled Nursing and Intermediate Care Facilities participating in the 

Rhode Island Medical Assistance Program from the Department of Mental Health, Retardation and Hospitals to the new division took place. At the same time, the staff for conducting on-site audits in Skilled Nursing and Intermediate Care Facilities was expanded to provide the capability of meeting federal auditing requirements.

This year saw the development of a Memorandum of Understand ing with the Rhode Island Professional Standard Review Organi zation to assure adequate coordination in meeting the requirements of the Professional Standard Review Organization law. The distinct task of a Professional Standard Review Organization is to certify that all Medicaid and Medicare admissions to hospitals are medically necessary, that the hospital length of stay is appropriate, and that a hospital level of care is required to meet the patient's needs. • Independent Professional Reviews were conducted on a semiannual basis of all the Medical Assistance patients in Intermediate Care Facilities in order to ensure that these patients are receiving the appropriate level of care. Previously, these reviews were conducted on an annual basis. The increased frequency of these reviews was required in order to meet Federal Utilization Review requirements. 363

NEWPORT, R. I.—The Newport Bridge was opened in June, 1969. 

It is a 2½ mile span and is the longest bridge in New England. Travelers crossing it are treated to an outstanding view of the Atlantic coastline as they ride 215 feet above Narragansett Bay with a vista of the Atlantic Ocean.

Office of the Director 

This agency has enabled the state to meet the current and future transportation needs through the integration of various modes of transportation into a single, correlated system. As a result, the director is responsible for statewide transportation planning, policy making and implementation. These functions include both short and long range plans for a united interstate and primary urban system. To achieve these objectives, a functional and upgraded highway system is maintained by a relationship with existing air, rail and port facilities. By dedicated compliance with national highway safety standards, the department is continuously updating the interstate and other existing highway networks. Highlights 

A 41.5 million dollar bond issue for non-interstate highway work, mass transit, airports and railroads was passed. 

A contract for 75 miles of call boxes for Interstate 95, 195 and 295 was awarded. ● Installation of safety improvements for I-95, from Route 2 to I-295 in West Warwick and Warwick took place along with installation of safety improvements for I-195, East Providence. • Steps were taken to reduce hazards and upgrade intersection safety at various intersections around the state. 

Quonset State Airport was opened as a public facility, with only daylight take-offs and landings allowed.

Under bridge contracts alone, there were 6 bridges which were either constructed, reconstructed or resurfaced. 367

Another pedestrian bridge, Mendon Road, Cumberland, was constructed. • Implementation of two additional commuter parking facilities for carpooling motorists was initiated. • Construction of Post Road Extension, including Greenwood and 

Gorton Railroad Bridge began.

PUBLIC RELATIONS/PUBLIC INFORMATION OFFICE 

Information detailing various programs, projects and inquiries are disseminated to the public and media through the newspaper, television, radio, telephone, public hearings and correspondence. In addition to informing the general public, the total concept of transportation, and the objectives of the state's interstate and non-interstate systems are delicately and sensibly communicated. Total responsibility for public information and public relations also involves airports, adjudication, motor vehicles, maintenance and planning. Highlights • Using its own equipment, this office coordinated and conducted highway, bridge and mass transportation ceremonies. 

The unit played a role in efforts to increase citizen participation. 

The media was made more aware of the various departments' programs. • Staff distributed official highway maps and other material about the state, including available maps of other states. • The office provided a continuous flow of information and materials to national magazine and trade journals. 

EQUAL EMPLOYMENT OPPORTUNITY OFFICE 

The primary duties of this unit are to solidify the equal employment opportunity functions of the state, and keep the communication fluid between management, enforcement agencies and the minority community.

Highlights • The department awarded a contract to a minority consultant firm to research and provide technical and/or managerial assistance to minority contractors under the Minority Business Enterprise Program. As a result of this contract the department exceeded its goal and expectations. 368

The unit was involved in the placement of minority youths in the 

External Youth Opportunity Program .These placements were with consultant firms and inter-departmental, with the hope that some youth will become interested in studying engineering. 

The department awarded a supportive services contract to train much needed welders in the highway construction industry. Fifty percent of these trainees are minorities. These trainees were also able to receive some basic training as back-hoe operators. 

The Construction EEO Supportive Services contract was awarded to the minority community in connection with the Interim 7-2 (2) highway construction program. Placements were made, and training was given in various job classifications. 

OFFICE OF SPECIAL COUNSEL 

This office is responsible for all court matters emanating from the department, and defends the state in land acquisition cases where property owners bring action against the state for assessment of damages in eminent domain matters, for the land taken and appurtenant damages to the remainder. Other legal matters, not involving trials, are also assigned to staff counsel. Included are escrow cases, settlements, negotiations, argument of motions, answering calendar calls in the various counties and appellate proceedings.

This office also represents the Division of Motor Vehicles in all appeals from the decisions involving license and/or registration suspensions, and all appeals from the Administration Adjudication Division. 

Legal counsel are also responsible for preparing and servicing complaints on those persons who have accumulated three offenses in which they have been convicted of in a three year period. Highlights • Twenty condemnation matters were tried to conclusions resulting in awards of $617,986.66 plus interest of $115,493.01. Ten condemnation matters were settled administratively resulting in judgments of $149,425.00 plus interest of $17,185.17. A total of $900,090.04 was paid for these matters that were tried in Superior 

Court and settled administratively. 

Fourteen new petitions for assessment of damages were filed in 

Superior Court.

Forty-three registry appeals were adjudicated by the court. Nine decisions of the Safety Responsibility Section were sustained by the court resulting in the suspension of motor vehicle licenses 369

and/or registrations. Five decisions of the Safety Responsibility 

Section were reversed by the court and four matters were discontinued. Thirteen decisions of the breathalyzer hearing section. were sustained by the court resulting in the suspension of motor vehicle licenses and five decisions were reversed. Two decisions of the Adjudication Division were sustained and four miscellaneous registry matters were dismissed in court. 

One decision was rendered from Supreme Court that denied an appeal and sustained the decision of the Division of Motor 

Vehicles. 

• Sixty-six habitual offender complaints were brought by special counsel and heard by the courts as follows: 36 complaints were denied and dismissed; 30 judgments were entered, and those persons have been denied the privilege of operating a motor vehicle for a period of five years.

One hundred forty-seven registry appeals were filed in court.

This division provides fiscal assistance to the director and is responsible for control of all state appropriations and federal aid, and the deposit of federal and municipal receipts into proper state accounts.

Administrative Adjudication Division 

The division is responsible for the adjudication of the majority of traffic offenses for the state. Since their decriminalization, most traffic offenses may be heard administratively. Hearings are conducted for those not eligible to pay a violation by mail. The pay-by-mail provision has been continued with the conditions of eligibility rigorously enforced. A driver retraining capability is included in the system interfaced with the hearing process and a computer based, data system has been implemented to support all phases of the administrative adjudication process.

The overall goal is to improve the processing and disposition of tra'fic offenses in terms of time to disposition, consistency of sanctions to the circumstances of the violation and the motorist's driving history. The anticipated effect of these improvements will be a more positive attitude among the public toward traffic law adjudication and a lesser rate of subsequent driving problems among violators.

VIOLATION SECTION 

This section is responsible for the following activities: issue and coutrol of summons books to all police departments in the state; receipt and control of copies of summonses issued by all police departments; receipt and processing of pay-by-mail responses to summonses; preparation and control of summonses and pay-by-mail records for entry into 372

the data processing system; distinguishing pay-by-mail violations from required hearings based on the driving history; generation of suspension notices for violators who do not comply.

Highlights 

Number of summonses paid by mail: 50,414; dollar amount collected: $1,048,375.00.

HEARING SECTION 

The Hearing Section is responsible for the actual conduct of the hearing process. This section is composed of three commissioners (Hearing 

Officers) and support personnel. Working within the guidelines of regulations and the enabling legislation, this section has taken over the adjudication of all decriminalized traffic offenses. The objectives of the section are: to provide swift, fair and impartial adjudication of traffic offenses; to establish a non-criminal process for motorists to be heard; to conduct adjudication as primarily a traffic safety process rather than a punitive one; to relate sanctions to the circumstances of offenses and to motorist's driving history; to minimize the number of appearances required by issuing police officers. Highlights ● Number of hearing disposed: 20,096; amount collected in fines: $497,773.00.

DRIVER RETRAINING SECTION 

The innovative aspect of this demonstration program is in the opportunity to determine whether applied driver retraining, or more aptly, driver refresher programs, will effect a change in driver behavior which will reduce violations, improve driving habits and reduce accidents. The section conducts three programs: 1) A DWI Counterattack Program, designed to teach the dangers of driving under the influence of alcohol and drugs and in addition, to identify and refer to proper treatment those persons who are in need of professional assistance; 2) Defensive 

Driving Course, a standardized driver refresher program conducted in four two-hour sessions. Developed by National Safety Council, it provides common-sense methods of vehicle operation and methods of driving to prevent accidents; 3) General Education Session, a two hour program which presents a practical system for safe driving, and an exercise in the perception of driving hazards. These programs are conducted in cooperation with the Rhode Island Junior College, at the Knight 

Campus in Warwick. 373

Highlights As of June 30, 1977, a total of 4,568 persons were referred to one or more of the above programs. • The section also operates, in conjunction with Family Court, driver retraining for juvenile traffic offenders. Of 39 referrals from Family 

Court, 35 completed driver retraining.

DATA PROCESSING SECTION 

This section is responsible for the computerization of all the major transactions involved in the administrative adjudication process. Included are the generation and maintenance of driver histories, fine accounting, summons control, verification of eligibility to pay by mail, generation of suspension notices, and hearing docket scheduling, as well as printing of certain reports for management and police departments. 

Division of Airports 

The primary function of the division is to operate all state-owned airports, heliports, and other facilities for air transportation, including passenger and cargo terminals, parking facilities, and emergency and security services. The division also regulates aeronautical matters and supervises the location, maintenance, operation, and use of privately owned civil airports, landing areas, air schools, and flying clubs. 

The state airport system consists of six airports which complement each other and provide accommodation for all levels of aeronautical activity. The principal airport, Theodore Francis Green State Airport, is the air-carrier airport for the entire state, and is served by five scheduled airlines. The satellite airports, North Central, Westerly, Newport, Block

Island, and Quonset State Airport are exclusively for general aviation. 

These airports account for most of the aircraft operations in Rhode Island.

Highlights 

Under the 1976 amendments to the Airport and Airways Develop ment Act of 1970, Congress declared that the nation's airport and airways system is inadequate to meet the current and projected growth in aviation. It declared, further, that substantial expansion of the airport and airways system is required to meet the demands of interstate commerce, the postal service, and the national defense. 

No later than January 1, 1978, the U.S. Secretary of Transportation will consult with the Civil Aeronautics Board and with each state 374

and airport sponsor, and ultimately a revised national airport system plan for the development of public airports in the United States will be prepared. T. F. Green State Airport and the five general aviation airports operated by the state Department of Transportation are part of this system. 

The plan includes all types of airport development eligible for federal funding, terminal area development considered essential for the efficient accommodation of people and goods at public airports, and the conduct of functions in operational support of the airport.

On projects considered to be of a safety nature, such as runways, security and safety installations and equipment, the following formula applies: 

Federal-90% through Fiscal 1978 

Local -10% through Fiscal 1978

Federal-80% through Fiscal 1979 and 1980 

Local -20% through Fiscal 1979 and 1980 

If all safety and security requirements are first provided for, projects may be approved for funding terminal development in nonrevenue-producing public areas. This participation would be limited to 50% of eligible project costs.

Division of Maintenance 

Maintenance responsibilities performed by the division are defined by the American Association of State Highway Officials: Physical Main tenance "The preservation and upkeep of a highway, including all its elements in as nearly as practical or as constructed condition, or its subsequent improved condition;" Traffic Service—“The operations of the highway facilities and service incidentals, and to provide safe, convenient and economical highway transportation."

BRIDGE MAINTENANCE SECTION 

This section is responsible for the maintenance of the more than 700 bridges on the state and federal highway systems. During the past fiscal year, this section performed maintenance and repairs on 130 bridges for an expenditure of $168,256. The work ranged from routine maintenance to complete reconstruction of major parts on some of these bridges. 

The deteriorated portions of concrete bridge decks were replaced, concrete pier columns and caps were rebuilt, as were abutments, retaining walls, sidewalk, curbing and bridge rails. Repairing bridge deck metalexpansion joints is a full time year round job for one crew. This type of work activity accounted for one-third of all bridge repairs. The cleaning and repairing of bridge deck drainage systems is another area 377

requiring constant attention. During the winter months, crews also participate in snow and ice control operations.

Highlights • During the year, contracts were completed for the following projects: Jamestown Bridge-$113,050 for concrete pier repairs and $4,550 for maintenance of electrical lights and navigational systems; Sakonnet River Bridge-$168,820 for repainting approximately one-half of structural steels; South Water Street Bridge— $5,036 for concrete pier repair; Barrington Bridge—$46,567 for underwater repairs and restoration of ice damaged stone masonry piers. • Underwater inspections were made on 20 bridges for $6,750. • The maintenance of protective highway barriers or impact attenuator devices (sand containers and water-filled types) has been extended to 22 bridge locations. During this period, 29 vehicular hits at these locations required replacement or repair of the damaged units. Fortunately, these safety devices have proven effective in preventing extensive property damage, saving lives and serious personal injury. In conjunction with the Governor's Highway Safety Program, the section published the fifth annual report on these impact attenuating devices.

TRAFFIC CONTROL MAINTENANCE SECTION 

Highlights 

Traffic Control Signs: Approximately 1,319 work orders for place ment of all types of traffic control signs were processed during the fiscal year. The greater portion of sign replacements continued to be those caused by vehicular accidents, vandalism or storm damage. • Within its capabilities, the section continued to conduct year round routine inspection and maintenance of state traffic control signs. 

This procedure augmented with calls, special notices and reports from police, the Office of the Director, the State Highway Commission, and general public, tends to assure a continuous and somewhat effective survey of all highway traffic control devices. 

The total number of signs used for the fiscal year was 2,408 requir ing 13,484 square feet of sign material. The total number of poles used was 1,809; the total number of U Irons used was 194. 

Traffic Signals: Approximately 2,006 service calls were made during the fiscal year. Of this amount, many calls were treated as emergencies. The emergency calls made after normal working hours amounted to 2,270 man hours of work. At the present time the 378

section is responsible for maintenance of 397 controllers, 67 "Flasher" signals, and three barricades. Currently, all flasher units are in excellent operational condition. Although signal lights are placed on "Flasher" when inoperative, it is necessary to treat them as emergencies when the entire unit is burned, as a result of electric storms or when damaged through accidents.

Highway Street Lighting: At the end of the fiscal year, the section had maintenance responsibility for more than 2,300 street lights. 

Additional maintenance responsibility is anticipated as construction of segments of the interstate highways are completed and turned over to the state. • Some 781 street lights were activated by replacing 220-400 watt mercury vapor lamps, 231 photo-electric cells, 66 fuses, 39 used luminaires and 8 major wire breaks. 

• Highway Traffic Control Line Striping: During the period of this report 2,587 odometer miles of state highway were striped with pavement marking. Striping consisted of center lines, lane division lines, and pavement edge lines. Both full and intermittent lines. were used as required. This activity consumed 9,500 man hours, 22,173 gallons of yellow paint, 15,471 gallons of white paint, 325 gallons of thinner, 170 gallons of red paint, 170 gallons of blue paint, and 269,320 pounds of beads. This past year this section painted red, white and blue traffic lines in 8 communities. 

• Crosswalks, Stop Bars and Traffic Control Islands: The section was responsible for maintennce of approximately 595 crosswalks, 1,250 stop bars and 179 traffic control islands. It is anticipated that these traffic control devices will be increased as traffic flow and congestion is determined by the State Highway Commission at designated locations. For the purpose of this report approximately 

15% of the foregoing has been completed; however, as in the case of the line striping program, 75% completion is also expected during the normal painting period of April through November. 

During this period this section salvaged, painted and reflectorized 150 55-gallon steel drums for use by the division garages. The barrels are painted by the traffic line painters during inclement weather. 

Signs: Other State Agencies:

During this period, this section ac commodated the Departments of Education, Health, Natural Re sources, Corrections and other divisions with regular traffic and special signs and billed a total of 83 signs in the amount of $368.96. 379

Division of Motor Vehicles 

The division is responsible for observing, administering and enforcing the provision of all laws regulating the operation of vehicles and the use of the highways. The assistant director (Registrar) is also responsible for the issuance, suspension and revocation of vehicle registrations and licenses, inspection of motor vehicles, administration of highway safety programs and supervision of the compilation, study and analysis of motor vehicle accidents.

SAFETY RESPONSIBILITY SECTION This section enforces the Safety Responsibility Act. 

Highlights • Of the 43,529 motor vehicles involved in reportable accidents in the state, 10,220 vehicles were found to be uninsured and 75.8% of the uninsured motor vehicle owners and/or operators complied with the provisions of the Act by either depositing security, entering into settlement with the other parties involved, or were exempted as a result of hearings. 

Some 24.2% of uninsured vehicle owners and/or operators had their driving privileges suspended for failure to comply with the provisions of the Safety Responsibility Act. Hearings given as a result of the 1971 Supreme Court decision (Bell vs. Burson) numbered 1,100 for 1976. The Attorney General's special counsel worked closely with the section in these important hearings to insure that the rights of all parties were protected. • As of December 31, 1976, a total of $335,830.80 in security deposits collected by this agency remained on deposit in the General Treasurer's office of which $157,622.00 is in the form of cash and $132,- 

350.00 in real estate bonds and $45,858.80 in pass book accounts. 

Financial Responsibility Unit 

This unit continues to maintain the insurance filings for those required to make such filings in accordance with the provisions of the 

Safety Responsibility Act. Approximately 485 leasing company licenses were also issued through this agency in 1976.

Research and Traffice Safety Promotion Unit 

This unit is responsible for detailed analysis of all reportable highway traffic accidents occurring in Rhode Island. The coding of each accident 380

report provides data on each phase of accident causes, and the information is made available for study by all traffic officials. Traffic accident statistics are used to comprise the data base for all aspects of any coordinated highway safety program. The compilation of these various data provides summaries, tabulations and analyses to local, state and federal governments, and other agencies. Highlights The state recorded 25,197 motor vehicle highway accidents for the year of 1976. One hundred and twenty-one persons lost their lives as a result of the 114 fatal accidents and 10,825 persons were injured as a result of the 8,182 personal injury accidents. There were also 16,901 property damage accidents showing a 4% decrease over 1975. The economic loss resulting from these accidents totaled approximately $63,314,530.00. There was an increase of 

12% in fatal accidents; a 8% increase in fatalities. Total accidents decreased 2% and the persons injured increased 2%. Thirty-two of the 121 fatalities were pedestrians. Six or 19% were children under 15 years of age, and 4 or 13% were 65 years of age or older. 

Three bicyclists were killed showing an increase of 200%, there was a 32% decrease in the number of bicyclists injured this year. A total of 46,978 drivers were involved, and 8,342 or 17% of these drivers were teenagers. Twenty-two or 19% of the drivers involved in fatal accidents were teenagers. Motorcycle involvement in all accidents shows a 4% increase, and an 85% increase in motorcycle fatal accidents during the year of 1976. ⚫ Studies were made on all pending motor vehicle legislation affecting the division, and recommendations were made thereon. All motor vehicle bills that became law were compiled and indexed. 

Bills were categorized, and the Rhode Island Motor Vehicle Code Act was compiled. 

All applications for vanity plates were approved or rejected to ascertain whether the requests carried any connotations that might be deemed offensive to good taste and decency. 381

Highlights 

INSPECTION SECTION 

• The Inspection Section in the calendar year of 1976, issued a total of 1,206 licenses to the various inspection stations. ⚫ With the assistance of four bus mechanics, and the facilities of the 

Rhode Island Public Transit Authority Garage, school bus inspec tion continued. The inspection included the vehicle being raised on a lift, all wheels were removed, and each drum was "miked" for excessive wear. At the completion of the inspection, the vehicle was classified in one of the following four categories: unfit and unsafe; unsafe as school bus; minor defects; no defects. During the calendar year, 1,097 school buses were inspected. In addition, 309 garage inspections were performed at other installations. Upon completion of the garage inspection, 625 buses had to be rechecked at different locations to determine if the defect had been corrected. 

Ten general inspections for public plates were conducted in the field in connection with this program. 

During the year, inspection personnel, with the assistance of the various police departments, conducted random road checks; and, as such, "checked" 1,596 vehicles of which 575 vehicles were tagged after finding one or more defects. In 135 cases, plates were removed from the vehicle at the time of inspection when it was determined that the vehicle was either not properly registered or unsafe for highway use. The police who assisted in this program made 72 arrests for the various motor vehicle violations disclosed. 

The office staff continued to process and follow-up the Notice and 

Demand Tags, (Defective Equipment Tags), that were issued by all law enforcement officers; and as such, processed 20,667 tags. 

A total of 2,598 tags resulted in suspension orders being issued to the public for failure to comply with the notice and demand. 

The 1976 general inspection period ended on July 31, 1976; as a result 1,139 vehicles were officially rejected. Of those vehicles that were officially rejected, 460 vehicles' registrations were suspended when they failed to have their vehicle repaired within the fourteen days that were allowed.

The section continued to supervise and inspect the stations for compliance with the minimum required equipment, and attempted to visit all stations at least once. These routine inspections provided for a means for the section personnel to ascertain that the inspection equipment was maintained in good order, and that all necessary reports were being filed. 383

The division received 30 ecology complaints, and an investigator was assigned to each one. At least three attempts were made to locate each of the vehicles involved for further inspection. Of the ecology reports, 6 were visual inspections, and they were found not valid and 18 resulted in defective equipment tags being issued for repairs. The remaining 6 were unable to contact, repaired or disposed of prior to inspection. 

Field investigators received and investigated 185 complaints that resulted in warnings or station revocations. From these complaints and other investigations, including routine inspections, the division revoked 20 inspection station appointments.

During the year, field investigators were able to inspect 673 vehicles registered as taxis, jitneys, and publics, including the various vehicles that were suspended for defective equipment. 

During the calendar year, the office staff collected fees for the licenses and inspection stickers that were sold. The total sum collected amount to $62,649.30.

The section examined 5,000 inspection records of vehicles approved during the 1976 program. This examination disclosed that 

63.02% of the vehicles had one or more defects repaired before approval was issued. The three most common defects reported were lights, brakes and exhaust. 384
