Highlights 

OPERATOR CONTROL SECTION 

• This section reviewed approximately 9,854 court abstracts, and thousands of reports from various sources and in accordance with the Motor Vehicle Code, revoked, suspended, expired, or cancelled the Rhode Island licenses or non-resident driving privileges of 2,533 drivers. All persons whose licenses or privileges were revoked or suspended, because of a conviction, also may have had registrations suspended in accordance with the law, pending the establishment of financial responsibility. 

The Record File Unit kept records of the court abstracts it received from the various courts in Rhode Island, checked about 1,000 records for the police and typed about 9,500 records for individuals, insurance companies, government agencies, and other persons or firms. • As a result of reports from the state hospital, investigators, the police, or other sources, drivers who were reported not to be able by reason of physical or mental disability to operate a motor vehicle with safety were suspended pending establishment of physical fitness by supporting evidence from a hospital or doctor. The division's Medical Advisory Board held 12 meetings throughout the year, and they reviewed 150 medical cases. • There were 250 breathalyzer hearings scheduled throughout the year. However, there were 157 breathalyzer hearings actually heard.

The Hearing Unit interviewed approximately 10,778 persons, and the unit referred to the Registrar's Board 884 written hearings or explanations for its consideration. 

There were 21 transcriptions of breathalyzer hearings submitted to the attorney general's office. • The section enrolled 60 subjects in the DWI Counter-attack program at Rhode Island Junior College in Warwick, Rhode Island. 

Records regarding course completion and financial responsibility filings were kept.

REGISTRATION SECTION 

The Registration Section consists of six sub-sections, all of which proportionally share the duties of the registration and license renewal process. The mail unit disseminates all incoming mail throughout the division, and this unit also handles registration transactions by mail. 386

The counter and license file unit personnel review each application for accuracy and completeness and establish the vehicle weight and appropriate fee. Each license application is checked for authenticity and for possible suspension or revocation. The cashiers collect all fees charged. 

The registration files and teletype unit maintains the registration file system by name of owner, vehicle identification number and registration. plate number. All transfers and change of address notices are so attached to the proper applications. The teletype, operating 24 hours a day and seven days a week, answers all law enforcement agency questions regarding license and registration information. The dealer and courtesy plate counter handles all transactions from motor vehicle dealers and issues courtesy plates, farm plates and all types of miscellaneous registrations.

TITLE SECTION 

The primary duty of this section is to issue certificates of title for model year vehicles as a means of establishing true ownership. Highlights • As of August 19, 1977, the section has issued approximately 295,865 title certificates. All title certificates that are issued are checked through the National Crime Investigation Center files at the Scituate State Police Barracks. They are compared with the list of stolen vehicles reported throughout the country. • The impact of the title law during the three years that it has been in effect has resulted in the registration of just two stolen vehicles both of which were subsequently recovered by the state police and returned to their rightful owners. 

As a result of legislation passed by the 1977 General Assembly, Title 31 of the General Laws has been amended by adding Chapter 46, "Rhode Island Salvage Law", establishing a procedure for reporting salvage vehicles to the Division of Motor Vehicles and the subsequent issuance of a salvage certificate of title. • As a result of a 1975 Legislative amendment to the title law, “ail trailers without motive power and designed for carrying property, to be drawn by a motor vehicle and having a capacity of 3,000 pounds or less" are exempted from the requirement of obtaining a certificate of title. 

As a result of a 1975 legislative amendment to the title law, a lien holder may now perfect a security interest in a vehicle separate and apart from any titling procedures. 387

Planning Division 

This division assists agencies responsible for state planning to prepare transportation elements of the long-range State Guide Plan. The division prepares functional and area plans, project plans, improvement programs and implementation programs which are consistent with the long-range State Guide Plan. The divison undertakes corridor, route locations, feasibility, facility needs and other studies as required to support the work of the department. Also, it collects and analyzes statistical and other data on all types of transportation needs and facilities. Highlights • The draft of the FY 78 Work Program was submitted to the Federal Highway Administration for review.

The A-95 notification to state review was approved on April 1, 1977 by the Technical Committee of the State Planning Council for 

Parts I and II of the Program for federal funds. Part II—Re search and Development was submitted and conditionally approved on May 25, 1977.

The scribing of Westerly and Hopkinton base maps were completed as well as Charlestown, Richmond and West Greenwich base maps. Culture is being applied to Westerly, Hopkinton, Charlestown, Richmond and West Greenwich maps.

The division has printed Motor Vehicle Accident Reporting System, Vol. 1 Users Manual; covers for TOPICS Report; and title sheets and legends for scribed county maps. Staff answered various map requests from private and public agencies. 

A total of 134 special manual turning movement counts were conducted and 34 manual classification counts were conducted. 

The certification of truck size and weight enforcement was prepared and submitted to FHWA.

Special traffic counts were conducted at a total of 110 stations. 

The division's continuous counting program was fully instituted during the beginning of April, 1977.

Division representatives visited the Streeter-Amet Divison of Mangood Traffic Counting Manufacturer, Illinois, to examine the latest traffic counting techniques and equipment for possible use on 

Rhode Island highways. During June, 1977, new traffic counting loops were installed in five locations on I-195, East Providence. 388

A study of the existing traffic patterns on routes serving the Quonset-Davisville Complex and Route 44 in Glocester-Smithfield was completed. Also, a traffic analysis was completed, evaluating the proposed safety improvements at I-195 from west of Route 114 to the state line.

Work continued on the year 2000 traffic volumes for the evaluation of safety improvements on I-195 from west of Route 114 to the state line.

For the Smithfield Avenue Bridge Project, the evaluation of traffic impact on existing facilities-Lonsdale Avenue, Branch Avenue and Charles Street was completed. This analysis was for the alternative of rebuilding the bridge on existing alignment and rerouting of traffic. • Staff has developed a scope of work for an in-house evaluation of the short range and long term impacts of the Civic Center Interchange on the existing Route 95 from Route 146 to south of Allens Avenue. The techniques obtained from the "Computer 

Workshop on Development of Priority Strategies for Freeways 

(TREQ3CP)" will be utilized. Phase two of the study will evaluate the remainder of I-95 and I-195 within the metropolitan area.

Route 4 Extension: The draft Environmental Impact Statement 

(EIS) was completed and circulated to all federal, state and local agencies in addition to interested citizen groups. A public hearing was held on June 30, 1977 at the North Kingstown High School auditorium. • I-84: FHWA completed its review of the final EIS, and submitted several comments to the division, which is in the process of formulating a response to those comments.

I-895 Corridor Location Study/EIS: Efforts have resulted in the completion of most of the analysis work and a heavy concentration on the actual writing of the draft EIS. The analysis tasks nearing completion during this quarter include: Develop Travel Estimates, Community Impact Analysis, Natural Systems Impact Analysis, Cost/Benefit Analysis, Relocation Factors Analysis, Preliminary De sign Studies, and Special Studies. The consultant is expected to submit a preliminary draft EIS to this department. 

In the area of community participation, the third round of public workshops was held on April 12, 14 and 21 for Aquidneck Island, Washington County, and East Shore respectively. These workshops were supplemented by a round of Project Area Com- 389

mittee meetings in June.

Also, charts were drafted for I-895 work shop meetings (Location/EIS Study). 

Woonsocket Industrial Highway: Aerial photographic mapping for the overall study area has been obtained. Environmental constraints and the route alignment will be plotted on the mapping for use during the assessment process. A typical section for the project has been developed by the consultant and submitted to the state for approval. The draft supplement to the FEIS has been completed, and it is presently being printed. Prior to the printing of the document, it was submitted to FHWA for review and comment. The public participation program for this project continued with two workshops in the towns of Lincoln and North Smithfield, and also with two additional PAC meetings. The attendance and participation at these meetings has been fair. The consultant is presently working on the preliminary 10% design plans to be presented at the public hearing.

Various noise analysis reports, in differing stages of completion, have been conducted by this division, such as the I-295-Route 146 interchange report; the Quaker Lane Project in Warwick; and the 

Park Avenue Project in Cranston. 

The Urban, Primary and Secondary Federal Aid systems have been given final approval by FHWA, and system maps have been prepared to indicate the network. 

A statewide windshield survey was undertaken of sites being used by commuters for park and ride. • A revised scope of work for the Narragansett Bikeway was prepared, as well as a draft report on the feasibility of a Narragansett bicycle route.

This division, the city of Providence, FHWA, and the Statewide Planning Program worked together to select a consultant for the 

Downtown Providence Traffic Study. This division commented on the scope of the study and reviewed the consultant proposals. • An interdisciplinary team was formed within the department, Statewide Planning Program and FHWA for the purposes of re writing the Action Plan. The Action Plan will be rewritten to reflect changes resulting from the approval of addenda and current policy and operating procedures of the department. Additional sections are proposed for the Action Plan which will deal specifically with public hearing procedures, levels of activity and 390

environmental assessments. Until such time that policy matters are resolved, it would be impractical to make any major revisions to the plan.

The Planning Division is presently working on the highway ac cess sections of the Airport Master Plan Overview. 

• Staff has been working with Senior Citizen Transport, Inc.'s management in planning its budget and funding sources for FY '78. 

There was also coordination between MHRH's Division of Retardation and DOT to ensure that the General Assembly consider a budget allocation for an integrated statewide public transportation system.

A draft of a "special paper" on the continuation of state financing of elderly and handicapped transportation provided by Senior Citizen Transport, Inc., at various levels of service was prepared and circulated to the Division of Aging, DCA, and the Family and the Adult Services Division of SRS. The paper will be revised, and it will serve as the basis for a recommendation to the state Budget Office for review of the fiscal year 1978 budget request for Senior Citizen Transport, Inc. Recommendations to the Budget Office were made with regard to financing of specialized transportation services for the elderly and handicapped. The recommendations followed a review of a "special paper" by DOA, Department of Administration, Social and Rehabilitative Services, and DOT staff.

A revised capital improvements program for RIPTA and Senior Citizen Transport, Inc., was developed for inclusion in the recent transportation bond issue.

A procedure for billing the state for subsidies incurred under a voluntary reduced fare program for the elderly has been agreed on by the Bonanza Bus Co., DOT, and the Budget Office. DOT and 

RIPTA met with Washington County community groups to plan for the introduction of new RIPTA service by Senior Citizen

Transport, Inc., vans in that county. 

A methodology for computerizing RIPTA's routes and schedules, in order to provide quick response, relative to the availability and scheduling of transit service, between any two points in the state, was investigated. 391

A memo of understanding was drawn up and signed between the DOT and DOA in order to allow the pass through of up to $72,000 from DOT for transportation services to the elderly and handicapped by Senior Citizen Transport, Inc.

The entire grant process has been completed, with both the 

FY '76 and '77 grant application ready for the Urban Mass

Transit Administration approval. Of the 19 completed applica tions, four were funded, expending the total amount allocated in federal funds.

During the month of August, a revision of the High Accident Locations Listing was completed. In addition, detailed accident studies were performed on all highways proposed for striping, Route 102, the Rural Interstate System and the Providence River Bridge.

Statistical studies were performed on grade crossing accidents, fixed object accidents and accidents by town for various state agencies.

Meetings were held with the Federal Railroad Administration and Northeast Corridor officials concerning local and corridor issues. • Efforts were made to assist local shippers with service problems, especially at Allens Avenue. Proposed service changes in Providence to local switching service were not made due to this division's objections made in July, 1976.

Appraisals, track and acquisition application for Newport were forwarded to the Federal Railroad Administration and bids have been solicited by ConRail for accelerated maintenance. 

Inspection of the NERCOM Rail Rehabilitation Project has begun. • Activity to collect more recent data and to assess possible changes to the State Rail Plan were made. Drafting of the plan is underway.

A proposed Bristol operation description was written and cost analysis conducted. An estimate of $16,000,000 for implementation was received. UMTA regulations require further study of alternatives in Bristol Corridor prior to preliminary engineering studies. 392

Train service between Westerly and Providence was dropped as of June 5, 1977 as a result of cost/benefit studies and public hearing.

Comments were forwarded on Amtrak schedule changes which adversely affected Rhode Island. Changes requested by Connecticut, Massachusetts and Rhode Island were implemented by Amtrak on June 12, 1977. 

An evaluation of the state's Carpool Program through FY '76 has been drafted for the FHWA and is under review. 

Allendale Insurance Company took delivery of two vans in January, 1977, and started the state's first vanpool program, having been organized and coordinated by the division. Electric Boat's 

Quonset Point Division is working closely with the division in developing short range transportation alternatives to alleviate severe congestion problems at the shipbuilding facility. 

The division is working with a private bus company, Atlantic Suburban Traction, to initiate bus service along the Newport- 

Westerly corridor to Groton.

Meetings with the greater Providence Chamber of Commerce were held to discuss that organization's role in the potential Central 

Business District (CBD) ride-share market.

A transportation energy conservation scenario was developed for presentation to the Governor's staff for possible action on the part of the Executive Office. Awarding of a grant by the state Energy Office for the promotion of ride-sharing, including transit, is expected. 

The final "Combined Corridor and Design Study Report" for the South Kingstown Railroad Grade Crossing Elimination Proj ect has been approved by FHWA, and the department has been allowed to proceed to the design stage of this project. 393

Division of Public Works OFFICE OF THE CHIEF ENGINEER 

This division coordinates all activities related to the construction, design, upgrading and improvement of the state's highway system. The 

Chief Engineer's Office is the main circuit of communication with the Federal Highway Administration in all matters dealing with public works projects. All correspondence to and from the Federal Highway 

Administration, pertaining to public works projects, are channeled through the Chief Engineer's Office to appropriate division personnel. 

Another important function of this office is its Overweight and Oversize Permit Unit which is responsible for issuing permits for vehicles in excess of Rhode Island's weight and size limits. Highlights This office issued 2,041 permits for vehicles in excess of Rhode Island's weight and size limits. 

The office awarded contract for the installation of emergency call boxes on interstate highways. 

BRIDGE DESIGN UNIT 

The principal functions of this unit are to advance the development of bridge contract plans, specifications, detailed estimates and contract documents from the planning stage through the design stage, all in conformance with sound engineering practices, economics and the requirements of the state and federal government. Highlights • During the past fiscal year the Bridge Design Unit prepared or supervised consultants in advertising construction contracts for the following projects: Mendon Road Pedestrian Bridge; rehabilitation of the Hussey Memorial Bridge; Red Bridge Demolition; Gorton R.R. Bridge (Post Road Extension); repainting of certain portions of the Jamestown Bridge; I-95 Safety Improvements. • This unit also reviewed shop drawings for structural steel, prestressed concrete, overhead and ground-mounted sign structures, prepared structural steel inspection contracts and performed structural steel inspection in fabrication plants. 394

BRIDGE INSPECTION UNIT 

Because bridge collapse is becoming a frequent occurrence, the federal government is stepping up its continuing effort, which began with the Federal Highway Acts of 1968 and 1970, to implement and improve corrective action for bridge rehabilitation-for both safety and maintenance consideration. In this state inspections of more than 700 bridges, scheduled on a two-year cycle, are recorded by this unit. Inspection and other data coming from the cities are processed as well as sundry inspection problems from towns. Weight restrictive posting required by structural deficiencies is initiated by this unit. 

Along with inspection the unit applies for a special bridge replacement program, 75% federally funded, as the first step for participation in the program. Highlights After extensive research, this unit prepared the basic concepts of a proposal to purchase a mobile crane that provides a platform underslung by means of booms from a bridge deck, to the underside, below, in a region where inspection had been difficult or impossible without extensive rigging being provided. This device should be ready for use early next spring for the inspection of large waterway crossings. • Presently, there are two teams of inspectors examining and reporting their findings on bridges—principally the grade separation type. 

COMPUTER UNIT 

The Computer Unit serves the needs of the various divisions and sections of the department by developing and acquiring computer programs. The responsibility for the maintenance and operation of these programs also lies with the Computer Unit.

Highlights During the past year the unit switched from punched card data entry to a key to "disk" system. A new digital plotter was also acquired to replace the older model made obsolete by last year's conversion to remote job entry.

The unit made available its data processing facilities for use by participants of the Freeway Priority Strategies Workshop which was hosted by the department last fall. 395

A workshop was also hosted by the department which familiarized participants with the use of Roadway Design System (RDS).

RDS is currently being modified by the unit for use by the department.

A new computer system has been developed to meet federal requirements for structural inventory and appraisal data. 

CONSTRUCTION FINALS AND PROCEDURES REVIEW SECTION 

The section operates as a staff function responsible to the Chief Engineer. It reviews all completed work items for documentation, substantiation and verification. Finals and Procedures conducts site inspections for compliance of construction practices, and on site progress reviews to expedite the final review. It plans, organizes, and conducts seminars, courses of inspection pertaining to uniform construction practices and record keeping. This section provides procedures (initial and revisions) and direction relative to field construction methods, uniform method of record keeping and interpretation of the R.I. 

Standard Specifications manual. 

Highlights • Staff reviewed and finalized all records for 34 construction projects. It conducted 30 on site progress reviews. 

The section officially assumed responsibility for all functions connected with certificates from the various sections of the Public Works Division for final closeout requirements and all information coordination between the Federal Highway Administration and the Design, Construction, Audit and Finance Sections of the division. • A mid-winter construction training workshop was conducted during the months of January, February and March, for all construction and maintenance personnel of the division. Subjects of instruction were traffic control through construction and maintenance work areas (3 phases); the operation and use of the planimeter; and the operation and use of the laser beam. 

A bridge inspection training course was conducted at R.I. College from July 12-July 30, 1976. This course, planned by the division and arranged by this section, was contracted to Singer Simulation Products Division, of Silver Spring, Maryland. It was 396

designed to meet requirements set by U.S. Department of Transportation for qualified and certified trained bridge inspectors. 

Each trainee candidate met specific educational requirements prior to selection. All subjects taught were of highly technical nature relative to all types of bridge structures. Eighteen selected personnel received this instruction, and successfully completed 90 hours of specialized training for which they received official certificates. 

CONSTRUCTION OPERATIONS SECTION 

The prime responsibility of this section is the supervision of all construction of highways and bridges, including but not limited to such highway programs as resurfacing, restoration and rehabilitation. Highlights The continuation of the safety program was emphasized in this reporting period.

The upgrading of Interstate 95 from the Connecticut line north to Big River has been completed. Work included such items as removing of guard rail, signs, sealing and cleaning pavement joints and cracks, drainage, lighting, grading, loam and seeding, and all other incidental work pertaining to the safety and upgrading of the highway. In addition, safety improvements on

Interstate 95 from Big River to Route 2 have also been completed. 

Additional safety upgrading was started on Interstate 95 from

Route 2 north to I-295. This work included the item as shown on the previous I95 safety contracts, along with additional work on bridges and the installation of median barriers. 

Another leg of Memorial Boulevard in Newport, from Long Wharf to Van Zandt Avenue, was completed. This was at a cost of approximately $937,009.64 with federal participation of funds in the amount of 70%. • The Bridge Program has been active this period. A contract was awarded to replace the Charles Street Bridge in Providence at a cost of $546,956.80 with the federal government paying 100% of the cost. Reconstruction was also started on the Broad and 

Cross Street Bridges in Pawtucket, at a cost of $59,818.14, with 

70% federal participation. • A pedestrian bridge is being constructed at Mendon Road, Cumberland. This will allow the student to go from the high school 397

to the athletic field without crossing highly travelled Mendon Road. A cost of $129,650.70 is estimated, with 70% federal participation. The reconstruction of the deck on Clarence L. Hussey 

Memorial Bridge in Wickford has been started at a cost of $134,- 

541.00 with 70% federal participation. • The steel erection for the Gorton Pond and Railroad Bridge has been completed, which is part of the last leg of the Post Road Extension contract in Warwick. The total cost of this bridge is $1,207,197.02 with the federal government paying 70% and 100% 

funding for the Post Road Extension. 

A contract has been awarded for the demolition of the old Red 

Bridge, at a cost of $139,540.00, with 70% federal funding. • A statewide striping contract has been awarded to stripe both white and yellow lines throughout all counties of the state for $136,482.96 with 100% federal funds.

The Construction Section has been given the responsibility of supervising and inspecting the Railroad Track Rehabilitation Program for the state. To accomplish this a small Railroad Rehabilitation Unit within the section has been established with one individual completing the Railroad School in Utah on track inspection, and another individual in the process of arranging to attend the school.

With the addition of the Bristol and Newport Rail System to the Department of Transportation, this program will become a major work area within the Construction Section. 

CONTRACTS AND SPECIFICATIONS SECTION 

The function of this section is to process all preliminary engineering, construction, reconstruction and maintenance contracts. Its responsibili ites include preparing proper documentation for contracts prior to and during advertising, awarding, and the signing of the contracts. This is to insure that the contractor meets all the requirements of the Rhode 

Island Department of Transportation and the Federal Highway Ad ministration. Another important function of this section is negotiating fees with consulting firms for contracts involving work, such as corridor location studies, design studies and final design projects. This section also institutes the procedures approved by the FHWA for selecting consultants. When all procedures and requirements have been fulfilled, the 398

construction contracts and/or consultant agreements are awarded and signed by the state purchasing agent. All executed documents and signed contracts are then reordered and filed in the section. Highlights During the fiscal year, 27 construction contracts were awarded in the amount of $11,909,887.43.

Nine consultant contracts and/or agrements were awarded in the amount of $572,096.48.

DRAFTING UNIT 

The Drafting Unit is responsible for all drafting, filing, blue printing, and microfilming of highway, bridge, right-of-way and other incidental drafting projects in the Design Section. 

All blueprinting is done by this unit, along with the ordering of supplies, and the maintenance of the blueprint machines. Highlights All road and bridge plans were distributed from the drafting room. 

All state highway plans were filmed in the microfilm unit. 

Information about road construction and right-of-way plats was filed and stored in this office. Requests for such information by the general public, utilities, engineering firms, were reviewed and handled at this office. Copies of blueprints were given to engineers, and other state agencies or divisions involved in state work.

Providing ground control for statewide microwave tower site loca tions for the Motor Aid Communications System; Construction—

staking lines and grades for jobs actually being built; Right-of-

MATERIALS SECTION 

This section is responsible for testing and evaluating all materials used in the construction and maintenance of highways and bridges. 

Additionally, the Construction and Design Sections, and the Mainten ance Division are serviced on a consulting basis to provide recommendations for the design and use of various construction materials. Highlights • Research testing was performed on bridge decks, using a potentiometer, to detect corrosion of steel reinforcement in the concrete.

Benkleman Beam tests were performed to determine strength and serviceability of roadway pavement structures. • Sub-surface exploration was completed to provide design data for bridge and highway structures.

Skid testing of pavements were conducted in connection with the Planning Division's highway safety program to monitor pavement skid resistance. ⚫ Staff used nuclear density apparatus to make compaction tests on embankments and bituminous pavements. 

Laboratory and field tests of a multitude of materials were conducted, using a variety of instruments, to determine compliance with specifications and to assure quality of construction. 400

RIGHT-OF-WAY ACQUISITION SECTION 

This office operates as a service agency to all divisions of the department in real estate matters. The majority of the work load originates from the Division of Public Works, for which real estate or rights to real estate are acquired for highway and bridge purposes. Duties include title examination, appraising real estate, review of appraisals to détermine fair market value, negotiating with owners, relocation of occupants, and management of the real estate acquired. Acquisition can be accomplished by deed, condemnation, lease, easement and agreement. 

The Relocation Assistance and Property Management Unit is respon sible for selling and leasing land and/or improvement to property acquired by the department and declared excess to highway need. -( ¨´An additional function of the section is the control of outdoor advertising and junkyards along the interstate and primary state highway system.

Highlights The Appraisal and Appraisal Review Unit, appraised, reviewed and set values on 69 properties for highway use, sale and lease of excess state land and agreements for drainage and slope purposes. This unit also appraised 20 signs scheduled to be acquired because they do not conform with present outdoor advertising laws. • The Outdoor Advertising and Junkyard Control Unit condemned one non-conforming sign, removed eight non-conforming signs and 108 illegal signs in various locations throughout the state. 

Nearly 300 small signs were also removed. 

The Relocation Assistance and Property Management Unit com pleted several surveys for projects in both the planning and design stages. During this reporting period the unit relocated three families in conjunction with highway projects. • Approximately $1,200 in moving payments and $15,600 in relocation housing payments were made to various dislocatees. 

This unit also collected $13,938 in rents from tenants and former owners occupying properties acquired by condemnation. Management expenses totaled $296 resulting in a net gain of $13,642. 

Between the main Right-of-Way Acquisition Section Office and the Relocation Assistance and Property Management Unit, 9 sales of excess land were transacted totalling $43,342 in monies received.

Road Extension, Warwick. 

• The Negotiating Unit made 371 personal contacts in effecting 28 settlements on old and current acquisitions costing $89,746.06 in state and federal funds.

The primary function of this unit is designing and/or improving interstate, primary and secondary highways, according to the latest design and safety criteria. This work includes the preparation of plans, quantities, specification and contract documents.

Highlights • During the past fiscal year, 22 projects were processed through design to construction. Most of these projects were brought to completion by road design staff personnel. During this same time period, design personnel worked on additional projects, some of which are scheduled for construction in the future.

ROADSIDE LANDSCAPE UNIT 

The unit is comprised of two sections-Landscape Design and Land scape Maintenance. Landscape Design is responsible for the landscape enhancement along state highways and other state roads. This section is also responsible for the inspection of plant materials and seeding operations during beautification and construction projects. Landscape 

Maintenance is responsible for the mowing along state roads, removing dead or hazardous trees along state roads, litter pick up and brush clearing operations.

Highlights One of the outstanding accomplishments for the unit was the 

Governors Bowl Award for Beautification in May, 1977. The 

Award was conferred upon the city of Newport for outstanding accomplishments in restoration and beautification during the past several years. For the first time since the Awards origin, a special award for beautification was conferred upon Miss Doris Duke for her generous contributions and personal interests in the beautification and restoration projects which she undertook. The unit planted a Dawn Redwood Sequoia tree in Eisenhower 

Park in her honor. 

Landscape enhancement continued on Interstate 295 from Hartford Avenue to Greenville Avenue; Interstate 295 from Moun taindale Road to Mowry Road; Narragansett Pier and Scar borough Beach; and the Mendon Road Pedestrian Bridge.

STATE-AID TO CITIES AND TOWNS 

This unit administers and provides funds appropriated by the Legislature to cities and towns and provides consultation and referral services to the city/town highway departments. The unit administers and inspects the federal off systems highway aid program for local communities. Highlights The new "Safer Off Systems" local aid program forms and methods were prepared for submission to the local communities, as was the Safer Road Demonstration Program for off systems roads. the program was explained to new local administrations and additions to the state highway system were processed. 404

This year New Shoreham became eligible for the $10,000 maximum. Now 100% of the 39 local communities are eligible for the maximum funding.

Last year's off system program was successful, with 100% participation of the 22 eligible communities. To date 16 communities, 73%, have completed their projects. 

TRAFFIC ENGINEERING UNIT 

The section's main function is the design of traffic signals, highway lights, channelization and whatever other traffic control measures are necessary for the safe operation of the highways by motorists. These measures include the continuing upgrading and redesigning whenever necessary of existing facilities in conjunction with the design of traffic systems for new streets and highways. To insure the execution of these responsibilities, Traffic Engineering works hand-in-hand with other sections which design, construct and maintain our highway system par ticularly the Traffic Maintenance Section. 

Highlights As new highways and roads are opened to traffic and as the volume of cars has increased, the state, naturally, has increased responsibility for the maintenance of "Stop/Go" signal lights and "Hasher signals'. During the fiscal year 1976-1977, the traffic signal staff, with recommendations from the State Traffic Commission, studied 19 locations and installed 7 signals and 1 flashing signal. 

Also in this past year, the staff has completed the design of, or are in the process of designing 7 signals for installation throughout the state.

This unit was also involved with the estimation of traffic signal costs for future state and federal projects, and assisting the state's cities and towns with technical knowledge on traffic signals. 

The Traffic Engineering Unit has asusmed responsibility for advising the State Traffic Commission and making recommendations with regards to traffic control, parking and speed regulations on the entire state highway system. After verifying requests from local authorities for traffic control devices, the Commission approves or disapproves them at monthly meetings. One of the criteria for the Commission's decision is whether a request at hand meets the warrants; i.e., minimum requirements for any type of action on said request. The warrant information is supplied by the Traffic Engineering Unit. 405

Having received the Commission's decision, the Unit issues work orders, if necessary, and informs officials who originally made the request concerning the Commission's decision. Verification, upon their request, is also sent to state and local police authorities concerning requests that have been acted upon by the Commission. These authorities can then apply the verification as court information for traffic violations. Examples of this might be the updating and revising of speed limits on state highways in cities or towns.

Electrical Inspection: The unit is responsible for the inspection of the work being done by electrical contractors and subcontractors installing street lights and traffic signals on state and federal projects. Inspections were made to insure that state and federal regulations and specifications were followed at these installations. 

The unit was responsible for estimating the costs of signs for federal-aid appropriated State Secondary or Primary Highway Systems. These projected costs were used to approximate low bid costs for the consultor and contractor involved, and include sign locations, structure sizes, lettering series and materials specified by federal and state regulations. Sign design contracts were also submitted to the unit by consultant engineering firms in 10, 30, 90 and 100 percent stages, with plans, specifications and estimate forms, which are analyzed and reviewed. The unit adjusted shop drawings and listed numerically all parts of the plan for final construction. Once a contract was awarded and installation begun, the unit inspected the work of the sign contractors and subcontractors to ensure that the signs were erected in accordance with federal and state regulations and specifications. The unit was also responsible for field investigations of already existing signs and issuing work orders for the updating and replacement of them by the Traffic Maintenance Section. 

The unit reviewed all plans submitted by consultants for the purpose of installing street lights on state and interstate highways. 

It also prepared plans for street lighting systems needed through. out the state highway system. The state owns and maintains over 

2500 mercury vapor street lights at this time. 

Designation of the size and location of curb cuts for access to any property on the state highways is empowered to the Traffic 

Engineering Unit for safety reasons. Decisions were made with re- 406

gard to existing state standards. Applicants were given approval or recommendations after a careful investigation.

UTILITIES UNIT 

This unit coordinates utility rearrangements between the state and private utility companies, railroads and municipalities, during road and bridge construction. Pre-bid conferences are arranged between the state and various contractors to clarify specifications, and to assure that contract documents are understood by prospective bidders. The unit makes certain that all the involved utilities, municipalilties, and railroads are present at these meetings to answer and discuss any questions relative to the adjustment of their facilities made necessary by the project. 

Pre-construction conferences are held after the contract has been awarded to again notify the appropriate utility companies. Engineering duties such as reviewing plans, field checking, taking quantities, preparing cost estimates are also undertaken. The maintaining of numerous office files is a major facet of the utilities operations. Highlights • Numerous railroad highway grade crossing safety improvements were a primary target undertaken by the department. The Utilities 

Unit processed construction and maintenance agreements toward their completion. 

Demolition of the old Red Bridge, Atwells Avenue Bridge and Charles Street Bridge Superstructure/Replacements were undertaken. The Utilities Unit played a major role during their construction. 

Reconstruction of Route 116, in Lincoln, opposite the entrances to the new Lincoln Mall, and reconstruction of Diamond Hill Road to the new Lincoln Mall, and reconstruction of Diamond Hill Road in Cumberland, were high priorities this year. The unit again was an instrumental factor in coordinating utility work towards construction completion. 407

NORTH KINGSTOWN--Wickford, R. I.—The annual outdoor art festival of Wickford is perhaps the oldest such festival in Rhode Island. 

Hundreds of artists and craftsmen display their works over a gala weekend of activity. The exhibits are arranged all through the section where 

Wickford's colonial houses are concentrated making it an ideal setting for such a festival.

RHODE ISLAND STATE COUNCIL ON THE ARTS DONALD J. ALDRICH, Chairman Established 1967-Statutory Authority, Chapter 16-46 

Office-4365 Post Road, East Greenwich 02818 (As of September 7, 1977-334 Westminster Mall, Providence)

19 $975,105 The arts are where the people are. In FY '77 attendance at arts events funded by Rhode Island State Council on the Arts grants or programs was approximately 750,000. The Council's mandate to insure the role of the arts as a contributor to the lives of the citizens of the state is fulfilled primarily in two ways: first, through direct grants to arts organizations and individual artists which insures both a means for artists to make a living and the availability of quality performances, exhibitions, workshops and special events to Rhode Island citizens; and second, through Council developed and administered programs which reach into the community, hiring artists to share their talents with all segments of the public, young and old, in schools, libraries, drop-in centers, hospitals, senior citizen homes, the ACI and community arts councils, to name a few.

Highlights • Institutional Support: This grant program was developed to assist the major arts organizations of the state in meeting their regular operating expenses. The purpose of these grants is not to absorb the complete operating deficit of any organization but to develop appropriate participation by the state in the support of its public cultural assets. Thirteen organizations received such grants including the Trinity Square Repertory Company, the Rhode Island 

School of Design's Museum of Fine Art, the Rhode Island Phil harmonic Orchestra, Artists Internationale, Rites and Reason and 

The Puppet Workshop. 

Special Projects: The Special Projects grants provide funds to assist and develop specific projects in any art area which will be of benefit to the Rhode Island public. Among projects funded this year were: Hera Educational Foundation-a series of exhibitions, lectures and performances in all the arts for the enrichment of 411

southern Rhode Island cultural life; City of Providence, Department of Parks—a festival of conceptual/environmental art to explore the possibility of alternate forms of public events and to offer artists a framework for creation in the park. The Puppet 

Workshop-the development of storefront theatre in downtown Providence for performances by The Puppet Workshop and other performing groups at low cost; and Rhode Island Indian Councilfor the expansion and improvement of ongoing cultural enrichment programs for the Indian population of Rhode Island. • Touring Grants: Touring grants are awarded to arts organizations to help defray the cost of performances and exhibitions which travel throughout the state. Three organizations received touring grants: Image Company, New Music Ensemble and Young Rhode Island Shakespeare Company. 

Grants-in-Aid to Individual Artists: Grants-in-Aid to individual 

Artists is designed to meet the needs of the qualified Rhode Island artist directly involved in the creative process. The Council awarded unconditional grants of $2,500 apiece of five artists: a composer, a writer, a two dimensional artist, a three dimensional artist and a film-maker. 

• Dance Touring Program: The Council coordinates Rhode Island's participation in the National Endowment for the Arts Dance Residency Program under which nationally recognized dance companies offer local performances and workshops. Five sponsoring agencies, including Artists Internationale and the Pawtucket Parks and Recreation Department, participated in the program and presented six dance companies in residence. • Arts in Education Program: Arts in Education, a program available to all Rhode Island schools, was developed in the belief that the working artist represents a valuable educational resource. The program helps to provide the services of artists to work with edu cators to plan and implement projects which integrate the arts into the educational process. Among the programs funded were a dramatics project at Fox Point School, Providence; metal-smithing in Middletown Public Schools, Middletown; a mural project at 

Northern Lincoln Elementary School, Lincoln. In FY '77 Arts in 

Education had projects in 30 schools working with 6,068 students and 270 teachers. • Artists-in-Residence: The Council employs six artists-in-residencea visual artist, dancer, writer, composer, filmmaker and craftsmanthrough a grant from the National Endowment for the Arts- 412

Artists-in-Schools. These artists-in-residence serve the Council as artists in their discipline, program developers, recruiters of arts specialists and consultants to the state's school systems and community organizations. This program is designed so that the artists may spend a portion of their time doing their own work. • Services to Schools: Through Services to Schools the Council administers funds contracted with major state organizations, Trinity Square Repertory Company, the Rhode Island Philharmonic and the Rhode Island School of Design's Museum of Arts, for arts programs rendered to the school systems by those organizations. • Center for Career Education in the Arts: This program began its second year fully accredited by the Providence School Department and was funded by a Vocational Training Act grant from the federal Department of Education and HEW. It conducted a program in vocational training and art skills in five disciplines, theatre, dance, music, 2and 3-dimensional art and writing for high school students interested in making the arts their career. This year more than 60 students were involved in the program attending three hours a day, five days a week. Additional funding from the state's 

Department of Vocational Education supported ARTSWORKS, the outreach component of this program.

• Providence City Spirit: The Providence City Spirit Project is a process of community involvement education that utilizes the arts. as a mechanism to help fourth-grade students and teachers explore the resources in themselves and their city. This year 300 fourth-grade students in the Providence School System explored the city through the arts using photography, painting, poetry, drama, video and architecture. This program is a cooperative venture between the National Endowment for the Arts, the Council and the city of Providence. 

• Community Arts Program: The Community Arts Program assists in the continuing development and strengthening of arts programs throughout the state providing workshops, performances by professional artists and technical assistance to hospitals, senior citizen centers, ethnic, low-income and rural communities. This year the 

Council's Arts and the Aging Program was incorporated into the 

Community Arts Program. The Community Arts Program is fund ed through a grant from the National Endowment for the Arts. 

Expansion Arts, the Council and local funds. In FY '77 this program employed 38 artists in 26 sites reaching over 5,000 people. 

Arts in Corrections: Arts in Corrections consists of workshops con ducted by professional artists, performances and exhibitions 413

throughout the facilities of the state's Department of Corrections serving over 400 inmates. Interest and participation in the program has doubled during the past year. Among highlights of this year's program were the publication of “Cold Storage”, an inmate publication of poetry, photographs and paintings, and four major inmate art shows at the Jewish Community Center, Roger Wil liams Park, the Warwick Mall and the Cranston Library. This program is funded by a grant from the National Endowment for the Arts Expansion Arts, the Council and the Rhode Island Department of Corrections. 

• CETA Program: Under the Comprehensive Employment and 

Training Act (CETA) the Council employs four artists-dancer, theatre specialist, craftsman and visual artist-to work in various community programs as arts specialists in their discipline, program developers and consultants. • Final Steps: Final steps assists in the strengthening of local community arts councils and arts associations, capable of assessing the arts needs of each community, assuming the responsibility for funding of arts projects and for the design of an overall community program. Under this program community arts councils and associations may apply to the Council for amounts up to $1,000 to be matched by their local municipal governments for arts programming. In addition, Final Steps Program coordinated four technical assistance seminars in the areas of organizational goals and objectives, management, fund raising and publicity. • Ticket Endowment: The Ticket Endowment Program subsidizes one-half of the cost of the lowest priced ticket for qualified art and cultural events. Its growth over the years has been consistent and particularly noticeable among senior citizens and inner-city community organizations. In FY '77, 12,363 individuals benefited from the program. 

• Rhode Island Public Arts Employment Program (RIPAEP) : 

Operating with a grant from the U. S. Department of Commerce for calendar 1976, the state created jobs for unemployed and underemployed artists by providing matching funds to public and private employers for arts projects. More than 200 Rhode Islanders were employed full or part time as a result of this program. Thirtynine positions have continued after the initial grant period ended. 

Among them are actors and technicians at Trinity Square Repertory Company and an exhibition designer for R. I. Historical Society. 414

RHODE ISLAND ATOMIC ENERGY COMMISSION ARTHUR L. QUIRK, Chairman Established 1955-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-27 

Main Office-Rhode Island Nuclear Science Center South Ferry Road, Narragansett 02882 Full Time Employees 

Total Expenditures (including debt service) 11 $323,000

The Commission has responsibility to: study and make recommendations concerning atomic regulations; advise the Governor and the General Assembly about atomic industrial development in the state; coordinate development and regulatory activities of the state; construct, operate, and make available for research a nuclear reactor; inform the Governor of the appropriate time for transferring some U. S. Nuclear Regulatory 

Commission regulatory duties to the state. 

The major activities of the commission during fiscal year 1975 were associated with studies concerning the feasibility of a nuclear generating station at Charlestown and with the research programs and operation of the research reactor at the R. I. Nuclear Science Center.

Highlights • The preliminary safety analysis report of the New England Power Company for the construction of a nuclear generating station at Charlestown, R.I. was received. It is expected that the review by the U. S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission will require at least two years. During that period, mainly by participation in studies performed at the state level, the Commission expects to formulate its position concerning the proposal. • During the last legislative session, 76-H 7459, an act relating to Radiation Control was passed. The act creates a Radiation Control 

Advisory Commission and specifies that one member be a repre sentative of the R. I. Atomic Energy Commission. The Commission has been appointed and several meetings have been held to consider the proposal of the Radiation Control Administration for the regulation of some radioactive materials and radiation producing machine. It is expected that the review will be completed early in the next fiscal year with a public hearing planned for September 1977. 415

Three unannounced inspections of the Nuclear Science Center were performed by the U. S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission. 

They consisted of two general inspections of operating records and techniques and one safeguard (uranium accountability) inspection. No areas of noncompliance were found and no citations were issued. However, the NRC again recommended that the 

RIAEC reorganize to provide an individual, not a part of the daily operation of the Science Center, to oversee and audit its operations for the RIAEC. This recommendation has also been made by the office of the Auditor General. This reorganization was accomplished late in the year and becomes effective in fiscal year 1978. 

The total of all grants for the conduct of research programs at the Science Center is approximately $1,000,000. • In October, in conjunction with the URI Graduate School of Oceanography, a public open house was held on a Saturday and Sunday. In spite of inclement weather over 6000 individuals visited the reactor. • In order to provide neutrons and gamma rays for over 35 experiment progrms, the reactor operated at a power level of 2 megawatts, an average of 33 hours per week. (Thirty-five hurs per week is the maximum for single shift operations). During this operation, 3,600 irradiations involving more than 6,500 samples were performed. • Based on research performed at the Science Center, 3 Doctor of 

Philosophy Degrees and 4 Master of Science Degrees were awarded this fiscal year. Through June 1977, 21 Ph.D's and 23 M.S. degrees have been awarded by seven universities. There are currently about 24 additional graduate students working towards advanced degrees. 416

BLACKSTONE VALLEY DISTRICT 

FRANCIS A. FLEURANT, Chairman Established 1947-Statutory Authority, Chapter 46-21 

Street Address—102 Campbell Avenue, East Providence Mail Address-P. O. Box A, Rumford 02916  

55 $2,321,084.91

The Commission is authorized to combat water pollution within or emanating from the Blackstone Valley District, defined to consist of the cities of East Providence, Central Falls and Pawtucket, the towns of Cumberland and Lincoln, and a portion of the town of Smithfield, and to plan, construct, and operate facilities to that end. The facilities constructed with the proceeds of $20,400,000 worth of bonds and $2,- 000,000 in short-term notes issued to date are known as the Blackstone 

Valley District Project. 

Highlights • Work was completed in September, 1976, on the separation of the sanitary and storm water sewer connection at Hatch Street, Cumberland, at a total cost of $18,200. • The Commission voted, in July, 1976, to proceed forthwith with the construction of a septic dumping facility in Lincoln. As of 

June 30, 1977, engineering and EPA requirements for this project had been met, and land was in the process of being condemned on which to erect the facility. Construction of the septic dumping facility is expected to begin in December, 1977, with completion expected by June, 1978. The total cost of the project, which will serve all of the communities within the District, is estimated to be $280,000. • All appointed members of the Blackstone Valley District Commission filed financial statements with the Conflict of Interest Commission. • The Commission's Regulation No. 5 was amended, and Regulation No. 6 became effective May 5, 1977, after public notice. 417

• Engineering studies continued on the rehabilitation of the Bucklin Point Wastewater Treatment Plant; a public environmental assessment hearing was held in April, 1977. Construction on this 

71½ million dollar project is expected to begin in December, 1978. • In August, 1976, purchase orders totalling $102,278.00 were issued to Worthington Pump Corporation, BIF, and Graybar Electric

Company for various equipment needed to implement the 1½ million dollar correction of the pump system at the treatment plant; installation of this equipment has been under way, performed by Westcott Construction Corporation at a cost of $134,000, and should be completed by December 30, 1977.

The Commission is studying the feasibility of construction of the 

West Sneech Pond Road Interceptor in Cumberland, Rhode Is land, should federal and state funds become available.

RHODE ISLAND CONSUMERS' COUNCIL EDWIN P. PALUMBO, Executive Director Established 1966–Statutory Authority Chapter 42-42 of 

General Laws of 1956, as amended 

Main Office-365 Broadway, Providence 02909  

$161,800.00

The Consumer's Council represents the public in matters affecting consumer interests and its legislative charge ranges from the conducting of studies and the recommendation of legislative programs, to the furthering of consumer education and the mediation and resolving of individ ual consumer complaints. The council is the people's official advocate in regulatory matters, such as utilities, insurance and health. It participates before administrative hearing bodies and reports to the appropriate authorities for prosecution violations of laws or regulations affecting consumers.

Division of Consumer Affairs 

The division is responsible for council activities designed to assure effective protection against commercial injustice. 

Mediation: The division makes every effort to resolve consumer-business disputes by mediation. For example, when a buyer files an oral or written complaint about a product or service with the council, the seller involved is notified of the complaint (usually by letter) in order to afford the seller an opportunity to present an account of the transaction. 

Such an approach creates an atmosphere in which the division can best concentrate its efforts toward bringing accord between the disputing parties. Where circumstances indicate, referrals are made to other governmental departments for enforcement and where warranted, prosecution. 

Consumer Complaint Statistics: The number and monetary value of the consumer complaints processed by the division rises steadily each year with the vast majority of the complaints satisfactorily resolved. 

Referrals number in the thousands and their dollar value in the hundreds of thousands.

Education: The division sponsors a meaningful and comprehensive consumer education program to enable consumers to make sound buying 420

decisions, acquire skill in personal money management, and recognize and avoid consumer frauds and deceptions. The program is tailored, through qualified speakers, to the interests of junior high school, high school and college students; young families, middle age families, the elderly, and the poor as separate groups. Whether the class is in a school room, community center, or other facility, the program introduces current materials for consumer education and utilities resource. persons to update information and understanding of the marketplace.

Information: The division aims its consumer information program at the entire community using all forms of publicity and communication. 

The program includes the publication of pamphlets on tips to help the consumer be better informed and more adequately prepared before making decisions, as well as to have a greater appreciation of consumer rights and respnsibilities. It also includes the distribution of consumer. interest publications of federal departments and agencies. 

Legislation: Recognizing a pressing need to make law do more for consumers, the division is particularly active in initiating and promoting all types of consumer legislation. An indication of its initiative to obtain remedial legislation is the number of new laws passed by the state legislature. Among the major enactments are those that protect the consumer by: -Expanding the availability of the small claims procedure in the district court system in all cases where the amount of controversy does not exceed $500. --Providing consumers with a three day cancellation clause in health spa contracts. -Requiring that the unit price must be marked on items. -Directing stores to provide for cash refunds on returned merchandise in instances where they fail to prominently display their exchange policy. -Providing for the licensing of travel agents and agencies. —Allowing doctors to prescribe brand or generic drugs. Signing appropriate line on prescription form. -Offering free bus service to senior citizens between the hours of 

10:00 a.m. to 3:00 p.m.

Exempting from the state sales tax the amount of money granted the buyer as a trade-in allowance on a car being traded-in towards the purchase of a new or used motor vehicle purchase. -Providing for a contract lay-a-way sales and right of cancellation. —Mandating a pull date for packaged bakery products. 421

-Requiring the registration of charitable organizations and professional fund raisers and limits their expenses. -Establishing rules and regulations to assist consumers in the assistance of consumer utility billing service matters. 

Regulation Affairs: To provide the public with competent representation in public utility matters, the council administers a vigorous program to protect the peoples' interests in matters ranging from utility requests for rate increases or service curtailments to requested changes in Blue Cross and Blue Shield rates to automobile insurance proposals to higher charges and other regulated matters. 

The council's efforts have helped control numerous increases sought by various regulated industries and has brought about many significant reforms. Among these are the introduction of an economy telephone service available to Rhode Island subscribers, as well as a reduction in service charges to the elderly. In addition, Rhode Island law now allows any utility to offer discount rates to subscribers.

The council's role includes matters which come before federal regulatory bodies as well. The interest and purpose of this extended representation is to provide Rhode Island consumers with the most complete and comprehensive representation and protection available. By exercising a continuing surveillance the consumer's council is able to provide consumers with the type of active interest and review necessary to achieve effective public utility consumer protection. 

Division of Credit Counseling 

The division is responsible for a rehabilitation and rescue service as well as a preventive effort. The non-profit counseling service offered by the division is of real benefit to people who are especially vulnerable to problems that grow from misuse of credit and lack of debt prevention education. These people have limited resources which lead them to unwise purchases, to credit buying that is deceptive in terms of high interest rates and exaggerated claims, to financial commitments they do not understand, and to other market-place pitfalls.

Other beneficiaries of the service are people in financial distress through misfortune. Even though people may be cautious in their credit buyings, unforeseen difficulties can arise such as prolonged illnesses, deaths in the family and losses of jobs. The service is unique in that it is completely sponsored by a state agency. Moreover, it has been so successful that other states have asked for advice on how to establish similar programs. 422

National statistics indicate that nine out of ten bankruptcies are personal. This serious situation has prompted many communities across the country to establish non-profit credit counseling services. The net result, however, has been almost insignificant compared to the mounting size of the problem.

RHODE ISLAND HISTORICAL PRESERVATION COMMISSION ANTOINETTE F. DOWNING, Chairman ERIC HERTFELDER, Executive Director Established 1968-Statutory Authority, Chapter 42-45 

Office-The Old State House, 150 Benefit Street, Providence 02903 

Full Time Employees

Total Expenditures 

18 $329,995.00

The Historical Preservation Commission ensures that Rhode Island's historic and cultural resources receive consideration for their preservation and utilization in statewide and community development planning. The Commission is appointed by the Governor and works in association with the State Historic Preservation Officer, also appointed by the Governor. The Commission is responsible for conducting a statewide survey of historic structures, sites, districts and objects; compiling an inventory of statewide historic property; nominating sites for inclusion in the National Register of Historic Places; administering a grantsin-aid program for the acquisition or restoration of properties listed on the National Register; and developing a State Historic Preservation Plan. 

Additional duties including preparing a State Register of Historic Places; conducting a curatorial program for state-owned properties; regulating archaeological investigations; reviewing federally funded development projects for impact on cultural resources; and providing preservation information to other agencies, organizations and the public. Highlights Statewide Historical Survey: During the fiscal year, survey activity continued to focus on the nine Rhode Island Communities with Community Development, block-grant programs. Surveys, including published reports, were completed for East Providence, Woon-socket and the West Broadway Neighborhood in Newport. Re- 423

ports will be published in FY 1978 for Central Falls, Pawtucket, Narragansett Pier, Warwick, the Southern Thames Street Neigh borhood in Newport and the South Providence Neighborhood in Providence. A survey in Cranston was initiated in FY 1977 and will be published as a report in the coming year. A tenth community, North Kingstown, has begun an intensive survey supported by their newly created Community Development program and a 

Commission grant.

In addition to these efforts, surveys were continued in Foster and Bristol and a survey has begun in Lincoln with local sponsorship coming from the town's Bicentennial Committee. The Historical Preservation Commission also assisted in a thematic survey of the engineering sites in Rhode Island sponsored by the Historic 

American Engineering Record (HAER), a branch of the National

Park Service, and supervised locally by the staff of the Old Slater 

Mill Museum in Pawtucket. 

Preliminary "Broadbrush" surveys of Cumberland, Johnston,

Exeter, and Hopkinton were published during FY 1977 and similar publications are being readied for Westerly, Coventry, North Providence and Richmond. In all, some 8,000 archeological sites. and historic structures, districts and objects were recorded during the year. From this number, approximately 1900 individual properties and some 70 districts were selected for inclusion in the State Inventory. 

National Register: Twenty-five historic sites and districts were entered on the National Register of Historic Places during the year bringing the Rhode Island total to 235 entries, 41 of which are historic districts. Properties listed on the National Register are also listed on the State Register. Two of the new entries (one district and one individual property) are National Historic Landmarks.

The Commission continued its policy of notifying a large number of concerned individuals, officials and state and local agencies at each stage during the nomination process. In addition, public meetings were held in connection with historic district nominations. • Grants-in-Aid: Twenty-seven projects were awarded grants totalling $110,000.00 in 1977. When matched equally by the grant recipients, the awards represent at least $220,000.00 in preservation work for Rhode Island. The projects include a wide range of architectural periods and consist of both publicly and privately 424

owned properties. In order to benefit the entire population of 

Rhode Island, a conscious effort was made to award grants to proj ects throughout the state. 

In Warren, the Massasoit Historical Society was awarded $5,- 000.00 to restore the James Maxwell Birthplace, one of the earliest homes in that town. The Kentish Guards were given $1,500.00 to replace the electrical system in their East Greenwich Armory. In 

Newport, the owner of the Isaac Rice House received $5,000.00 to restore the exterior of a building important to the Black history of 

Rhode Island. Pawtucket's newly formed Children's Museum, Inc. 

was awarded $12,000.00 to restore the exterior of the Pitcher-Goff 

House, the finest remaining early Italianate mansion in that city. 

In Wickford, the Episcopal Diocese was given $2,000.00 to complete the interior painting of the historic Old Narragansett Church. 

During 1977, a full-time restoration architect was added to the Grants staff, ensuring that the work completed under this program is of the highest quality and meets the rigid professional standards of the National Park Service. 

Environmental Review: The State Historic Preservation Officer and the Historical Preservation Commission are responsible for reviewing and commenting on federally funded or licensed projects which may affect the state's historic resources. Major reviews were conducted during the year in response to Community Development Act projects, highways, the Local Public Works Program and the Northeast Corridor Rail Project. As in previous years, the effort devoted to environmental review increased as the Commission and the State Historic Preservation Officer responded to environmental impact statements, surplus federal property notices and state coastal resources management reviews. 

• Intergovernmental Relations: An important role of the Commis sion is the coordination of historic preservation activities among federal, state and municipal agencies, as well as private groups. 

Throughout the year, the Commission assisted agencies in activities and in finding ways to minimize harmful impacts where they occurred. Close cooperation with state agencies such as the Department of Transportation, Statewide Planning, the Department of Natural Resources and the Coastal Resources Management 

Council ensured that historic resources were given timely and thorough consideration in project planning and development. 

As in previous years, the Commission assisted local planning officials and historic districts commissions with technical advice and 425

information from the statewide survey. The Commission also aided private developers interested in adaptive reuse projects and began implementation of the Tax Reform Act of 1976, which encourages the rehabilitation of registered historic commercial structures through tax incentives. • Public Information and Education: The Commission functions as an information source, disseminating preservation data to state, municipal and private organizations, businesses and individuals through its Newsletter and other publications, conferences and public meetings. Various members of the staff spoke extensively throughout the year, before state, municipal and private organizations, explaining the various programs of the Commission. Staff answered approximately 200 specific requests for preservation information from institutions, students and private citizens. 

A new development in FY 1977 was an attempt by the Commission to encourage use of its publications in Rhode Island school systems as supplemental texts in local history courses. Indepth and preliminary survey reports are distributed to home owners in the area surveyed and to local, state and national officials, libraries and civic groups. It became apparent that use of these reports in schools would serve to make preservation information available to greater numbers of residents. Schools in Woonsocket, Newport, Johnston, Exeter, Hopkinton and Cumberland will incorporate survey reports in this year's curriculum. The Commission will continue to offer material to these and other school departments in the future. 

• National Participation: During the year the State Historic Pres ervation Officer for Rhode Island was elected President of the 

National Conference of State Historic Preservation Officers. In addition, Rhode Island's statewide survey and publications program was recognized by the National Park Service and the National Trust for Historic Preservation as being among the finest in the nation. 426

COMMISSION FOR HUMAN RIGHTS CLEON E. HARVEY, Chairman 

JAMES R. WARRICK, JR., Executive Director

Established 1949, Statutory Authority in Chapter 28-5 

Central Office-334 Westminster Mall, Providence 02903  

10 $189,564.00 The Commission is an administrative enforcement agency whose purpose is to eradicate the pervasive discrimination that exists in Rhode Island. Five commissioners are the policymakers of the agency who run the agency, pass judgment on cases, and delegate duties and responsi bilities to the director. Since its inception in 1949, the Commission's responsibility has been increased from eliminating discrimination in employment, to protection against housing, public accommodations, and credit discrimination. This provides citizens protection against illegal acts of discrimination on the basis of race and color, ancestral origin, sex, physical handicap, marital status, and religion. The Commission is not authorized to protect persons against discrimination because of age. This is the responsibility of the Department of Labor. 

The Commission has in the past two years been called upon to play an increasingly significant role in the enforcement of civil rights. The 

Governor's Executive Order No. 14 establishes equal employment op portunity requirements for all agencies of government and for all contractors and vendors benefited by doing business with the state. The 

Commission enforces compliance with that order. At the federal level, the Commission has been designated an Equal Employment Opportunity Commission 706 deferral agency. The federal agency defers complaints originating in the state for state agency resolutions of the controversy. Like most state civil rights agencies, the Commission dedicates a substantial part of the resources provided by the legislature to the enforcement of civil rights laws. 427

Total expenditures over three years were $497,162. Each year there was an average increase of $26,801, representing a response to inflationary trends and the addition of one employee. During the period costs to the state were offset by federal funds. They were used to defray 30% of agency expenditures and contributed to staffing of 50% of the investigator positions. 

The commission awarded more than $106,220 in annualized damages to victims of discrimination and also secured eight jobs for 428

complaining parties. In addition to compensating the individuals for being subjected to illegal acts, state revenue was increased with the payment of taxes on the awards. 

The number of cases originating on Aquidneck Island increased from 11 in the previous year to 27 this year. This is due primarily to the positioning of an office in Newport.

The Human Rights Act, 1977 failed passage during the period. 

It was placed in the sub-committee of the senate judiciary committee.

RHODE ISLAND LOTTERY COMMISSION PETER J. O'CONNELL, Executive Director Established 1974-Statutory Authority, Chapter 20, Section 1, Title 42-61 of Public Laws of 1974 Office-39 Warren Avenue, East Providence 02914 Full Time Employees 

45 

Gross Revenue from Ticket Sales 

$19,589,725

Prize Awards 

$ 8,904,381

Operating, Advertising and Commission Expense 

$ 3,261,466

Net Income 

$ 8,329,200 The Rhode Island Lottery Commission was established in 1974 as a self-supporting, independent revenue-producing arm of state government. 

All operating expenses are defrayed through the revenues produced by the sale of tickets of lottery games and related activities. By law, a minimum of 30 percent of gross revenues from ticket sales is returned to the state as general income each fiscal year. A minimum of 45 percent of gross ticket sales revenues is returned each year to ticket buyers in prizes. The remaining 25 percent of gross ticket sales revenues is available for operating and administrative expense and if not expended, is also turned over to the state.

Highlights At the end of fiscal 1977 the Lottery accrued to the State General Fund the sum of $8,330,00, as its net income, which will be paid to the state in the Lottery's fiscal year 1978. This sum represented 

42.5% of gross ticket sales revenues an increase of 25.0% over net income returned to the state in the previous fiscal year. 429

Gross Income from ticket sales was $19,589,725 an increase of $1,883,235 (10.6%) over the previous year. • Prize awards were $8,904,381 (45.5%) an increase of $450,230 over fiscal 1976.

Since its date of inception the Lottery has accrued and/or paid to the State of Rhode Island $20,656,000. This sum represents net income from ticket sales and operations from May, 1974 (date of initial drawing) thru June 1977 (end of fiscal 1977). • Because of the Lottery's successful experience with Play Ball, the

Lottery introduced two new instant winner games during fiscal 1977, Double Bingo and Instant Baseball. Both games were highly successful and were conducted concurrently during the baseball "season." Additionally, the Grand Lot, a 50-cent weekly game, was continued throughout the year, as was The Numbers, a daily game with televised drawings six times a week, and Lots of Luck, another weekly game, ran for part of the fiscal year.

As the fiscal year ended, a new instant winner game, Cup Challenge, was about to be introduced to be run concurrently with the America's Cup trials and races in Newport. Advance interest indicated that Cup Challenge would be another popular instant winner game. • Progress was being made rapidly to install on-line betting machines early in the new fiscal year. The on-line system will enable the 

Lottery to accept considerably more bets a day and have drawing shortly after sales closing times.
